Preface

These notes are designed for those who either plan to work in differential geometry,
or at least want to have a good reason
not
to do it.
It should be more than sufficient for a semester-long course.

Differential geometry exploits several branches of mathematics, including
real analysis,
measure theory,
calculus of variations,
differential equations,
elementary and convex geometry,
topology, and more.
This subject is wide even at the beginning.
For that reason, it is fun and painful both to teach and to study.

In this book, we discuss smooth curves and surfaces — the main gate to differential geometry.
This subject provides a collection of examples and ideas critical for further study.
It is wise to become a master in this subject before making further steps — there is no need to rush.

We give a general overview of the subject, keeping it
problem-centered,
elementary,
visual,
and virtually rigorous; we allow gaps that belong to other branches of mathematics, most of these subjects discussed briefly in the preliminaries.

We focus on the techniques that are absolutely essential for further study.
For that reason we omit a number of topics that are traditionally included in the introductory texts;
for example, we almost do not touch
minimal surfaces
and the Peterson–Codazzi formulas.

At the same time, we get to applications that are not in the scope of typical introductory texts.

The first example is the theorem of Vladimir Ionin and Herman Pestov about the Moon in a puddle (
7E
).
This theorem might be the simplest meaningful example of the so-called
local to global theorems
which lies in the heart of differential geometry;
for that reason, it is a good answer to the main question of this book — ‘‘What is differential geometry?’’.

Other examples include the theorem of Sergei Bernstein on saddle graphs (
13C
) and the theorem of Stephan Cohn-Vossen
on a two-sided infinite geodesic (
17E
).

These notes are based on the lectures given at the MASS program (Mathematics Advanced Study Semesters at Pennsylvania State University) in the fall of 2018.
Many of the topics were presented by Yurii Burago in his lectures teaching the first author at Leningrad University.
We extensively used textbooks
by Wilhelm Blaschke and Kurt Leichtweiss
[
11
]
,
by Aleksei Chernavskii
[
22
]
,
and by Victor Toponogov
[
93
]
, and also the lecture notes of Sergei Ivanov
[
46
]
;
many advanced exercises are taken from
[
77
]
.
The last chapter is based on the introductory material in the book by Stephanie Alexander, Vitali Kapovitch, and the first author
[
5
]
.
We want to thank
Stephanie Alexander,
Yurii Burago,
Berk Ceylan,
Nina Lebedeva,
Alexander Lytchak,
Benjamin McKay,
and all the students in our class
for their help.

The present work is partially supported by NSF grant DMS-2005279
and by the Simons Foundation under grant #584781.

Anton Petrunin and

Sergio Zamora Barrera.

Preliminaries

The first section in this chapter about metric spaces should be read before going further.
The rest of the chapter should be used as a quick reference while reading the rest of the book;
it contains statements of necessary results and references to complete proofs.

A
Metric spaces

We assume familiarity with the notion of distance in the
Euclidean space.
In this section, we briefly discuss its generalization and fix notations that will be used further.
All these topics are discussed in detail in the introductory part of the book by Dmitri Burago, Yuri Burago, and Sergei Ivanov
[
13
]
.

Definitions

Metric
is a function that returns a real value
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits(x,y)
for any pair of elements
x,y
in a given set
\mathcal{X}
and satisfies the following axioms for any triple
x,y,z\in\mathcal{X}
:

(a)

Positiveness:

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits(x,y)\geqslant 0.

(b)

x=y
if and only if

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits(x,y)=0.

(c)

Symmetry:

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits(x,y)=\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits(y,x).

(d)

Triangle inequality:

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits(x,z)\leqslant\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits(x,y)+\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits(y,z).

A set with a metric is called a
metric space
, and the elements of the set are called
points
.

Shortcut for distance

Usually, we consider only one metric on a set, therefore we can denote the metric space and its underlying set by the same letter, say
\mathcal{X}
.
In this case, we also use the shortcut notations
|{x}-\penalty{y}|
or
|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{X}}
for the
distance

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits(x,y)
from
x
to
y
in
\mathcal{X}
.
For example, the triangle inequality can be written as

|{x}-\penalty{z}|_{\mathcal{X}}\leqslant|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{X}}+|{y}-\penalty{z}|_{\mathcal{X}}.

The Euclidean space and plane, as well as the real line, will be the most important examples of metric spaces.
In these examples, the introduced notation
|{x}-\penalty{y}|
for the distance from
x
to
y
has perfect sense as the norm of the vector
x-y
.
However, let us emphasise that in a general metric space, the difference of points
x-y
has no meaning.

More examples

Usually, if we say
plane
or
space
we mean the
Euclidean
plane or space.
However, the plane (as well as the space) admits many other metrics; for example, the so-called
Manhattan metric
from the following exercise.

0.1. Exercise.

Consider the function

\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits(p,q)=|x_{1}-x_{2}|+|y_{1}-y_{2}|,

where
p=(x_{1},y_{1})
and
q=(x_{2},y_{2})
are points in the coordinate plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Show that
\mathop{\rm dist}\nolimits
is a metric on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Another example: the
discrete space
— an arbitrary nonempty set
\mathcal{X}
with the metric defined as
|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{X}}=0
if
x=y
and
|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{X}}=1
otherwise.

Subspaces

Any subset of a metric space is also a metric space, by restricting the original metric to the subset;
the obtained metric space is called a
subspace
.
In particular, all subsets of the Euclidean space are metric spaces.

Balls

Given a point
p
in a metric space
\mathcal{X}
and a real number
R\geqslant 0
, the set of points
x
on the distance less than
R
(at most
R
) from
p
is called the
open
(respectively
closed
)
ball
of radius
R
with center
p
.
The open ball is denoted as
B(p,R)
or
B(p,R)_{\mathcal{X}}
;
the second notation is used if we need to emphasize that the ball is from
\mathcal{X}
.
Formally speaking

B(p,R)=B(p,R)_{\mathcal{X}}=\left\{\,{x\in\mathcal{X}}\,:\,{|{x}-\penalty{p}|_{\mathcal{X}}<R}\,\right\}.

Analogously, the closed ball is denoted as
\bar{B}[p,R]
or
\bar{B}[p,R]_{\mathcal{X}}
and

\bar{B}[p,R]=\bar{B}[p,R]_{\mathcal{X}}=\left\{\,{x\in\mathcal{X}}\,:\,{|{x}-\penalty{p}|_{\mathcal{X}}\leqslant R}\,\right\}.

0.2. Exercise.

(a)

Let
p
and
q
be points in
\mathcal{X}
be a metric space.
Show that if
\bar{B}[p,2]\subset\penalty\bar{B}[q,1]
, then
\bar{B}[p,2]=\penalty\bar{B}[q,1]
.

(b)

Construct a metric space
\mathcal{X}
with two points
p
and
q
such that the strict inclusion

B(p,\tfrac{3}{2})\subsetneq B(q,1)
holds.

Isometries and motions

Let
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
be two metric spaces.
A map
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathcal{Y}

is
called
distance-preserving
if

|{f(x)}-\penalty{f(y)}|_{\mathcal{Y}}=|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{X}}

for any
x,y\in{\mathcal{X}}
.

A bijective distance-preserving map is called an
isometry
.
An isometry from a metric space to itself is also referred a
motion
.
Two metric spaces are called
isometric
if there exists an isometry between them.

0.3. Exercise.

Show that any distance-preserving map
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
is
injective
;
that is,
f(x)\neq f(y)
for any pair of distinct points
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
.

Continuity

0.4. Definition.

Let
{\mathcal{X}}
be a metric space.
A sequence of points
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots
in
{\mathcal{X}}

converges

if there is a point

x_{\infty}\in{\mathcal{X}}
such that
|{x_{\infty}}-\penalty{x_{n}}|\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.
That is, for every
\varepsilon>0
, there is a natural number
N
such that for all
n\geqslant N
, we have

|{x_{\infty}}-\penalty{x_{n}}|_{\mathcal{X}}<\varepsilon.

In this case, we say that the sequence
x_{n}

converges
to
x_{\infty}
,
or
x_{\infty}
is the
limit
of the sequence
x_{n}
.
Notationally, we write
x_{n}\to x_{\infty}
as
n\to\infty

or
x_{\infty}=\lim_{n\to\infty}x_{n}
.

0.5. Definition.

Let
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
be metric spaces.
A map
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
is called
continuous
if, for any convergent sequence
x_{n}\to x_{\infty}
in
{\mathcal{X}}
,
we have
f(x_{n})\to f(x_{\infty})
in
\mathcal{Y}
.

Equivalently,
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
is continuous if, for any
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
and any
\varepsilon>0
,
there is
\delta>0
such that

|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{X}}<\delta\quad\text{ implies that }\quad|{f(x)}-\penalty{f(y)}|_{\mathcal{Y}}<\varepsilon.

0.6. Exercise.

Let
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
be a
distance non-expanding
map between metric spaces; that is,

|{f(x)}-\penalty{f(y)}|_{\mathcal{Y}}\leqslant|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathcal{X}}

for any
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
.
Show that
f
is continuous.

B
Topology

The following material is covered in any introductory text to topology;
one of our favorites is a textbook by Czes Kosniowski
[
50
]
.

Closed and open sets

0.7. Definition.

A subset
C
of a metric space
\mathcal{X}
is called
closed
if whenever a sequence
x_{n}
of points from
C
converges in
\mathcal{X}
, we have that
\lim_{n\to\infty}x_{n}\in C
.

A set
\Omega\subset\mathcal{X}
is called
open
if, for any
z\in\Omega
,
there is
\varepsilon>0
such that
B(z,\varepsilon)\subset\Omega
.

0.8. Exercise.

Let
Q
be a subset of a metric space
\mathcal{X}
.
Show that
Q
is closed if and only if its complement
\Omega=\mathcal{X}\setminus Q
is open.

An open set
\Omega
that contains a given point
p
is called a
neighborhood of
p
.
A closed subset
C
that contains
p
together with its neighborhood is called a
closed neighborhood of
p
.

A point
p
lies on the
boundary
of a set
Q
(briefly,
p\in\partial Q
) if any neighborhood of
p
contains points in
Q
and in the complement of
Q
.

Compact sets

A subset
K
of a metric space is called
compact
if any sequence of points in
K
has a subsequence that converges to a point in
K
.

The following properties follow directly from the definition:

\diamond

A closed subset of a compact set is compact.

\diamond

An image of a compact set under a continuous map is compact.

0.9. Heine–Borel lemma.

A subset of Euclidean space is compact if and only if it is closed and bounded.

Homeomorphisms and embedding

A bijection
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
between metric spaces is called
homeomorphism
if
f
and its inverse
f^{-1}
are continuous.
A homeomorphism to its image is called an
embedding
.

If there exists a homeomorphism
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
,
we say that
{\mathcal{X}}
is
homeomorphic
to
\mathcal{Y}
,
or
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
are
homeomorphic
.

If a metric space
\mathcal{X}
is homeomorphic to a known space, for example, plane, sphere, disc, circle, and so on,
then we may also say that
\mathcal{X}
is a
topological
plane, sphere, disc, circle, and so on.

The following theorem characterizes homeomorphisms between compact spaces:

0.10. Theorem.

A continuous bijection
f
between compact metric spaces has a continuous inverse.
In particular, we have the following.

(a)

Any continuous bijection between compact metric spaces
is a homeomorphism.

(b)

Any continuous injection from compact metric spaces to another metric space
is an embedding.

Connected sets

Recall that a continuous map
\alpha
from the unit interval
[0,1]
to a Euclidean space is called a
path
.
If
p=\alpha(0)
and
q=\alpha(1)
, then we say that
\alpha
connects
p
to
q
.

A nonempty set
X
in the Euclidean space is called
path-connected
if any two points
x,y\in X
can be connected by a path lying in
X
.

A nonempty set
X
in the Euclidean space is called
connected
if one cannot cover
X
with two disjoint open sets
V
and
W
such that both intersections
X\cap V
and
X\cap W
are nonempty.

Notice that path-connected and connected set are nonempty by the definition.

0.11. Proposition.

Any path-connected set is connected.

Moreover, any open connected set in the Euclidean space or plane is path-connected.

Given a point
x\in X
, the maximal connected subset of
X
containing
x
is called the
connected component
of
x
in
X
.

Jordan’s theorem

The first part of the following theorem was proved by Camille Jordan, the second part is due to Arthur Schoenflies:

0.12. Theorem.

The complement of any simple closed curve
\gamma
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
has exactly two connected components.

Moreover, there is a homeomorphism
h\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
that maps the unit circle to
\gamma
.
In particular,
\gamma
bounds a topological disc.

This theorem is known for its simple formulation and quite hard proof.
For the first statement, a very short proof based on a somewhat developed technique is given by Patrick Doyle
[
25
]
,
among elementary proofs, one of our favorites is the proof given by Aleksei Filippov
[
30
]
.

We use mostly the smooth case of this theorem which is simpler.
An amusing proof of this case was given by Gregory Chambers and Yevgeny Liokumovich
[
20
]
.

C
Elementary geometry

Internal angles

Polygon is defined as a compact set bounded by a closed polygonal line.
Recall that the internal angle of a polygon
P
at a vertex
v

is defined as the angular measure of the intersection of
P
with a small circle centered at
v
.

0.13. Theorem.

The sum of all the internal angles of an
n
-gon is
(n-\penalty 2){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
.

A clean proof of this theorem can be found, for example, in the paper of Gary Meisters
[
65
]
.
It uses induction on
n
and is based on the following nontrivial statement:

0.14. Claim.

Suppose
P
is an
n
-gon with
n\geqslant 4
.
Then a diagonal of
P
lies completely in
P
.

Angle monotonicity

The
measure
of angle with sides
[p,x]
and
[p,y]
will be denoted by
\measuredangle[p\,{}^{x}_{y}]
;
it takes a value in the interval
[0,\pi]
.

The following lemma is simple and useful.
It says that the angle of a triangle monotonically depends on the opposite side, assuming we keep the other two sides fixed.
It follows directly from the cosine rule.

0.15. Monotonicity lemma.

Let
x
,
y
,
z
,
x^{*}
,
y^{*}
, and
z^{*}
be 6 points such that
|{x}-\penalty{y}|=\penalty|x^{*}-y^{*}|>0
and
|y-z|=|y^{*}-z^{*}|>0
.
Then

\measuredangle[y\,{}^{x}_{z}]\geqslant\measuredangle[y^{*}\,{}^{x^{*}}_{z^{*}}]\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad|x-z|\geqslant|x^{*}-z^{*}|.

Spherical triangle inequality

The following theorem says that the triangle inequality holds for angles between half-lines from a fixed point.
In particular, it implies that a sphere with the angle metric is a metric space.

0.16. Theorem.

The following inequality holds for any three line segments
[o,a]
,
[o,b]
, and
[o,c]
in the Euclidean space:

\measuredangle[o\,{}^{a}_{b}]+\measuredangle[o\,{}^{b}_{c}]\geqslant\measuredangle[o\,{}^{a}_{c}]

Most of the authors use this theorem without mentioning, but the proof is not that simple.
A short elementary proof can be found in the classical textbook in Euclidean geometry by Andrey Kiselyov
[
48
, §47]
.

Area of spherical triangle

0.17. Lemma.

Let
\Delta
be a spherical triangle;
that is, an intersection of three closed half-spheres in the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.
Then

\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits\Delta=\alpha+\beta+\gamma-\pi,

➊

where
\alpha
,
\beta
, and
\gamma
are the angles of
\Delta
.

The value
\alpha+\beta+\gamma-\pi
is called the
excess
of the triangle
\Delta
,
so the lemma says that the area of a spherical triangle equals its excess.

This lemma will be important for the intuitive understanding of the Gauss–Bonnet formula.
By that reason, we present its proof.

Proof.

Recall that

\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits\mathbb{S}^{2}=4{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi.

➋

Note that the area of a spherical slice
S_{\alpha}
between two meridians meeting at angle
\alpha
is proportional to
\alpha
.
Since
S_{\pi}
is a half-sphere, from
➋
, we get
\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits S_{\pi}=\penalty 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
.
Therefore, the coefficient is 2; that is,

\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits S_{\alpha}=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\alpha

➌

for any
\alpha
.

Extending the sides of
\Delta
we get 6 slices: two
S_{\alpha}
, two
S_{\beta}
, and two
S_{\gamma}
.
They cover most of the sphere once,
but the triangle
\Delta
and its centrally symmetric copy
\Delta^{*}
are covered 3 times.
It follows that

2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits S_{\alpha}+2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits S_{\beta}+2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits S_{\gamma}=\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits\mathbb{S}^{2}+4{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits\Delta.

It remains to apply
➋
and
➌
.
∎

D
Convex geometry

A set
X
in the Euclidean space is called
convex
if, for any two points
x,y\in X
, any point
z
between
x
and
y
lies in
X
.
It is called
strictly convex
if, for any two points
x,y\in X
, any point
z
between
x
and
y
lies in the interior of
X
.

From the definition, it is easy to see that the intersection of an arbitrary family of convex sets is convex.
The intersection of all convex sets containing
X
is called the
convex hull
of
X
;
it is the minimal convex set containing the set
X
.

These definitions and the following statements should appear on the first pages of any introductory text in convex geometry;
see for example the book by Roger Webster
[
97
]
.

Separating and supporting planes

We will use the following corollary of the so-called
hyperplane separation theorem
.

0.18. Lemma.

Let
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a closed convex set.
Then for any point
p\notin K
there is a plane
\Pi
that separates
K
from
p
;
that is,
K
and
p
lie on opposite open half-spaces separated by
\Pi
.

Moreover, for any boundary point
p\in\partial K
, there is a plane
\Pi

supporting

K
at
p
;
that is,
\Pi\ni p
and
K
lies in a closed half-space bounded by
\Pi

E
Linear algebra

The following theorem can be found in any textbook in linear algebra;
the book by Sergei Treil
[
95
]
will do.

0.19. Spectral theorem.

Any symmetric matrix is diagonalizable by an orthogonal matrix.

We will use this theorem only for
2{\times}2
matrices.
In this case, it can be restated as follows:
Consider a function

f(x,y)=\begin{pmatrix}x&y\end{pmatrix}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\begin{pmatrix}\ell&m\\
m&n\end{pmatrix}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\begin{pmatrix}x\\
y\end{pmatrix}=\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x^{2}+2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}m{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y+n{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y^{2},

that is defined on a
(x,y)
-coordinate plane.
Then after proper rotation of the coordinates,
the expression for
f
in the new
(x,y)
-coordinates will be

\bar{f}(x,y)=\begin{pmatrix}x&y\end{pmatrix}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\begin{pmatrix}k_{1}&0\\
0&k_{2}\end{pmatrix}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\begin{pmatrix}x\\
y\end{pmatrix}=k_{1}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x^{2}+k_{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y^{2}.

F
Analysis

The following material is discussed in any course of real analysis, the classical book by Walter Rudin
[
83
]
is one of our favorites.

Measurable functions

A function is called
measurable
if the inverse image of any Borel set is a Borel set.
Virtually all functions that naturally appear in geometry are measurable.

The following theorem makes it possible to extend many statements about continuous functions to measurable functions.

0.20. Lusin’s theorem.

Let
\varphi\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}
be a measurable function.
Then for any
\varepsilon>0
, there is a continuous function
\psi_{\varepsilon}\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}
that coincides with
\varphi
outside a set of measure at most
\varepsilon
.
Moreover, if
\varphi
is bounded above and/or below, then we may assume that so is
\psi_{\varepsilon}
.

Lipschitz condition

Recall that a function
f
between metric spaces is called
Lipschitz
if there is a constant
L
such that

|{f(x)}-\penalty{f(y)}|\leqslant L{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{x}-\penalty{y}|

for all values
x
and
y
in the domain of definition of
f
.

The following theorem makes it possible to extend many of results about smooth functions to Lipschitz functions.
Recall that
almost all
means all values, with exceptions in a set of zero
Lebesgue measure
.

0.21. Rademacher’s theorem.

Let
f\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}
be a Lipschitz function.
Then its derivative
f^{\prime}
is a bounded measurable function defined almost everywhere in
[a,b]
.
Moreover, it satisfies the fundamental theorem of calculus; that is, the following identity

f(b)-f(a)=\int_{a}^{b}f^{\prime}(x){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dx,

holds if the integral is understood in the sense of Lebesgue.

Uniform continuity and convergence

Let
f\colon{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathcal{Y}
be a map between metric spaces.
If for any
\varepsilon>0
there is
\delta>0
such that

|{x_{1}}-\penalty{x_{2}}|_{\mathcal{X}}<\delta\quad\Longrightarrow\quad|{f(x_{1})}-\penalty{f(x_{2})}|_{\mathcal{Y}}<\varepsilon,

then
f
is called
uniformly continuous
.

Evidently, every uniformly continuous function is continuous;
the converse does not hold.
For example, the function
f(x)=x^{2}
is continuous, but not uniformly continuous.

0.22. Heine–Cantor theorem.

Any continuous function defined on a compact metric space is uniformly continuous.

If the condition above holds for any function
f_{n}
in a sequence, and
\delta
depends solely on
\varepsilon
,
then the sequence
f_{n}
is called
uniformly equicontinuous
.
More precisely,
a sequence of functions
f_{n}:{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathcal{Y}
is called
uniformly equicontinuous
if
for any
\varepsilon>0
there is
\delta>0
such that

|{x_{1}}-\penalty{x_{2}}|_{\mathcal{X}}<\delta\quad\Longrightarrow\quad|{f_{n}(x_{1})}-\penalty{f_{n}(x_{2})}|_{\mathcal{Y}}<\varepsilon

for any
n
.

We say that a sequence of functions
f_{i}\colon{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathcal{Y}

converges uniformly
to a function
f_{\infty}\colon{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathcal{Y}
if, for any

\varepsilon>0
, there is a natural number
N
such that for all
n\geqslant N
, we have
|{f_{\infty}(x)}-\penalty{f_{n}(x)}|<\varepsilon

for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
.

0.23. Arzelá–Ascoli theorem.

Suppose
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
are compact metric spaces.
Then any uniformly equicontinuous sequence of function
f_{n}\colon\mathcal{X}\to\penalty\mathcal{Y}
has a subsequence that converges uniformly to a continuous function
f_{\infty}\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
.

Cutoffs and mollifiers

Here we explain how to construct smooth functions that mimic the behavior of certain model functions.
These functions are used to smooth model geometric objects keeping their shape nearly unchanged.

As an example, consider the following functions

\displaystyle h(t)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}\begin{cases}0&\text{if}\ t\leqslant 0,\\
t&\text{if}\ t>0.\end{cases}

\displaystyle f(t)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}\begin{cases}0&\text{if}\ t\leqslant 0,\\
\frac{t}{e^{1\!/\!t}}&\text{if}\ t>0.\end{cases}

The functions
h
and
f
behave alike —
both vanish at
t\leqslant 0
and grow to infinity for positive
t
.
The function
h
is not smooth — its derivative at
0
is undefined.
Unlike
h
, the function
f
is smooth.
Indeed, the existence of all derivatives
f^{(n)}(x)
at
x\neq 0
is evident, and direct calculations show that
f^{(n)}(0)=0
for all
n
.

Other useful examples of that type are the so-called
bell function
— a smooth function that is positive in an
\varepsilon
-neighborhood of zero and vanishing outside this neighborhood.
Such functions can be obtained from the function
f
constructed above, say

b_{\varepsilon}(t)\mathrel{:=}c{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f(\varepsilon^{2}-t^{2});

typically one chooses the constant
c
so that
\int b_{\varepsilon}=1
.

Another useful example is a
sigmoid
— nondecreasing function that vanishes for
t\leqslant-\varepsilon
and takes value
1
for any
t\geqslant\varepsilon
.
It mimics the step function and can be defined the following way:

\sigma_{\varepsilon}(t)\mathrel{:=}\int_{-\infty}^{t}b_{\varepsilon}(x){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dx.

G
Multivariable calculus

The following material is discussed in any course of multivariable calculus;
for example, in the already mentioned book by Walter Rudin
[
83
]
.

Regular values

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be an open subset.
Any map
\bm{f}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
can be thaut of as an array of its coordinate functions

f_{1},\dots,f_{n}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}.

The map
\bm{f}
is called
smooth
if each function
f_{i}
is smooth;
that is, all partial derivatives of
f_{i}
are defined in
\Omega
.

The
Jacobian matrix
of
\bm{f}
at
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
is defined as

\mathop{\rm Jac}\nolimits_{\bm{x}}\bm{f}=\begin{pmatrix}\dfrac{\partial f_{1}}{\partial x_{1}}&\cdots&\dfrac{\partial f_{1}}{\partial x_{m}}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\dfrac{\partial f_{n}}{\partial x_{1}}&\cdots&\dfrac{\partial f_{n}}{\partial x_{m}}\end{pmatrix};

we assume the right-hand side is evaluated at
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
.

If the Jacobian matrix defines a surjective linear map
\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
(that is, if
\mathop{\rm rank}\nolimits(\mathop{\rm Jac}\nolimits_{\bm{x}}\bm{f})=n
), then we say that

\bm{x}
is a
regular point
of
\bm{f}
.

If
\bm{x}
is regular anytime
\bm{f}(\bm{x})=\bm{y}
,
then we say that
\bm{y}
is a
regular value
of
\bm{f}
.
The following lemma states that
most
values of a smooth map are regular.

0.24. Sard’s lemma.

Given a smooth map
\bm{f}\colon\Omega\to\penalty\mathbb{R}^{n}
defined on an open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
, almost all values in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
are regular.

The words
almost all
mean all values, with the possible exceptions belonging to a set with vanishing
Lebesgue measure
.
In particular, if one chooses a random value equidistributed in an arbitrarily small ball
B\subset\penalty\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then it is a regular value of
\bm{f}
with probability 1.

Note that if
m<n
, then any point
\bm{y}=\bm{f}(\bm{x})
is
not
a regular value of
\bm{f}
.
Therefore, the only regular values of
\bm{f}
are the points in the complement of the image
\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits\bm{f}
.
In this case, the theorem states that almost all points in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, do
not
belong to
\mathop{\rm Im}\nolimits\bm{f}
.

Inverse function theorem

The
inverse function theorem
gives an if condition for a map
\bm{f}
to be invertible in a neighborhood of a given point
\bm{x}
.
The condition is formulated in terms of the Jacobian matrix of
\bm{f}
at
\bm{x}
.

The
implicit function theorem
is its close relative and a corollary.
It is used when we need to pass from parametric to implicit descriptions of curves and surfaces.

Both theorems reduce the existence of a map satisfying a certain equation to a question in linear algebra.
We use these two theorems only for
n\leqslant 3
.

0.25. Inverse function theorem.

Let
\bm{f}=(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a smooth map
defined on an open set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Assume the Jacobian matrix

\mathop{\rm Jac}\nolimits_{\bm{x}}\bm{f}

is invertible at
\bm{x}\in\Omega
.
Then there is a smooth map
\bm{h}\colon\Phi\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
defined in an open neighborhood
\Phi
of
{\bm{y}}=\penalty\bm{f}(\bm{x})
that is a
local inverse of
\bm{f}
at
\bm{x}
;
that is, there is a neighborhood
\Psi\ni\bm{x}
such that

\bm{f}
defines a homeomorphism
\Psi\leftrightarrow\Phi
, and

\bm{h}\circ\bm{f}
is an identity map on
\Psi
.

Moreover, if
|\det[\mathop{\rm Jac}\nolimits_{\bm{x}}\bm{f}]|>\varepsilon>0
, the domain
\Omega
contains an
\varepsilon
-neighborhood of
\bm{x}
,
and the first and second partial derivatives
\tfrac{\partial f_{i}}{\partial x_{j}}
,
\tfrac{\partial^{2}f_{i}}{\partial x_{j}\partial x_{k}}
are bounded by a constant
C
for all
i
,
j
, and
k
, then we can assume that
\Phi
is a
\delta
-neighborhood of
\bm{y}
, for some
\delta>0
that depends only on
\varepsilon
and
C
.

0.26. Implicit function theorem.

Let
\bm{f}=(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a smooth map, defined on an open subset
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+m}
, where

m,n\geqslant\penalty 1
.
Let us consider
\mathbb{R}^{n+m}
as a product space
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}
with coordinates

x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{m}
.
Consider the following matrix

M=\begin{pmatrix}\dfrac{\partial f_{1}}{\partial x_{1}}&\cdots&\dfrac{\partial f_{1}}{\partial x_{n}}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\dfrac{\partial f_{n}}{\partial x_{1}}&\cdots&\dfrac{\partial f_{n}}{\partial x_{n}}\end{pmatrix}

formed by the first
n
columns of the Jacobian matrix.
Assume
M
is invertible at
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots y_{m})\in\Omega
, and
\bm{f}(\bm{x})=0
.
Then there is a neighborhood
\Psi\ni\bm{x}

and a smooth function
\bm{h}\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
defined in a neighborhood
\Phi\ni 0
such that
for any
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots y_{m})\in\penalty\Psi
, the equality

\bm{f}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots y_{m})=0

holds if and only if

(x_{1},\dots x_{n})=\bm{h}(y_{1},\dots y_{m}).

Multiple integral

Let
\bm{f}\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a smooth map (maybe partially defined).
Set

\mathop{\rm jac}\nolimits_{\bm{x}}\bm{f}\mathrel{:=}|\det[\mathop{\rm Jac}\nolimits_{\bm{x}}\bm{f}]|;{\@index{\@indexphrase[10j]{$\mathop{\rm jac}\nolimits$ (Jacobian determinant)}}}

that is,
\mathop{\rm jac}\nolimits_{\bm{x}}\bm{f}
is the absolute value of the determinant of the Jacobian matrix of
\bm{f}
at
\bm{x}
.

The following theorem plays the role of a substitution rule for multiple variables.

Borel subsets
are defined as the class of subsets that are generated from open sets by applying the following operations recursively: countable union, countable intersection, and complement.
Since the complement of a closed set is open and the other way around, these sets can be also generated from all closed sets.
This class of sets includes virtually all sets that naturally appear in geometry but does not include pathological examples that create problems with integration.

0.27. Theorem.

Let
h\colon K\to\mathbb{R}
be a continuous function on a Borel subset
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Assume
\bm{f}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is an injective smooth map that is defined on an open set
\Omega\supset K
.
Then

\idotsint_{\bm{x}\in K}h(\bm{x}){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathop{\rm jac}\nolimits_{\bm{x}}\bm{f}=\idotsint_{\bm{y}\in\bm{f}(K)}h\circ\bm{f}^{-1}(\bm{y}).

Convex functions

The following statements will be used only for
n\leqslant 3
.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth function (maybe partially defined).
Choose a vector
\text{\sc{w}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Given a point
p\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
consider the function
\varphi(t)=f(p+t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{w}})
.
Then the
directional derivative

(D_{\text{\sc{w}}}f)(p)
of
f
at
p
with respect to vector
w
is defined by

(D_{\text{\sc{w}}}f)(p)=\varphi^{\prime}(0).

А function
f
is called
(strictly) convex
if
its epigraph
z\geqslant f(\bm{x})
is a (strictly) convex set in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
.

0.28. Theorem.

A smooth function
f\colon K\to\mathbb{R}
defined on a convex subset
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is convex if and only if one of the following equivalent conditions holds:

(a)

The second directional derivative of
f
at any point in the direction of any vector is nonnegative; that is,

(D_{\text{\sc{w}}}^{2}f)(p)\geqslant 0

for any
p\in K
and
\text{\sc{w}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

(b)

The so-called
Jensen’s inequality

f\left((1-t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x_{0}+t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x_{1}\right)\leqslant(1-t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f(x_{0})+t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f(x_{1})

holds for any
x_{0},x_{1}\in K
and
t\in[0,1]
.

(c)

For any
x_{0},x_{1}\in K
, we have

f\left(\frac{x_{0}+x_{1}}{2}\right)\leqslant\frac{f(x_{0})+f(x_{1})}{2}.

H
Ordinary differential equations

The following material is discussed at the beginning of any course of ordinary differential equations; the classical book by Vladimir Arnold
[
6
]
is one of our favorites.

First-order equations

The following theorem guarantees existence and uniqueness of solutions of an initial value problem
for a system of ordinary first-order differential equations

\begin{cases}x_{1}^{\prime}&=f_{1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},t),\\
&\,\,\vdots\\
x_{n}^{\prime}&=f_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},t),\end{cases}

where each
t\mapsto x_{i}=x_{i}(t)
is a real-valued function defined on a real interval
\mathbb{J}

and each
f_{i}
is a smooth function defined on an open subset
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
.

The array of functions
(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})
can be packed into one vector-valued function

\bm{f}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
;
the same way the function array
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
can be packed into a vector-valued function
\bm{x}\colon\mathbb{J}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Therefore, the system can be rewritten as one vector equation

\bm{x}^{\prime}=\bm{f}(\bm{x},t).

0.29. Theorem.

Suppose
\bm{f}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a smooth function defined on an open subset
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
.
Then for any initial data
\bm{x}(t_{0})=\bm{u}
such that
(\bm{u},t_{0})\in\Omega
the differential equation

\bm{x}^{\prime}=\bm{f}(\bm{x},t)

has a unique solution
t\mapsto\bm{x}(t)
defined at a maximal interval
\mathbb{J}
that contains
t_{0}
.
Moreover,

(a)

if
\mathbb{J}\neq\mathbb{R}
(that is, if an end
b
of
\mathbb{J}
is finite), then
\bm{x}(t)
does not have a limit point in
\Omega
as
t\to b
;
1
1

1

In other words, if
\bm{x}(t_{n})
converges for a sequence
t_{n}\to b
, then its limit does not lie in
\Omega
.

(b)

the function
w\colon(\bm{u},t_{0},t)\mapsto\bm{x}(t)
has an open domain of definition in
\Omega\times\mathbb{R}
that contains all points
(\bm{u},t_{0},t_{0})
for
(\bm{u},t_{0})\in\Omega
, and
w
is smooth in this domain.

Higher order

Consider an ordinary differential equation of order
k

\bm{x}^{(k)}=\bm{f}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime},\dots,\bm{x}^{(k-1)},t),

➍

where
t\mapsto\bm{x}=\bm{x}(t)
is a function from a real interval to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

This equation can be rewritten as
k
first-order equations with
k-1
new vector variables

\bm{y}_{1}=\bm{x}^{\prime}
,

\bm{y}_{2}=\bm{x}^{\prime\prime},\dots,\bm{y}_{k-1}=\bm{x}^{(k-1)}
:

\begin{cases}\bm{x}^{\prime}(t)&=\bm{y}_{1}(t),\\
\bm{y}_{1}^{\prime}(t)&=\bm{y}_{2}(t),\\
&\,\,\vdots\\
\bm{y}_{k-2}^{\prime}(t)&=\bm{y}_{k-1}(t),\\
\bm{y}_{k-1}^{\prime}(t)&=\bm{f}(\bm{x},\bm{y}_{1},\dots,\bm{y}_{k-1},t).\end{cases}

➎

Thus, we have derived the following.

0.30. Theorem.

The
k
-th order equation
➍
is equivalent to the system
➎
consisting of
k
first-order equations.

This trick reduces a higher-order ordinary differential equation to a first-order equation.
In particular, we get local existence and uniqueness for solutions of higher-order equations as in Theorem
0H
;
one only has to assume that

\Omega\subset\penalty\mathbb{R}^{n{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}k}\times\mathbb{R}
, and the initial data consists of
\bm{x}(t_{0})
,
\bm{x}^{\prime}(t_{0}),\dots,\bm{x}^{(k-1)}(t_{0})
.

Part I:   Curves

Chapter 1
Definitions

In most cases, by looking at tire tracks of a bicycle,
it is possible to tell in which direction it traveled and to tell apart the trails made by the front and rear wheels;
plus one can find the distance between the wheels.

Try to figure out how to do this.
This question might motivate the reader to reinvent a considerable part of the differential geometry of curves.
To learn more about this problem check the reference given after Exercise
6A
.

A
Before we start

The notion of curve comes with many variations.
Some of them are described by nouns (path, arc, and so on) and
others by adjectives (closed, open, proper, simple, smooth, and so on).
The following picture gives an idea about four of these variations.

This chapter covers all these definitions.
One may skip this chapter and use it as a quick reference while reading the book.

B
Simple curves

Recall that metric spaces were discussed in Section
0A
.
The Euclidean plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and space
\mathbb{R}^{3}
are the main examples of metric spaces one should keep in mind.

Recall that a
real interval
is a connected subset of the real line.
A bijective continuous map
f\colon X\to Y
between subsets of some metric spaces is called a
homeomorphism
if its inverse
f^{-1}\colon Y\to\penalty X
is continuous.

1.1. Definition.

A connected subset
\gamma
in a metric space is called a
simple curve
if it is
locally
homeomorphic to a real interval;
that is, any point
p\in\gamma
has a neighborhood in
\gamma
that is homeomorphic to a real interval.

It turns out that any simple curve
\gamma
can be
parametrized
by a real interval or circle.
That is, there is a homeomorphism
\mathbb{G}\to\gamma

where
\mathbb{G}
is a real interval (open, closed, or semi-open) or the circle

\mathbb{S}^{1}=\left\{\,{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\,:\,{x^{2}+y^{2}=1}\,\right\}.

A complete proof of the latter statement is given by David Gale
[
34
]
.
The proof is not hard, but it would take us away from the main subject.

The parametrization
\mathbb{G}\to\gamma
describes the curve completely.
We will denote a curve and its parametrization by the same letter;
for example, we may say a curve
\gamma
is given with a parametrization
\gamma\colon(a,b]\to\penalty\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Note, however, that any simple curve admits many parametrizations.

1.2. Exercise.

(a)

Show that the image of any continuous injective map
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a simple arc.

(b)

Find a continuous injective map
\gamma\colon(0,1)\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that its image is not a simple curve.

C
Parametrized curves

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a circle or a real interval (open, closed, or semi-open), and let
\mathcal{X}
be a metric space.
A
parametrized curve
is defined as a continuous map
\gamma\colon\mathbb{G}\to\mathcal{X}
.
For a parametrized curve, we do
not
assume that the map is injective; in other words, a parametrized curve might have
self-intersections
.

From the previous section, we know that a simple curve can be parametrized.
The term
curve
can be used if we do not want to specify whether it is parametrized or simple.

If
\mathbb{G}
is an open interval or a circle, we say that
\gamma
is a
curve without endpoints
;
otherwise, it is called a
curve with endpoints
.
In the case when
\mathbb{G}
is a circle, we say that the curve is
closed
.
If the domain
\mathbb{G}
is a closed interval
[a,b]
, then the curve is called an
arc
.
Further, if it is the unit interval
[0,1]
, then it is also called a
path
.

If an arc
\gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathcal{X}
satisfies
p=\gamma(a)=\gamma(b)
, then
\gamma
is called a
loop
;
in this case, the point
p
is called the
base
of the loop.

Suppose
\mathbb{G}_{1}
and
\mathbb{G}_{2}
are either real intervals or circles.
A continuous onto map
\tau\colon\mathbb{G}_{1}\to\mathbb{G}_{2}
is called
monotone
if, for any
t\in\mathbb{G}_{2}
, the set
\tau^{-1}\{t\}
is connected.
If
\mathbb{G}_{1}
and
\mathbb{G}_{2}
are intervals, then, by the intermediate value theorem, a monotone map is either nondecreasing or nonincreasing;
that is, our definition agrees with the standard one when
\mathbb{G}_{1}
and
\mathbb{G}_{2}
are intervals.

1.3. Exercise.

Construct a monotone (and, in particular, onto) map
(0,1)\to[0,1]
.

Suppose
\gamma_{1}\colon\mathbb{G}_{1}\to\mathcal{X}
and
\gamma_{2}\colon\mathbb{G}_{2}\to\mathcal{X}
are two parametrized curves such that

\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}\circ\tau
for a monotone map
\tau\colon\mathbb{G}_{1}\to\mathbb{G}_{2}
.
Then we say that
\gamma_{2}
is
reparametrization
1
1

1

In general,
\gamma_{1}
is
not
a reparametrization of
\gamma_{2}
.
In other words, according to our definition, the described relation
being a reparametrization
is not symmetric;
in particular, it is not an equivalence relation.
Usually, it is fixed by extending it to the minimal equivalence relation that includes ours
[
13
, 2.5.1]
.
But we will stick to our version.

of
\gamma_{1}
by
\tau
.

1.4. Advanced exercise.

Let
X
be a subset of the plane.
Suppose two distinct points
p,q\in X
can be connected by a path in
X
.
Show that there is a simple arc in
X
connecting
p
to
q
.

Any loop and any simple closed curve can be described by a
periodic
parametrized curve
\gamma\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathcal{X}
;
that is, a curve such that
\gamma(t+\ell)=\gamma(t)
for a fixed period
\ell>0
and all
t
.
For example, the unit circle in the plane can be described by the
2{{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}}\pi
-periodic parametrization
\gamma(t)=(\cos t,\sin t)
.

In the reverse direction, any curve with periodic parametrization yields a loop that can be also considered as a closed curve.

D
Smooth curves

Curves in the Euclidean space or plane are called
space curves
or, respectively,
plane curves
.

A parametrized space curve can be described by its coordinate functions

\gamma(t)=(x(t),y(t),z(t)).

Plane curves can be considered as a special case of space curves with
z(t)\equiv 0
.

Recall that a real-to-real function is called
smooth
if its derivatives of all orders are defined everywhere in the domain of definition.
If each of the coordinate functions
x(t),y(t)
, and
z(t)
are smooth, then the parametrization is called

smooth
.

If the
velocity vector

\gamma^{\prime}(t)=(x^{\prime}(t),y^{\prime}(t),z^{\prime}(t))

does not vanish at any point, then the parametrization
\gamma
is called
regular
.

A parametrized curve is called
smooth
if its parametrization is smooth and regular.
A simple space curve is called
smooth
if it admits a regular smooth parametrization;
for closed curves the parametrization is assumed to be periodic.
These curves are the main objects in the first part of the book.
Pedantically, one could call them
regular smooth curves
.

Notice that the closed curve described by a smooth loop might fail to be smooth at its base; an example is shown on the picture.

The following exercise shows that curves with smooth parametrizations might have corners and might fail to be smooth curves.

1.5. Exercise.

Recall that (see Section
0F
) that the following function is smooth:

f(t)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if}\ t\leqslant 0,\\
\frac{t}{e^{1\!/\!t}}&\text{if}\ t>0.\end{cases}

Show that
\alpha(t)=(f(t),f(-t))
gives a smooth parametrization of the curve shown on the picture;
it is a simple curve formed by the union of two half-axis in the plane.

Show that any smooth parametrization of this curve has a vanishing velocity vector at the origin.
Conclude that this curve is not smooth;
that is, it does not admit a regular smooth parametrization.

1.6. Exercise.

Describe the set of real numbers
\ell

such that the the parametrization
\gamma_{\ell}(t)=(t+\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sin t,\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos t)
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
is

(a)

smooth;

(b)

regular;

(c)

simple.

1.7. Exercise.

Find a parametrization of the cubic parabola
y=x^{3}
in the plane that is smooth, but not regular.

E
Implicitly defined curves

Suppose
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is a smooth function;
that is, all its partial derivatives are defined everywhere.
Let
\gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be its level set described by the equation
f(x,y)=0
.

Assume
0
is a
regular value
of
f
; that is, the gradient
\nabla_{p}f
does not vanish at any point
p\in\gamma
.
In other words, if
f(p)=0
, then

f_{x}(p)\neq 0
or
f_{y}(p)\neq 0
.
2
2

2

Here
f_{x}
is a shortcut notation for the partial derivative

\tfrac{\partial f}{\partial x}
.

If
\gamma
is connected, then by the implicit function theorem (
0G
), it is a smooth simple curve.

The described condition is sufficient but
not necessary
.
For example, zero is
not
a regular value the function
f(x,y)=y^{2}
, but the equation
f(x,y)=0
describes a smooth curve — the
x
-axis.

Similarly, assume
(f,h)
is a pair of smooth functions defined in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
The system of equations

\begin{cases}f(x,y,z)=0,\\
h(x,y,z)=0\end{cases}

defines a smooth space curve if the set
\gamma
of solutions is connected, and
0
is a regular value of the map
F\colon\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
defined by

F\colon(x,y,z)\mapsto(f(x,y,z),h(x,y,z)).

It means that the gradients
\nabla f
and
\nabla h
are linearly independent at any point
p\in\gamma
.
In other words, the Jacobian matrix

\mathop{\rm Jac}\nolimits_{p}F=\begin{pmatrix}f_{x}&f_{y}&f_{z}\\
h_{x}&h_{y}&h_{z}\end{pmatrix}

for the map
F\colon\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
has rank 2 at any point
p\in\gamma
.

If a curve
\gamma
is described in such a way,
then we say that it is
implicitly defined
.

The implicit function theorem guarantees the existence of regular smooth parametrizations for any implicitly defined curve.
However, when it comes to calculations, it is usually easier to work directly with implicit representations.

1.8. Exercise.

Consider the set in the plane described by the equation

y^{2}=x^{3}
.
Is it a simple curve?
Is it a smooth curve?

1.9. Exercise.

Describe the set of real numbers
\ell

such that the system of equations

\begin{cases}x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2}&=1\\
x^{2}+\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x+y^{2}&=0\end{cases}

describes a smooth curve.

F
Proper, closed, and open curves

A parametrized curve
\gamma
in a metric space
\mathcal{X}
is called
proper
if, for any compact set
K\subset\mathcal{X}
, the inverse image
\gamma^{-1}(K)
is compact.

For example, the curve
\gamma(t)=(e^{t},0,0)
defined on the real line is not proper.
Indeed, the half-line
(-\infty,0]
is not compact, but it is the inverse image of the closed unit ball around the origin.

1.10. Exercise.

Suppose
\gamma\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a proper curve.
Show that
|\gamma(t)|\to\penalty\infty
as
t\to\pm\infty
.

Recall that a closed bounded intervals are compact (
0B
) and closed subsets of a compact set are compact;
see Section
0B
.
Since circles and closed intervals are compact,
it follows that closed curves and arcs are proper.

A simple curve is called proper if it admits a proper parametrization.

1.11. Exercise.

Show that a simple space curve is proper if and only if it is a closed set.

A proper simple curve is called
open
if it is not closed and has no endpoints.
So any simple proper curve without endpoints is either closed or open.
The terms
open curve
and
closed curve
have nothing to do with open and closed sets.

1.12. Exercise.

Use Jordan’s theorem (
0B
) to show that any simple open plane curve divides the plane in two connected components.

Chapter 2
Length

A
Definitions

A sequence

a=t_{0}<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{k}=b.

is called a
partition
of the interval
[a,b]
.

2.1. Definition.

Let
\gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathcal{X}
be a curve in a metric space.
The
length
of
\gamma
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma

\displaystyle=\sup\left\{\,{|{\gamma(t_{0})}-\penalty{\gamma(t_{1})}|_{\mathcal{X}}+\dots+|{\gamma(t_{k-1})}-\penalty{\gamma(t_{k})}|_{\mathcal{X}}}\,\right\},

where the least upper bound is taken over all partitions
t_{0},\dots,t_{k}
of
[a,b]
.

The length of a closed curve is defined as the length of the corresponding loop.
If a curve is parametrized by an open or semi-open interval, then its length is defined as the least upper bound of the lengths of all its restrictions to closed intervals.

A curve is called
rectifiable
if its length is finite.

2.2. Exercise.

Suppose curve
\gamma_{1}\colon[a_{1},b_{1}]\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a reparametrization of
\gamma_{2}\colon[a_{2},b_{2}]\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
Show that

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{1}=\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{2}.

Suppose
\gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a parametrized space curve.
For a partition
a=t_{0}<t_{1}<\cdots\penalty<t_{k}=b
, set
p_{i}=\gamma(t_{i})
.
Then the polygonal line
p_{0}\dots p_{k}
is said to be
inscribed
in
\gamma
.
If
\gamma
is closed, then
p_{0}=p_{k}
, so the inscribed polygonal line is also closed.

Note that the length of a space curve can be defined as the least upper bound of the lengths of its inscribed polygonal lines.

2.3. Exercise.

Let
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a path.
Suppose that
\beta_{n}
is a sequence of polygonal lines inscribed in
\gamma
with vertices
\gamma(\tfrac{i}{n})
for
i\in\penalty\{0,\dots,n\}
.
Show that

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\beta_{n}\to\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma\quad\text{as}\quad n\to\infty.

2.4. Exercise.

Let
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a simple path.
Suppose that a path
\beta\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
has the same image as
\gamma
;
that is,
\beta([0,1])=\gamma([0,1])
.
Show that

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\beta\geqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma.

Try to prove the same assuming that only that
\beta([0,1])\supset\gamma([0,1])
.

2.5. Exercise.

Assume
\gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a smooth curve.
Show that

(a)

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma\geqslant\int_{a}^{b}|\gamma^{\prime}(t)|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt
;

(b)

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma\leqslant\int_{a}^{b}|\gamma^{\prime}(t)|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt
.

Conclude that

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma=\int_{a}^{b}|\gamma^{\prime}(t)|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt.

➊

2.6. Advanced exercises.

(a)

Show that the formula
➊
holds for any Lipschitz curve
\gamma\colon[a,b]\to\penalty\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

(b)

Construct a non-constant curve
\gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that
\gamma^{\prime}(t)=0
almost everywhere.
(In this case, the formula
➊
does not hold for
\gamma
despite that both sides are defined.)

B
Nonrectifiable curves

Let us describe the so-called
Koch snowflake
—
a classical example of a nonrectifiable curve.

Start with an equilateral triangle.
For each side, divide it into three segments of equal length, and then add an equilateral triangle with the middle segment as its base.
Repeat this construction recursively with the obtained polygons.
The Koch snowflake is the boundary of the union of all the polygons.
Two iterations and the resulting Koch snowflake are on the picture.

2.7. Exercise.

(a)

Show that the Koch snowflake is a simple closed curve; in particular, it can be parametrized by a circle.

(b)

Show that the Koch snowflake is not rectifiable.

C
Semicontinuity of length

The lower limit of a sequence of real numbers
x_{n}
will be denoted by

\varliminf_{n\to\infty}x_{n}.

It is defined as the lowest partial limit; that is, the lowest possible limit of a subsequence of
x_{n}
.
The lower limit is defined for any sequence of real numbers, and it lies in the extended real line
[-\infty,\infty]
.

2.8. Theorem.

Assume a sequence
of curves
\gamma_{n}\colon[a,b]\to\mathcal{X}
in a metric space
\mathcal{X}
converges pointwise
to a curve
\gamma_{\infty}\colon[a,b]\to\mathcal{X}
;
that is, for any fixed
t\in[a,b]
, we have
\gamma_{n}(t)\to\penalty\gamma_{\infty}(t)
as
n\to\infty
.
Then

\varliminf_{n\to\infty}\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{n}\geqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{\infty}.

➋

2.9. Corollary.

Length is lower semicontinuous with respect to the pointwise convergence of curves.

Proof.

Fix a partition
a=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{k}=b
.
Set

\displaystyle\Sigma_{n}

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}|{\gamma_{n}(t_{0})}-\penalty{\gamma_{n}(t_{1})}|+\dots+|{\gamma_{n}(t_{k-1})}-\penalty{\gamma_{n}(t_{k})}|,

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\infty}

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}|{\gamma_{\infty}(t_{0})}-\penalty{\gamma_{\infty}(t_{1})}|+\dots+|{\gamma_{\infty}(t_{k-1})}-\penalty{\gamma_{\infty}(t_{k})}|.

For each
i
we have

|{\gamma_{n}(t_{i-1})}-\penalty{\gamma_{n}(t_{i})}|\to|{\gamma_{\infty}(t_{i-1})}-\penalty{\gamma_{\infty}(t_{i})}|,

and therefore

\Sigma_{n}\to\Sigma_{\infty}

as
n\to\infty
.
Note that

\Sigma_{n}\leqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{n}

for each
n
.
Hence,

\varliminf_{n\to\infty}\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{n}\geqslant\Sigma_{\infty}.

Since the partition was arbitrary, the definition of length implies inequality
➋
.
∎

The inequality
➋
might be strict.
For example, the diagonal
\gamma_{\infty}
of the unit square
can be approximated by stairs-like polygonal lines
\gamma_{n}

with sides parallel to the sides of the square (
\gamma_{6}
and
\gamma_{\infty}
are in the picture).
In this case,

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{\infty}=\sqrt{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{n}=2\quad\text{for any}\quad n.

D
Arc-length parametrization

We say that a curve
\gamma
has an
arc-length parametrization
(also called
natural parametrization
)
if

t_{2}-t_{1}=\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma|_{[t_{1},t_{2}]}

for any two parameter values
t_{1}<t_{2}
;
that is, the arc of
\gamma
from
t_{1}
to
t_{2}
has length
t_{2}-t_{1}
.

2.10. Exercise.

Let
\gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathcal{X}
be a rectifiable curve in a metric space.
Given
t\in[a,b]
, denote by
s(t)
the length of the arc
\gamma|_{[a,t]}
.
Show that the function
t\mapsto s(t)
is continuous.

Conclude that
\gamma
admits an arc-length parametrization.

By Exercise
2A
, a smooth curve
\gamma(t)=(x(t),y(t),z(t))
is an arc-length parametrization if and only if it has unit velocity vector at all times;
that is,

|\gamma^{\prime}(t)|=\sqrt{x^{\prime}(t)^{2}+y^{\prime}(t)^{2}+z^{\prime}(t)^{2}}=1

for all
t
; by that reason smooth curves equipped with an arc-length parametrization are also called
unit-speed
curves.
Observe that smooth unit-speed parametrizations are automatically regular (see Section
1D
).

2.11. Proposition.

If
t\mapsto\gamma(t)
is a smooth curve,
then its arc-length parametrization is also smooth and regular.
Moreover, the arc-length parameter
s
of
\gamma
can be written as an integral

s(t)=\int_{t_{0}}^{t}|\gamma^{\prime}(\tau)|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}d\tau.

➌

Most of the time we will use
s
for an arc-length parameter of a curve.

Proof.

Since
\gamma
is smooth,
|\gamma^{\prime}(t)|>0
for any
t
.
Therefore, the function
t\mapsto|\gamma^{\prime}(t)|
is smooth.

By the fundamental theorem of calculus,
s^{\prime}(t)=|\gamma^{\prime}(t)|
.
Therefore,
t\mapsto s(t)
is a smooth increasing function with positive derivative.

By the inverse function theorem (
0G
), the inverse function
s^{-1}(t)
is also smooth
and
|(\gamma\circ s^{-1})^{\prime}|\equiv 1
.
Therefore,
\gamma\circ s^{-1}
is a unit-speed reparametrization of
\gamma
by
s
.
By construction,
\gamma\circ s^{-1}
is smooth, and since
|(\gamma\circ s^{-1})^{\prime}|\equiv 1
, it is regular.
∎

2.12. Exercise.

Reparametrize the
helix

\gamma_{a,b}(t)=(a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos t,a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sin t,b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t)

by its arc-length.

We will be interested in the properties of curves that are invariant under reparametrizations.
Therefore, we can always assume that any given smooth curve comes with an arc-length parametrization.
A nice property of arc-length parametrizations is that they are almost canonical — these parametrizations differ only by a sign and an additive constant.
For that reason, they make it easier to define parametrization-independent quantities.
This observation will be used in the definitions of curvature and torsion.

On the other hand, it is usually impossible to find an explicit arc-length parametrization.
Therefore, when it comes to calculations, it is often more convenient to use the original parametrization.

E
Convex curves

A simple plane curve is called
convex
if it bounds a convex region.
Since the boundary of any region is closed, any convex curve is either closed or open (see Section
1F
).

2.13. Proposition.

Assume that a closed convex curve
\alpha
lies inside the domain bounded by a simple closed plane curve
\beta
.
Then

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\alpha\leqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\beta.

To prove Proposition
2E
it is sufficient to show that the perimeter of any polygon inscribed in
\alpha
is less or equal than the length of
\beta
.
Since any polygon inscribed in
\alpha
is convex, it is sufficient to prove the following lemma.

2.14. Lemma.

Assume that a convex polygon
P
lies in a figure
F
bounded by a simple closed curve.
Then

\mathop{\rm perim}\nolimits P\leqslant\mathop{\rm perim}\nolimits F,

where
\mathop{\rm perim}\nolimits F
denotes the perimeter of
F
.

Proof.

A
chord
in
F
is defined to be a line segment in
F
with endpoints in its boundary.
Suppose
F^{\prime}
is a figure obtained from
F
by cutting it along a chord and removing one side.
By the triangle inequality, we have

\mathop{\rm perim}\nolimits F^{\prime}\leqslant\mathop{\rm perim}\nolimits F.

Observe that there is a decreasing sequence of figures

F=F_{0}\supset F_{1}\supset\dots\supset F_{n}=P

such that
F_{i+1}
is obtained from
F_{i}
by cutting along a chord.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm perim}\nolimits F=\mathop{\rm perim}\nolimits F_{0}

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathop{\rm perim}\nolimits F_{1}\geqslant\dots\geqslant\mathop{\rm perim}\nolimits F_{n}=\mathop{\rm perim}\nolimits P.

∎

Comment.

Two other proofs of
2E
can be obtained by applying Crofton’s formulas (see
2F
) and the nearest-point projection (see Lemma
14C
).

2.15. Corollary.

Any convex closed plane curve is rectifiable.

Proof.

Any closed curve is bounded.
Indeed, the curve can be described as an image of a loop
\alpha\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
\alpha(t)=(x(t),y(t))
.
The coordinate functions
t\mapsto x(t)
and
t\mapsto y(t)
are continuous functions defined on
[0,1]
.
This implies that both coordinate functions are bounded by some constant
C
.
Therefore,
\alpha
lies in the square defined by the inequalities
|x|\leqslant C
and
|y|\leqslant C
.

By Proposition
2E
, the length of the curve cannot exceed the perimeter of the square; hence the result.
∎

Recall that the
convex hull
of a set
X
is the smallest convex set that contains
X
;
equivalently, the convex hull of
X
is the intersection of all convex sets containing
X
.

2.16. Exercise.

Let
\alpha
be a simple closed plane curve.
Denote by
K
the convex hull of
\alpha
; let
\beta
be the boundary curve of
K
.
Show that

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\alpha\geqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\beta.

Try to show that the statement holds for arbitrarily closed plane curves, assuming only that
K
has a nonempty interior.

F
Crofton’s formulas

For a function
f\colon\mathbb{S}^{1}\to\mathbb{R}
, we will denote its average value as
\overline{f(\text{\sc{u}})}
;
that is,

\overline{f(\text{\sc{u}})}=\frac{1}{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\int_{\text{\sc{u}}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}}f(\text{\sc{u}}).

For a vector
w
and a unit vector
u
, we will denote by
\text{\sc{w}}_{\text{\sc{u}}}
the orthogonal projection of
w
to the line in the direction of
u
;
that is,

\text{\sc{w}}_{\text{\sc{u}}}=\langle\text{\sc{u}},\text{\sc{w}}\rangle{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{u}}.

2.17. Theorem.

For any plane curve
\gamma
we have

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma=\tfrac{\pi}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\overline{\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{\text{\sc{u}}}},

➍

where
\gamma_{\text{\sc{u}}}
is the curve defined by
\gamma_{\text{\sc{u}}}(t)\mathrel{:=}(\gamma(t))_{\text{\sc{u}}}
.

Proof.

The magnitude of any vector
w
is proportional to the average magnitude of its projections; that is,

|{\text{\sc{w}}}|=k{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\overline{|{\text{\sc{w}}}_{\text{\sc{u}}}|}

for some
k\in\mathbb{R}
.
(The exact value of
k
can be found by integration
1
1

1

It is the average value of
|\cos x|
.
, but we will find it differently.)
Assume
\gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a smooth curve.
Note that

\gamma_{\text{\sc{u}}}^{\prime}(t)=(\gamma^{\prime}(t))_{\text{\sc{u}}}\quad\text{and}\quad|\gamma^{\prime}_{\text{\sc{u}}}(t)|=|\langle\text{\sc{u}},\gamma^{\prime}(t)\rangle|

for any
t\in[a,b]
. Then, according to Exercise
2A
,

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{b}|\gamma^{\prime}(t)|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt=

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{b}k{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\overline{|\gamma_{\text{\sc{u}}}^{\prime}(t)|}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt=

\displaystyle=k{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\overline{\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{\text{\sc{u}}}}.

Since
k
is a universal constant, we can compute it by taking
\gamma
to be the unit circle.
In this case,

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi.

For any unit plane vector
u
, the curve
\gamma_{\text{\sc{u}}}
runs back and forth along an interval of length 2.
Hence
\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{\text{\sc{u}}}=4
for any
u
, and

\overline{\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{\text{\sc{u}}}}=4.

It follows that
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi=k{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}4
.
Therefore,
➍
holds for smooth curves.

Applying the same argument together with
2A
, we get that
➍
holds for arbitrary Lipschitz curves.
Further, since an arc length parametrization of any rectifiable curve is Lipschitz,
2D
implies
➍
for arbitrary rectifiable curves.

It remains to consider the nonrectifiable case;
we have to show that

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma=\infty\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\overline{\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{\text{\sc{u}}}}=\infty.

By the definition of length,

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{\text{\sc{u}}}+\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{\text{\sc{v}}}\geqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma

for any plane curve
\gamma
and any pair
(\text{\sc{u}},\text{\sc{v}})
of orthonormal vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Therefore, if
\gamma
has infinite length, then the average length of
\gamma_{\text{\sc{u}}}
is infinite as well.
∎

2.18. Exercise.

Suppose a simple closed plane curve
\gamma
bounds a figure
F
.
Let
s
be the average length of the projections of
F
to lines.
Show that
\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma\geqslant\pi{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s
.
Moreover, equality holds if and only if
\gamma
is convex.

Use this statement to give another solution to Exercise
2E
.

The following exercise gives analogous formulas in the Euclidean space.

As before, we denote by
\text{\sc{w}}_{\text{\sc{u}}}
the orthogonal projection of
w
to the line passing thru the origin with direction
u
.
Further, let us denote by
\text{\sc{w}}_{\text{\sc{u}}}^{\bot}
the projection of
w
to the plane orthogonal to
u
;
that is,

\text{\sc{w}}_{\text{\sc{u}}}^{\bot}=\text{\sc{w}}-\text{\sc{w}}_{\text{\sc{u}}}.

We will use the notation

\overline{f(\text{\sc{u}})}
for the average value
of a function
f
defined on
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.

2.19. Advanced exercise.

Show that the length of a space curve is proportional to

(a)

the average length of its projections to all lines; that is,

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma=k_{1}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\overline{\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{\text{\sc{u}}}}

for some
k_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
.

(b)

the average length of its projections to all planes; that is,

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma=k_{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\overline{\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{\text{\sc{u}}}^{\bot}}

for some
k_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
.

Find the values
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
.

G
Length metric

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a metric space.
Given two points
x,y
in
\mathcal{X}
, denote by
\ell(x,y)
the greatest lower bound of lengths of all paths connecting
x
to
y
; if there is no such path, then
\ell(x,y)=\infty
.

It is straightforward to see that the function
\ell
satisfies all the axioms of a metric except it might take infinite values.
Therefore, if any two points in
\mathcal{X}
can be connected by a rectifiable curve, then
\ell
defines a new metric on
\mathcal{X}
;
in this case,
\ell
is called the
induced length-metric
.

Evidently,
\ell(x,y)\geqslant|{x}-\penalty{y}|
for any pair of points
x,y\in\mathcal{X}
.
If the equality holds for all pairs, then the metric
|{{*}}-\penalty{{*}}|
is said to be a
length-metric
, and the corresponding metric space is called a
length-metric space
.

2.20. Exercise.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a metric space with a well-defined induced length-metric
(x,y)\mapsto\ell(x,y)
.
Show that
\ell
is a length-metric.

Most of the time we consider length-metric spaces.
In particular, the Euclidean space is a length-metric space.
A subspace
A
of a length-metric space
\mathcal{X}
is not necessarily a length-metric space;
the induced length distance between points
x
and
y
in the subspace
A
will be denoted as
|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{A}
;
that is,
|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{A}
is the greatest lower bound of the lengths of paths in
A
from
x
to
y
.

2.21. Exercise.

Let
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a closed subset.
Show that
A
is convex if and only if

|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{A}=|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}

for any
x,y\in A

H
Spherical curves

Let us denote by
\mathbb{S}^{2}
the unit sphere in the space; that is,

\mathbb{S}^{2}=\left\{\,{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\,:\,{x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2}=1}\,\right\}.

A space curve
\gamma
is called
spherical
if it runs in
\mathbb{S}^{2}
;
that is,
|\gamma(t)|=1
for any
t
.

Recall that
\measuredangle(u,v)
denotes the angle between vectors
u
and
v
.

2.22. Observation.

For any
u,v\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
, we have

|{u}-\penalty{v}|_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}=\measuredangle(u,v).

Proof.

Let
\gamma
be the short arcof a great circle
2
2

2

A great circle is the intersection of the sphere with a plane passing thru its center.

from
u
to
v
in
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.
Note that
\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma=\measuredangle(u,v)
.
Therefore,

|{u}-\penalty{v}|_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\leqslant\measuredangle(u,v).

It remains to prove the opposite inequality.
In other words, we need to show that given a polygonal line
\beta=p_{0}\dots p_{n}
inscribed in
\gamma
there is a polygonal line

\beta_{1}=q_{0}\dots q_{n}
inscribed in any given spherical path
\gamma_{1}
connecting
u
to
v
such that

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\beta_{1}\geqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\beta.

➎

Define
q_{i}
as the first point on
\gamma_{1}
such that
|u-p_{i}|=|u-q_{i}|
, but set
q_{n}=v
.
Clearly,
\beta_{1}
is inscribed in
\gamma_{1}
, and, according to the triangle inequality for angles (
0C
), we have that

\displaystyle\measuredangle(q_{i-1},q_{i})

\displaystyle\geqslant\measuredangle(u,q_{i})-\measuredangle(u,q_{i-1})=

\displaystyle=\measuredangle(u,p_{i})-\measuredangle(u,p_{i-1})=

\displaystyle=\measuredangle(p_{i-1},p_{i}).

By the angle monotonicity (
0C
),

|q_{i-1}-q_{i}|\geqslant|p_{i-1}-p_{i}|

and
➎
follows.
∎

2.23. Hemisphere lemma.

Any closed spherical curve of length less than
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
lies in an open hemisphere.

This lemma will play a key role in the proof of Fenchel’s theorem (
3E
).
The following proof is due to Stephanie Alexander;
it is not as simple as one may think.
Try to prove the lemma before reading further.

Proof.

Let
\gamma
be a closed curve in
\mathbb{S}^{2}
of length
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell
.
Suppose
\ell<\pi
.

Let us subdivide
\gamma
into two arcs
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
of length
\ell
;
denote their common endpoints by
p
and
q
.
By
2H
,

\displaystyle\measuredangle(p,q)

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{1}=

\displaystyle=\ell<

\displaystyle<\pi.

Denote by
z
be the midpoint between
p
and
q
in
\mathbb{S}^{2}
;
that is,
z
is the midpoint of the short arc of a great circle from
p
to
q
in
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.
We claim that
\gamma
lies in the open hemisphere with the pole at
z
.
If not,
\gamma
intersects the equator at some point
r
.
Without loss of generality, we may assume that
r
lies on
\gamma_{1}
.

Rotate the arc
\gamma_{1}
by the angle
\pi
around the line thru
z
and the center of the sphere.
The obtained arc
\gamma_{1}^{*}
together with
\gamma_{1}
forms a closed curve of length
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell
passing thru
r
and its antipodal point
r^{*}
.
Applying
2H
again, we get

\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma=\ell\geqslant\measuredangle(r,r^{*})=\pi

— a contradiction.
∎

2.24. Exercise.

Describe a simple closed spherical curve that does not pass thru a pair of antipodal points and does not lie in any hemisphere.

2.25. Exercise.

Suppose a simple closed spherical curve
\gamma
divides
\mathbb{S}^{2}
in two regions of equal area.
Show that

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma\geqslant 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi.

2.26. Exercise.

Find a flaw in the solution of the following problem.
Come up with a correct argument.

Problem.

Suppose a closed plane curve
\gamma
has length at most 4.
Show that
\gamma
lies in a unit disc.

Wrong solution.

It is sufficient to show that the
diameter
of
\gamma
is at most 2;
that is,

|p-q|\leqslant 2

➏

for any two points
p
and
q
on
\gamma
.

The length of
\gamma
cannot be smaller than the closed inscribed polygonal line which goes from
p
to
q
and back to
p
.
Therefore,

2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|p-q|\leqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma\leqslant 4;

whence
➏
follows.

∎

2.27. Advanced exercises.

Given unit vectors
{\text{\sc{u}}},{\text{\sc{w}}}\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
, denote by
{\text{\sc{w}}}^{*}_{\text{\sc{u}}}
the nearest point to
w
on the equator with the pole at
u
;
in other words, if
{\text{\sc{w}}}^{\perp}_{\text{\sc{u}}}
is the projection of
w
to the plane perpendicular to
u
, then
{\text{\sc{w}}}^{*}_{\text{\sc{u}}}
is the unit vector in the direction of
{\text{\sc{w}}}^{\perp}_{\text{\sc{u}}}
.
The vector
{\text{\sc{w}}}^{*}_{\text{\sc{u}}}
is defined if
{\text{\sc{w}}}\neq\pm{\text{\sc{u}}}
.

(a)

Show that for any rectifiable
spherical curve
\gamma
we have

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma=\overline{\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma^{*}_{\text{\sc{u}}}},

where
\overline{\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma^{*}_{\text{\sc{u}}}}
denotes the average length of
\gamma^{*}_{\text{\sc{u}}}
with
u
varying in
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.
(This is a spherical analog of Crofton’s formula.)

(b)

Use
2H
to give another proof of the hemisphere lemma (
2H
).

Spherical Crofton’s formula can be rewritten the following way:

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma=\overline{n}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi,

where
\overline{n}
denotes the average number of intersection points of
\gamma
with equators.
The equivalence can be proved using Levi’s monotone convergence theorem.

Chapter 3
Curvature

Тhe term curvature is used for anything that measures how much a geometric object deviates from being
straight
, whatever that may mean.
For curves, the curvature is a pointwise quantitative measure of how much the curve differs from a straight line.

A
Acceleration of a unit-speed curve

Recall that any smooth curve can be reparametrized by its arc-length (
2D
).
The obtained parametrized curve, say
\gamma
, remains to be smooth, and it has unit speed;
that is,
|\gamma^{\prime}|=1
.
From mechanics, you might know that acceleration and velocity are perpendicular if the speed is constant.
Let us restate it.

3.1. Proposition.

Assume
\gamma
is a smooth unit-speed space curve.
Then
\gamma^{\prime}(s)\perp\gamma^{\prime\prime}(s)
for any
s
.

The
scalar product
(also known as
dot product
) of two vectors
v
and
w
will be denoted by
\langle\text{\sc{v}},\text{\sc{w}}\rangle
.
Recall that the derivative of a scalar product satisfies the product rule;
that is, if
\text{\sc{v}}=\text{\sc{v}}(t)
and
\text{\sc{w}}=\text{\sc{w}}(t)
are smooth vector-valued functions of a real parameter
t
, then

\langle\text{\sc{v}},\text{\sc{w}}\rangle^{\prime}=\langle\text{\sc{v}}^{\prime},\text{\sc{w}}\rangle+\langle\text{\sc{v}},\text{\sc{w}}^{\prime}\rangle.

Proof.

The identity
|\gamma^{\prime}|=1
can be rewritten as
\langle\gamma^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}\rangle=1
.
Differentiating both sides, we get

2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\gamma^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime}\rangle=\langle\gamma^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}\rangle^{\prime}=0
;
whence
\gamma^{\prime\prime}\perp\gamma^{\prime}
.
∎

B
Curvature

For a unit-speed smooth space curve
\gamma
, the magnitude of its acceleration
|\gamma^{\prime\prime}(s)|
is called its
curvature
at time
s
.
If
\gamma
is simple, then we can say that
|\gamma^{\prime\prime}(s)|
is the curvature at the point
p=\gamma(s)
without ambiguity.
The curvature is usually denoted by
\kappa(s)
or
\kappa(s)_{\gamma}
, and, in the case of simple curves, it might be also denoted by
\kappa(p)
or
\kappa(p)_{\gamma}
.

3.2. Exercise.

Show that a smooth simple space curve has zero curvature at each point if and only if it is a segment of a straight line.

3.3. Exercise.

Let
\gamma
be a smooth simple space curve, and let
\gamma_{\lambda}
be a scaled copy of
\gamma
with factor
\lambda>0
;
that is,
\gamma_{\lambda}(t)=\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\gamma(t)
for any
t
.
Show that

\kappa(\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}p)_{\gamma_{\lambda}}=\frac{\kappa(p)_{\gamma}}{\lambda}

for any
p\in\gamma
.

3.4. Exercise.

Show that any smooth spherical curve has curvature at least 1.

C
Tangent indicatrix

Let
\gamma
be a smooth space curve.
The curve

\text{\sc{t}}(t)=\tfrac{\gamma^{\prime}(t)}{|\gamma^{\prime}(t)|};

➊

it is called a
tangent indicatrix
of
\gamma
.
Note that
|\text{\sc{t}}(t)|=1
for any
t
;
that is,
t
is a spherical curve.

If
s\mapsto\gamma(s)
is a unit-speed parametrization, then
\text{\sc{t}}(s)=\gamma^{\prime}(s)
.
In this case, we have the following expression for the curvature:

\kappa(s)=\penalty|\text{\sc{t}}^{\prime}(s)|=\penalty|\gamma^{\prime\prime}(s)|.

For a general parametrization
t\mapsto\gamma(t)
,
we have instead

\kappa(t)=\frac{|\text{\sc{t}}^{\prime}(t)|}{|\gamma^{\prime}(t)|}.

➋

Indeed, for an arc-length reparametrization by
s(t)
, we have
s^{\prime}(t)=|\gamma^{\prime}(t)|
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\kappa

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{d\text{\sc{t}}}{ds}\right|=\left|\frac{d\text{\sc{t}}}{dt}\right|/\left|\frac{ds}{dt}\right|=\frac{|\text{\sc{t}}^{\prime}|}{|\gamma^{\prime}|}.

It follows that the tangent indicatrix of a smooth curve
\gamma
is smooth if the curvature of
\gamma
does not vanish.

3.5. Exercise.

Use the formulas
➊
and
➋
to show that
for any smooth space curve
\gamma
we have the following expressions for its curvature:

(a)

\kappa=\frac{|\text{\sc{w}}|}{|\gamma^{\prime}|^{2}},

where
w
is the projection of
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t)
to the plane normal to
\gamma^{\prime}(t)
;

(b)

\kappa=\frac{|\gamma^{\prime\prime}\times\gamma^{\prime}|}{|\gamma^{\prime}|^{3}},

where
\times
denotes the
vector product
(also known as
cross product
).

3.6. Exercise.

Apply the formulas in the previous exercise to show that if
f
is a smooth real function,
then its graph
y=f(x)
has curvature

\kappa=\frac{|f^{\prime\prime}(x)|}{(1+f^{\prime}(x)^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}}

at the point
(x,f(x))
.

3.7. Advanced exercise.

Show that any smooth curve
\gamma\colon\mathbb{I}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}^{3}
with curvature at most 1 can be approximated by smooth curves with constant curvature 1.

In other words, construct a sequence
\gamma_{n}\colon\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
of smooth curves with constant curvature 1 such that
\gamma_{n}(t)\to\gamma(t)
for any
t
as
n\to\infty
.

D
Tangents

Let
\gamma
be a smooth space curve, and let
t
be its tangent indicatrix.
The line thru
\gamma(t)
in the direction of
\text{\sc{t}}(t)
is called the
tangent line
to
\gamma
at
t
.
Any vector proportional to
\text{\sc{t}}(t)
is called the
tangent
to
\gamma
at
t
.

The tangent line could be also defined as a unique line that has
first-order contact
with
\gamma
at
t
;
that is,
\rho(\ell)=o(\ell)
, where
\rho(\ell)
denotes the distance from
\gamma(t+\ell)
to the line.

3.8. Advanced exercise.

Construct a smooth closed space curve without parallel tangent lines.

We say that smooth curves
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are
tangent
at
s_{1}
and
s_{2}

if
\gamma_{1}(s_{1})=\gamma_{2}(s_{2})
and the tangent line of
\gamma_{1}
at
s_{1}
coincides with the tangent line of
\gamma_{2}
at
s_{2}
;
if both curves are simple we can also say that they are tangent at the point
p=\gamma_{1}(s_{1})=\penalty\gamma_{2}(s_{2})
without ambiguity.

E
Total curvature

Let
\gamma\colon\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a smooth unit-speed curve.
The integral

\Phi({\gamma})\mathrel{:=}\int_{\mathbb{I}}\kappa(s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}ds

is called the
total curvature of

\gamma
, and it measures the total change of direction along
\gamma
.

Rewriting the above integral using a change of variables produces a formula for a general parametrization
t\mapsto\gamma(t)
:

\Phi({\gamma})\mathrel{:=}\int_{\mathbb{I}}\kappa(t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|\gamma^{\prime}(t)|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt.

➌

3.9. Exercise.

Find the curvature of the helix

\gamma_{a,b}(t)=(a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos t,a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sin t,b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t),

its tangent indicatrix, and the total curvature of its arc
\gamma_{a,b}|_{[0,2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi]}
.

Note that for a unit-speed smooth curve,
the speed of its tangent indicatrix equals its curvature.
Therefore, we get the following.

3.10. Observation.

The total curvature of a smooth curve is the length of its tangent indicatrix.

3.11. Fenchel’s theorem.

The total curvature of any closed smooth space curve is at least
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
.

Proof.

Fix a closed smooth space curve
\gamma
.
We can assume that
\gamma
is described by a unit-speed loop
\gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
;
in this case,
\gamma(a)=\gamma(b)
and
\gamma^{\prime}(a)=\gamma^{\prime}(b)
.

Consider its tangent indicatrix
\text{\sc{t}}=\gamma^{\prime}
.
Recall that
|\text{\sc{t}}(s)|=1
for any
s
; that is,
t
is a closed spherical curve.

Let us show that
t
cannot lie in a hemisphere.
Arguing by contradiction, we can assume that it lies in the hemisphere defined by the inequality
z>0
in
(x,y,z)
-coordinates.
In other words, if
\gamma(t)=(x(t),y(t),z(t))
, then
z^{\prime}(t)>0
for any
t
.
Therefore,

z(b)-z(a)=\int_{a}^{b}z^{\prime}(s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}ds>0.

In particular,
\gamma(a)\neq\gamma(b)
— a contradiction.

Applying the observation (
3E
) and the hemisphere lemma (
2H
), we get

\Phi({\gamma})=\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\text{\sc{t}}\geqslant 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi.

∎

3.12. Exercise.

Show that a closed space curve
\gamma
with curvature at most
1
cannot be shorter than the unit circle;
that is,

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma\geqslant 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi.

3.13. Advanced exercise.

Suppose
\gamma
is a smooth space curve that does not pass thru the origin.
Consider the spherical curve
\sigma
defined by
\sigma(t)\mathrel{:=}\penalty\frac{\gamma(t)}{|\gamma(t)|}
.
Show that

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\sigma<\Phi({\gamma})+\pi.

Moreover, if
\gamma
is closed, then

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\sigma\leqslant\Phi({\gamma}).

The last inequality gives an alternative proof of Fenchel’s theorem.
Indeed, without loss of generality, we can assume that the origin lies on a chord of
\gamma
.
In this case, the closed spherical curve
\sigma
goes from a point to its antipode and comes back;
it takes length at least
\pi
each way,
whence

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\sigma\geqslant 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi.

Recall that the curvature of a spherical curve is at least
1

(see
3B
).
In particular, the length of a spherical curve cannot exceed its total curvature.
The following theorem shows that the same inequality holds for
closed
curves in a unit ball.
