3.14. DNA theorem.

Let
\gamma
be a smooth closed curve that lies in a unit ball.
Then

\Phi({\gamma})\geqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma.

Several proofs of this theorem are collected by Serge Tabachnikov
[
90
]
.
The 2-dimensional case of this theorem was proved by István Fáry
[
28
]
.
It was generalized by Don Chakerian
[
18
]
to higher dimensions.
The following exercise guides you thru another proof of him
[
19
]
.
Yet another proof is given in Section
4E
.

3.15. Exercise.

Let
\gamma\colon[0,\ell]\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a smooth unit-speed closed curve that lies in the unit ball; that is,
|\gamma|\leqslant 1
.

(a)

Show that

\langle\gamma^{\prime\prime}(s),\gamma(s)\rangle\geqslant-\kappa(s)

for any

.

3E
to show that

\int_{0}^{\ell}\langle\gamma(s),\gamma^{\prime}(s)\rangle^{\prime}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}ds\geqslant\ell-\Phi({\gamma}).

(c)

Suppose
\gamma(0)=\gamma(\ell)
and
\gamma^{\prime}(0)=\gamma^{\prime}(\ell)
.
Show that

\int_{0}^{\ell}\langle\gamma(s),\gamma^{\prime}(s)\rangle^{\prime}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}ds=0.

Use this equality together with part
3E
to prove
3E
.

F
Convex curves

In this section, we show that the tangent indicatrix of a convex curve rotates monotonically.
The following exercise provides a key observation for the proof.

3.16. Exercise.

Let
\gamma
be a smooth convex plane curve;
denote by
F
the convex set bounded by
\gamma
.
Show that a line
\ell
is tangent to
\gamma
at point
p
if and only if it
supports

F
at
p
;
that is,
\ell\ni p
and
F
lies in a closed half-plane bounded by
\ell
.

Recall that a map is monotone if the inverse image of any point in the target space is a connected set (in particular, nonempty).

3.17. Proposition.

Let
\gamma
be a smooth convex plane curve.

(a)

Suppose
\gamma
is closed and it is parametrized as
\gamma\colon\mathbb{S}^{1}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Then its tangent indicatrix
\text{\sc{t}}\colon\mathbb{S}^{1}\to\mathbb{S}^{1}
is a monotone map.

(b)

Suppose
\gamma
is open, it is parametrized as
\gamma\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Then its tangent indicatrix
\text{\sc{t}}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{S}^{1}
defines a monotone map to an interval in a closed semicircle.

The following corollary says that
for convex curves we have equality in Fenchel’s theorem
(
3E
).
Later we, in
4D
, we will show that equality holds
only
for convex curves.

3.18. Corollary.

Let
\gamma
be a convex plane curve.

(a)

If
\gamma
is closed, then
\Phi({\gamma})=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
.

(b)

If
\gamma
is open, then
\Phi({\gamma})\leqslant\pi
.

Proof.

Follows from
3F
,
3E
and
2A
.
∎

Proof of
3F
;
3F
.

Since
\gamma
is closed, it bounds a compact convex set
F
.
We may assume that
F
lies on the left from
\gamma
.

Choose a unit vector
u
.
Let
(x,y)
be the coordinates of the plane with
x
-axis in the direction of
u
.

From Exercise
3F
, it follows that
\text{\sc{u}}=\text{\sc{t}}(s)
if and only if
p=\gamma(s)
is a minimum point of the
y
-coordinate function on
F
.
Indeed suppose
p
is a minimum point.
Let
\ell
be the horizontal line thru
p
.
Then
\ell
supports
F
at
p
.
By the exercise,
\ell
is tangent to
\gamma
at
p
.
Since
F
lies on the left from
\gamma
, we get that
\text{\sc{t}}(s)=\text{\sc{u}}
.
And the other way around, if
\text{\sc{t}}(s)=\text{\sc{u}}
then, the tangent line at
p=\gamma(s)
is horizontal,
and, by the exercise, it supports
F
.
Since
F
lies on the left from
\gamma
, we get that the
y
-coordinate on
F
has a minimum at
p
.

Since
F
is compact, there is a minimum point
p=\gamma(s)
for the
y
-coordinate.
The point
p
might be unique, in this case,
\text{\sc{t}}^{-1}\{\text{\sc{u}}\}=s
,
or
f
might have a line segment of minimal points in
\gamma
, in this case,
\text{\sc{t}}^{-1}\{\text{\sc{u}}\}
is an arc of
\mathbb{S}^{1}
.
It follows that
\text{\sc{t}}\colon\mathbb{S}^{1}\to\mathbb{S}^{1}
is monotone.

3F

The same argument in
3F
shows that
t
is a monotone map to its image.
Evidently, the image is a connected set in
\mathbb{S}^{1}
.
It remains to show that the image lies in a semicircle;
in other words,

\measuredangle(\text{\sc{w}},\text{\sc{t}}(s))\geqslant\tfrac{\pi}{2}\quad\text{for some}\quad\text{\sc{w}}\quad\text{and any}\quad s.

➍

Since
\gamma
is open, it bounds an unbounded convex closed region
F
.
As before we assume that
F
lies on the left from
\gamma
.

There is a half-line, say
h
, that lies in
F
.
Indeed, we can assume that the origin
o
lies in
F
.
Consider a sequence of points
q_{n}\in F
such that
|q_{n}|\to\penalty\infty
as
n\to\infty
.
Denote by
\text{\sc{v}}_{n}
the unit vector in the direction of
q_{n}
; that is
\text{\sc{v}}_{n}=\tfrac{q_{n}}{|q_{n}|}
.

Since the unit circle is compact, we can pass to a subsequence of
q_{n}
such that
\text{\sc{v}}_{n}
converges to a unit vector, say
v
.
Let us draw a half-line
h
from
o
in the direction of
v
.
Any point on
h
can be approximated by points from the segments
[o,q_{n}]
as
n\to\infty
.
Since the set
F
is closed, the half-line
h
lies in
F

Let
w
be the counterclockwise rotation of
v
by an angle
\tfrac{\pi}{2}
, let

\ell
be the tangent line to
\gamma
at the point
p=\gamma(s)
,
and let
H
be the closed left half-plane bounded by
\ell
;
that is,
H
lies to the left of
\ell
in the direction of
\text{\sc{t}}(s)
.
The same reasoning as in

shows that
F
, and therefore

,
lie in

.
In particular,
v
points from

into

, which is equivalent to
➍
.
∎

G
Bow lemma

The following lemma proved by Erhard Schmidt
[
85
]
; it generalizes the result of by Axel Schur
[
87
]
.

This lemma is a differential-geometric analog of the so-called
arm lemma
of Augustin-Louis Cauchy.
The arm lemma says that

if
p_{0}\dots p_{n}
is a convex plane polygon and
q_{0}\dots q_{n}
is a polygonal line in the space such that

\displaystyle|p_{i}-p_{i-1}|

\displaystyle=|q_{i}-q_{i-1}|,

\displaystyle\measuredangle[p_{i}\,{}^{p_{i+1}}_{p_{i-1}}]

\displaystyle\leqslant\measuredangle[q_{i}\,{}^{q_{i+1}}_{q_{i-1}}]

for each
i
, then
|p_{0}-p_{n}|\leqslant|q_{0}-q_{n}|
.

(Intuitively, if you extend all the joints in your arm, then the distance from your shoulder to the tip of your middle finger increases.)

3.19. Lemma.

Let
\gamma_{1}\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\gamma_{2}\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
be two smooth unit-speed curves.
Suppose
\kappa(s)_{\gamma_{1}}\geqslant\kappa(s)_{\gamma_{2}}
for any
s

and the curve

\gamma_{1}
is an arc of a convex curve; that is, it runs in the boundary of a convex plane figure.
Then the distance between the endpoints of
\gamma_{1}
cannot exceed the distance between the endpoints of
\gamma_{2}
; that is,

|\gamma_{1}(b)-\gamma_{1}(a)|\leqslant|\gamma_{2}(b)-\gamma_{2}(a)|.

The following exercise states that the condition that
\gamma_{1}
is a convex arc is necessary.
It is instructive to do this exercise before reading the proof of the lemma.

3.20. Exercise.

Construct two simple smooth unit-speed plane curves
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
\kappa(s)_{\gamma_{1}}>\kappa(s)_{\gamma_{2}}>0
for any
s
and

|\gamma_{1}(b)-\gamma_{1}(a)|>|\gamma_{2}(b)-\gamma_{2}(a)|.

Proof.

We can assume that
\gamma_{1}(a)\neq\gamma_{1}(b)
;
otherwise the statement is evident.
By convexity, the curve
\gamma_{1}
lies on one side of the line
\ell
thru
\gamma(a)
and
\gamma(b)
;
we may assume
\ell
is horizontal and
\gamma_{1}
lies below
\ell
.

Let
s_{0}
be the lowest point on
\gamma_{1}
;
that is,
\gamma_{1}(s_{0})
has minimal
y
-coordinate.

Denote by
\text{\sc{t}}_{1}
and
\text{\sc{t}}_{2}
the tangent indicatrixes of
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
, respectively.
Consider two unit vectors

\text{\sc{u}}_{1}=\text{\sc{t}}_{1}(s_{0})=\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(s_{0})\quad\text{and}\quad\text{\sc{u}}_{2}=\text{\sc{t}}_{2}(s_{0})=\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(s_{0}).

Note that
\gamma_{1}(b)
lies in the direction of
\text{\sc{u}}_{1}
from
\gamma_{1}(a)
.

Lets us show that

\measuredangle(\gamma^{\prime}_{1}(s),\text{\sc{u}}_{1})\geqslant\measuredangle(\gamma^{\prime}_{2}(s),\text{\sc{u}}_{2})

➎

for any
s
.
We will prove it for
s\leqslant s_{0}
; the case
s\geqslant s_{0}
is analogous.

Note that

\measuredangle(\gamma^{\prime}_{1}(s),\text{\sc{u}}_{1})=\measuredangle(\text{\sc{t}}_{1}(s),\text{\sc{u}}_{1})=\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits(\text{\sc{t}}_{1}|_{[s,s_{0}]}).

➏

for any
s\leqslant s_{0}
.
Indeed, by
3F
, the
y
-coordinate of
\gamma_{1}
is nonincreasing in the interval
[a,s_{0}]
.
Therefore, the arc
\text{\sc{t}}_{1}|_{[a,s_{0}]}
lies in one of the unit semicircles with endpoints
\text{\sc{u}}_{1}
and
-\text{\sc{u}}_{1}
.
It remains to apply
3E
,
3F
, and
2A
.

By
2H
, we also have

\measuredangle(\gamma^{\prime}_{2}(s),\text{\sc{u}}_{2})=\measuredangle(\text{\sc{t}}_{2}(s),\text{\sc{u}}_{2})\leqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits(\text{\sc{t}}_{2}|_{[s,s_{0}]}).

➐

Further,

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits(\text{\sc{t}}_{1}|_{[s,s_{0}]})

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{s_{0}}|\text{\sc{t}}_{1}^{\prime}(t)|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt=

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{s_{0}}\kappa_{1}(t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt\geqslant

\displaystyle\geqslant\int_{s}^{s_{0}}\kappa_{2}(t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt=

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{s_{0}}|\text{\sc{t}}_{2}^{\prime}(t)|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt=

\displaystyle=\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits(\text{\sc{t}}_{2}|_{[s,s_{0}]}).

This inequality, together with
➏
and
➐
, implies
➎
.

Since
1=|\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(s)|=|\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(s)|=|\text{\sc{u}}_{1}|=|\text{\sc{u}}_{2}|
,
we have

\langle\gamma^{\prime}_{1}(s),\text{\sc{u}}_{1}\rangle=\cos\measuredangle(\gamma^{\prime}_{1}(s),\text{\sc{u}}_{1})\quad\text{and}\quad\langle\gamma^{\prime}_{2}(s),\text{\sc{u}}_{2}\rangle=\cos\measuredangle(\gamma^{\prime}_{2}(s),\text{\sc{u}}_{2}).

The cosine is decreasing in the interval
[0,\pi]
; therefore,
➎
implies

\langle\gamma^{\prime}_{1}(s),\text{\sc{u}}_{1}\rangle\leqslant\langle\gamma^{\prime}_{2}(s),\text{\sc{u}}_{2}\rangle

➑

for any
s
.

Further, since
\gamma_{1}(b)
lies in the direction of
\text{\sc{u}}_{1}
from
\gamma_{1}(a)
, we have that

|\gamma_{1}(b)-\gamma_{1}(a)|=\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{1},\gamma_{1}(b)-\gamma_{1}(a)\rangle.

Since
\text{\sc{u}}_{2}
is a unit vector, we have that

|\gamma_{2}(b)-\gamma_{2}(a)|\geqslant\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{2},\gamma_{2}(b)-\gamma_{2}(a)\rangle.

Integrating
➑
, we get

\displaystyle|\gamma_{1}(b)-\gamma_{1}(a)|

\displaystyle=\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{1},\gamma_{1}(b)-\gamma_{1}(a)\rangle=

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{b}\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{1},\gamma^{\prime}_{1}(s)\rangle{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}ds\leqslant\int_{a}^{b}\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{2},\gamma^{\prime}_{2}(s)\rangle{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}ds=

\displaystyle=\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{2},\gamma_{2}(b)-\gamma_{2}(a)\rangle\leqslant|\gamma_{2}(b)-\gamma_{2}(a)|.

∎

3.21. Advanced exercise.

Let
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
be as in the bow lemma (
3G
);
denote by
\text{\sc{t}}_{1}
and

Set

\displaystyle\text{\sc{w}}_{i}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{i}(b)-\gamma_{i}(a),

\displaystyle=\measuredangle(\text{\sc{t}}_{i}(a),\text{\sc{w}}_{i}),

\displaystyle=\measuredangle(\text{\sc{t}}_{i}(b),\text{\sc{w}}_{i}).

Suppose that

.
Show that
\alpha_{1}\geqslant\alpha_{2}
.

does not hold in general.

3.22. Exercise.

, and

.

.

.

3.26. Advanced exercise.

Let
\gamma
be a closed smooth space curve with curvature at most 2.
Suppose

for any
t
.
Show that if

, then

|\gamma(t)|\leqslant\sin[\alpha(t)]

denotes the angle between
\gamma(t)
and

.

Chapter 4
Polygonal lines

This chapter reinterprets the curvature of curves via angles of inscribed polygonal lines;
it should help to build a firm geometric intuition about curvature.

A
Piecewise smooth curves

Assume
\alpha\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
\beta\colon[b,c]\to\penalty\mathbb{R}^{3}
are two curves such that
\alpha(b)=\penalty\beta(b)
.
These two curves can be combined into one
\gamma\colon[a,c]\to\penalty\mathbb{R}^{3}
by the rule

\gamma(t)=\begin{cases}\alpha(t)&\text{if}\quad t\leqslant b,\\
\beta(t)&\text{if}\quad t>b.\end{cases}

The obtained curve
\gamma
is called the

concatenation
of
\alpha
and
\beta
.
(The condition
\alpha(b)=\beta(b)
ensures that the map
t\mapsto\gamma(t)
is continuous.)

The same definition of concatenation can be applied if
\alpha
and/or
\beta
are defined on semiopen intervals

(a,b]
and/or
[b,c)
.

The assumption that the time intervals of
\alpha
and
\beta
fit together is not essential — one can concatenate if the endpoint of
\alpha
coincides with the starting point of
\beta
.
To do this, the time intervals of the curves should be shifted so that they fit together.

If in addition
\beta(c)=\alpha(a)
, then we can do cyclic concatenation of these curves;
this way we obtain a closed curve.

If
\alpha^{\prime}(b)
and
\beta^{\prime}(b)
are defined, then the angle
\theta=\penalty\measuredangle(\alpha^{\prime}(b),\beta^{\prime}(b))
is called the
external angle
of
\gamma
at time
b
.
If
\theta=\pi
, then we say that
\gamma
has a
cusp
at time
b
.

A space curve
\gamma
is called
piecewise smooth
if it can be presented as an iterated concatenation of a finite number of smooth curves; if
\gamma
is closed, then the concatenation is assumed to be cyclic.

If
\gamma
is a concatenation of smooth arcs
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}
, then the total curvature of
\gamma
is defined as a sum of the total curvatures of
\gamma_{i}
and the external angles;
that is,

\Phi({\gamma})=\Phi({\gamma_{1}})+\dots+\Phi({\gamma_{n}})+\theta_{1}+\dots+\theta_{n-1}

where
\theta_{i}
is the external angle at the joint between
\gamma_{i}
and
\gamma_{i+1}
.

If
\gamma
is closed, then the total curvature of
\gamma
is defined by

\Phi({\gamma})=\Phi({\gamma_{1}})+\dots+\Phi({\gamma_{n}})+\theta_{1}+\dots+\theta_{n},

where
\theta_{n}
is the external angle at the joint between
\gamma_{n}
and
\gamma_{1}
.

In particular, for a smooth loop
\gamma\colon[a,b]\to\penalty\mathbb{R}^{3}
, the total curvature of the corresponding closed curve
\hat{\gamma}
is

\Phi({\hat{\gamma}})\mathrel{:=}\Phi({\gamma})+\theta,

where
\theta=\measuredangle(\gamma^{\prime}(a),\gamma^{\prime}(b))
.

B
Generalized Fenchel’s theorem

4.1. Theorem.

Let
\gamma
be a closed piecewise smooth space curve.
Then

\Phi({\gamma})\geqslant 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi.

Proof.

Suppose
\gamma
is a cyclic concatenation of smooth arcs
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}
.
Denote by
\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{n}
its external angles.
We need to show that

\Phi({\gamma_{1}})+\dots+\Phi({\gamma_{n}})+\theta_{1}+\dots+\theta_{n}\geqslant 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi.

➊

Consider the tangent indicatrix
\text{\sc{t}}_{i}
for each arc
\gamma_{i}
;
these are spherical arcs.

The same argument as in the proof of Fenchel’s theorem (
3E
) shows that the curves
\text{\sc{t}}_{1},\dots,\text{\sc{t}}_{n}
cannot lie in an open hemisphere.

The spherical distance from the endpoint of
\text{\sc{t}}_{i}
to the starting point of
\text{\sc{t}}_{i+1}
is equal to the external angle
\theta_{i}
(we enumerate the arcs modulo
n
, so
\gamma_{n+1}=\gamma_{1}
).
Let us connect the endpoint of
\text{\sc{t}}_{i}
to the starting point of
\text{\sc{t}}_{i+1}
by a short arc of a great circle in the sphere.
This way we get a closed spherical curve that is
\theta_{1}+\dots+\theta_{n}
longer than the total length of
\text{\sc{t}}_{1},\dots,\text{\sc{t}}_{n}
.

Applying the hemisphere lemma (
2H
) to the obtained closed curve, we get that

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\text{\sc{t}}_{1}+\dots+\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\text{\sc{t}}_{n}+\theta_{1}+\dots+\theta_{n}\geqslant 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi.

By
3E
, the statement follows.

∎

4.2. Chord lemma.

Let
\gamma\colon[a,b]\to\penalty\mathbb{R}^{3}

be a smooth arc, and

\ell
be its chord.
Assume
\gamma
meets
\ell
at angles
\alpha
and
\beta
at
\gamma(a)
and
\gamma(b)
, respectively;
that is,

\alpha=\measuredangle(\text{\sc{w}},\gamma^{\prime}(a))\quad\text{and}\quad\beta=\measuredangle(\text{\sc{w}},\gamma^{\prime}(b)),

where
\text{\sc{w}}=\gamma(b)-\gamma(a)
.
Then

\Phi({\gamma})\geqslant\alpha+\beta.

➋

Proof.

Let us parametrize the chord
\ell
from
\gamma(b)
to
\gamma(a)
and consider the cyclic concatenation
\hat{\gamma}
of
\gamma
and
\ell
.
The closed curve
\hat{\gamma}
has two external angles
\pi-\alpha
and
\pi-\beta
.

Since the curvature of
\ell
vanishes, we get

\Phi({\hat{\gamma}})=\Phi({\gamma})+(\pi-\alpha)+(\pi-\beta).

According to the generalized Fenchel’s theorem (
4B
),

\Phi({\hat{\gamma}})\geqslant 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
;
hence
➋
follows.
∎

4.3. Exercise.

Show that the estimate in the chord lemma is optimal.

More precisely, given two distinct points
p,q
and two unit vectors
\text{\sc{u}},\text{\sc{v}}
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
construct a smooth curve
\gamma
that starts at
p
in the direction
u
and ends at
q
in the direction
v
such that

\Phi({\gamma})
is arbitrarily close to
\measuredangle(\text{\sc{w}},\text{\sc{u}})+\measuredangle(\text{\sc{w}},\text{\sc{v}})
, where vector
w
points from
p
to
q
.

C
Polygonal lines

Polygonal lines are a particular case of piecewise smooth curves;
each arc in its concatenation is a line segment.
Since the curvature of a line segment vanishes, the total curvature of a polygonal line is the sum of its external angles.

4.4. Exercise.

Let
a
,
b
,
c
,
d
, and
x
be distinct points in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
Show that the total curvature of the polygonal line
abcd
cannot exceed the total curvature of
abxcd
; that is,

\Phi({abcd})\leqslant\Phi({abxcd}).

Use this statement to show that any closed polygonal line has curvature at least
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
.

4.5. Proposition.

Assume a polygonal line
p_{0}\dots p_{n}
is inscribed in a smooth curve
\gamma
.
Then

\Phi({\gamma})\geqslant\Phi({p_{0}\dots p_{n}}).

Moreover, if
\gamma
is closed, we allow the inscribed polygonal line
p_{0}\dots p_{n}
to be closed.

Proof.

Assume that
\gamma
is closed.
Set

\displaystyle p_{i}

\displaystyle=\gamma(t_{i}),

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}

\displaystyle=\measuredangle(\text{\sc{w}}_{i},\text{\sc{v}}_{i}),

\displaystyle\text{\sc{w}}_{i}

\displaystyle=p_{i+1}-p_{i},

\displaystyle\beta_{i}

\displaystyle=\measuredangle(\text{\sc{w}}_{i-1},\text{\sc{v}}_{i}),

\displaystyle\text{\sc{v}}_{i}

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\prime}(t_{i}),

\displaystyle\theta_{i}

\displaystyle=\measuredangle(\text{\sc{w}}_{i-1},\text{\sc{w}}_{i}).

Let us use indexes modulo
n
;
so
p_{n+1}=\penalty p_{1}
.

Since the curvature of line segments vanishes,
the total curvature of the polygonal line
p_{0}\dots p_{n}
is the sum of external angles
\theta_{i}
.

By triangle inequality for angles
0C
, we get that

\theta_{i}\leqslant\alpha_{i}+\beta_{i}.

By the chord lemma, the total curvature of the arc of
\gamma
from
p_{i}
to
p_{i+1}
is at least
\alpha_{i}+\beta_{i+1}
.
Therefore, if
\gamma
is a closed curve, we have

\displaystyle\Phi({p_{0}\dots p_{n}})

\displaystyle=\theta_{1}+\dots+\theta_{n}\leqslant

\displaystyle\leqslant\beta_{1}+\alpha_{1}+\dots+\beta_{n}+\alpha_{n}=

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1}+\beta_{2})+\dots+(\alpha_{n}+\beta_{1})\leqslant

\displaystyle\leqslant\Phi({\gamma}).

If
\gamma
is not closed, the calculations are analogous:

\displaystyle\Phi({p_{0}\dots p_{n}})

\displaystyle=\theta_{1}+\dots+\theta_{n-1}\leqslant

\displaystyle\leqslant\beta_{1}+\alpha_{1}+\dots+\beta_{n-1}+\alpha_{n-1}\leqslant

\displaystyle\leqslant(\alpha_{0}+\beta_{1})+\dots+(\alpha_{n-1}+\beta_{n})\leqslant

\displaystyle\leqslant\Phi({\gamma}).

∎

4.6. Exercise.

Use the above results to give another solution to Exercise
3G
.

4.7. Exercise.

Suppose a closed curve
\gamma
crosses a line at four points
a
,
b
,
c
, and
d
.
Assume these points appear on the line in the order
a
,
b
,
c
,
d

and they appear on the curve
\gamma
in the order
a
,
c
,
b
,
d
.
Show that

\Phi({\gamma})\geqslant 4{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi.

D
What if
\bm{\Phi(\gamma)=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi}
?

4.8. Proposition.

The equality case in Fenchel’s theorem holds only for convex plane curves;
that is, the total curvature of a smooth space curve
\gamma
equals
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
if and only if
\gamma
is a convex plane curve.

Proof of
4D
.

The if part is proved in Corollary
3F
;
it remains to prove the only-if part.

Consider an inscribed quadrangle
abcd
in
\gamma
.
By the definition of total curvature, we have that

\displaystyle\Phi({abcd})

\displaystyle=(\pi-\measuredangle[a\,{}^{d}_{b}])+(\pi-\measuredangle[b\,{}^{a}_{c}])+(\pi-\measuredangle[c\,{}^{b}_{d}])+(\pi-\measuredangle[d\,{}^{c}_{a}])=

\displaystyle=4{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi-(\measuredangle[a\,{}^{d}_{b}]+\measuredangle[b\,{}^{a}_{c}]+\measuredangle[c\,{}^{b}_{d}]+\measuredangle[d\,{}^{c}_{a}]))

By the triangle inequality for angles (
0C
),

\measuredangle[b\,{}^{a}_{c}]\leqslant\measuredangle[b\,{}^{a}_{d}]+\measuredangle[b\,{}^{d}_{c}]\quad\text{and}\quad\measuredangle[d\,{}^{c}_{a}]\leqslant\measuredangle[d\,{}^{c}_{b}]+\measuredangle[d\,{}^{b}_{a}].

➌

The sum of angles in any triangle is
\pi
, so combining these inequalities, we get that

\displaystyle\Phi({abcd})\geqslant 4{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi

\displaystyle-(\measuredangle[a\,{}^{d}_{b}]+\measuredangle[b\,{}^{a}_{d}]+\measuredangle[d\,{}^{b}_{a}])-

\displaystyle-(\measuredangle[c\,{}^{b}_{d}]+\measuredangle[d\,{}^{c}_{b}]+\measuredangle[b\,{}^{d}_{c}])=

\displaystyle=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi.

By
4C
,

\Phi({abcd})\leqslant\Phi({\gamma})\leqslant 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi.

Therefore, we have equalities in
➌
.
It means that point
d
lies in the angle
abc

and point
b
lies in the angle
cda
.
The latter implies that
abcd
is a convex plane quadrangle.

It follows that any quadrangle inscribed in
\gamma
is a convex plane quadrangle.
Therefore, all points of
\gamma
lie in one plane defined by three points on
\gamma
.
Further, since any quadrangle inscribed in
\gamma
is convex,
we get that
\gamma
is convex as well.
∎

E
Generalized DNA theorem

4.9. Theorem.

Let
p_{1}\dots p_{n}
be a closed polygonal line in a unit ball.
Then

\Phi({p_{1}\dots p_{n}})>\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits(p_{1}\dots p_{n}).

Exercise
4F
and this theorem imply the original DNA theorem (
3E
).
Therefore,
4E
generalizes
3E
.

Proof.

We assume that
p_{n}=p_{0}
,
p_{n+1}=p_{1}
, and so on.
Denote by
\theta_{i}
the external angle at
p_{i}
.

Denote by
o
the center of the ball.
Consider a sequence of triangles

\triangle q_{0}q_{1}s_{0}\cong\triangle p_{0}p_{1}o,\ \ \triangle q_{1}q_{2}s_{1}\cong\triangle p_{1}p_{2}o,\ \dots

such that the points
q_{0},q_{1},\dots
lie on one line in that order and all the
s_{i}
’s lie on one side from this line.

Note that
s_{0}s_{n}q_{n}q_{0}
is a parallelogram; hence

|s_{n}-s_{0}|=|q_{n}-q_{0}|=\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits(p_{1}\dots p_{n}).

Therefore

|s_{0}-s_{1}|+\dots+|s_{n-1}-s_{n}|\geqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits(p_{1}\dots p_{n}).

Furthermore,

|q_{i}-s_{i-1}|=|q_{i}-s_{i}|=|p_{i}-o|\leqslant 1

and

\theta_{i}\geqslant\measuredangle[q_{i}\,{}^{s_{i-1}}_{s_{i}}]

for each
i
.
It follows that

\theta_{i}>|s_{i-1}-s_{i}|

for each
i
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\Phi({p_{1}\dots p_{n}})

\displaystyle=\theta_{1}+\dots+\theta_{n}>

\displaystyle>|s_{0}-s_{1}|+\dots+|s_{n-1}-s_{n}|\geqslant

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits(p_{1}\dots p_{n}).

∎

Let us mention the following generalization of the DNA inequality;
it was proved by Jeffrey Lagarias and Thomas Richardson
[
52
]
; another proof is given by Alexander Nazarov and Fedor Petrov
[
70
]
.
Both proofs are annoyingly tricky.

4.10. Theorem.

Let
\alpha
be a closed curve that lies in a convex plane figure bounded by a curve
\gamma

Then the average curvature of
\alpha
is not less than the average curvature of
\gamma
.

F
Generalized curvature

The following exercise states that the inequality in
4C
is optimal.

4.11. Exercise.

Show that for any smooth space curve
\gamma
we have

\Phi({\gamma})=\sup\{\Phi({\beta})\},

where the least upper bound is taken over all polygonal lines
\beta
inscribed in
\gamma

(if
\gamma
is closed, then we assume so is
\beta
).

This equality can be used to define the total curvature of an arbitrary curve
\gamma
.
Namely, it can be defined as
the least upper bound on the total curvatures of nondegenerate polygonal lines inscribed in
\gamma
.

This theory was reinvented and developed several times; see
[
81
, III § 1]
,
[
3
]
, and
[
89
]
.
It is possible to generalize most of the statements in this chapter to the curves of finite total curvature in this generalized sense.

4.12. Exercise.

Suppose that a curve
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
has bounded total curvature in the generalized sense;
that is, there is an upper bound on the total curvatures of polygonal lines inscribed in
\gamma
.

Show that
\gamma
is rectifiable.
Construct an example showing that the converse does not hold.

Chapter 5
Torsion

This chapter provides mostly practice in computations.
Except for the definitions in Section
5A
,
it will not be used in the sequel.

Just like curvature measures how much a curve fails to be a straight line, the torsion measures how much a space curve fails to be a plane curve (see
5D
).

A
Frenet frame

Let
\gamma
be a smooth space curve.
Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\gamma
has an arc-length parametrization,
so the velocity vector
\text{\sc{t}}(s)=\penalty\gamma^{\prime}(s)
is unit.

Assume its curvature does not vanish at time
s
;
in other words,
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(s)\neq\penalty 0
.
Then we can define the so-called
normal vector
at
s
as

\text{\sc{n}}(s)=\frac{\gamma^{\prime\prime}(s)}{|\gamma^{\prime\prime}(s)|}.

Note that

\text{\sc{t}}^{\prime}(s)=\gamma^{\prime\prime}(s)=\kappa(s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{n}}(s).

According to
3A
,
\text{\sc{n}}(s)\perp\text{\sc{t}}(s)
.
Therefore, the vector product

\text{\sc{b}}(s)=\text{\sc{t}}(s)\times\text{\sc{n}}(s)

is a unit vector.
Moreover, the triple
\text{\sc{t}}(s),\text{\sc{n}}(s),\text{\sc{b}}(s)
an oriented orthonormal basis in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
;
it is called the
Frenet frame
of
\gamma
at
s
.
In particular, we have that

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{t}}\rangle

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{n}},\text{\sc{n}}\rangle

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{b}},\text{\sc{b}}\rangle

\displaystyle=1,

➊

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{n}}\rangle

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{n}},\text{\sc{b}}\rangle

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{b}},\text{\sc{t}}\rangle

\displaystyle=0.

\text{\sc{t}}(s)
,
\text{\sc{n}}(s)
, and
\text{\sc{b}}(s)
are called
tangent
,
normal
, and
binormal
vectors of the Frenet frame, respectively.
The frame
\text{\sc{t}}(s),\text{\sc{n}}(s),\text{\sc{b}}(s)
is defined only if
\kappa(s)\neq\penalty 0
.

The plane
\Pi_{s}
thru
\gamma(s)
spanned by vectors
\text{\sc{t}}(s)
and
\text{\sc{n}}(s)
is called the
osculating plane
at
s
;
equivalently it can be defined as a plane thru
\gamma(s)
that is perpendicular to the binormal vector
\text{\sc{b}}(s)
.
This is the unique plane that has a
second-order contact
with
\gamma
at
s
;
that is,
\rho(\ell)=o(\ell^{2})
, where
\rho(\ell)
denotes the distance from
\gamma(s+\ell)
to
\Pi_{s}
.

B
Torsion

Let
\gamma
be a smooth unit-speed space curve,
and let
\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{n}},\text{\sc{b}}
be its Frenet frame.
The value

\tau(s)=\langle\text{\sc{n}}^{\prime}(s),\text{\sc{b}}(s)\rangle

is called the
torsion
of
\gamma
at
s
.

The torsion
\tau(s_{0})
is defined if
\kappa(s_{0})\neq\penalty 0
.
Indeed, since the function
s\mapsto\kappa(s)
is continuous,

\kappa(s_{0})\neq\penalty 0
implies that
\kappa(s)\neq\penalty 0
for all
s
near
s_{0}
.
Therefore, the Frenet frame is also defined in an open interval containing
s_{0}
.
Clearly,
\text{\sc{t}}(s)
,
\text{\sc{n}}(s)
, and
\text{\sc{b}}(s)
depend smoothly on
s
in their domains of definition.
Therefore,
\text{\sc{n}}^{\prime}(s_{0})
is defined, and so is the torsion.

5.1. Exercise.

Given real numbers
a
and
b
, calculate the curvature and the torsion of the helix

\gamma_{a,b}(t)=(a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos t,a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sin t,b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t)
.

Conclude that for any
\kappa>0
and
\tau
there is a helix with constant curvature
\kappa
and torsion
\tau
.

C
Frenet formulas

Assume the Frenet frame
\text{\sc{t}}(s),\text{\sc{n}}(s),\text{\sc{b}}(s)
of a curve
\gamma
is defined at
s
.
Recall that

\text{\sc{t}}^{\prime}=\kappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{n}}.

➋

Let us write the remaining derivatives
\text{\sc{n}}^{\prime}
and
\text{\sc{b}}^{\prime}
in the frame
\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{n}},\text{\sc{b}}
.

Let us show that

\text{\sc{n}}^{\prime}=-\kappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{t}}+\tau{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{b}}.

➌

Since the frame
\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{n}},\text{\sc{b}}
is orthonormal, the above formula is equivalent to the following three identities:

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{n}}^{\prime},\text{\sc{t}}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\kappa,

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{n}}^{\prime},\text{\sc{n}}\rangle

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{n}}^{\prime},\text{\sc{b}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\tau,

➍

The last identity follows from the definition of torsion.
The second one is a consequence of the identity
\langle\text{\sc{n}},\text{\sc{n}}\rangle=\penalty 1
in
➊
.
By differentiating the identity
\langle\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{n}}\rangle=\penalty 0
in
➊

we get

\langle\text{\sc{t}}^{\prime},\text{\sc{n}}\rangle+\langle\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{n}}^{\prime}\rangle=0.

Applying
➋
, we get the first equation in
➍
.

Differentiating the third identity in
➊
, we get that
\text{\sc{b}}^{\prime}\perp\text{\sc{b}}
.
Taking further derivatives of the other identities with
b
in
➊
, we get that

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{b}}^{\prime},\text{\sc{t}}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle\text{\sc{b}},\text{\sc{t}}^{\prime}\rangle=-\kappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{b}},\text{\sc{n}}\rangle=0,

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{b}}^{\prime},\text{\sc{n}}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\langle\text{\sc{b}},\text{\sc{n}}^{\prime}\rangle=\tau.

Since the frame
\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{n}},\text{\sc{b}}
is orthonormal, it follows that

\text{\sc{b}}^{\prime}=-\tau{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{n}}.

➎

The equations
➋
,
➌
, and
➎
are called
Frenet formulas
.
All three can be written as one matrix identity:

\begin{pmatrix}\text{\sc{t}}^{\prime}\\
\text{\sc{n}}^{\prime}\\
\text{\sc{b}}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\kappa&0\\
-\kappa&0&\tau\\
0&-\tau&0\end{pmatrix}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\begin{pmatrix}\text{\sc{t}}\\
\text{\sc{n}}\\
\text{\sc{b}}\end{pmatrix}.

Since
b
is the normal vector to the osculating plane, equation
➎
shows that the torsion measures how fast the osculating plane rotates when one travels along
\gamma
.

5.2. Exercise.

Deduce the formula
➎
from
➋
and
➌
by differentiating the identity

\text{\sc{b}}=\text{\sc{t}}\times\text{\sc{n}}
.

5.3. Exercise.

Let
\gamma
be a smooth space curve with nonvanishing curvature.
Show that
\gamma
lies in a plane if and only if its torsion vanishes.

5.4. Exercise.

Let
\gamma_{0}\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a smooth space curve with Frenet frame
\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{n}},\text{\sc{b}}
.
Consider the curve
\gamma_{1}(t)=\gamma_{0}(t)+\text{\sc{b}}(t)
.
Show that

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{1}\geqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{0}.

5.5. Exercise.

Suppose
\gamma
is a smooth space curve.
Let
\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{n}},\text{\sc{b}}
be its Frenet frame and
\tau
be its torsion.
Show that

\text{\sc{b}}=\frac{\gamma^{\prime}\times\gamma^{\prime\prime}}{|\gamma^{\prime}\times\gamma^{\prime\prime}|}\quad\text{and}\quad\tau=\frac{\langle\gamma^{\prime}\times\gamma^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime\prime}\rangle}{|\gamma^{\prime}\times\gamma^{\prime\prime}|^{2}}.

5.6. Exercise.

Find curvature
\kappa(t)
and torsion
\tau(t)
of the
moment curve

\gamma\colon t\mapsto\penalty(t,t^{2},t^{3})
at
\gamma(t)
.

The following exercise is closely related to the bow lemma (
3G
).

5.7. Exercise.

Let
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
be two smooth unit-speed curves.
Assume that

|{\gamma_{1}(t_{1})}-\penalty{\gamma_{1}(t_{2})}|\geqslant|{\gamma_{2}(t_{1})}-\penalty{\gamma_{2}(t_{2})}|

for any
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
.
Show that
\kappa(t_{0})_{\gamma_{1}}\leqslant\kappa(t_{0})_{\gamma_{2}}
for any
t_{0}
.

5.8. Advanced exercise.

Let
\gamma
be a closed smooth space curve with positive torsion.
Show that the tangent indicatrix of
\gamma
has a self-intersection.

D
Curves of constant slope

We say that a smooth space curve
\gamma
has
constant slope
if its velocity vector makes a constant angle with a fixed direction.
The following theorem was proved by Michel Ange Lancret
[
57
]
.

5.9. Theorem.

Let
\gamma
be a smooth curve;
denote by
\kappa
and
\tau
its curvature and torsion.
Suppose
\kappa(s)>0
for all
s
.
Then
\gamma
has a constant slope if and only if the ratio
\tfrac{\tau}{\kappa}
is constant.

5.10. Proof-guided exercise.

Let
\gamma
be a smooth space curve with nonvanishing curvature,
\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{n}},\text{\sc{b}}

its Frenet frame, and
\kappa
,
\tau
its curvature and torsion.

(a)

Assume
\langle\text{\sc{w}},\text{\sc{t}}\rangle
is constant for a fixed nonzero vector
w
.
Show that
\langle\text{\sc{w}},\text{\sc{n}}\rangle=0
.
Conclude that
\langle\text{\sc{w}},\text{\sc{b}}\rangle
is constant.
Show that

\tau{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{w}},\text{\sc{b}}\rangle-\kappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{w}},\text{\sc{t}}\rangle=0.

Conclude that
\tfrac{\tau}{\kappa}
is constant.

(b)

Assume
\tfrac{\tau}{\kappa}
is constant, show that the vector
\text{\sc{w}}=\tfrac{\tau}{\kappa}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{t}}+\text{\sc{b}}
is constant.
Conclude that
\gamma
has a constant slope.

Let
\gamma
be a smooth unit-speed curve and
s_{0}
a fixed real number.
Then the curve

\alpha(s)=\gamma(s)+(s_{0}-s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\gamma^{\prime}(s)

is called the
evolvent
of
\gamma
.
Note that if
\ell(s)
denotes the tangent line to
\gamma
at
s
,
then
\alpha(s)\in\ell(s)
and
\alpha^{\prime}(s)\perp\ell
for all
s
.

5.11. Exercise.

Show that the evolvent of a constant slope curve is a plane curve.

E
Spherical curves

5.12. Theorem.

Suppose
\gamma
is a smooth space curve with nonvanishing torsion
\tau
and (therefore) curvature
\kappa
.
Then
\gamma
lies on a unit sphere if and only if
the following identity holds true:

\left|\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{\tau}\right|=\kappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sqrt{\kappa^{2}-1}.

5.13. Proof-guided exercise.

Suppose
\gamma
is a smooth unit-speed space curve.
Denote by
\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{n}},\text{\sc{b}}
its Frenet frame and by
\kappa
,
\tau
its curvature and torsion.

Assume
\gamma
is spherical; that is,
|\gamma(s)|=1
for any
s
.
Show that

(a)

\langle\text{\sc{t}},\gamma\rangle=0
; conclude that
\langle\text{\sc{n}},\gamma\rangle^{2}+\langle\text{\sc{b}},\gamma\rangle^{2}=1
.

(b)

\langle\text{\sc{n}},\gamma\rangle=-\tfrac{1}{\kappa}
;

(c)

\langle\text{\sc{b}},\gamma\rangle^{\prime}=\tfrac{\tau}{\kappa}
.

(d)

Use
5E
to show that if
\gamma
is closed, then
\tau(s)=0
for some
s
.

(e)

Assume the torsion of
\gamma
does not vanish.
Use
5E
–
5E
to show that

\left|\frac{\kappa^{\prime}}{\tau}\right|=\kappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sqrt{\kappa^{2}-1}.

Now assume
\gamma
is a space curve that satisfies the identity in
5E
.

(f)

Show that
p=\gamma+\tfrac{1}{\kappa}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{n}}+\tfrac{\kappa^{\prime}}{\kappa^{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\tau}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{b}}
is constant; conclude that
\gamma
lies in the unit sphere centered at
p
.

For a unit-speed curve
\gamma
with nonzero curvature and torsion at
s
,
the sphere
\Sigma_{s}
that passes thru
\gamma(s)
and has the center at

p(s)=\gamma(s)+\tfrac{1}{\kappa(s)}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{n}}(s)+\tfrac{\kappa^{\prime}(s)}{\kappa^{2}(s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\tau(s)}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{b}}(s)

is called the
osculating sphere
of
\gamma
at
s
.
This is the unique sphere that has
third-order contact
with
\gamma
at
s
;
that is,
\rho(\ell)=o(\ell^{3})
, where
\rho(\ell)
denotes the distance from
\gamma(s+\ell)
to
\Sigma_{s}
.

F
Fundamental theorem of space curves

5.14. Theorem.

Let
s\mapsto\kappa(s)
and
s\mapsto\tau(s)
be two smooth real-valued functions defined on a real interval
\mathbb{I}
.
Suppose
\kappa(s)>0
for all
s
.
Then there is a smooth unit-speed curve
\gamma\colon\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
with curvature
\kappa(s)
and torsion
\tau(s)
for every
s\in\mathbb{I}
.
Moreover,
\gamma
is uniquely defined up to an orientation-preserving rigid motion of the space.

The proof is an application of the theorem on the existence and uniqueness of solutions of ordinary differential equations (
0H
).

Proof.

Fix a parameter value
s_{0}
, a point
\gamma(s_{0})
, and an oriented orthonormal frame
\text{\sc{t}}(s_{0})
,
\text{\sc{n}}(s_{0})
,
\text{\sc{b}}(s_{0})
.
Consider the following system of differential equations

\begin{cases}\gamma^{\prime}=\text{\sc{t}},\\
\text{\sc{t}}^{\prime}=\kappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{n}},\\
\text{\sc{n}}^{\prime}=-\kappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{t}}+\tau{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{b}},\\
\text{\sc{b}}^{\prime}=-\tau{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{n}}\end{cases}

➏

with the initial condition formed by
\gamma(s_{0})
,
\text{\sc{t}}(s_{0})
,
\text{\sc{n}}(s_{0})
,
\text{\sc{b}}(s_{0})
.
(The system of equations has four vector equations, so it can be rewritten as a system of 12 scalar equations.)

By
0H
, this system has a unique solution which is defined in a maximal subinterval
\mathbb{J}\subset\mathbb{I}
containing
s_{0}
.
Let us show that actually
\mathbb{J}=\mathbb{I}
.

First note that

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{t}}\rangle

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{n}},\text{\sc{n}}\rangle

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{b}},\text{\sc{b}}\rangle

\displaystyle=1,

➐

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{n}}\rangle

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{n}}\rangle

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{b}},\text{\sc{t}}\rangle

\displaystyle=0

at any parameter value
s
.

Indeed, by
➏
, we have the following system of scalar equations:

\begin{cases}\langle\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{t}}\rangle^{\prime}&=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{t}}^{\prime}\rangle=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\kappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{n}}\rangle,\\
\langle\text{\sc{n}},\text{\sc{n}}\rangle^{\prime}&=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{n}},\text{\sc{n}}^{\prime}\rangle=-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\kappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{n}},\text{\sc{t}}\rangle+2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\tau{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{n}},\text{\sc{b}}\rangle,\\
\langle\text{\sc{b}},\text{\sc{b}}\rangle^{\prime}&=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{b}},\text{\sc{b}}^{\prime}\rangle=-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\tau\langle\text{\sc{b}},\text{\sc{n}}\rangle,\\
\langle\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{n}}\rangle^{\prime}&=\langle\text{\sc{t}}^{\prime},\text{\sc{n}}\rangle+\langle\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{n}}^{\prime}\rangle=\kappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{n}},\text{\sc{n}}\rangle-\kappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{t}}\rangle+\tau{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{b}}\rangle,\\
\langle\text{\sc{n}},\text{\sc{b}}\rangle^{\prime}&=\langle\text{\sc{n}}^{\prime},\text{\sc{b}}\rangle+\langle\text{\sc{n}},\text{\sc{b}}^{\prime}\rangle=\kappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{b}}\rangle+\tau{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{b}},\text{\sc{b}}\rangle-\tau{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{n}},\text{\sc{n}}\rangle,\\
\langle\text{\sc{b}},\text{\sc{t}}\rangle^{\prime}&=\langle\text{\sc{b}}^{\prime},\text{\sc{t}}\rangle+\langle\text{\sc{b}},\text{\sc{t}}^{\prime}\rangle=-\tau{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{n}},\text{\sc{t}}\rangle+\kappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{b}},\text{\sc{n}}\rangle.\end{cases}

➑

The constants in
➐
give a solution of this system.
Moreover, since we choose
\text{\sc{t}}(s_{0})
,
\text{\sc{n}}(s_{0})
,
\text{\sc{b}}(s_{0})
to be an oriented orthonormal frame,

➐
solves our initial value problem for
➑
.

Assume
\mathbb{J}\varsubsetneq\mathbb{I}
.
Then an end of
\mathbb{J}
, say
b
, lies in the interior of
\mathbb{I}
.
The Theorem
0H
is applicable for
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{12}\times\mathbb{I}
;
therefore, at least one of the values
\gamma(s)
,
\text{\sc{t}}(s)
,
\text{\sc{n}}(s)
,
\text{\sc{b}}(s)

escapes to infinity as
s\to b
.
But this is impossible — the vectors
\text{\sc{t}}(s)
,
\text{\sc{n}}(s)
,
\text{\sc{b}}(s)
remain unit, and
|\gamma^{\prime}(s)|=\penalty|\text{\sc{t}}(s)|=1
;
so
\gamma
travels only a finite distance as
s\to b
— a contradiction.
Hence,
\mathbb{J}=\mathbb{I}
, and the first statement follows.

Now assume there are two curves
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
with the given curvature and torsion functions.
Applying a motion of the space, we can assume that
\gamma_{1}(s_{0})=\gamma_{2}(s_{0})
, and the Frenet frames of the curves coincide at
s_{0}
.
Then
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}
by the uniqueness of solutions to the system (
0H
).
Hence, the last statement follows.
∎

5.15. Exercise.

Assume a curve
\gamma\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
has constant speed, curvature, and torsion.
Show that
\gamma
is a helix, possibly degenerating to a circle;
that is, in a suitable coordinate system we have

\gamma(t)=(a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos t,a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sin t,b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t)

for some constants
a
and
b
.

5.16. Advanced exercise.

Let
\gamma
be a smooth space curve such that the distance
|\gamma(t)-\gamma(t+\ell)|
depends only on
\ell
.
Show that
\gamma
is a helix, possibly degenerate to a line or a circle.

Chapter 6
Signed curvature

On the plane, it makes sense to think about left (right) turns as positive (respectively negative).
This leads to the so-called
signed curvature
of plane curves.
If you drive a car along a plane curve, then the signed curvature describes the position of the steering wheel at a given time.

A
Definitions

Suppose
\gamma
is a smooth unit-speed plane curve,
so
\text{\sc{t}}(s)=\gamma^{\prime}(s)
is its unit tangent vector for any
s
.

Let us rotate
\text{\sc{t}}(s)
by the angle
\tfrac{\pi}{2}
counterclockwise;
denote the obtained vector by
\text{\sc{n}}(s)
.
The pair
\text{\sc{t}}(s),\text{\sc{n}}(s)
is an oriented orthonormal frame in the plane which is analogous to the Frenet frame
defined in Section
5A
;
we will keep the name
Frenet frame
for it.

Recall that
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(s)\perp\gamma^{\prime}(s)
(see
3A
).
Therefore,

\text{\sc{t}}^{\prime}(s)=k(s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{n}}(s).

➊

for a real number
k(s)
;
the value
k(s)
is called
signed curvature
of
\gamma
at
s
.
We may use the notation
k(s)_{\gamma}
if we need to specify the curve
\gamma
.

Note that

\kappa(s)=|k(s)|;

that is, up to sign, the signed curvature
k(s)
equals the curvature
\kappa(s)
of
\gamma
at
s
defined in Section
3B
;
the sign prescribes the direction of turn — if
\gamma
turns left at time
s
, then
k(s)>0
.
If we want to emphasize that we are working with the
non-signed
curvature of a curve,
we call it
absolute curvature
.

If we reverse the parametrization of the curve or change the orientation of the plane, then
the signed curvature changes its sign.

Since
\text{\sc{t}}(s),\text{\sc{n}}(s)
is an orthonormal frame, we have

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{t}}\rangle

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{n}},\text{\sc{n}}\rangle

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{n}}\rangle

\displaystyle=0.

Differentiating these identities we get

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{t}}^{\prime},\text{\sc{t}}\rangle

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{n}}^{\prime},\text{\sc{n}}\rangle

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{t}}^{\prime},\text{\sc{n}}\rangle+\langle\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{n}}^{\prime}\rangle

\displaystyle=0.

By
➊
,
\langle\text{\sc{t}}^{\prime},\text{\sc{n}}\rangle=k
.
Therefore
\langle\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{n}}^{\prime}\rangle=-k
.
Hence, we get

\text{\sc{n}}^{\prime}(s)=-k(s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{t}}(s).

➋

The equations
➊
and
➋
are the Frenet formulas for plane curves.
They can be written in a matrix form as:

\begin{pmatrix}\text{\sc{t}}^{\prime}\\
\text{\sc{n}}^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&k\\
-k&0\end{pmatrix}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\begin{pmatrix}\text{\sc{t}}\\
\text{\sc{n}}\end{pmatrix}.

6.1. Exercise.

Let
\gamma_{0}\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a smooth curve and
t
its tangent indicatrix.
Consider another curve
\gamma_{1}\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
defined by
\gamma_{1}(t)\mathrel{:=}\penalty\gamma_{0}(t)+\text{\sc{t}}(t)
.
Show that

(a)

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{0}\leqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{1}
;

(b)

\Phi({\gamma_{0}})\leqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{1}
.

The curves
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}
in the exercise above describe the tracks of an idealized bicycle with distance 1 from the rear to the front wheel.
Thus, by the exercise, the front wheel must have a longer track.
For more on the geometry of bicycle tracks, see the survey of Robert Foote, Mark Levi, and Serge Tabachnikov
[
32
]
and the references therein.

B
Fundamental theorem of plane curves

6.2. Theorem.

Let
s\mapsto k(s)
be a smooth real-valued function defined on a real interval
\mathbb{I}
.
Then there is a smooth unit-speed curve
\gamma\colon\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
with signed curvature
k(s)
.
Moreover,
\gamma
is uniquely defined up to a orientation-preserving motion of the plane.

This theorem is a partial case of its 3-dimensional analog (
5F
), but we present a direct proof.

Proof.

Fix
s_{0}\in\mathbb{I}
.
Consider the function

\theta(s)=\int_{s_{0}}^{s}k(t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt.

By the fundamental theorem of calculus, we have
\theta^{\prime}(s)=\penalty k(s)
for all
s
.

Set

\text{\sc{t}}(s)=(\cos[\theta(s)],\sin[\theta(s)])
,
and let
\text{\sc{n}}(s)
be its counterclockwise rotation by angle
\tfrac{\pi}{2}
; so

\text{\sc{n}}(s)=(-\sin[\theta(s)],\cos[\theta(s)])
.
Consider the curve

\gamma(s)=\int_{s_{0}}^{s}\text{\sc{t}}(s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}ds.

Since
|\gamma^{\prime}|=|\text{\sc{t}}|=1
, the curve
\gamma
is unit-speed, and its Frenet frame is
\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{n}}
.

Note that

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime\prime}(s)

\displaystyle=\text{\sc{t}}^{\prime}(s)=

\displaystyle=(\cos[\theta(s)]^{\prime},\sin[\theta(s)]^{\prime})=

\displaystyle=\theta^{\prime}(s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(-\sin[\theta(s)],\cos[\theta(s)])=

\displaystyle=k(s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{n}}(s).

So,
k(s)
is the signed curvature of
\gamma
at
s
.

This proves the existence;
it remains to prove the uniqueness.

Assume
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are two curves that satisfy the assumptions of the theorem.
Applying a rigid motion, we can assume that
\gamma_{1}(s_{0})=\penalty\gamma_{2}(s_{0})
, and both curves have the same Frenet frame at
s_{0}
.
Let us denote by
\text{\sc{t}}_{1},\text{\sc{n}}_{1}
and
\text{\sc{t}}_{2},\text{\sc{n}}_{2}
the Frenet frames of
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
, respectively.
Both triples
\gamma_{i},\text{\sc{t}}_{i},\text{\sc{n}}_{i}
satisfy the following system of ordinary differential equations

\begin{cases}\gamma_{i}^{\prime}=\text{\sc{t}}_{i},\\
\text{\sc{t}}_{i}^{\prime}=k{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{n}}_{i},\\
\text{\sc{n}}_{i}^{\prime}=-k{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{t}}_{i}.\end{cases}

Moreover, they have the same initial values at
s_{0}
.
By the uniqueness of solutions of ordinary differential equations (
0H
), we have
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}
.
∎

Suppose
\gamma\colon\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a unit-speed curve.
A continuous function
\theta\colon\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{R}
is called
continuous argument
of
\gamma
if

\gamma^{\prime}(s)=(\cos[\theta(s)],\sin[\theta(s)])

for any
s
.
The proof of the theorem implies the following.

6.3. Corollary.

For any smooth curve unit-speed curve
\gamma\colon\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
there is a continuous argument
\theta\colon\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{R}
.
Moreover

\theta^{\prime}(s)=k(s),

where
k
denotes the signed curvature of
\gamma
.

C
Total signed curvature

Let
\gamma\colon\mathbb{I}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a smooth unit-speed plane curve.
The
total signed curvature
of
\gamma
, denoted by
\Psi({\gamma})
, is defined as the integral

\Psi({\gamma})=\int_{\mathbb{I}}k(s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}ds,

➌

where
k
denotes the signed curvature of
\gamma
.

If
\mathbb{I}=[a,b]
, then

\Psi({\gamma})=\theta(b)-\theta(a),

➍

where
\theta
is a continuous argument of
\gamma
(see
6B
).

If
\gamma
is a piecewise smooth plane curve, then we define its total signed curvature as the sum of the total signed curvatures of its arcs plus the sum of the
signed
external angles at its joints;
they are positive where
\gamma
turns left, negative where
\gamma
turns right, and 0 where
\gamma
goes straight.
It is undefined if
\gamma
turns exactly backwards;
that is, if it has a cusp.

In other words, if
\gamma
is a concatenation of smooth arcs
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}
, then

\Psi({\gamma})=\Psi({\gamma_{1}})+\dots+\Psi({\gamma_{n}})+\theta_{1}+\dots+\theta_{n-1},

where
\theta_{i}
is the signed external angle at the joint between
\gamma_{i}
and
\gamma_{i+1}
.
If
\gamma
is closed, then the concatenation is cyclic, and

\Psi({\gamma})=\Psi({\gamma_{1}})+\dots+\Psi({\gamma_{n}})+\theta_{1}+\dots+\theta_{n},

where
\theta_{n}
is the signed external angle at the joint between
\gamma_{n}
and
\gamma_{1}
.

Since
\left|\int k(s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}ds\right|\leqslant\int|k(s)|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}ds
, we have

|\Psi({\gamma})|\leqslant\Phi({\gamma})

➎

for any smooth plane curve
\gamma
;
that is, the total signed curvature
\Psi
cannot exceed the total curvature
\Phi
in absolute value.
The equality holds if and only if the signed curvature does not change the sign.

6.4. Exercise.

A trochoid is a curve traced out by a point fixed to a wheel as it rolls along a straight line.

A family of
trochoids

\gamma_{a}\colon[0,2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
(see the picture) can be parametrized as

\gamma_{a}(t)=(t+a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sin t,a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos t).

(a)

Given
a\in\mathbb{R}
, find
\Psi({\gamma_{a}})
if it is defined.

(b)

Given
a\in\mathbb{R}
, find
\Phi({\gamma_{a}})
.

6.5. Proposition.

Any simple closed smooth plane curve
\gamma
has total signed curvature
\pm 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
; it is
+2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi

if the region bounded by
\gamma
lies on the left from it and
-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
otherwise.

Moreover, the same statement holds for any simple closed piecewise smooth plane curve
\gamma
if its total signed curvature is defined.

This proposition is called sometimes
Umlaufsatz
; it is a differential-geometric analog of the theorem about the sum of the internal angles of a polygon (
0C
) which we use in the proof.
A more conceptual proof was given by Heinz Hopf
[
43
]
,
[
44
, p. 42]
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\gamma
is oriented in such a way that the region bounded by
\gamma
lies on its left.
We can also assume that
\gamma
is unit-speed.

Consider a closed polygonal line
p_{1}\dots p_{n}
inscribed in
\gamma
.
We can assume that the arcs between the vertices are sufficiently small
so that the polygonal line is simple, and each arc
\gamma_{i}
from
p_{i}
to
p_{i+1}
has small total absolute curvature, say
\Phi({\gamma_{i}})<\pi
for each
i
.

Assume
p_{i}=\gamma(t_{i})
.
As usual, we use indexes modulo
n
; in particular,
p_{n+1}=\penalty p_{1}
.
Set

\displaystyle\text{\sc{w}}_{i}

\displaystyle=p_{i+1}-p_{i},

\displaystyle\text{\sc{v}}_{i}

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\prime}(t_{i}),

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}

\displaystyle=\measuredangle(\text{\sc{v}}_{i},\text{\sc{w}}_{i}),

\displaystyle\beta_{i}

\displaystyle=\measuredangle(\text{\sc{w}}_{i-1},\text{\sc{v}}_{i}),

where
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}\in(-\pi,\pi)
are signed angles —
\alpha_{i}
is positive if
\text{\sc{w}}_{i}
points to the left from
\text{\sc{v}}_{i}
.

By
➍
, the value

\Psi({\gamma_{i}})-\alpha_{i}-\beta_{i+1}

➏

is a multiple of
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
.
Since
\Phi({\gamma_{i}})<\pi
, the chord lemma (
4B
) implies that
|\alpha_{i}|+\penalty|\beta_{i}|<\pi
.
By
➎
, we have that
|\Psi({\gamma_{i}})|\leqslant\penalty\Phi({\gamma_{i}})
;
therefore the value in
➏
vanishes.
In other words, for each
i
we have

\Psi({\gamma_{i}})=\alpha_{i}+\beta_{i+1}.

Note that

\delta_{i}=\pi-\alpha_{i}-\beta_{i}

➐

is the internal angle of the polygonal line at
p_{i}
;

\delta_{i}\in(0,2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi)
for each
i
.
Recall that the sum of the internal angles of an
n
-gon is
(n-2){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
(see
0C
); that is,

\delta_{1}+\dots+\delta_{n}=(n-2){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\Psi({\gamma})

\displaystyle=\Psi({\gamma_{1}})+\dots+\Psi({\gamma_{n}})=

➑

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1}+\beta_{2})+\dots+(\alpha_{n}+\beta_{1})=

\displaystyle=(\beta_{1}+\alpha_{1})+\dots+(\beta_{n}+\alpha_{n})=

\displaystyle=(\pi-\delta_{1})+\dots+(\pi-\delta_{n})=

\displaystyle=n{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi-(n-2){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi=

\displaystyle=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi.

The case of piecewise smooth curves is done the same way;
we need to subdivide the arcs in the cyclic concatenation further to meet the requirement above, and, instead of equation
➐
, we have

\delta_{i}=\pi-\alpha_{i}-\beta_{i}-\theta_{i},

where
\theta_{i}
is the signed external angle of
\gamma
at
p_{i}
; it vanishes if the curve
\gamma
is smooth at
p_{i}
.
Therefore, instead of equation
➑
, we have

\displaystyle\Psi({\gamma})

\displaystyle=\Psi({\gamma_{1}})+\dots+\Psi({\gamma_{n}})+\theta_{1}+\dots+\theta_{n}=

\displaystyle=(\alpha_{1}+\beta_{2})+\dots+(\alpha_{n}+\beta_{1})+\theta_{1}+\dots+\theta_{n}=

\displaystyle=(\beta_{1}+\alpha_{1}+\theta_{1})+\dots+(\beta_{n}+\alpha_{n}+\theta_{n})=

\displaystyle=(\pi-\delta_{1})+\dots+(\pi-\delta_{n})=

\displaystyle=n{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi-(n-2){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi=

\displaystyle=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi.

∎

6.6. Exercise.

Draw a smooth closed plane curve
\gamma
such that

(a)

\Psi({\gamma})=0
;

(b)

\Psi({\gamma})=\Phi({\gamma})=10{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
;

(c)

\Psi({\gamma})=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
and
\Phi({\gamma})=4{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
.

6.7. Exercise.

Let
\gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a smooth plane curve with Frenet frame
\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{n}}
.
Given a real parameter
\ell
, consider
the curve
\gamma_{\ell}(t)=\penalty\gamma(t)+\penalty\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{n}}(t)
; it is called a
parallel curve
of
\gamma
at signed distance
\ell
.

(a)

Show that the parametrization
\gamma_{\ell}
is a regular if
\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}k(t)_{\gamma}\neq 1
for all
t
.

(b)

Set
L(\ell)=\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{\ell}
.
Show that

L(\ell)=L(0)-\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\Psi({\gamma})

➒

for all
\ell
sufficiently close to
0
.

(c)

Describe an example showing that formula
➒
may not hold for some values
\ell
.

D
Osculating circle

6.8. Proposition.

Given a point
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
a unit vector
t

and a real number
k
, there is a unique smooth unit-speed curve
\sigma\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}

that starts at
p
in the direction of
t
and signed curvature
k
.

Moreover, if
k=0
, then it is a line
\sigma(s)=p+s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{t}}
;
if
k\neq 0
, then
\sigma
runs around a circle of radius
\tfrac{1}{|k|}
with center at
p+\tfrac{1}{k}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{n}}
, where
\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{n}}
is an oriented orthonormal frame.

Proof.

Choose a coordinate system such that
p
is its origin and
t
points in the direction of the
x
-axis.
Therefore,
n
points in the direction of the
y
-axis.
Then

\displaystyle\theta(s)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{s}k{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt=k{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s

is a continuous argument of
\gamma
, see
6B
.
Therefore,

\sigma^{\prime}(s)=(\cos[k{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s],\sin[k{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s]).

It remains to integrate the last identity.
If
k=0
, we get
\sigma(s)=(s,0)

which describes the line
\sigma(s)=p+s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{t}}
.

If
k\neq 0
, we get

\sigma(s)=(\tfrac{1}{k}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sin[k{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s],\tfrac{1}{k}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(1-\cos[k{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s]));

it is the circle of radius
r=\tfrac{1}{|k|}
centered at
(0,\tfrac{1}{k})=p+\tfrac{1}{k}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{n}}
.
∎

6.9. Definition.

Let
\gamma
be a smooth unit-speed plane curve;
denote by
k(s)
the signed curvature of
\gamma
at
s
.

The unit-speed curve
\sigma_{s}
of constant signed curvature
k(s)
that starts at
\gamma(s)
in the direction
\gamma^{\prime}(s)
is called the
osculating circle
of
\gamma
at
s
.

The center and radius of the osculating circle at a given point are called the
center of curvature
and
radius of curvature
of the curve at that point.

The
osculating circle
might be a circle or a line.
In the latter case, the center of curvature is undefined and the radius of curvature is infinite.

The osculating circle
\sigma_{s}
can be also defined as the unique circle (or line) that has
second-order contact
with
\gamma
at
s
;
that is,
\rho(\ell)=\penalty o(\ell^{2})
, where
\rho(\ell)
denotes the distance from
\gamma(s+\ell)
to
\sigma_{s}
.

The following exercise is recommended to the reader familiar with the notion of
inversion
.

6.10. Advanced exercise.

Suppose
\gamma
is a smooth plane curve that does not pass thru the origin.
Let
\hat{\gamma}
be the inversion of
\gamma
with respect to a circle centered at the origin.
Show that the osculating circle of
\hat{\gamma}
at
s
is the inversion of the osculating circle of
\gamma
at
s
.

E
Spiral lemma

The following lemma was proved by Peter Tait
[
92
]

and later rediscovered by Adolf Kneser
[
49
]
.

6.11. Lemma.

Assume
\gamma
is a smooth plane curve with strictly decreasing positive signed curvature. Then the osculating circles of
\gamma
are nested; that is, if
\sigma_{s}
denotes the osculating circle of
\gamma
at
s
,
then
\sigma_{s_{0}}
lies in the open disc bounded by
\sigma_{s_{1}}
for any
s_{0}<s_{1}
.

The picture shows a curve
\gamma
as in the theorem and its osculating circles.
They form a peculiar foliation of the annulus; it meets the following property:
if a smooth function is constant on each osculating circle, then it must be constant in the annulus
[
33
, Lecture 10]
.
Also, the curve
\gamma
is tangent to a circle of the foliation at each of its points.
However, it does not run along any of those circles.

Proof.

Let
\text{\sc{t}}(s),\text{\sc{n}}(s)
be the Frenet frame,

\omega(s)
,
r(s)

the center and radius of curvature of
\gamma
.
By
6D
, we have

\omega(s)=\gamma(s)+r(s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{n}}(s).

Since
k>0
, we have that
r(s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}k(s)=1
.
Therefore, applying Frenet formula
➋
, we get that

\displaystyle\omega^{\prime}(s)

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\prime}(s)+r^{\prime}(s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{n}}(s)+r(s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{n}}^{\prime}(s)=

\displaystyle=\text{\sc{t}}(s)+r^{\prime}(s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{n}}(s)-r(s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}k(s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{t}}(s)=

\displaystyle=r^{\prime}(s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{n}}(s).

Since
k(s)
is decreasing,
r(s)
is increasing;
therefore
r^{\prime}\geqslant 0
.
It follows that
|\omega^{\prime}(s)|=r^{\prime}(s)
and
\omega^{\prime}(s)
points in the direction of
\text{\sc{n}}(s)
.

Since
\text{\sc{n}}^{\prime}(s)=-k(s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{t}}(s)
, the direction of
\omega^{\prime}(s)
cannot be constant on a nontrivial interval;
in other words, the curve
s\mapsto\omega(s)
contains no line segments.

In particular,
|\omega(s_{1})-\omega(s_{0})|<\penalty\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits(\omega|_{[s_{0},s_{1}]})
for any
s_{0}<s_{1}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle|\omega(s_{1})-\omega(s_{0})|

\displaystyle<\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits(\omega|_{[s_{0},s_{1}]})=

\displaystyle=\int_{s_{0}}^{s_{1}}|\omega^{\prime}(s)|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}ds=

\displaystyle=\int_{s_{0}}^{s_{1}}r^{\prime}(s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}ds=

\displaystyle=r(s_{1})-r(s_{0}).

In other words, the distance between the centers of
\sigma_{s_{1}}
and
\sigma_{s_{0}}

is strictly less than the difference between their radii, hence the result.
∎

The curve
s\mapsto\omega(s)
is called the
evolute
of
\gamma
;
it traces the centers of curvature of the curve.
The evolute of
\gamma
can be written as

\omega(t)=\gamma(t)+\tfrac{1}{k(t)}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{n}}(t);

in the proof, we showed that
(\tfrac{1}{k})^{\prime}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{n}}
is its velocity vector.

6.12. Exercise.

Let
\omega
be the evolute of a smooth plane curve
\gamma
.
Suppose
\gamma
has positive signed curvature
k
and
k^{\prime}\neq 0
at all points.
Find the Frenet frame and curvature of
\omega
in terms of
k
and Frenet frame
(\text{\sc{t}},\text{\sc{n}})
of
\gamma
.

The following theorem states formally that

if you drive on the plane and turn the steering wheel to the left all the time,
then you will not be able to come back to the place you started.

6.13. Theorem.

Assume
\gamma
is a smooth plane curve with positive strictly monotone signed curvature.
Then
\gamma
is simple.

The same statement holds true without assuming positivity of curvature; the proof requires only minor modifications.

Proof.

Note that
\gamma(s)
lies on the osculating circle
\sigma_{s}
of
\gamma
at
s
.
If
s_{1}\neq s_{0}
, then by
6E
,
\sigma_{s_{0}}
does not intersect
\sigma_{s_{1}}
.
Therefore,
\gamma(s_{1})\neq\gamma(s_{0})
,
hence the result.∎

6.14. Advanced exercise.

Show that a 3-dimensional analog of the theorem does not hold.
That is, there are self-intersecting smooth space curves with strictly monotone curvature.

6.15. Exercise.

Assume
\gamma
is a smooth plane curve with positive strictly monotone signed curvature.

(a)

Show that no line can be tangent to
\gamma
at two distinct points.

(b)

Show that no circle can be tangent to
\gamma
at three distinct points.

Part
6E
does not hold if we allow the curvature to be negative; an example is shown on the picture.

6.16. Advanced exercise.

Show that a smooth spherical curve with nonvanishing torsion has no self-intersections.

Chapter 7
Supporting curves

When two plane curves touch each other without crossing, it is possible to control the signed curvature of one of them in terms of the signed curvature of the other.
This will be proved and used to study the global behavior of plane curves.

A
Cooriented tangent curves

Suppose
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are smooth plane curves.
Recall that the curves
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are tangent at the time parameters
t_{1}
and
t_{2}

if
\gamma_{1}(t_{1})=\gamma_{2}(t_{2})

and they share the tangent line at these time parameters.
In this case, the point
p=\gamma_{1}(t_{1})=\gamma_{2}(t_{2})
is called a
point of tangency
of the curves.

In this case, the velocity vectors
\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(t_{1})
and
\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(t_{2})
are parallel.

If
\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(t_{1})
and
\gamma_{2}^{\prime}(t_{2})
have the same direction, we say that the curves are
cooriented
,
if their directions are opposite, the curves are called
counteroriented
.

If we reverse the parametrization of one of the curves, then cooriented curves become counteroriented and vice versa; so we can always assume the curves are cooriented at any given point of tangency.

B
Supporting curves

Let
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
be two smooth plane curves that share a point

p=\gamma_{1}(t_{1})=\gamma_{2}(t_{2});

we assume that
p
is not an endpoint for
\gamma_{1}
nor
\gamma_{2}
.
Suppose that there is
\varepsilon>0
such that the arc
\gamma_{2}|_{[t_{2}-\varepsilon,t_{2}+\varepsilon]}
lies in a closed plane region
R
with the arc
\gamma_{1}|_{[t_{1}-\varepsilon,t_{1}+\varepsilon]}
in its boundary,
then we say that
\gamma_{1}

locally supports

\gamma_{2}
at the time parameters
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
.
Furthermore, if the curves on the picture are oriented according to the arrows, then
\gamma_{1}
supports
\gamma_{2}
from the right at
p
(as well as
\gamma_{2}
supports
\gamma_{1}
from the left at
p
).

Suppose
\gamma_{1}
is a simple curve that cuts the plane into two closed regions, one lies on the left and the other on the right from
\gamma_{1}
.
We say that
\gamma_{1}

globally supports

\gamma_{2}
at point
p=\gamma_{2}(t_{2})

if
\gamma_{2}
runs in one of these closed regions, and

p
lies on
\gamma_{1}
.

Further, suppose
\gamma_{2}
is a simple closed plane curve.
By Jordan’s theorem (
0B
),
\gamma_{2}
cuts from the plane two closed regions, one is bounded and the other is unbounded.
We say that a point
p
lies
inside
(respectively,
outside
)
\gamma_{2}
if
p
lies in the bounded region (respectively, unbounded) region.
In this case, we say that
\gamma_{1}
supports
\gamma_{2}

from the inside
(
from the outside
) if
\gamma_{1}
supports
\gamma_{2}
and lies inside
\gamma_{2}
(respectively outside
\gamma_{2}
).

If
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
share a point
p=\gamma_{1}(t_{1})=\gamma_{2}(t_{2})
and are not tangent at
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
, then at time
t_{2}
the curve
\gamma_{2}
crosses
\gamma_{1}
moving from one of its sides to the other.
It follows that
\gamma_{1}
cannot locally support
\gamma_{2}
at the time parameters
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
.
Whence we get the following.

7.1. Definition-Observation.

Let
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
be two smooth plane curves.
Suppose
\gamma_{1}
locally supports
\gamma_{2}
at time parameters
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
.
Then
\gamma_{1}
is tangent to
\gamma_{2}
at
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
.

In such a case, if the curves are cooriented, and the region
R
in the definition of supporting curves lies on the right (left) from the arc of
\gamma_{1}
, then we say that

\gamma_{1}
supports
\gamma_{2}
from the left (respectively right).

We say that a smooth plane curve
\gamma
has a
vertex
at
s

if the signed curvature function is critical at
s
;
that is, if
k^{\prime}(s)_{\gamma}=0
.
If in addition to that,
\gamma
is simple, we could say that the point
p=\gamma(s)
is a vertex of
\gamma
.

7.2. Exercise.

Assume the osculating circle
\sigma_{s}
of a smooth plane curve
\gamma
at
s
locally supports
\gamma
at
p=\gamma(s)
.
Show that
p
is a vertex of
\gamma
.

C
Supporting test

The following proposition resembles the second derivative test.

7.3. Proposition.

Let
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
be two smooth plane curves.

Suppose
\gamma_{1}
locally supports
\gamma_{2}
from the left (right) at the time parameters
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
.
Then

k_{1}(t_{1})\geqslant k_{2}(t_{2})\quad(\text{respectively}\quad k_{1}(t_{1})\leqslant k_{2}(t_{2})),

where
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
denote the signed curvature of
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
, respectively.

A partial converse also holds.
Namely, if
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
tangent and cooriented at the time parameters
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
,
and

k_{1}(t_{1})>k_{2}(t_{2})\quad(\text{respectively}\quad k_{1}(t_{1})<k_{2}(t_{2})),

then
\gamma_{1}
locally supports
\gamma_{2}
from the left (right) at the time parameters
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
.

Proof.
Without loss of generality, we can assume that
t_{1}=t_{2}=0
, the shared point
\gamma_{1}(0)=\gamma_{2}(0)
is the origin, and the velocity vectors
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}(0)
,
\gamma^{\prime}_{2}(0)
point in the direction of
x
-axis.
Then small arcs
\gamma_{1}|_{[-\varepsilon,+\varepsilon]}
and
\gamma_{2}|_{[-\varepsilon,+\varepsilon]}
can be described as a graph

y=f_{1}(x)
and
y=f_{2}(x)
for smooth functions
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
such that
f_{i}(0)=0
and
f_{i}^{\prime}(0)=0
.
Note that
f_{1}^{\prime\prime}(0)=k_{1}(0)
, and
f_{2}^{\prime\prime}(0)=k_{2}(0)
(see
3C
)

Clearly,
\gamma_{1}
supports
\gamma_{2}
from the left (right) if

f_{1}(x)\geqslant f_{2}(x)\quad(\text{respectively}\quad f_{1}(x)\leqslant f_{2}(x))

for all sufficiently small values of
x
.
Applying the second derivative test the the function
f_{1}-f_{2}
, we get the result.

The partial converse can be proved along the same lines.
∎

7.4. Advanced exercise.

Suppose that two smooth unit-speed simple plane curves
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}
are tangent and cooriented at the point
p=\penalty\gamma_{0}(0)=\penalty\gamma_{1}(0)
.
Assume
k_{0}(s)\leqslant k_{1}(s)
for any
s
.
Show that
\gamma_{0}
locally supports
\gamma_{1}
from the right at
p
.

Give an example of two simple curves
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}
satisfying the above condition such that
\gamma_{0}
is closed, but does not support
\gamma_{1}
at
p
globally.

According to
3E
, for any closed smooth curve that runs in a unit disc, the average of its absolute curvature is at least 1; in particular, there is a point where the absolute curvature is at least 1.
The following exercise says that this statement also holds for loops.

7.5. Exercise.

Assume a smooth plane loop
\gamma
runs in a unit disc.
Show that there is a point on
\gamma
with absolute curvature at least 1.

7.6. Exercise.

Assume a closed smooth plane curve
\gamma
runs between two parallel lines at distance 2 from each other.
Show that there is a point on
\gamma
with absolute curvature at least 1.

Try to prove the same for a smooth plane loop.

7.7. Exercise.

Assume a closed smooth plane curve
\gamma
runs inside a triangle
\triangle
with inradius 1; that is, a unit circle is tangent to all three sides of
\triangle
.
Show that
\gamma
has curvature at least
1
at some point.

The three exercises above are baby cases of
7E
, but try to find a direct solution.

7.8. Exercise.

Let
F
be a plane figure bounded by two circle arcs
\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
of signed curvature 1 that run from
x
to
y
.
Suppose
\sigma_{1}
is shorter than
\sigma_{2}
.

\gamma
runs in
F
and has both endpoints on
\sigma_{1}
.
Show that the absolute curvature of
\gamma
is at least 1 at some parameter value.

D
Convex curves

Recall that a plane curve is
convex
if it bounds a convex region.

7.9. Proposition.

Suppose a simple closed smooth plane curve
\gamma
bounds a figure
F
.
Then
F
is convex if and only if the signed curvature of
\gamma
does not change the sign.

7.10. Lens lemma.

Let
\gamma
be a smooth simple plane curve that runs from
x
to
y
.
Assume
\gamma
runs strictly on the right side (left side) of the oriented line
xy
; only its endpoints
x
and
y
lie on the line.
Then
\gamma
has a point with positive (respectively negative) signed curvature.

The lemma fails for curves with self-intersections.
For example, the curve
\gamma
on the picture always turns right,
so it has negative curvature everywhere, but it lies on the right side of the line
xy
.

Proof.

Choose points
p
and
q
on the line
xy

so that the points
p,x,y,q
appear in that order.
We can assume that
p
and
q
lie sufficiently far from
x
and
y
, so the half-disc with diameter
pq
contains
\gamma
.

Consider the smallest disc segment with chord
[p,q]
that contains
\gamma
.
Its arc
\sigma
supports
\gamma
at some point
w=\gamma(t_{0})
.

Let us parametrize
\sigma
from
p
to
q
.
Note that
\gamma
and
\sigma
are tangent and cooriented at
w
.
If not, then the arc of
\gamma
from
w
to
y
would be trapped in the curvilinear triangle
xwp
bounded by the line segment
[p,x]
and the arcs of
\sigma
,
\gamma
.
But this is impossible since
y
does not belong to this triangle.

It follows that
\sigma
supports
\gamma
at
t_{0}
from the right.
By
7C
,

k(w)_{\gamma}\geqslant k_{\sigma}>0.

∎

Remark.

Instead of taking the minimal disc segment, one can take a point
w
on
\gamma
that maximizes the distance to the line
xy
.
The same argument shows that the curvature at
w
is nonnegative, which is slightly weaker than the required positive curvature.

Proof of
7D
; only-if part.

If
F
is convex, then every tangent line of
\gamma
supports
\gamma
.
If a point moves along
\gamma
, the figure
F
has to stay on one side from its tangent line;
that is, we can assume that each tangent line supports
\gamma
on one side, say on the right.
Since a line has vanishing curvature, the supporting test (
7C
) implies that
k\geqslant 0
at each point.

If part.

Denote by
K
the convex hull of
F
.
If
F
is not convex, then
F
is a proper subset of
K
.
Therefore, the boundary
\partial K
contains a line segment that is not a part of
\partial F
.
In other words, there is a line that supports
\gamma
at two points, say
x
and
y
.
These points divide
\gamma
in two arcs
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
, both distinct from the line segment
[x,y]
.

One of the arcs
\gamma_{1}
or
\gamma_{2}
is parametrized from
x
to
y
and the other from
y
to
x
.
Passing to a smaller arc if necessary we can ensure that only its endpoints lie on the line.
Applying the lens lemma, we get that the arcs
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
contain points with signed curvatures of opposite signs.
∎

7.11. Exercise.

Suppose
\gamma
is a smooth simple closed plane curve of diameter larger than 2.
Show that
\gamma
has a point with absolute curvature less than 1.

7.12. Exercise.

Let
\gamma

and

.
Suppose that

|p-q|
is the maximal distance between points on
\gamma
.
Show that the arc
\gamma

[p,q]
