7.13. Exercise.

Show that any simple smooth plane curve
\gamma
with curvature at least 1 has diameter at most 2.

Try to prove that
\gamma
lies in a unit disc.

E
Moon in a puddle

The following theorem is a slight generalization of the theorem proved by Vladimir Ionin and German Pestov
[
45
]
.
For convex curves, this result was known earlier
[
10
, §24]
.

7.14. Theorem.

Assume
\gamma
is a simple smooth plane loop with absolute curvature bounded by 1.
Then it surrounds a unit disc.

This theorem gives a simple but nontrivial example of the so-called
local to global
theorems — based on some local data (in this case, the curvature of a curve) we conclude a global property (in this case, the existence of a unit disc surrounded by the curve).

A straightforward approach would be to start with a disc in the region bounded by the curve and blow it up to maximize its radius.
However, as one may see from the spinner-like example on the picture it does not always lead to a solution — a closed plane curve of curvature at most 1 may surround a disc of radius smaller than 1 that cannot be enlarged continuously.

7.15. Key lemma.

Assume
\gamma
is a simple smooth plane loop.
Then at one point of
\gamma
(distinct from its base), its osculating circle globally supports
\gamma
from the inside.

First, let us show that the theorem follows from the lemma.

Proof of
7E
modulo
7E
.

Since
\gamma
has absolute curvature at most 1, each osculating circle has radius at least 1.
According to the key lemma, one of the osculating circles
\sigma
globally supports
\gamma
from the inside.
In particular,
\sigma
lies inside
\gamma
, whence the result.
∎

Proof of
7E
.

Denote by
F
the closed region surrounded by
\gamma
.
We can assume that
F
lies on the left from
\gamma
.
Arguing by contradiction,
assume that the osculating circle at each point
p\in\gamma
does not lie in
F
.

Given a point
p\in\gamma
let us consider the maximal circle that lies completely in
F
and tangent to
\gamma
at
p
.
This circle, say
\sigma
, will be called the
incircle
of
F
at
p
;
its curvature
k_{\sigma}
has to be larger than
k(p)_{\gamma}
.
Indeed, since
\sigma
supports
\gamma
from the left, by
7C
we have
k_{\sigma}\geqslant k(p)_{\gamma}
; in the case of equality,
\sigma
is the osculating circle at
p
.
The latter is impossible by our assumption.

It follows that
\sigma
has to touch
\gamma
at another point.
Otherwise, we can increase
\sigma
slightly while keeping it inside
F
.

Indeed, since
k_{\sigma}>k(p)_{\gamma}
,
by
7C
we can choose a neighborhood
U
of
p
such that after a slight increase of
\sigma
, the intersection
U\cap\sigma
is still in
F
.
On the other hand, if
\sigma
does not touch
\gamma
at another point, then after some (maybe smaller) increase of
\sigma
the complement
\sigma\setminus U
is still in
F
.
That is, a slightly increased
\sigma
is still in
F
— a contradiction.

Choose a point
p_{1}
on
\gamma
that is distinct from its base point.
Let
\sigma_{1}
be the incircle at
p_{1}
.
Denote by
\gamma_{1}
an arc of
\gamma
from
p_{1}
to a first point
q_{1}
on
\sigma_{1}
.
Denote by
\hat{\sigma}_{1}
and
\check{\sigma}_{1}
two arcs of
\sigma_{1}
from
p_{1}
to
q_{1}
such that the cyclic concatenation of
\hat{\sigma}_{1}
and
\gamma_{1}
surrounds
\check{\sigma}_{1}
.

Two ovals pretend to be circles.

Let
p_{2}
be the midpoint of
\gamma_{1}
.
Denote by
\sigma_{2}
the incircle at
p_{2}
.

The circle
\sigma_{2}
cannot intersect
\hat{\sigma}_{1}
.
Otherwise, if
\sigma_{2}
intersects
\hat{\sigma}_{1}
at some point
s
, then
\sigma_{2}
has to have two more common points with
\check{\sigma}_{1}
, say
x
and
y
— one for each arc of
\sigma_{2}
from
p_{2}
to
s
.
Therefore,
\sigma_{1}=\penalty\sigma_{2}
since these two circles have three common points:
s
,
x
, and
y
.
On the other hand, by construction,
p_{2}\in\sigma_{2}
and
p_{2}\notin\sigma_{1}
— a contradiction.

Recall that
\sigma_{2}
has to touch
\gamma
at another point.
From above, it follows that
\sigma_{2}
can only touch
\gamma_{1}
.
Therefore we can choose an arc
\gamma_{2}\subset\gamma_{1}
that runs from
p_{2}
to a first point
q_{2}
on
\sigma_{2}
.
Since
p_{2}
is the midpoint of
\gamma_{1}
, we have that

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{2}<\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{1}.

➊

Repeating this construction recursively,
we get an infinite sequence of arcs
\gamma_{1}\supset\gamma_{2}\supset\dots
;
by
➊
, we also get that

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{n}\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad n\to\infty.

Therefore, the intersection
\gamma_{1}\cap\gamma_{2}\cap\dots

contains a single point; denote it by
p_{\infty}
.

Let
\sigma_{\infty}
be the incircle at
p_{\infty}
; it has to touch
\gamma
at another point, say
q_{\infty}
.
The same argument as above shows that
q_{\infty}\in\gamma_{n}
for any
n
.
It follows that
q_{\infty}=p_{\infty}
— a contradiction.
∎

7.16. Exercise.

Assume a closed smooth curve
\gamma
lies in a figure
F
bounded by a simple closed plane curve.
Suppose
R
is the maximal radius of discs that lie in
F
.
Show that the absolute curvature of
\gamma
is at least
\tfrac{1}{R}
at some parameter value.

F
Four-vertex theorem

Recall that a vertex of a smooth curve is defined as a critical point of its signed curvature;
in particular, any local minimum (or maximum) of the signed curvature is a vertex.
For example, every point of a circle is a vertex.

7.17. Тheorem.

Any smooth simple closed plane curve has at least four vertices.

Evidently, any closed smooth curve has at least two vertices — where the minimum and the maximum of the curvature are attained.
On the picture the vertices are marked;
the first curve has one self-intersection and exactly two vertices;
the second curve has exactly four vertices and no self-intersections.

The four-vertex theorem was first proved by Syamadas Mukhopadhyaya
[
68
]
for convex curves.
One of our favorite proofs was given by Robert Osserman
[
71
]
.
Our proof of the following stronger statement is based on the key lemma in the previous section.
For more on the subject, see
[
76
]
and the references therein.

7.18. Theorem.

Any smooth simple closed plane curve is globally supported by its osculating circle at least at 4 distinct points;
two from the inside and two from the outside.

Proof of
7F
modulo
7F
.

It is sufficient to show that if an osculating circle
\sigma
at a point
p
supports
\gamma
locally, then
p
is a vertex.

If not, then a small arc around
p
has monotone curvature.
Applying the spiral lemma (
6E
) we get that the osculating circles at this arc are nested.
In particular, the curve
\gamma
crosses
\sigma
at
p
.
Therefore
\sigma
does not support
\gamma
locally at
p
.
∎

Proof of
7F
.

According to the key lemma (
7E
), there is a point
p\in\gamma
such that its osculating circle supports
\gamma
from the inside.
The curve
\gamma
can be considered as a loop with the base at
p
.
Therefore, the key lemma implies the existence of another point
q\in\gamma
with the same property.

It shows gives a pair osculating circles that support
\gamma
from the inside;
it remains to find another pair.

In order to get the osculating circles supporting
\gamma
from the outside, one can repeat the proof of the key lemma taking instead of incircle the circle (or line) of maximal signed curvature that supports the curve from the outside, assuming that
\gamma
is oriented so that the region on the left from it is bounded.∎

Alternative end of proof.

If one applies to
\gamma
an inversion with respect to a circle whose center lies inside
\gamma
, then the obtained curve
\gamma_{1}
also has two osculating circles that support
\gamma_{1}
from the inside.
According to
6D
, these osculating circles are inverses of the osculating circles of
\gamma
.
Note that the region lying inside
\gamma
is mapped to the region outside
\gamma_{1}
, and the other way around.
Therefore, these two circles (or lines) correspond to the osculating circles supporting
\gamma
from the outside.∎

7.19. Exercise.

Assume that a smooth simple closed plane curve
\gamma
is contained in a square with side 2 and surrounds a square with diagonal 2.
Show that
\gamma
contains a point with curvature 1.

7.20. Exercise.

Suppose a smooth simple plane loop bounds a region of area
a
.
Show that it has curvature
\sqrt{\pi/a}
at some point.

7.21. Advanced exercise.

Suppose
\gamma
is a simple closed smooth plane curve, and
\sigma
is a circle.
Assume
\gamma
crosses
\sigma
at the points
p_{1},\dots,p_{2{{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}}n}
, and these points appear in the same cycle order on
\gamma
and on
\sigma
.
Show that
\gamma
has at least
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}n
vertices.

Construct an example of a simple closed smooth plane curve
\gamma
with only 4 vertices that crosses a given circle at arbitrarily many points.

7.22. Advanced exercise.

Let
\gamma
be a simple smooth plane curve with absolute curvature bounded by 1.
Show that
\gamma
surrounds two disjoint open unit discs if and only if its diameter is at least
4
;
that is,
|{p}-\penalty{q}|\geqslant 4
for some points
p,q\in\gamma
.

The following exercise is a version of the four-vertex theorem for space curves without parallel tangents; they exist by
3D
.

7.23. Advanced exercise.

Let
\gamma
be a closed smooth space curve that has no pair of points with parallel tangent lines.
Assume the curvature of
\gamma
does not vanish at any point.
Show that
\gamma
has at least four points with zero torsion.

Part II:   Surfaces

Chapter 8
Definitions

A
Topological surfaces

We will be most interested in smooth surfaces defined in the following section.
The following general definition will be used only a few times.

A connected subset
\Sigma
in the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{3}

is called a
topological surface
(more precisely an
embedded topological surface without boundary
)
if any point of
p\in\Sigma
admits a neighborhood
W
in
\Sigma

that can be parametrized by an open subset in the Euclidean plane;
that is, there is a homeomorphism
U\to W
from an open set
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
; see Section
0B
.

B
Smooth surfaces

Recall that a function
f
of two variables
x
and
y
is called
smooth
if all its partial derivatives
\frac{\partial^{m+n}}{\partial x^{m}\partial y^{n}}f
are defined and are continuous in the domain of definition of
f
.

A connected set
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
is called a
smooth surface
1
1

1

We use it as a shortcut for the more precise term
smooth regular embedded surface
.
if it can be described locally as a graph of a smooth function in an appropriate coordinate system.

More precisely, for any point
p\in\Sigma
one can choose a coordinate system
(x,y,z)
and a neighborhood
U\ni p
such that
the intersection
W=U\cap\Sigma
is a graph
z=f(x,y)
of a smooth function
f
defined in an open domain of the
(x,y)
-plane.

Examples.

The simplest example of a smooth surface is the
(x,y)
-plane

\Pi=\left\{\,{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\,:\,{z=0}\,\right\}
.
The plane
\Pi
is a surface since
it can be described as the graph of the function
f(x,y)=0
.

All other planes are smooth surfaces as well since one can choose a coordinate system so that it becomes the
(x,y)
-plane.
We may also present a plane as a graph of a linear function

f(x,y)=a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x+b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y+c
for some constants
a
,
b
, and
c

(assuming the plane is not perpendicular to the
(x,y)
-plane, in which case a different coordinate system is needed).

A more interesting example is the unit sphere

\mathbb{S}^{2}=\left\{\,{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\,:\,{x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2}=1}\,\right\}.

This set is not a graph,
but it is locally a graph —
it can be covered by the following 6 graphs:

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=f_{\pm}(x,y)=\pm\sqrt{1-x^{2}-y^{2}},

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=g_{\pm}(x,z)=\pm\sqrt{1-x^{2}-z^{2}},

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=h_{\pm}(y,z)=\pm\sqrt{1-y^{2}-z^{2}},

where each function
f_{+}
,
f_{-}
,
g_{+}
,
g_{-}
,
h_{+}
, and
h_{-}
is defined in an open unit disc.
Any point
p\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
lies in one of these graphs therefore
\mathbb{S}^{2}
is a smooth surface.

C
Surfaces with boundary

A connected subset in a surface that is bounded by one or more piecewise
smooth curves is called a
surface with boundary
; such curves form the
boundary line
of the surface.

When we say
surface
we usually mean a
smooth surface without boundary
.
If needed, one may use the term
surface with possibly nonempty boundary
.

D
Proper, closed, and open surfaces

If the surface
\Sigma
is formed by a closed set in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, then it is called
proper
.
For example, for any smooth function
f
defined on the whole plane, its graph
z=f(x,y)
is a proper surface.
The sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}
gives another example of a proper surface.

On the other hand, the open disc

\left\{\,{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\,:\,{x^{2}+y^{2}<1,\ z=0}\,\right\}

is not a proper surface; this set is neither open nor closed in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

A compact surface without boundary is called
closed

(this term is closely related to the closed curve, but has nothing to do with closed set).

A proper noncompact surface without boundary is called
open
(again, the term open curve is relevant, but open set is not).

For example, the paraboloid
z=x^{2}+y^{2}

is an open surface; the
sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}
is a closed surface.
Note that
any proper surface without boundary is either closed or open
.

The following claim is a three-dimensional analog of the plane separation theorem (
1F
).
It might look obvious, but the proof is nontrivial; it uses the so-called
Alexander’s duality

[
41
]
.
We omit its proof; it would take us far away from the main subject.

8.1. Claim.

The complement of any proper topological surface without boundary (or, equivalently any open or closed topological surface) has exactly two connected components.

E
Implicitly defined surfaces

8.2. Proposition.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth function.
Suppose
0
is a regular value of
f
;
that is,
\nabla_{p}f\neq 0
at any point
p
such that
f(p)=0
.
Then any connected component
\Sigma
of the level set
f(x,y,z)=0
is a smooth surface.

Proof.

Fix
p\in\Sigma
.
Since
\nabla_{p}f\neq 0
we have

f_{x}(p)\neq 0
,

f_{y}(p)\neq 0
, or

f_{z}(p)\neq 0
.
We may assume that
f_{z}(p)\neq 0
;
otherwise, permute the coordinates
x,y,z
.

The implicit function theorem (
0G
) implies that a neighborhood of
p
in
\Sigma
is the graph
z=h(x,y)
of a smooth function
h
defined on an open domain in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
It remains to apply the definition of smooth surface (Section
8B
).
∎

8.3. Exercise.

For which constants
\ell
is the level set
x^{2}+y^{2}-z^{2}=\ell

a smooth surface?

F
Local parametrizations

Let
U
be an open domain in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and
s\colon U\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a smooth map.
We say that
s
is
regular
if its Jacobian matrix has maximal rank;
in this case, it means that the derivatives
s_{u}
and
s_{v}
are linearly independent at any
(u,v)\in U
;
equivalently
s_{u}\times s_{v}\neq 0
, where
\times
denotes the vector product.

8.4. Proposition.

If
s\colon U\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a smooth regular embedding of an open connected set
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, then its image
\Sigma=s(U)
is a smooth surface.

Proof of
8F
.

Let
s(u,v)=(x(u,v),y(u,v),z(u,v))
.
Since
s
is regular, its Jacobian matrix

\mathop{\rm Jac}\nolimits s=\begin{pmatrix}x_{u}&x_{v}\\
y_{u}&y_{v}\\
z_{u}&z_{v}\end{pmatrix}

has rank two at any point
(u,v)\in U
.

Choose a point
p\in\Sigma
; by shifting the
(x,y,z)
and
(u,v)
coordinate systems, we may assume that
p=(0,0,0)=s(0,0)
.
Permuting the coordinates
x,y,z
if necessary, we may assume that
the matrix
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x_{u}&x_{v}\\
y_{u}&y_{v}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

is invertible at the origin.
Note that this is the Jacobian matrix of the map
(u,v)\mapsto(x(u,v),y(u,v))
.

The inverse function theorem (
0G
) implies that there is a smooth regular map

w\colon(x,y)\mapsto(u,v)
defined on an open set
W\ni 0
in the
(x,y)
-plane
such that
w(0,0)=(0,0)
and
s\circ w(x,y)=(x,y,f(x,y))
where
f=z\circ w
.
That is, the subset
s\circ w(W)\subset\Sigma
is
the graph of
f
.

Again, by the inverse function theorem,
w(W)
is an open subset of
U
.
Since
s
is an embedding, our graph is open in
\Sigma
;
that is, there is an open set
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that
s\circ w(W)=V\cap\Sigma
is a graph of smooth function.

Since
p
is arbitrary, we get that
\Sigma
is a smooth surface.
∎

8.5. Exercise.

Construct a smooth regular injective map
s\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that its image is not a surface.

If we have
s
and
\Sigma
as in the proposition, then we say that
s
is a
smooth parametrization
of the surface
\Sigma
.

Not all smooth surfaces can be described by such a parametrization;
for example, the sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}
cannot.
However,
any smooth surface
\Sigma
admits a local parametrization at any point
p\in\Sigma
; that is,
p
admits an open neighborhood
W\subset\Sigma
with a smooth regular parametrization
s
.
In this case, any point in
W
can be described by two parameters, usually denoted by
u
and
v
,
which are called
local coordinates
at
p
.
The map
s
is called a
chart
of
\Sigma
.

If
W
is a graph
z=h(x,y)
of a smooth function
h
, then the map

s\colon(u,v)\mapsto(u,v,h(u,v))

is a chart.
Indeed,
s
has an inverse
(u,v,h(u,v))\mapsto(u,v)
which is continuous;
that is,
s
is an embedding.
Further,

s_{u}=(1,0,h_{u})
, and
s_{v}=(0,1,h_{v})
.
Whence the partial derivatives
s_{u}
and
s_{v}
are linearly independent;
that is,
s
is a regular map.

8.6. Corollary.

A connected set
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a smooth surface if and only if a neighborhood of any point in
\Sigma
can be covered by a chart.

A function
g\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}
defined on a smooth surface
\Sigma
is said to be
smooth
if for any chart
s\colon U\to\Sigma
,
the composition
g\circ s
is smooth; that is, all partial derivatives
\frac{\partial^{m+n}}{\partial u^{m}\partial v^{n}}(g\circ s)
are defined and continuous in the domain of definition.

8.7. Exercise.

Let
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a smooth surface.
Show that a function
g\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}
is smooth if and only if for any point
p\in\Sigma
there is a smooth function
h\colon N\to\mathbb{R}
defined in a neighborhood
N\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
of
p
such that the equality
g(q)=h(q)
holds for
q\in\Sigma\cap N
.

Construct a smooth surface
\Sigma
and a smooth function
g\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}
that cannot be extended to a smooth function
h\colon\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
.

8.8. Exercise.

Consider the following map

s(u,v)=(\tfrac{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}u}{1+u^{2}+v^{2}},\tfrac{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}v}{1+u^{2}+v^{2}},\tfrac{2}{1+u^{2}+v^{2}}).

Show that
s
is a chart of the unit sphere centered at
(0,0,1)
; describe the image of
s
.

Let
\gamma(t)=(x(t),y(t))
be a plane curve.
Recall that the
surface of revolution
of the curve
\gamma
around the
x
-axis can be described as the
image of the map

(t,s)\mapsto(x(t),y(t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos s,y(t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sin s).

The parameters
t
and
s
are called
latitude
and
longitude
respectively;
for fixed
t
or
s
the obtained curves are called
parallels
or

meridians
respectively.
The parallels are formed by circles in the plane perpendicular to the axis of rotation.
The curve
\gamma
is called the
generatrix
of the surface.

8.9. Exercise.

Assume
\gamma
is a simple closed smooth plane curve that does not intersect the
x
-axis.
Show that the surface of revolution around the
x
-axis with generatrix
\gamma
is a smooth surface.

G
Global parametrizations

A surface can be described by an embedding from a known surface.

For example, consider the ellipsoid

\Theta=\left\{\,{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\,:\,{\tfrac{x^{2}}{a^{2}}+\tfrac{y^{2}}{b^{2}}+\tfrac{z^{2}}{c^{2}}=1}\,\right\}

for positive constants
a
,
b
, and
c
.
By
8E
,
\Theta
is a smooth surface.
Indeed, let
h(x,y,z)=\tfrac{x^{2}}{a^{2}}+\tfrac{y^{2}}{b^{2}}+\tfrac{z^{2}}{c^{2}}
,
then

\nabla h(x,y,z)=(\tfrac{2}{a^{2}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x,\tfrac{2}{b^{2}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y,\tfrac{2}{c^{2}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}z).

Therefore,
\nabla h\neq 0
if
h=1
; that is,
1
is a regular value of
h
.
It remains to observe that
\Theta
is connected.

The surface
\Theta
can be defined as the image of the map
\mathbb{S}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
, defined as the restriction of the following map to the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}
:

(x,y,z)\mapsto\penalty(a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x,b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y,c{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}z).

A map
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
(or
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
) is smooth if each of its coordinate functions is smooth.
Further, a smooth map
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
is called a

smooth parametrized surface
if it is an embedding, and for any chart
s\colon U\to\Sigma
,
the composition
f\circ s
is regular;
that is, the two vectors

\frac{\partial}{\partial u}(f\circ s)
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial v}(f\circ s)
are linearly independent.
In this case, the image
\Sigma^{*}=f(\Sigma)
is a smooth surface.
The latter follows since for any chart
s\colon U\to\Sigma
the composition
f\circ s\colon U\to\Sigma^{*}
is a chart of
\Sigma^{*}
.

The map
f
is called a
diffeomorphism
from
\Sigma
to
\Sigma^{*}
;
the surfaces
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{*}
are said to be
diffeomorphic
if there is a diffeomorphism
f\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma^{*}
.
The following exercise implies that
being diffeomorphic
is an equivalence relation for surfaces.

8.10. Exercise.

Show that the inverse of a diffeomorphism is a diffeomorphism.

8.11. Advanced exercise.

Show the following.

(a)

Complements of
n
-point sets in the plane are diffeomorphic to each other.

(b)

Open convex subsets of the plane bounded by smooth closed curves are diffeomorphic to each other.

(c)

Any pair of open convex subsets of the plane are diffeomorphic to each other.

(d)

Open star-shaped subsets of the plane are diffeomorphic to each other.

Chapter 9
First-order structure

A
Tangent plane

9.1. Definition.

Let
\Sigma
be a smooth surface.
A vector
w
is
tangent
to
\Sigma
at
p
if and only if there is a curve
\gamma
that runs in
\Sigma
and has
w
as a velocity vector at
p
;
that is,
p=\gamma(t)
and
\text{\sc{w}}=\gamma^{\prime}(t)
for some
t
.

9.2. Proposition and definition.

Let
\Sigma
be a smooth surface and
p\in\penalty\Sigma
.
Then the set of tangent vectors of
\Sigma
at
p
forms a plane;
this plane is called the
tangent plane
of
\Sigma
at
p
.

Moreover, if
s\colon U\to\Sigma
is a local chart and
p=s(u_{0},v_{0})
, then
the tangent plane of
\Sigma
at
p
is spanned by vectors
s_{u}(u_{0},v_{0})
and
s_{v}(u_{0},v_{0})
.

The tangent plane to
\Sigma
at
p
is usually denoted by
\mathrm{T}_{p}
or
\mathrm{T}_{p}\Sigma
.
This plane might be considered as a linear subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
or as a parallel plane passing thru
p
;
the latter is sometimes called the
affine tangent plane
.
The affine tangent plane can be interpreted as the best approximation at
p
of the surface
\Sigma
by a plane.
More precisely,
it has
first-order contact
with
\Sigma
at
p
;
that is,
\rho(q)=\penalty o(|p-q|)
, where
q\in\Sigma
, and
\rho(q)
denotes the distance from
q
to
\mathrm{T}_{p}
.

An assignment of a tangent vector
\text{\sc{v}}_{p}
to each point
p
of the surface
\Sigma
is called a
tangent vector field
if
\text{\sc{v}}_{p}
depends smoothly of
p
.
More precisely, for any chart
s
we have

\text{\sc{v}}_{s(u,v)}=a(u,v){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s_{u}+b(u,v){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s_{v}

for smooth functions
a
and
b
defined in the domain
s
.

Proof.

Fix a chart
s
at
p
.
Assume
\gamma
is a smooth curve that starts at
p
.
We can assume that
\gamma
is covered by the chart;
in particular, there are smooth functions
t\mapsto u(t)
and
t\mapsto v(t)
such that

\gamma(t)=s(u(t),v(t)).

Applying the chain rule, we get

\gamma^{\prime}=s_{u}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}u^{\prime}+s_{v}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}v^{\prime};

that is,
\gamma^{\prime}
is a linear combination of
s_{u}
and
s_{v}
.

The smooth functions
t\mapsto u(t)
and
t\mapsto v(t)
can be chosen arbitrarily.
Therefore any linear combination of
s_{u}
and
s_{v}
is a tangent vector at
p
.
∎

9.3. Exercise.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth function with
0
as a regular value, and
\Sigma
be a surface described as a connected component of the level set
f(x,y,z)=0
.
Show that the tangent plane
\mathrm{T}_{p}\Sigma
is perpendicular to the gradient
\nabla_{p}f
at any point
p\in\Sigma
.

9.4. Exercise.

Let
\Sigma
be a smooth surface and
p\in\Sigma
.
Choose
(x,y,z)
-coordinates.
Show that a neighborhood of
p
in
\Sigma
is a graph
z=f(x,y)
of a smooth function
f
defined on an open subset in the
(x,y)
-plane if and only if the tangent plane
\mathrm{T}_{p}
is not
vertical
; that is, if
\mathrm{T}_{p}
is not perpendicular to the
(x,y)
-plane.

9.5. Exercise.

Show that if a smooth surface
\Sigma
meets a plane
\Pi
at a single point
p
, then
\Pi
is tangent to
\Sigma
at
p
.

B
Directional derivative

In this section, we extend the definition of directional derivative to smooth functions defined on smooth surfaces.
First, let us recall the standard definition for the plane.

Given a point
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, a vector
\text{\sc{w}}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and a function
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
,
consider the function

h(t)=f(p+t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{w}})
.
Then the directional derivative of
f
at
p
along
w
is defined as

D_{\text{\sc{w}}}f(p)\mathrel{:=}h^{\prime}(0).

Recall that a function
f\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}
is said to be smooth if for any chart
s\colon U\to\Sigma
, the composition
f\circ s
is smooth.

9.6. Proposition-Definition.

Let
f
be a smooth function defined on a smooth surface
\Sigma
.
Suppose
\gamma
is a smooth curve in
\Sigma
that starts at
p
with velocity vector
\text{\sc{w}}\in\mathrm{T}_{p}
;
that is,
\gamma(0)=p
, and
\gamma^{\prime}(0)=\text{\sc{w}}
.
Then the derivative
(f\circ\gamma)^{\prime}(0)

depends only on
f
,
p
, and
w
;
it is called the
directional derivative
of
f
along
w
at
p

and is denoted by

D_{\text{\sc{w}}}f,\quad D_{\text{\sc{w}}}f(p),\quad\text{or}\quad D_{\text{\sc{w}}}f(p)_{\Sigma}

— we may omit
p
and
\Sigma
if it is clear from the context.

Moreover, if
(u,v)\mapsto s(u,v)
is a local chart and
\text{\sc{w}}=a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s_{u}+b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s_{v}
at
p
, then

D_{\text{\sc{w}}}f=a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(f\circ s)_{u}+b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(f\circ s)_{v}.

Our definition agrees with the standard definition of the directional derivative if
\Sigma
is a plane.
Indeed, in this case,
\gamma(t)=p+\text{\sc{w}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t
is a curve in
\Sigma
that starts at
p
with velocity vector
w
.
For a general surface, the point
p+\text{\sc{w}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t
might not lie on the surface, and the value
f(p+\text{\sc{w}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t)
might be undefined.
In this case, the standard definition does not work.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
p=s(0,0)
and the curve
\gamma
is covered by the chart
s
;
if not we can chop
\gamma
.
In this case,

\gamma(t)=s(u(t),v(t))

for smooth functions
u,v
defined in a neighborhood of
0
such that

u(0)=\penalty v(0)=\penalty 0
.

Applying the chain rule, we get that

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}(0)

\displaystyle=u^{\prime}(0){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s_{u}+v^{\prime}(0){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s_{v}

at
(0,0)
.
Since
\text{\sc{w}}=\gamma^{\prime}(0)
and the vectors
s_{u}
,
s_{v}
are linearly independent, we get that
a=u^{\prime}(0)
and
b=v^{\prime}(0)
.

Applying the chain rule again, we get that

(f\circ\gamma)^{\prime}(0)=a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(f\circ s)_{u}+b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(f\circ s)_{v}.

➊

at
(0,0)
.

Notice that the left-hand side in
➊
does not depend on the choice of the chart
s
, and the right-hand side depends only on
p
,
w
,
f
, and
s
.
It follows that
(f\circ\gamma)^{\prime}(0)
depends only on
p
,
w
, and
f
.

The last statement follows from
➊
.
∎

9.7. Advanced exercise.

Let
x
and
y
be vector fields on a smooth surface
\Sigma
.
Suppose that
\text{\sc{x}}_{p}
and
\text{\sc{y}}_{p}
are linearly independent at some point
p\in\Sigma
.
Construct two functions
u
and
v
in a neighborhood of
p
such that

\displaystyle D_{\text{\sc{x}}}u

\displaystyle>0,

\displaystyle D_{\text{\sc{y}}}u

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle D_{\text{\sc{x}}}v

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle D_{\text{\sc{y}}}v

\displaystyle>0.

Conclude that there is a chart of
\Sigma
at
p
such that
x
and
y
are tangent to its coordinate lines.

C
Tangent vectors as functionals

In this section, we introduce a more conceptual way to define tangent vectors.
We will not use this approach in the sequel, but it is better to know about it.

A tangent vector
\text{\sc{w}}\in\mathrm{T}_{p}
to a smooth surface
\Sigma

defines a linear functional
1
1

1

Term
functional
is used for functions that take a function as an argument and return a number.

D_{\text{\sc{w}}}
that swallows a smooth function
\varphi
on
\Sigma
and spits its directional derivative
D_{\text{\sc{w}}}\varphi
.
The functional
D
obeys the product rule:

D_{\text{\sc{w}}}(\varphi{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\psi)=(D_{\text{\sc{w}}}\varphi){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\psi(p)+\varphi(p){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(D_{\text{\sc{w}}}\psi).

➋

It is not hard to show that the tangent vector
w
is completely determined by the functional
D_{\text{\sc{w}}}
.
Moreover, tangent vectors at
p
can be
defined
as linear functionals on the space of smooth functions
that satisfy the product rule
➋
.

This definition grabs the key algebraic property of tangent vectors.
It might be a less intuitive way to think about tangent vectors, but it is often convenient to use in the proofs.
For example,
9B
becomes a tautology.

D
Differential of map

Recall that a smooth map from a domain on the plane to space is defined in
0G
.
Let
\Sigma_{0}
be a smooth surface in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
A map
s\colon\Sigma_{0}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
is called
smooth
if, for any chart
w\colon U\to\Sigma_{0}
, the composition
s\circ w
defines a smooth map
U\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

Any smooth map
s
from a surface
\Sigma_{0}
to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
can be described by its coordinate functions

s(p)=(x(p),y(p),z(p))
.
To take a directional derivative of the map we should take the directional derivative of each of its coordinate functions.

D_{\text{\sc{w}}}s\mathrel{:=}(D_{\text{\sc{w}}}x,D_{\text{\sc{w}}}y,D_{\text{\sc{w}}}z).

Assume
s
maps one smooth surface
\Sigma_{0}
to another
\Sigma_{1}
.
Let
p_{0}\in\Sigma_{0}
and
p_{1}=s(p_{0})
.
Choose a curve
\gamma_{0}
in
\Sigma_{0}
such that
\gamma_{0}(0)=p_{0}
and
\gamma_{0}^{\prime}(0)=\text{\sc{w}}
.
Observe that
\gamma_{1}=s\circ\gamma_{0}
is a smooth curve in
\Sigma_{1}
.
Curve
\gamma_{1}
lies in
\Sigma_{1}
and
\gamma_{1}(0)=\penalty p_{1}
;
hence its velocity vector
\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(0)
is in
\mathrm{T}_{p_{1}}\Sigma_{1}
.
By the definition of the directional derivative, we have
D_{\text{\sc{w}}}s=\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(0)
.
Therefore,
D_{\text{\sc{w}}}s\in\mathrm{T}_{p_{1}}\Sigma_{1}
for any
\text{\sc{w}}\in\mathrm{T}_{p_{0}}
.

By
9B
,

\text{\sc{w}}\mapsto D_{\text{\sc{w}}}s
defines a linear map
\mathrm{T}_{p_{0}}\Sigma_{0}\to\penalty\mathrm{T}_{p_{1}}\Sigma_{1}
;
that is,

D_{c{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{w}}}s=c{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}D_{\text{\sc{w}}}s\quad\text{and}\quad D_{\text{\sc{v}}+\text{\sc{w}}}s=D_{\text{\sc{v}}}s+D_{\text{\sc{w}}}s

for any
c\in\mathbb{R}
and
\text{\sc{v}},\text{\sc{w}}\in\mathrm{T}_{p_{0}}
.
The map
d_{p_{0}}s\colon\mathrm{T}_{p_{0}}\Sigma_{0}\to\penalty\mathrm{T}_{p_{1}}\Sigma_{1}
defined by

d_{p_{0}}s\colon\text{\sc{w}}\mapsto D_{\text{\sc{w}}}s

is called the
differential
of
s
at
p_{0}
.

The differential
d_{p_{0}}s
can be described by a
2{\times}2
-matrix
M
in orthonormal bases of
\mathrm{T}_{p_{0}}\Sigma_{0}
and
\mathrm{T}_{p_{1}}\Sigma_{1}
.
Set
\mathop{\rm jac}\nolimits_{p_{0}}s=|\det M|
; this value
does not depend on the choice of orthonormal bases in
\mathrm{T}_{p_{0}}\Sigma_{0}
and
\mathrm{T}_{p_{1}}\Sigma_{1}
.
2
2

2

The value
\mathop{\rm jac}\nolimits_{p_{0}}s
has the following geometric meaning:
if
R_{0}
is a region in
\mathrm{T}_{p_{0}}
, and
R_{1}=(d_{p_{0}}s)(R_{0})
is its image, then

\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits R_{1}=\mathop{\rm jac}\nolimits_{p_{0}}s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits R_{0}.

This identity will play a key role in the definition of surface area.

Let
r\colon\Sigma_{1}\to\Sigma_{2}
be another smooth map between smooth surfaces
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
,
then

d_{p_{0}}(r\circ s)=d_{p_{1}}r\circ d_{p_{0}}s,

and therefore,

\mathop{\rm jac}\nolimits_{p_{0}}(r\circ s)=\mathop{\rm jac}\nolimits_{p_{1}}r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathop{\rm jac}\nolimits_{p_{0}}s.

➌

The constructions above can be applied to a chart
s
;
in this case, the surface
\Sigma_{0}
is an open domain in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Then, the value
\mathop{\rm jac}\nolimits_{p_{0}}s
can be found using the following formulas:

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm jac}\nolimits s

\displaystyle=|s_{v}\times s_{u}|=

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\langle s_{u},s_{u}\rangle{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle s_{v},s_{v}\rangle-\langle s_{u},s_{v}\rangle^{2}}=

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\det[(\mathop{\rm Jac}\nolimits s)^{\top}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathop{\rm Jac}\nolimits s]},

where
\mathop{\rm Jac}\nolimits s
denotes the Jacobian matrix of
s
; see Section
0G
.

E
Surface integral and area

Let
\Sigma
be a smooth surface, and
h\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth function.
Let us define the integral of
h
along a Borel set
R\subset\Sigma
;
most of the time we will apply this definition to surfaces with boundary.

Recall that
\mathop{\rm jac}\nolimits_{p}s
is defined in the previous section.
Assume there is a chart
(u,v)\mapsto s(u,v)
of
\Sigma
defined on an open set
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
R\subset s(U)
.
In this case, set

\iint_{R}h\mathrel{:=}\iint_{s^{-1}(R)}h\circ s(u,v){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathop{\rm jac}\nolimits_{(u,v)}s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}du{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dv.

➍

By the substitution rule (
0G
), the right-hand side in
➍
does not depend on the choice of
s
.
That is, if
s_{1}\colon U_{1}\to\Sigma
is another chart such that
s_{1}(U_{1})\supset R
, then

\iint_{s^{-1}(R)}h\circ s(u,v){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathop{\rm jac}\nolimits_{(u,v)}s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}du{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dv=\iint_{s_{1}^{-1}(R)}h\circ s_{1}(u,v){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathop{\rm jac}\nolimits_{(u,v)}s_{1}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}du{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dv.

In other words, the defining identity
➍
makes sense.

A general region
R
can be subdivided into regions
R_{1},R_{2}\dots
such that each
R_{i}
lies in the image of a chart.
After that one could define the integral along
R
as the sum

\iint_{R}h\mathrel{:=}\iint_{R_{1}}h+\iint_{R_{2}}h+\dots

In case
R
is compact or
h\geqslant 0
, it is straightforward to check that the value
\iint_{R}h
does not depend on the choice of such subdivision.

The area of a region
R
in a smooth surface
\Sigma
is defined as the surface integral

\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits R=\iint_{R}1.

The following proposition is the substitution rule for surface integral.

9.8. Area formula.

Suppose
s\colon\Sigma_{0}\to\Sigma_{1}
is a diffeomorphism between a smooth surfaces.
Consider a region
R\subset\Sigma_{0}
and a smooth function
f\colon\Sigma_{1}\to\mathbb{R}
.
Assume that either
R
is compact or
f
is non-negative.
Then

\iint_{R}(f\circ s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathop{\rm jac}\nolimits s=\int_{s(R)}f.

In particular, if
f\equiv 1
, we have

\iint_{R}\mathop{\rm jac}\nolimits s=\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits[s(R)].

Proof.

Follows from
➌
and the definition of surface integral.
∎

Let
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
be two smooth surfaces.
A map
f\colon\Sigma_{1}\to\Sigma_{2}
is called
length-nonincreasing
if, for any curve
\gamma
in
\Sigma
we have
\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma\geqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits(f\circ\gamma)
.
The following theorem provides a more natural definition of area.
Despite its intuitive statement, the proof is well beyond the scope of our book;
it is based on a generalization of the area formula that works for Lipschitz maps
[
29
, 3.2.3]
.

9.9. Theorem.

The area functional satisfies the following properties:

(a)

Sigma-aditivity:
Let
R_{1},R_{2},\dots
be a sequence of disjoint Borel sets in a smooth surface.
Then

\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits(R_{1}\cup R_{2}\cup\dots)=\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits R_{1}+\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits R_{2}+{}\dots

(b)

Monotonicity:
Let
f\colon\Sigma_{1}\to\Sigma_{2}
be length-nonincreasing map between two smooth surfaces.
Suppose that
R_{1}\subset\Sigma_{1}
and
R_{2}\subset\Sigma_{2}
are Borel sets such that
f(R_{1})\supset R_{2}
.
Then

\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits R_{1}\geqslant\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits R_{2}.

(c)

Unit square has unit area.

Moreover, the area functional is uniquely defined by these properties on all Borel sets.

Remark.

The notion of the area of a surface is closely related to the length of a curve.
However, to define length we use a different idea — it was defined as the least upper bound on the lengths of inscribed polygonal lines.
It turns out that an analogous definition does not work even for very simple surfaces.
The latter is shown by a classical example —
Schwarz’s boot
.
This example and different approaches to the notion of the area are discussed in a popular article by Vladimir Dubrovsky
[
26
]
.

F
Normal vector and orientation

A unit vector that is normal to
\mathrm{T}_{p}
is usually denoted by
\nu(p)
;
it is uniquely defined up to sign.

A surface
\Sigma
is called
oriented
if it is equipped with a unit normal vector field
\nu
or
normal filed
;
that is, a continuous map
p\mapsto\nu(p)
such that
\nu(p)\perp\mathrm{T}_{p}
and
|\nu(p)|=1
for any
p
.
The choice of the field
\nu
is called the
orientation
of
\Sigma
.
A surface
\Sigma
is called
orientable
if it can be oriented.
Each orientable surface admits two orientations:
\nu
and
-\nu
.

Let
\Sigma
be a smooth oriented surface with unit normal field
\nu
.
The map
\nu\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{S}^{2}
defined by
p\mapsto\nu(p)
is called the
spherical map
or
Gauss map
.

For surfaces, the spherical map plays essentially the same role as the tangent indicatrix for curves.

The Möbius strip on the picture gives an example of a nonorientable surface — there is no choice of a normal vector field that is continuous along the middle of the strip (it changes the sign if you try to go around).

Each surface is locally orientable.
In fact, each chart
s(u,v)
admits an orientation

\nu=\frac{s_{u}\times s_{v}}{\left|s_{u}\times s_{v}\right|}.

Indeed, the vectors
s_{u}
and
s_{v}
are tangent vectors at
p
;
since they are linearly independent, their vector product does not vanish, and it is perpendicular to the tangent plane.
Evidently,
(u,v)\mapsto\nu(u,v)
is a continuous map.
Therefore,
\nu
is a unit normal field.

9.10. Exercise.

Suppose a smooth surface
\Sigma
has constant unit normal vector
\nu_{0}
.
Show that
\Sigma
lies in a plane perpendicular to
\nu_{0}
.

9.11. Exercise.

Let
0
be a regular value of a smooth function
h:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
.
Show that a connected component of the level set
h(x,y,z)=0
is an oriented surface.

Recall that any proper surface
\Sigma
without boundary in the Euclidean space divides it into two connected components (
8D
).
Therefore, we can choose the unit normal field on
\Sigma
that points into one of the components of the complement; so, we obtain the following.

9.12. Observation.

Any smooth proper surface without boundary in the Euclidean space is orientable.

In particular, the Möbius strip does not lie on a proper smooth surface without boundary.

G
Sections

9.13. Lemma.

Let
\Sigma
be a smooth surface.
Suppose
f\colon\Sigma\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
is a smooth function.
Then for any constant
r_{0}
there is an arbitrarily close value
r
such that
each connected component of the level set
L_{r}=\left\{\,{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\,:\,{f(x)=r}\,\right\}
is a smooth curve.

Proof.

The surface
\Sigma
can be covered by a countable set of charts
s_{i}\colon U_{i}\to\penalty\Sigma
.
The composition
f\circ s_{i}
is a smooth function for any
i
.
By Sard’s lemma (
0G
),
r
is a regular value of each
f\circ s_{i}
for almost all
r
.

Fix such a value
r
sufficiently close to
r_{0}
, and consider the level set
L_{r}
described by the equation
f(x,y,z)=r
.
Any point on
L_{r}
lies in the image of one of the charts.
From above, it admits a neighborhood which is a smooth curve;
hence the result.
∎

9.14. Advanced exercise.

Let
\Pi
be the
(x,y)
-plane and
A\subset\Pi
be any closed subset.
Construct an open smooth surface
\Sigma
such that
\Sigma\cap\Pi=A
.

The exercise above says that plane sections of a smooth surface might look complicated.
The following corollary makes it possible to perturb the plane so that the section becomes nice.

9.15. Corollary.

Let
\Sigma
be a smooth surface.
Then for any plane
\Pi
there is a parallel plane
\Pi^{*}
that lies arbitrarily close to
\Pi
and such that the intersection
\Sigma\cap\Pi^{*}
is a union of disjoint smooth curves.

Chapter 10
Curvatures

In the previous chapter, we learned that the tangent plane to any smooth surface has first-order contact with it.
This means that up to first order, all surfaces locally look the same at a given point.
This is not the case for second-order approximations, and this is the subject of this chapter.

A
Tangent-normal coordinates

Fix a point
p
in a smooth oriented surface
\Sigma
;
let
\nu
be its normal field.
Consider a coordinate system
(x,y,z)
with origin at
p
such that the
(x,y)
-plane coincides with
\mathrm{T}_{p}
, and the
z
-axis points in the direction of the normal vector
\nu(p)
. Such coordinates are called
tangent-normal coordinates
.
By
9A
, we can present
\Sigma
locally at
p
as a graph
z=f(x,y)
of a smooth function.
Note that

\displaystyle f(0,0)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle f_{x}(0,0)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle f_{y}(0,0)

\displaystyle=0.

The first equality holds since
p=(0,0,0)
lies on the graph, and the other two mean that the tangent plane at
p
is horizontal.

Set

\displaystyle\ell

\displaystyle=f_{xx}(0,0),

\displaystyle m

\displaystyle=f_{xy}(0,0)=f_{yx}(0,0),

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle=f_{yy}(0,0).

The
Taylor series

for
f
at
(0,0)
up to the second-order term can be then written as

f(x,y)=\tfrac{1}{2}(\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x^{2}+2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}m{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y+n{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y^{2})+o(x^{2}+y^{2}).

The values
\ell
,
m
, and
n
are completely determined by this equation.
The graph of Taylor polynomial

z=\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x^{2}+2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}m{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y+n{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y^{2})

is called
osculating paraboloid
.
It has
second-order contact
with
\Sigma
at
p
.

Note that

\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x^{2}+2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}m{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y+n{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y^{2}=\langle M_{p}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
y\end{smallmatrix}),(\begin{smallmatrix}x\\
y\end{smallmatrix})\rangle,

where

M_{p}=\begin{pmatrix}\ell&m\\
m&n\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}f_{xx}(0,0)&f_{xy}(0,0)\\
f_{yx}(0,0)&f_{yy}(0,0)\end{pmatrix}.

➊

is the so-called
Hessian matrix
of
f
at
(0,0)
.

B
Principal curvatures

The tangent-normal coordinates give an almost canonically depend on the point
p
;
it is unique up to a rotation of the
(x,y)
-plane.
Rotating the
(x,y)
-plane results in rewriting
the Hessian matrix
M_{p}
in the new basis.

Since the matrix
M_{p}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\ell&m\\
m&n\end{smallmatrix}\right)
is symmetric, by the spectral theorem (
0E
) it is diagonalizable by orthogonal matrices.
That is, by rotating the
(x,y)
-plane we can assume that
m=0
.
In this case,

M_{p}=\begin{pmatrix}k_{1}&0\\
0&k_{2}\end{pmatrix},

the diagonal components
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
of
M_{p}
are called the
principal curvatures
of
\Sigma
at
p
;
they might also be denoted as
k_{1}(p)
and
k_{2}(p)
, or
k_{1}(p)_{\Sigma}
and
k_{2}(p)_{\Sigma}
;
if we need to emphasize that we compute them at the point
p
for the surface
\Sigma
.
We will assume

k_{1}\leqslant k_{2}

unless indicated otherwise.

If
z=f(x,y)
is a local graph representation of
\Sigma
in these coordinates, then

f(x,y)=\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(k_{1}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x^{2}+k_{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y^{2})+o(x^{2}+y^{2}).

The principal curvatures can be also defined as the eigenvalues of the Hessian matrix
M_{p}
.
The eigendirections of
M_{p}
are called the
principal directions
of
\Sigma
at
p
.
If
k_{1}(p)\neq k_{2}(p)
, then
p
has exactly two principal directions, which are perpendicular to each other;
if
k_{1}(p)=\penalty k_{2}(p)
, then all tangent directions at
p
are principal.

If we reverse the orientation of
\Sigma
, then the principal curvatures switch their signs and indexes:
k_{1}
converts into
-k_{2}
and
k_{2}
into
-k_{1}
.

A smooth curve on a surface
\Sigma
that always runs in the principal directions is called a
line of curvature
of
\Sigma
.
If
k_{1}(p)\neq k_{2}(p)
, then there is a chart
(u,v)\mapsto s(u,v)
at
p
with coordinate lines formed by lines of curvature (it follows from
9B
).
Since principal directions are orthogonal we have
s_{u}\perp s_{v}
.

10.1. Exercise.

Assume a smooth surface
\Sigma
is mirror-symmetric with respect to a plane
\Pi
.
Suppose
\Sigma
and
\Pi
intersect along a smooth curve
\gamma
.
Show that
\gamma
is a line of curvature of
\Sigma
.

C
More curvatures

Let
p
be a point on an oriented smooth surface
\Sigma
.
Recall that
k_{1}(p)
and
k_{2}(p)
denote the principal curvatures at
p
.

The product

K(p)=k_{1}(p){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}k_{2}(p)

is called the
Gauss curvature
at
p
.
We may denote it by
K
,
K(p)
, or
K(p)_{\Sigma}
if we need to specify the point and the surface.

Since the determinant is equal to the product of the eigenvalues, we get

K=\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}n-m^{2},

where

M_{p}=\penalty(\begin{smallmatrix}\ell&m\\
m&n\end{smallmatrix})
is the Hessian matrix.

Reversing the orientation of
\Sigma
does not change the Gauss curvature.
In particular, the Gauss curvature is well-defined for nonoriented surfaces.

10.2. Exercise.

Show that any surface with positive Gauss curvature is orientable.

The sum

H(p)=k_{1}(p)+k_{2}(p)

is called the
mean curvature
1
1

1

Some authors define it as
\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(k_{1}(p)+k_{2}(p))
— the mean value of the principal curvatures. It suits the name better, but it is not as convenient when it comes to computations.

at
p
.
We may also denote it by
H(p)_{\Sigma}
.
The mean curvature can be also interpreted as the trace of the Hessian matrix
M_{p}=\penalty(\begin{smallmatrix}\ell&m\\
m&n\end{smallmatrix})
;
that is,

H=\ell+n.

Reversing the orientation of
\Sigma
changes the sign of the mean curvature.

A surface with vanishing mean curvature is called
minimal
.

10.3. Exercise.

Let
\Sigma
be an oriented surface, and let
\Sigma_{\lambda}
be a scaled copy of
\Sigma
with factor
\lambda>0
; that is,
\Sigma_{\lambda}
consists of the points
\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x
with
x\in\Sigma
. Show that

K(\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}p)_{\Sigma_{\lambda}}=\tfrac{1}{\lambda^{2}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}K(p)_{\Sigma}\quad\text{and}\quad H(\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}p)_{\Sigma_{\lambda}}=\tfrac{1}{\lambda}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}H(p)_{\Sigma}

for any
p\in\Sigma
.

D
Shape operator

In the following definitions, we use the notion of directional derivative and differential defined in
9B
and
9D
.

Let
p
be a point on a smooth surface
\Sigma
with orientation defined by the unit normal field
\nu
.
Given
\text{\sc{w}}\in\mathrm{T}_{p}
,
the
shape operator
2
2

2

The following bilinear forms on a tangent plane

\displaystyle\mathrm{I}(\text{\sc{v}},\text{\sc{w}})

\displaystyle=\langle\text{\sc{v}},\text{\sc{w}}\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathrm{II}(\text{\sc{v}},\text{\sc{w}})

\displaystyle=\langle\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits\text{\sc{v}},\text{\sc{w}}\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathrm{III}(\text{\sc{v}},\text{\sc{w}})

\displaystyle=\langle\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits\text{\sc{v}},\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits\text{\sc{w}}\rangle

are called the
first, second, and third fundamental forms
, respectively.
Historically, these forms were introduced before the shape operator, but we will not touch them.

of
w
is defined by

\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits_{p}\text{\sc{w}}=-D_{\text{\sc{w}}}\nu.

Equivalently, the shape operator can be defined by

\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits=-d\nu,

➋

where
d\nu
denotes the differential of the spherical map
\nu\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{S}^{2}
; that is,
d_{p}\nu(\text{\sc{v}})=(D_{\text{\sc{v}}}\nu)(p)
.

Recall that
d_{p}\nu
is a linear map
\mathrm{T}_{p}\Sigma\to\mathrm{T}_{\nu(p)}\mathbb{S}^{2}
.
Note that
\mathrm{T}_{p}\Sigma
coincides with
\mathrm{T}_{\nu(p)}\mathbb{S}^{2}
— both of them are normal subspaces to
\nu(p)
.
Therefore,
\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits_{p}
is indeed a linear operator
\mathrm{T}_{p}\to\mathrm{T}_{p}
(the latter also will follow from
10D
).

For a point
p\in\Sigma
, the shape operator of a tangent vector
\text{\sc{w}}\in\mathrm{T}_{p}
will be denoted by
\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits\text{\sc{w}}
if it is clear from the context which base point
p
and which surface we are working with;
otherwise, we may use the notations

\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits_{p}(\text{\sc{w}})\quad\text{or}\quad\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits_{p}(\text{\sc{w}})_{\Sigma}

as the situation requires.

10.4. Theorem.

Suppose
(u,v)\mapsto s(u,v)
is a smooth map to a smooth surface
\Sigma
with unit normal field
\nu
.
Then

\displaystyle\langle\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits(s_{u}),s_{u}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle s_{uu},\nu\rangle,

\displaystyle\langle\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits(s_{v}),s_{u}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle s_{uv},\nu\rangle,

\displaystyle\langle\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits(s_{u}),s_{v}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle s_{uv},\nu\rangle,

\displaystyle\langle\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits(s_{v}),s_{v}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle s_{vv},\nu\rangle,

\displaystyle\langle\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits(s_{u}),\nu\rangle

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\langle\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits(s_{v}),\nu\rangle

\displaystyle=0

for any
(u,v)
.

Proof.

We will use the shortcut
\nu=\nu(u,v)
for
\nu(s(u,v))
,
so

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits(s_{u})

\displaystyle=-D_{s_{u}}\nu=-\nu_{u},

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits(s_{v})

\displaystyle=-D_{s_{v}}\nu=-\nu_{v}.

➌

Note that
\nu
is a unit vector orthogonal to
s_{u}
and
s_{v}
;
therefore

\displaystyle\langle\nu,s_{u}\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv 0,

\displaystyle\langle\nu,s_{v}\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv 0,

\displaystyle\langle\nu,\nu\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv 1.

Taking partial derivatives of these two identities we get

\displaystyle\langle\nu_{u},s_{u}\rangle+\langle\nu,s_{uu}\rangle

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\langle\nu_{v},s_{u}\rangle+\langle\nu,s_{uv}\rangle

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\langle\nu_{u},s_{v}\rangle+\langle\nu,s_{uv}\rangle

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\langle\nu_{v},s_{v}\rangle+\langle\nu,s_{vv}\rangle

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\nu_{u},\nu\rangle

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\nu_{v},\nu\rangle

\displaystyle=0.

It remains to plug in the expressions from
➌
.
∎

10.5. Exercise.

Show that the shape operator is
self-adjoint
; that is,

\langle\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits\text{\sc{u}},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle=\langle\text{\sc{u}},\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits\text{\sc{v}}\rangle

for any
\text{\sc{u}},\text{\sc{v}}\in\mathrm{T}_{p}
.

Let us denote by
i
,
j
and
k
the standard basis in the
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
Recall that the components
\ell
,
m
, and
n
of the Hessian matrix are defined in Section
10A
.

10.6. Corollary.

Let
z=f(x,y)
be a local representation of a smooth surface
\Sigma
in the tangent-normal coordinates at
p
.
Suppose that its Hessian matrix at
p
is
(\begin{smallmatrix}\ell&m\\
m&n\end{smallmatrix})
.
Then

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits\text{\sc{i}}

\displaystyle=\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{i}}+m{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{j}},

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits\text{\sc{j}}

\displaystyle=m{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{i}}+n{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{j}},

for the standard basis
\text{\sc{i}},\text{\sc{j}},\text{\sc{k}}
.
That is, the multiplication by the Hessian matrix describes its shape operator.

This corollary illustrates the close relationship between the curvatures of a surface and its shape operator; the principal curvatures of
\Sigma
at
p
are the eigenvalues of
\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits_{p}
, the principal directions are the eigendirections of
\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits_{p}
, the Gaussian curvature is the determinant of
\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits_{p}
, and the mean curvature is the trace of
\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits_{p}
.

Since the Hessian matrix is symmetric, the corollary also implies that
\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits
is self-adjoint which gives a way to solve
10D
.

Proof.

Note that
s\colon(u,v)\mapsto(u,v,f(u,v))
is a chart of
\Sigma
at
p
.
Furthermore,

\displaystyle s_{u}(0,0)

\displaystyle=\text{\sc{i}},

\displaystyle s_{v}(0,0)

\displaystyle=\text{\sc{j}},

\displaystyle\nu(0,0)

\displaystyle=\text{\sc{k}},

\displaystyle s_{uu}(0,0)

\displaystyle=\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{k}},

\displaystyle s_{uv}(0,0)

\displaystyle=m{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{k}},

\displaystyle s_{vv}(0,0)

\displaystyle=n{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{k}}.

It remains to apply
10D
.
∎

10.7. Corollary.

Let
\Sigma
be a smooth surface with orientation defined by a unit normal field
\nu
.
Suppose the spherical map
\nu\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{S}^{2}
is injective.
Then

\iint_{\Sigma}|K|=\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits[\nu(\Sigma)].

Proof.

Choose an orthonormal basis of
\mathrm{T}_{p}
consisting of principal directions,
so the shape operator can be expressed by the matrix

(\begin{smallmatrix}k_{1}&0\\
0&k_{2}\end{smallmatrix})
.

Since
\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits_{p}=-d_{p}\nu
,
10D
implies that

\mathop{\rm jac}\nolimits_{p}\nu=|\det(\begin{smallmatrix}k_{1}&0\\
0&k_{2}\end{smallmatrix})|=|K(p)|.

By the area formula (
9E
), the statement follows.
∎

10.8. Exercise.

Let
\Sigma
be a smooth surface with orientation defined by a unit normal field
\nu
.
Suppose the principal curvatures of
\Sigma
are 1 at all points.

(a)

Show that
\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits_{p}(\text{\sc{w}})=\text{\sc{w}}
for any
p\in\Sigma
and
\text{\sc{w}}\in\mathrm{T}_{p}\Sigma
.

(b)

Show that
q=p+\nu(p)
is constant; that is, the point
q
does not depend on
p\in\Sigma
.
Conclude that
\Sigma
is a subset of the unit sphere centered at
q
.

10.9. Advanced exercise.

Let
\Sigma
be a smooth surface with orientation defined by a unit normal field
\nu
,
and let
Z_{0}\subset\Sigma
be a connected set with vanishing shape operator.
Show that
Z_{0}
lies in a plane.

We define the
angle
between two oriented surfaces at a point of their intersection
p
as the angle between their normal vectors at
p
.

10.10. Exercise.

Assume two smooth oriented surfaces
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
intersect at constant angle along a smooth curve
\gamma
.
Show that if
\gamma
is a curvature line in
\Sigma_{1}
, then it is also a curvature line in

.

Conclude that if a smooth surface
\Sigma
intersects a plane or sphere along a smooth curve
\gamma

then
\gamma
is a curvature line of

.

10.11. Exercise.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed smooth surface with orientation defined by a unit normal field
\nu
.

(a)

Show that if
t
is sufficiently close to zero, then the set

\Sigma_{t}=\left\{\,{p+t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nu(p)}\,:\,{p\in\Sigma}\,\right\}

is a smooth surface.

(b)

Show that for all
t
sufficiently close to zero we have

\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits\Sigma_{t}=\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits\Sigma-t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\iint_{\Sigma}H+t^{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\iint_{\Sigma}K,

where
H
and
K
denote mean and Gauss curvature of
\Sigma
.

10.12. Advanced exercise.

Let
\Sigma
be a smooth oriented surface parametrized by a coordinate rectangle as
(u,v)\mapsto s(u,v)
.
Set
\text{\sc{u}}=\tfrac{s_{u}}{|s_{u}|}
,
\text{\sc{v}}=\tfrac{s_{v}}{|s_{v}|}
;
denote by
\nu(u,v)
the normal unit vector at
s(u,v)
.

Suppose
u
and
v
are principal directions at each point;
let
0
and
k=\penalty k(u,v)
be their principal curvatures.
Assume
k
does not vanish and
|s_{u}|=1
on some
u
-coordinate line.

(a)

Show that
\nu(u,v)
,
\text{\sc{u}}(u,v)
, and
\text{\sc{v}}(u,v)
form an orthonormal frame for any
(u,v)
.

(b)

Show that
\nu(u,v)
,
\text{\sc{u}}(u,v)
, and
\text{\sc{v}}(u,v)
depend only on
v
.
Conclude that
u
-coordinate lines are line segments.

(c)

Show that
s_{uu}
is proportional to
s_{u}
at all points.
Use it to show that
|s_{u}|=1
at all points.

(d)

Show that for any fixed
v_{0}
the value
\tfrac{1}{k(u,v_{0})}
depends linearly on
u
.

This exercise is the key part in the proof of the following theorem:

Any open surface with vanishing Gauss curvature is
cylindrical
;
that is, the surface is swept out by a family of parallel lines.
Exercises
9B
and
18G
illustrate other parts of this proof.
The mentioned theorem was originally proved by Aleksey Pogorelov
[
82
, II §3 Thm 2]
in more general settings; it was rediscovered couple of times after that
[
40
,
64
]
.

Chapter 11
Curves in a surface

A
Darboux frame

Suppose
\gamma
is a smooth curve in a smooth oriented surface
\Sigma
.
As usual, we denote by
\nu
the unit normal field on
\Sigma
.
Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\gamma
is unit-speed;
in this case,
\text{\sc{t}}(s)=\gamma^{\prime}(s)
is its tangent indicatrix.
Further we will use the shortcut notation
\nu(s)=\penalty\nu(\gamma(s))
.

The unit vectors
\text{\sc{t}}(s)
and
\nu(s)
are orthogonal.
Therefore there is a unique unit vector
\mu(s)
such that

\text{\sc{t}}(s),\mu(s),\nu(s)
is an oriented orthonormal basis;
it is called the
Darboux frame
of
\gamma
in
\Sigma
.

Since
\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(s)}\perp\penalty\nu(s)
, the vector
\mu(s)
is tangent to
\Sigma
at
\gamma(s)
.
In fact,
\mu(s)
is a counterclockwise rotation of
\text{\sc{t}}(s)
by the angle
\tfrac{\pi}{2}
in the tangent plane
\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(s)}
.
This vector can also be defined as the vector product
\mu(s)\mathrel{:=}\penalty\nu(s)\times\text{\sc{t}}(s)
.

Since
\gamma
is unit-speed, we have that
\gamma^{\prime\prime}\perp\gamma^{\prime}
(see
3A
).
Therefore, the acceleration of
\gamma
can be written as a linear combination of
\mu
and
\nu
;
that is,

\gamma^{\prime\prime}(s)=k_{g}(s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mu(s)+k_{n}(s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nu(s).

The values
k_{g}(s)
and
k_{n}(s)
are called
geodesic
and
normal curvatures
of
\gamma
at
s
, respectively.
Since the frame
\text{\sc{t}}(s),\mu(s),\nu(s)
is orthonormal, these curvatures can be also written as the following scalar products:

\displaystyle k_{g}(s)

\displaystyle=\langle\gamma^{\prime\prime}(s),\mu(s)\rangle=

\displaystyle k_{n}(s)

\displaystyle=\langle\gamma^{\prime\prime}(s),\nu(s)\rangle=

\displaystyle=\langle\text{\sc{t}}^{\prime}(s),\mu(s)\rangle,

\displaystyle=\langle\text{\sc{t}}^{\prime}(s),\nu(s)\rangle.

Since
0=\langle\text{\sc{t}}(s),\nu(s)\rangle
we have
that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\langle\text{\sc{t}}(s),\nu(s)\rangle^{\prime}=

\displaystyle=\langle\text{\sc{t}}^{\prime}(s),\nu(s)\rangle+\langle\text{\sc{t}}(s),\nu^{\prime}(s)\rangle=

\displaystyle=k_{n}(s)+\langle\text{\sc{t}}(s),D_{\text{\sc{t}}(s)}\nu\rangle.

Applying the definition of the shape operator,
we get the following.

11.1. Proposition.

Assume
\gamma
is a smooth unit-speed curve in a smooth surface
\Sigma
.
Let
p=\gamma(s_{0})
and
\text{\sc{v}}=\gamma^{\prime}(s_{0})
.
Then

k_{n}(s_{0})=\langle\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits_{p}(\text{\sc{v}}),\text{\sc{v}}\rangle,

where
k_{n}
denotes the normal curvature of
\gamma
at
s_{0}
, and
\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits_{p}
is the shape operator at
p
.

According to the proposition, the normal curvature of a smooth curve in
\Sigma
is completely determined by the velocity vector
v
.
For that reason, the normal curvature is also denoted by
k_{\text{\sc{v}}}
,
and

k_{\text{\sc{v}}}=\langle\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits_{p}(\text{\sc{v}}),\text{\sc{v}}\rangle

for any unit vector
v
in
\mathrm{T}_{p}
.

B
Euler’s formula

Let
p
be a point on a smooth surface
\Sigma
.
Assume we choose tangent-normal coordinates at
p
so that the Hessian matrix is diagonalized, then we have

M_{p}=\begin{pmatrix}k_{1}(p)&0\\
0&k_{2}(p)\end{pmatrix}.

Consider a vector
{\text{\sc{w}}}=a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{i}}+b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{j}}
in the
(x,y)
-plane.
Then by
10D
, we have

\langle\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits\text{\sc{w}},\text{\sc{w}}\rangle=a^{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}k_{1}(p)+b^{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}k_{2}(p).

If
w
is unit, then
a^{2}+b^{2}=1
which implies the following.

11.2. Observation.

For any point
p
on an oriented smooth surface
\Sigma
,
the principal curvatures
k_{1}(p)
and
k_{2}(p)
are respectively the minimum and maximum of the normal curvatures at
p
.
Moreover, if
\theta
is the angle between a unit vector
{\text{\sc{w}}}\in\mathrm{T}_{p}
and the first principal direction at
p
, then

k_{\text{\sc{w}}}(p)=k_{1}(p){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(\cos\theta)^{2}+k_{2}(p){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(\sin\theta)^{2}.

The last identity is called
Euler’s formula
.

11.3. Exercise.

Let
p
be a point on a smooth surface.
Show that the sum of the normal curvatures for any pair of orthogonal tangent directions, at
p
is
H(p)
— the mean curvature at
p
.

11.4. Exercise.

Let
p
be a point on a smooth surface.
Show that
\tfrac{3}{8}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}H(p)^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}K(p)
is the average value squared normal curvatures at
p
;
that is, the average value of
k_{\text{\sc{w}}}^{2}
for all unit vectors
{\text{\sc{w}}}\in\mathrm{T}_{p}
.

11.5. Meusnier’s theorem.

Let
\gamma
be a smooth curve on a smooth oriented surface
\Sigma
.
Let
p=\gamma(t_{0})
,
{\text{\sc{v}}}=\penalty\gamma^{\prime}(t_{0})
, and
\alpha=\penalty\measuredangle(\nu(p),\text{\sc{n}}(t_{0}))
;
that is,
\alpha
is the angle between the normal vector to
\Sigma
at
p
and the normal vector in the Frenet frame of
\gamma
at
t_{0}
.
Then

\kappa(t_{0}){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\alpha=k_{n}(t_{0});

here
\kappa(t_{0})
and
k_{n}(t_{0})
are the curvature and the normal curvature of
\gamma
at
t_{0}
, respectively.

Proof.
Since
\gamma^{\prime\prime}=\text{\sc{t}}^{\prime}=\kappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{n}}
, we get that

\displaystyle k_{n}(t_{0})

\displaystyle=\langle\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t_{0}),\nu(p)\rangle=

\displaystyle=\kappa(t_{0}){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{n}}(t_{0}),\nu(p)\rangle=

\displaystyle=\kappa(t_{0}){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\alpha.

∎

11.6. Exercise.

Let
\Sigma
be a smooth surface,
p\in\Sigma
, and
{\text{\sc{v}}}\in\mathrm{T}_{p}\Sigma
a unit vector.
Assume that the normal curvature
k_{\text{\sc{v}}}(p)
does not vanish.

Show that the osculating circles at
p
of smooth curves in
\Sigma
that run in the direction
v
sweep out a sphere
S
with center at
p+\tfrac{1}{k_{\text{\sc{v}}}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nu
and radius
r=\tfrac{1}{|k_{\text{\sc{v}}}|}
.

11.7. Exercise.

Let
\gamma(s)=(x(s),y(s))
be a smooth unit-speed simple plane curve in the upper half-plane,
and
\Sigma
be the surface of revolution around the
x
-axis with generatrix
\gamma
.
Assume that
x^{\prime}\neq 0
.

(a)

Show that the parallels and meridians are lines of curvature on
\Sigma
.

(b)

Show that

\frac{|x^{\prime}(s)|}{y(s)}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{-y^{\prime\prime}(s)}{|x^{\prime}(s)|}

are the principal curvatures of
\Sigma
at
(x(s),y(s),0)
in the direction of the corresponding parallel and meridian, respectively.

(c)

Show that
\Sigma
has Gauss curvature
-1
at all points if and only if
y
satisfies the differential equation
y^{\prime\prime}=y
.

The case
y=e^{-s}
in
11B
is shown; this is the so-called
pseudosphere
.

is a minimal surface.

s(u,v)=(u{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sin v,u{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos v,v)

is a minimal surface.

Let

for a constant
a>0
and
x\in(0,\pi)
.
Show that the Gauss curvature of
\Sigma
does not exceed 1.

Let

be a smooth positive function and
\Sigma
be a surface of revolution of the graph
y=f(x)
around

-axis.
Suppose

has vanishing Gauss curvature.
Show that
