is a linear function; that is
f(x)=c{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x+d
for some constants
c
and
d
.

C
Lagunov’s fishbowl

The following question is a 3-dimensional analog of the moon in a puddle problem (
7E
).

11.12. Question.

Assume a set
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
is bounded by a closed surface
\Sigma
with
principal curvatures bounded in absolute value by 1.
Is it true that
V
contains a ball of radius 1?

11.13. Exercise.

Show that Question
11C
has an affirmative answer if
V
is a body of revolution.

Later (see
12B
)
we will show that the answer is ‘‘yes’’ for convex sets.
Now we are going to show by an example that the answer is ‘‘no’’ in the general case;
it was constructed by Vladimir Lagunov
[
54
]
.

Construction.

Let us start with a body of revolution
V_{1}
whose cross-section is shown on the next picture.
The boundary curve of the cross-section consists of 6 long horizontal line segments included in 3 simple closed smooth curves.
(To make the curves smooth, one has to use cutoffs and mollifiers from Section
0F
.)
The boundary of
V_{1}
has 3 components, each of which is a smooth sphere.

We assume that the curves have curvature at most 1.
Moreover, except for the almost horizontal parts, the curves have absolute curvature close to 1 all the time.
The only thick part in
V_{1}
is the place where all three boundary components come close together;
the remaining part of
V_{1}
is assumed to be very thin.
It could be arranged that the radius
r
of the maximal ball in
V_{1}
is just a bit above

r_{2}=\tfrac{2}{\sqrt{3}}-1
.
(The small black disc on the picture has radius
r_{2}
,
assuming that the three big circles are unit.)
In particular, we may assume that
r<\tfrac{1}{6}
.

Exercise
11B
gives formulas for the principal curvatures of the boundary of
V_{1}
;
which imply that both principal curvatures are at most 1 in absolute value.

It remains to modify
V_{1}
to make its boundary connected without increasing the bounds on its principal curvatures and without allowing larger balls inside.

Each sphere in the boundary contains two flat discs;
they come into three pairs closely lying to each other.
Let us drill thru two of such pairs and reconnect the holes by a body of revolution whose
axis is shifted but stays parallel to the axis of
V_{1}
.
Denote the obtained body by
V_{2}
; its cross-section is shown on the picture.

Let us repeat the operation for the other two pairs of discs.
Denote the obtained body by
V_{3}
.

Note that the boundary of
V_{3}
is connected.
Assuming that the holes are large, its boundary can be made so that its principal curvatures are still at most
1
; the latter can be proved in the same way as for
V_{1}
.
∎

The bound
r_{2}=\tfrac{2}{\sqrt{3}}-1
is optimal;
this and more was proved by Vladimir Lagunov and Abram Fet
[
53
,
55
,
56
]
.

The surface of
V_{3}
in Lagunov’s fishbowl has genus 2;
that is, it can be parametrized by a sphere with two handles.

Indeed, the boundary of
V_{1}
consists of three smooth spheres.

When we drill, we make four holes — two spheres get one hole in each, and one sphere gets two holes.
We reconnect two spheres with a tube and obtain one sphere.
By connecting the two holes of the other sphere with a tube we get a torus;
it is on the left side in the picture of
V_{2}
.
That is, the boundary of
V_{2}
is formed by one sphere and one torus.

To construct
V_{3}
from
V_{2}
, we make a torus from the remaining sphere and connect it to the other torus by a tube.
This way we get a sphere with two handles.

11.14. Exercise.

Modify Lagunov’s construction to make the boundary surface a sphere with 4 handles.

Chapter 12
Supporting surfaces

A
Definitions

We say two smooth surfaces
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
are
tangent
at a point
p\in\Sigma_{1}\cap\Sigma_{2}
if they share the tangent plane at this point.
If the surfaces are oriented, we say they are
cooriented
if they have a common unit normal vector at
p
.
If their normal vectors at
p
point in opposite directions, we say the surfaces are
counteroriented
.

If there is a neighborhood
U
of
p
such that
\Sigma_{1}\cap U
lies on one side from
\Sigma_{2}
in
U
, then we say that
\Sigma_{2}

locally supports

\Sigma_{1}
at
p
.
If this happens, then
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
have to be tangent at
p
, and after adjusting the orientation of one of them, we can assume they are cooriented.

Now, suppose
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
are cooriented at
p
.
Let us describe
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
locally at
p
in tangent-normal coordinates as the graphs of functions
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
respectively.

\Sigma_{2}
locally supports
\Sigma_{1}
at
p
if and only if either

f_{1}(x,y)\geqslant f_{2}(x,y)\quad\text{or}\quad f_{1}(x,y)\leqslant f_{2}(x,y)

holds for all
(x,y)
sufficiently close to the origin.
In the former case, we say
\Sigma_{2}
locally supports
\Sigma_{1}

from the outside
, and in the latter we say
\Sigma_{2}
locally supports
\Sigma_{1}

from the inside
.

12.1. Proposition.

Let
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
be smooth oriented surfaces.
Assume
\Sigma_{1}
locally supports
\Sigma_{2}
from the inside at the point
p
(equivalently
\Sigma_{2}
locally supports
\Sigma_{1}
from the outside).
Then

k_{1}(p)_{\Sigma_{1}}\geqslant k_{1}(p)_{\Sigma_{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad k_{2}(p)_{\Sigma_{1}}\geqslant\penalty k_{2}(p)_{\Sigma_{2}}.

12.2. Exercise.

Construct two surfaces
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
that have a common point
p
with a common unit normal vector that
do not support each other, but

k_{1}(p)_{\Sigma_{1}}>\penalty k_{1}(p)_{\Sigma_{2}}
and
k_{2}(p)_{\Sigma_{1}}>\penalty k_{2}(p)_{\Sigma_{2}}
.

Proof of
12A
.

We can assume that
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
are graphs
z=f_{1}(x,y)
and
z=f_{2}(x,y)
in common tangent-normal coordinates at
p
, so we have
f_{1}\geqslant f_{2}
.

Choose a common tangent unit vector
{\text{\sc{w}}}\in\mathrm{T}_{p}\Sigma_{1}=\mathrm{T}_{p}\Sigma_{2}
.
Consider the plane
\Pi
passing thru
p
and spanned by the normal vector
\nu_{p}
and
w
.
Let
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
be the curves of intersection of
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
with
\Pi
.

Let us orient
\Pi
so that the common normal vector
\nu_{p}
for both surfaces at
p
points to the left from
w
.
Further, let us parametrize both curves so that they are running in the direction of
w
at
p
and are therefore cooriented.
In this case, the curve
\gamma_{1}
supports the curve
\gamma_{2}
from the right.

By
7C
, we have the following inequality for the normal curvatures of
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
at
p
in the direction of
w
:

k_{\text{\sc{w}}}(p)_{\Sigma_{1}}\geqslant k_{\text{\sc{w}}}(p)_{\Sigma_{2}}.

➊

According to
11B
,

k_{1}(p)_{\Sigma_{i}}=\min_{\text{\sc{w}}}\left\{\,{k_{\text{\sc{w}}}(p)_{\Sigma_{i}}}\,\right\},

where
\text{\sc{w}}\in\mathrm{T}_{p}
and
|\text{\sc{w}}|=1
.
Fix
w
such that

k_{1}(p)_{\Sigma_{1}}=\penalty k_{\text{\sc{w}}}(p)_{\Sigma_{1}}.

By
➊
,

\displaystyle k_{1}(p)_{\Sigma_{1}}

\displaystyle=k_{\text{\sc{w}}}(p)_{\Sigma_{1}}\geqslant

\displaystyle\geqslant k_{\text{\sc{w}}}(p)_{\Sigma_{2}}\geqslant

\displaystyle\geqslant\min_{\text{\sc{v}}}\left\{\,{k_{\text{\sc{v}}}(p)_{\Sigma_{2}}}\,\right\}=

\displaystyle=k_{1}(p)_{\Sigma_{2}}.

Similarly, by
11B
,

k_{2}(p)_{\Sigma_{i}}=\max_{\text{\sc{w}}}\left\{\,{k_{\text{\sc{w}}}(p)_{\Sigma_{i}}}\,\right\},

where
\text{\sc{w}}\in\mathrm{T}_{p}
and
|\text{\sc{w}}|=1
.
Fix
w
such that

k_{2}(p)_{\Sigma_{2}}=k_{\text{\sc{w}}}(p)_{\Sigma_{2}}.

By
➊
,

\displaystyle k_{2}(p)_{\Sigma_{2}}

\displaystyle=k_{\text{\sc{w}}}(p)_{\Sigma_{2}}\leqslant

\displaystyle\leqslant k_{\text{\sc{w}}}(p)_{\Sigma_{1}}\leqslant

\displaystyle\leqslant\max_{\text{\sc{v}}}\left\{\,{k_{\text{\sc{v}}}(p)_{\Sigma_{1}}}\,\right\}=

\displaystyle=k_{2}(p)_{\Sigma_{1}}.

Both times we assume that
\text{\sc{v}}\in\mathrm{T}_{p}
and
|\text{\sc{v}}|=1
.∎

12.3. Corollary.

Let
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
be oriented smooth surfaces.
Assume
\Sigma_{1}
locally supports
\Sigma_{2}
from the inside at the point
p
.
Then:

(a)

H(p)_{\Sigma_{1}}\geqslant H(p)_{\Sigma_{2}}
;

(b)

If
k_{1}(p)_{\Sigma_{2}}\geqslant 0
, then
K(p)_{\Sigma_{1}}\geqslant K(p)_{\Sigma_{2}}
.

Proof;
12A
.

The statement follows from
12A
since
H=k_{1}+k_{2}
.

12A
.
Since
k_{2}(p)_{\Sigma_{i}}\geqslant k_{1}(p)_{\Sigma_{i}}
, and
k_{1}(p)_{\Sigma_{2}}\geqslant 0
, we get that all the principal curvatures

k_{1}(p)_{\Sigma_{1}}
,

k_{1}(p)_{\Sigma_{2}}
,

k_{2}(p)_{\Sigma_{1}}
, and

k_{2}(p)_{\Sigma_{2}}
are nonnegative.
By
12A
, it implies that

\displaystyle K(p)_{\Sigma_{1}}

\displaystyle=k_{1}(p)_{\Sigma_{1}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}k_{2}(p)_{\Sigma_{1}}\geqslant

\displaystyle\geqslant k_{1}(p)_{\Sigma_{2}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}k_{2}(p)_{\Sigma_{2}}=K(p)_{\Sigma_{2}}.

∎

12.4. Exercise.

Show that any closed surface in a unit ball has a point with Gauss curvature at least 1.
Conclude that any closed surface has a point with strictly positive Gauss curvature.

12.5. Exercise.

Show that any closed surface that lies at distance at most 1 from a straight line has a point with Gauss curvature at least 1.

B
Convex surfaces

A surface that bounds a convex region is called
convex
.

12.6. Exercise.

Show that the Gauss curvature of any convex smooth surface is nonnegative at each point.

12.7. Advanced exercise.

Assume
R
is a convex body in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
bounded by a surface with principal curvatures at most 1.
Show that
R
contains a unit ball.

Recall that a region
R
in the Euclidean space is called
strictly convex
if, for any two points
x,y\in R
, any point
z
between
x
and
y
lies in the interior of
R
.

For example, any open convex set is strictly convex.
The cube (as well as any convex polyhedron) gives an example of a non-strictly convex set.
Evidently,
a closed convex region is strictly convex if and only if its boundary does not contain a line segment
.

12.8. Lemma.

Let
z=f(x,y)
be the local description of a smooth surface
\Sigma
in tangent-normal coordinates at a point
p\in\Sigma
.
Assume both principal curvatures of
\Sigma
are positive at
p
.
Then the function
f
is strictly convex in a neighborhood of the origin and has a local minimum at the origin.

In particular, the tangent plane
\mathrm{T}_{p}
locally supports
\Sigma
from the outside at
p
.

Proof.

Since both principal curvatures are positive, by
10D
, we have

D^{2}_{\text{\sc{w}}}f(0,0)=\langle\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits_{p}({\text{\sc{w}}}),{\text{\sc{w}}}\rangle\geqslant k_{1}(p)>0

for any unit tangent vector
{\text{\sc{w}}}\in\mathrm{T}_{p}\Sigma
(in our coordinates,
w
is horizontal).

By continuity of the function
(x,y,{\text{\sc{w}}})\mapsto D^{2}_{\text{\sc{w}}}f(x,y)
,
we have that
D^{2}_{\text{\sc{w}}}f(x,y)>0
if
\text{\sc{w}}\neq 0
and
(x,y)
lies in a sufficiently small neighborhood of the origin.
This property implies that
f
is a strictly convex function in a neighborhood of the origin in the
(x,y)
-plane (see
0G
).

Finally, since
\nabla f(0,0)=0
, and
f
is strictly convex in a neighborhood of the origin,
f
has a strict local minimum at the origin.
∎

12.9. Exercise.

Let
\Sigma
be a smooth surface (without boundary) with positive Gauss curvature.
Show that any connected component of the intersection of
\Sigma
with a plane is either a single point or a smooth plane curve whose signed curvature has constant sign.

C
Convexity test

12.10. Theorem.

Suppose
\Sigma
is an open or closed smooth surface with positive Gauss curvature.
Then
\Sigma
bounds a strictly convex region.

In the proof we will have to use that the surface is connected (by the definition);
otherwise, a pair of spheres would give a counterexample.
It is true that
any closed smooth surface with nonnegative Gauss curvature bounds a convex region
, but the proof requires more work
[
39
,
36
,
84
]
.

The same holds for surfaces with possible self-intersections.
This was proved by James Stoker
[
88
]
, but he attributed the result to Jacques Hadamard who proved a closely relevant statement
[
39
, § 23]
.

From the next proof, one can also extract the following statement:
any closed connected locally convex region in the Euclidean space is convex
.

Proof.

Since the Gauss curvature is positive, we can assume that the principal curvatures are positive at any point.
Denote by
R
the region bounded by
\Sigma
that lies on the side of
\nu
;
that is,
\nu
points inside
R
at any point of
\Sigma
.
(The region
R
exists by
8D
.)

Let us show that
R
is
locally strictly convex
;
that is, for any point
p\in\Sigma
, the intersection of
R
with a small ball centered at
p
is strictly convex.

Indeed, suppose
z=f(x,y)
is a local description of
\Sigma
in the tangent-normal coordinates at
p
.
By
12B
,
f
is strictly convex in a neighborhood of the origin.
In particular, the intersection of a small ball centered at
p
with the epigraph
z\geqslant f(x,y)
is strictly convex.

Since
\Sigma
is connected, so is
R
;
moreover, any two points in the interior of
R
can be connected by a polygonal line in the interior of
R
.

Assume the interior of
R
is not convex;
that is, there are points
x,y\in R
, and a point
z
between
x
and
y
that does not lie in the interior of
R
.
Consider a polygonal line
\beta
from
x
to
y
in the interior of
R
.
Let
y_{0}
be the first point on
\beta
such that the chord
[x,y_{0}]
touches
\Sigma
, say at
z_{0}
.

Since
R
is locally strictly convex,
R\cap\penalty B(z_{0},\varepsilon)
is strictly convex for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
.
On the other hand,
z_{0}
lies between two points in the intersection
[x,y_{0}]\cap B(z_{0},\varepsilon)
.
Since
[x,y_{0}]\subset R
, we arrived to a contradiction.

Therefore, the interior of
R
is a convex set.
The region
R
is the closure of its interior, therefore
R
is convex as well.

Since
R
is locally strictly convex, its boundary
\Sigma
contains no line segments.
Therefore,
R
is strictly convex.
∎

12.11. Exercise.

Assume a closed surface
\Sigma
surrounds a unit circle.
Show that there is a point
p\in\Sigma
with
K(p)\leqslant 1
.

12.12. Exercise.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed convex smooth surface of diameter at least
\pi
;
that is, there is a pair of points
p,q\in\Sigma
such that
|p-q|\geqslant\pi
.
Show that
\Sigma
has a point with Gauss curvature at most 1.

D
More convexity

12.13. Theorem.

Suppose
\Sigma
is a closed smooth convex surface.
Then it is a smooth sphere; that is,
\Sigma
admits a smooth regular parametrization by
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.

12.14. Proof-guided exercise.

Assume a convex compact region
R
contains the origin in its interior and is bounded by a smooth surface
\Sigma
.

(a)

Show that any half-line that starts at the origin intersects
\Sigma
at a single point;
that is, there is a positive function
\rho\colon\mathbb{S}^{2}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
such that
\Sigma
consists of the points
q=\penalty\rho(\xi){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\xi
for
\xi\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
.

(b)

Show that
\rho\colon\mathbb{S}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is a smooth function.
Conclude that
\xi\mapsto\penalty\rho(\xi){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\xi
is a smooth regular parametrization
\mathbb{S}^{2}\to\penalty\Sigma
.

12.15. Theorem.

Suppose
\Sigma
is an open smooth surface bounds a strictly convex closed region.
Then there is a coordinate system in which
\Sigma
is the graph
z=\penalty f(x,y)
of a convex function
f
defined on a convex open region
\Omega
of the
(x,y)
-plane.
Moreover,
f(x_{n},y_{n})\to\infty
as
(x_{n},y_{n})\to(x_{\infty},y_{\infty})\in\penalty\partial\Omega
.

12.16. Proof-guided exercise.

Assume a strictly convex closed noncompact region
R
contains the origin in its interior and is bounded by a smooth surface
\Sigma
.

(a)

Show that
R
contains a half-line
\ell
.

(b)

Show that any line
m
parallel to
\ell
intersects
\Sigma
at most at one point.

(c)

Consider an
(x,y,z)
-coordinate system such that the
z
-axis points in the direction of
\ell
.
Show that the projection of
\Sigma
to the
(x,y)
plane is an open convex set; denote it by
\Omega
.

(d)

Conclude that
\Sigma
is a graph
z=f(x,y)
of a convex function
f
defined on
\Omega
.

(e)

Prove the last statement in
12D
.

12.17. Exercise.

Show that any open surface
\Sigma
with positive Gauss curvature is a topological plane;
that is, there is an embedding
\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
with image
\Sigma
.

Try to show that
\Sigma
is a smooth plane;
that is, the embedding
f
can be made smooth and regular.

12.18. Exercise.

Show that any open smooth surface
\Sigma
with positive Gauss curvature
lies inside an infinite circular cone.
In other words, there is an
(x,y,z)
-coordinate system in which
\Sigma
lies in the region
z\geqslant\penalty m{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}
for a positive constant
m
.

12.19. Exercise.

Assume
\Sigma
is a smooth convex surface with positive Gauss curvature
and
\nu\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{S}^{2}
is its spherical map.

(a)

Show that if
\Sigma
is closed, then
\nu\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{S}^{2}
is a bijection.
Conclude that

\iint_{\Sigma}K=4{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi.

(b)

Show that if
\Sigma
is open, then
\nu\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{S}^{2}

maps
\Sigma
bijectively into a subset of a hemisphere.
Conclude that

\iint_{\Sigma}K\leqslant 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi.

Chapter 13
Saddle surfaces

A
Definitions

A surface is called
saddle
if its Gauss curvature at each point is nonpositive;
in other words, the principal curvatures at each point have opposite signs or at least one of them is zero.

If the Gauss curvature is negative at each point,
then the surface is said to be
strictly saddle
;
equivalently, this means that the principal curvatures have opposite signs at each point.
In this case, the tangent plane cannot support the surface even locally — moving along the surface in the principal directions at a given point, one goes above and below the tangent plane.

13.1. Exercise.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth positive function.
Show that the surface of revolution of the graph
y=\penalty f(x)
around the
x
-axis
is saddle if and only if
f
is convex; that is, if
f^{\prime\prime}(x)\geqslant 0
for any
x
.

A surface
\Sigma
is called
ruled
if, for every point
p\in\Sigma
, there is a line
\ell_{p}\subset\Sigma
passing thru
p
.

13.2. Exercise.

Show that any ruled surface is saddle.

13.3. Exercise.

Let
\Sigma
be an open strictly saddle surface and
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\penalty\mathbb{R}
be a smooth convex function.
Show that the restriction of
f
to
\Sigma
does not have a point of local maximum.

A tangent direction on a smooth surface with vanishing normal curvature is called
asymptotic
.
A smooth curve that always runs in an asymptotic direction is called an

asymptotic line
.

Recall that a set
R
in the plane is called
star-shaped
if there is a point
p\in R
such that for any
x\in R
the line segment
[p,x]
belongs to
R
.

13.4. Advanced exercise.

Let
\gamma
be a closed smooth asymptotic line
in the graph
z=\penalty f(x,y)
of a smooth function
f
.
Assume the graph is strictly saddle in a neighborhood of
\gamma
.
Show that the region in the
(x,y)
-plane bounded by the projection
\bar{\gamma}
of
\gamma
cannot be star-shaped.

13.5. Advanced exercise.

Let
\Sigma
be a smooth surface and
p\in\Sigma
.
Assume
K(p)<0
.
Show that there is a neighborhood
\Omega
of
p
in
\Sigma

such that the intersection of
\Omega
with the tangent plane
\mathrm{T}_{p}
is a union of two smooth curves
intersecting transversally
at
p
;
that is, their velocity vectors at
p
are linearly independent.

B
Hats

A
closed surface cannot be saddle
.
Indeed, suppose
\Sigma
is a closed surface.
Consider the smallest sphere that surrounds
\Sigma
.
The sphere supports
\Sigma
at some point, and at this point, the principal curvatures must have the same sign.
The following more general statement is proved using the same idea.

13.6. Lemma.

Assume
\Sigma
is a compact saddle surface, and its boundary line lies in a closed convex region
R
.
Then the entire surface
\Sigma
lies in
R
.

Remark.

In the case of strictly saddle surface, the lemma can be deduced from
13A
.

Proof.

Arguing by contradiction,
assume there is a point
p\in\Sigma
that does not lie in
R
.
Let
\Pi
be a plane that separates
p
from
R
; it exists by
0D
.
Denote by
\Sigma^{\prime}
the part of
\Sigma
that lies with
p
on the same side of
\Pi
.

Since
\Sigma
is compact, it is surrounded by a sphere;
let
\sigma
be the circle of intersection of this sphere and
\Pi
.
Consider the smallest spherical dome
\Sigma_{0}
with boundary
\sigma
that surrounds
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

\Sigma_{0}
supports
\Sigma
at some point
q
.
Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\Sigma_{0}
and
\Sigma
are cooriented at
q
, and
\Sigma_{0}
has positive principal curvatures.
In this case,
\Sigma_{0}
supports
\Sigma
from the outside.
By
12A
, we have
K(q)_{\Sigma}\geqslant\penalty K(q)_{\Sigma_{0}}>0
, a contradiction.
∎

13.7. Exercise.

Construct a proper saddle surface that does not lie in the convex hull of its boundary line.
(By
13B
it cannot be compact.)

13.8. Exercise.

Let
\Delta
be a compact smooth saddle surface with boundary and
p\in\Delta
.
Assume the boundary curve of
\Delta
lies in the unit sphere centered at
p
.
Show that if
\Delta
is a topological disc, then
\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits(\partial\Delta)\geqslant 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
.

Show that the statement does not hold without assuming that
\Delta
is a topological disc.

Remark.

In fact,
\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits\Delta\geqslant\pi
;
that is, the unit plane disc has the minimal possible area.
This follows from the exercise by applying the so-called
coarea formula
.

13.9. Exercise.

Show that an open saddle surface
cannot lie inside an infinite circular cone.

A topological disc
\Delta
in a surface
\Sigma
is called a
hat
of
\Sigma

if its boundary line
\partial\Delta
lies in a plane
\Pi
and
\Delta\setminus\partial\Delta
lies strictly on one side of
\Pi
.

13.10. Proposition.

A smooth surface
\Sigma
is saddle if and only if it has no hats.

A saddle surface can contain a closed plane curve.
For example, the hyperboloid
x^{2}+y^{2}-z^{2}=1
contains the unit circle in the
(x,y)
-plane.
However, according to the proposition (as well as the lemma), a plane curve cannot bound a topological disc (as well as any compact set) in a saddle surface.

Proof.

Since a plane is convex, the only-if part follows from
13B
;
it remains to prove the if part.

Assume
\Sigma
is not saddle; that is, it has a point
p
with strictly positive Gauss curvature;
or equivalently, the principal curvatures
k_{1}(p)
and
k_{2}(p)
have the same sign.
From now on we assume
0<k_{1}(p)\leqslant k_{2}(p)
, the other case being analogous.

Let
z=f(x,y)
be a graph representation of
\Sigma
in tangent-normal coordinates at
p
.
Consider the set
F_{\varepsilon}
in the
(x,y)
-plane defined by the inequality
f(x,y)\leqslant\varepsilon
.
By
12B
,
f
is convex in a small neighborhood of
(0,0)
.
Therefore,
F_{\varepsilon}
is convex, for sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
.
In particular,
F_{\varepsilon}
is a topological disc.

The map
(x,y)\mapsto(x,y,f(x,y))
defines a homeomorphism from
F_{\varepsilon}

to

\Delta_{\varepsilon}=\left\{\,{(x,y,f(x,y))\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\,:\,{f(x,y)\leqslant\varepsilon}\,\right\};

so
\Delta_{\varepsilon}
is a topological disc for any small
\varepsilon>0
.
The boundary line of
\Delta_{\varepsilon}
lies on the plane
z=\varepsilon
, and the entire disc lies below it;
that is,
\Delta_{\varepsilon}
is a hat of
\Sigma
.
∎

The following exercise shows that
\Delta_{\varepsilon}
is in fact a smooth disc.
This can be used to prove a slightly stronger version of
13B
;
namely in the definition of hats one can assume that the disc is smooth.

13.11. Exercise.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth strictly convex function with a minimum at the origin.
Show that the set
F_{\varepsilon}
in the graph
z=f(x,y)
defined by the inequality
f(x,y)\leqslant\varepsilon
is a smooth disc for any
\varepsilon>0
;
that is, there is a diffeomorphism

F_{\varepsilon}
to the unit disc
\Delta=\penalty\left\{\,{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\,:\,{x^{2}+y^{2}\leqslant 1}\,\right\}
.

13.12. Exercise.

Let
L\colon\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
be an
affine transformation
; that is, a bijection
\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
that sends any plane to a plane.
Show that for any saddle surface
\Sigma
the image
L(\Sigma)
is also a saddle surface.

C
Saddle graphs

The following theorem was proved by Sergei Bernstein
[
8
]
.

13.13. Theorem.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth function.
Assume its graph
z=f(x,y)
is a strictly saddle surface in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
Then
f
is not bounded;
that is, there is no constant
C
such that

|f(x,y)|\leqslant C
for any
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

The theorem states that a saddle graph cannot lie between two horizontal planes;
combining this with
13B
, we get that saddle graphs cannot lie between two parallel planes.
The following exercise shows that the theorem does not hold for saddle surfaces that are not graphs.

13.14. Exercise.

Construct an open strictly saddle surface that lies between two parallel planes.

Since
\exp(x-y^{2})>0
,
the following exercise shows that there are strictly saddle graphs with functions bounded on one side;
that is, both (upper and lower) bounds are needed in the proof of the theorem.

13.15. Exercise.

Show that the graph

z=\exp(x-y^{2})

is strictly saddle.

The following exercise leads to a stronger version of Bernstein’s theorem.

13.16. Advanced exercise.

Let
\Sigma
be an open smooth strictly saddle surface in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
Assume there is a compact subset
K\subset\Sigma
such that the complement
\Sigma\setminus K
is the graph
z=f(x,y)
of a smooth function defined in an open domain of the
(x,y)
-plane.
Show that the surface
\Sigma
is a graph.

The following lemma gives an analogue of
13B
, and will be used in the proof of Theorem
13C
.

13.17. Lemma.

There is no proper strictly saddle smooth surface that has its boundary line in a plane
\Pi
and lies at a bounded distance from a line contained in
\Pi
.

Proof.

By
13B
, the statement can be reformulated in the following way:

There is no proper strictly saddle smooth surface
with its boundary line in the
(x,y)
-plane
and contained in a region of the form:

R=\left\{\,{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\,:\,{0\leqslant z\leqslant r,0\leqslant y\leqslant r}\,\right\}.

Assume the contrary, let
\Sigma
be such a surface.
Consider the projection
\hat{\Sigma}
of
\Sigma
to the
(x,z)
-plane.
It lies in the upper half-plane and below the line
z=r
.

Consider the open upper half-plane
H=\left\{\,{(x,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\,:\,{z>0}\,\right\}
.
Let
\Theta
be the connected component of the complement
H\setminus\hat{\Sigma}
that contains all the points above the line
z=r
.

The set
\Theta
is convex.
If not, then there is a line segment
[p,q]
for some
p,q\in\Theta
that cuts from
\hat{\Sigma}
a compact piece.

Consider the plane
\Pi
thru
[p,q]
that is perpendicular to the
(x,z)
-plane.

\Pi
cuts from
\Sigma
a compact region
\Delta
.
By a general position argument (see
9G
),
we can assume that
\Delta
is a compact surface with its boundary line in
\Pi
, and the remaining part of
\Delta
lies strictly on one side from
\Pi
.
Since the plane
\Pi
is convex, this statement contradicts
13B
.

Summarizing,
\Theta
is an open convex set of
H
that contains all points above
z=r
.
By convexity, together with any point
w
, the set
\Theta
contains all points on the half-lines that start as
w
and
point up
; that is, in directions with positive
z
-coordinate.
In other words, with any point
w
,
the set
\Theta
contains all points with larger
z
-coordinates.

Since
\Theta
is open it can be described by an inequality
z>r_{0}
.
It follows that the plane
z=r_{0}
supports
\Sigma
at some point (in fact at many points).
By
12A
, the latter is impossible — a contradiction.
∎

Proof of
13C
.

Denote by
\Sigma
the graph
z=f(x,y)
.
Assume the contrary; that is,
\Sigma
lies between two planes
z=\pm C
.

The function
f
cannot be constant.
It follows that the tangent plane
\mathrm{T}_{p}
at some point
p\in\Sigma
is not horizontal.

Denote by
\Sigma^{+}
the part of
\Sigma
that lies above
\mathrm{T}_{p}
.
Note that
\Sigma^{+}
has at least two connected components which are approaching
p
from both sides
in the principal direction with positive principal curvature.
Otherwise, there is a curve that runs in
\Sigma^{+}
and approaches
p
from both sides.
This curve cuts a topological disc, say
\Delta
, from
\Sigma
with the boundary line above or on
\mathrm{T}_{p}
and some points strictly below
\mathrm{T}_{p}
;
the latter contradicts
13B
.
Summarizing,
\Sigma^{+}
has at least two connected components.

The surface
\Sigma
seen from above.

Let
z=h(x,y)=a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x+b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y+c
be the equation of
\mathrm{T}_{p}
.
Notice that
\Sigma^{+}=\penalty\left\{\,{(x,y,f(x,y))\in\Sigma}\,:\,{h(x,y)\leqslant f(x,y)}\,\right\}
, hence it contains the connected set

R_{-}=\left\{\,{(x,y,f(x,y))\in\Sigma}\,:\,{h(x,y)<-C}\,\right\}

and is disjoint from

R_{+}=\left\{\,{(x,y,f(x,y))\in\Sigma}\,:\,{h(x,y)>C}\,\right\}.

Whence one of the connected components, say
\Sigma^{+}_{0}
, lies in the strip

R_{0}=\left\{\,{(x,y,f(x,y))\in\Sigma}\,:\,{|h(x,y)|\leqslant C}\,\right\}.

This set lies at a bounded distance from the line of intersection of
\mathrm{T}_{p}
with the
(x,y)
-plane.

Let us move
\mathrm{T}_{p}
slightly upward and cut from
\Sigma^{+}_{0}
the piece above the obtained plane, say
\bar{\Sigma}^{+}_{0}
.
By the general position argument (
9G
),
we can assume that
\bar{\Sigma}^{+}_{0}
is a surface with a smooth boundary line;
by construction the boundary line lies in the plane.
The obtained surface
\bar{\Sigma}^{+}_{0}
still lies at a bounded distance to a line.
The latter is impossible by
13C
.
∎

D
Remarks

Bernstein’s theorem and the lemma in its proof do not hold for nonstrictly saddle surfaces;
counterexamples can be found among cylindrical surfaces over smooth curves; see the end of
10D
.
In fact, it can be shown that these are the only counterexamples;
a proof is based on the same idea, but it is more technical.

By
13B
, saddle surfaces can be defined as smooth surfaces without hats.
This definition can be used for arbitrary surfaces, not necessarily smooth.
Some results, for example, Bernstein’s characterization of saddle graphs, can be extended to these generalized saddle surfaces.
However, this class of surfaces is far from being understood; see
[
4
, Chapter 4]
and the references therein.

Part III:   Geodesics

Chapter 14
Shortest paths

A
Intrinsic geometry

Geometry of surfaces is divided into intrinsic and extrinsic.
We say that something is
intrinsic
if it can be checked or measired within the surface;
for example, lengths of curves on the surface or angles between them.
Otherwise, if the definition of something essentially uses the ambient space, then this something is called
extrinsic
;
examples include normal curvature.

The mean curvature, as well as the Gauss curvature, are defined via principal curvatures, which are extrinsic.
Later (
18H
) it will be shown that remarkably the Gauss curvature is intrinsic — it can be calculated via measurements within the surface.
The mean curvature is not intrinsic; for example, the intrinsic geometry of the plane is not distinguishable from the intrinsic geometry of the graph
z=\penalty(x+y)^{2}
.
However, the mean curvature of the former vanishes at all points, while the mean curvature of the latter does not vanish, say at the origin.

The following exercise should help you get in the right mood;
it might look like a tedious problem in calculus, but it actually is an amusing problem in geometry.

14.1. Exercise.

A cowboy stands at the bottom of a frictionless ice-mountain formed by a cone with a circular base with the angle of inclination
\theta
.
He throws up his lasso which slips neatly over the tip of the cone, pulls it tight, and tries to climb.

What is the critical angle
\theta
at which the cowboy cannot climb the ice-mountain?

B
Definition

Let
p
and
q
be two points on a surface
\Sigma
.
Recall that
|{p}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}
denotes the induced length distance from
p
to
q
;
that is, the greatest lower bound of the lengths of paths in
\Sigma
from
p
to
q
.

A path
\gamma
from
p
to
q
in
\Sigma
that minimizes the length is called a
shortest path
from
p
to
q
.
The image of such a shortest path will be denoted by
[p,q]
or
[p,q]_{\Sigma}
.
If we write
[p,q]_{\Sigma}
, we are assuming that such a shortest path exists, and we have chosen one of them.
In general, there might be no shortest path between two given points, and there might be many of them;
this is shown in the following two examples.

Nonuniqueness.
There are plenty of shortest paths between the poles of a round sphere — each meridian is a shortest path.
The latter follows from
2H
.

Nonexistence.
Let
\Sigma
be the
(x,y)
-plane with the origin removed.
Consider two points
p=(1,0,0)
and
q=(-1,0,0)
in
\Sigma
.
Let us show that
there is no shortest path from
p
to
q
in
\Sigma
.

Note that
|{p}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}=2
.
Indeed, given
\varepsilon>\penalty 0
, consider the point
s_{\varepsilon}=(0,\varepsilon,0)
.
The polygonal path
ps_{\varepsilon}q
lies in
\Sigma
, and its length
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sqrt{1+\varepsilon^{2}}
approaches
2
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
It follows that
|{p}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}\leqslant 2
.
Since
|{p}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}\geqslant\penalty|{p}-\penalty{q}|_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}=2
, we get
|{p}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}=2
.

It follows that a shortest path from
p
to
q
, if it exists, must have length 2.
By the triangle inequality, any curve of length 2 from
p
to
q
must run along the line segment
[p,q]
;
in particular, it must pass thru the origin.
Since the origin does not lie in
\Sigma
, there is no shortest path from
p
to
q
in
\Sigma
.

14.2. Proposition.

Any two points in a proper smooth surface (possibly with boundary) can be joined by a shortest path.

Proof.

Choose two points
p
and
q
in a proper smooth surface
\Sigma
.
Set
\ell=|{p}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}
.

Since
\Sigma
is smooth, the points
p
and
q
can be joined by a piecewise smooth path in
\Sigma
.
Since any such path is rectifiable,
\ell
has to be finite.

By the definition of induced length-metric (Section
2G
),
there is a sequence of paths
\gamma_{n}
from
p
to
q
in
\Sigma
such that

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{n}\to\ell\quad\text{as}\quad n\to\infty.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{n}<\ell+1
for any
n
, and each
\gamma_{n}
is parametrized proportional to its arc-length.
In particular, each path
\gamma_{n}\colon[0,1]\to\Sigma
is
(\ell+1)
-Lipschitz;
that is,

|\gamma(t_{0})-\gamma(t_{1})|\leqslant(\ell+1){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|t_{0}-t_{1}|

for any
t_{0},t_{1}\in[0,1]
.

The image of
\gamma_{n}
lies in the closed ball
\bar{B}[p,\ell+1]
for any
n
.
It follows that the coordinate functions of all
\gamma_{n}
are uniformly equicontinuous and uniformly bounded.
By the Arzelá–Ascoli theorem (
0F
),
there is a convergent subsequence of
\gamma_{n}
, and its limit, say
\gamma_{\infty}\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
, is continuous;
that is,
\gamma_{\infty}
is a path.
Evidently,
\gamma_{\infty}
runs from
p
to
q
;
in particular

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{\infty}\geqslant\ell.

Since
\Sigma
is a closed set,
\gamma_{\infty}
lies in
\Sigma
.

Since length is semicontinuous (
2C
), we get
\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{\infty}\leqslant\ell
.
Therefore,
\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{\infty}=\penalty\ell
; equivalently,
\gamma_{\infty}
is a shortest path from
p
to
q
.
∎

C
Nearest-point projection

The following statement belongs to convex geometry,
but we present its proof since it plays a significant role in the sequel.

14.3. Lemma.

Let
R
be a closed convex set in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
Then for every point
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
there is a unique point
\bar{p}\in R
that minimizes the distance to
R
;
that is,
|p-\bar{p}|\leqslant|p-x|
for any point
x\in R
.

Moreover, the map
p\mapsto\bar{p}
is short;
that is,

|p-q|\geqslant|\bar{p}-\bar{q}|

➊

for any pair of points
p,q\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

The map
p\mapsto\bar{p}
is called the
nearest-point projection
;
it maps the Euclidean space onto
R
.
If
p\in R
, then
\bar{p}=p
.
In particular
\bar{\bar{p}}=\bar{p}
for any point
p
.

Proof.

Fix a point
p
, and set

\ell=\inf\left\{\,{|p-x|}\,:\,{x\in R}\,\right\}.

Choose a sequence
x_{n}\in R
such that
|p-x_{n}|\to\ell
as
n\to\infty
.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that all the points
x_{n}
lie in a ball of radius
\ell+1
centered at
p
.
Therefore, we can pass to a
partial limit

\bar{p}
of
x_{n}
; that is,
\bar{p}
is a limit of a subsequence of
x_{n}
.
Since
R
is closed,
\bar{p}\in R
.
By construction

\displaystyle|p-\bar{p}|

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}|p-x_{n}|=\ell.

Hence, the existence follows.

Assume there are two distinct points
\bar{p},\bar{p}^{\prime}\in R
that minimize the distance to
p
.
Since
R
is convex, their midpoint
m=\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(\bar{p}+\bar{p}^{\prime})
lies in
R
.
Since
|p-\bar{p}|=\penalty|p-\bar{p}^{\prime}|=\ell
, the triangle
[p\bar{p}\bar{p}^{\prime}]
is isosceles; therefore the triangle
[p\bar{p}m]
has a right angle at
m
.
Since a leg of a right triangle is shorter than its hypotenuse, we have
|p-m|<\ell
, a contradiction.

It remains to prove
➊
.
We can assume that
\bar{p}\neq\bar{q}
; otherwise, there is nothing to prove.

If
\measuredangle[\bar{p}\,{}^{p}_{\bar{q}}]<\tfrac{\pi}{2}
, then
|{p}-\penalty{x}|<\penalty|{p}-\penalty{\bar{p}}|
for a point
x\in[\bar{p},\bar{q}]
close to
\bar{p}
.
Since
[\bar{p},\bar{q}]\subset K
,
the latter is impossible.

Therefore,
\measuredangle[\bar{p}\,{}^{p}_{\bar{q}}]\geqslant\tfrac{\pi}{2}
or
p=\bar{p}
.
In both cases, the orthogonal projection of
p
to the line
\bar{p}\bar{q}
lies behind
\bar{p}
, or coincides with
\bar{p}
.
The same way we show that the orthogonal projection of
q
to the line
\bar{p}\bar{q}
lies behind
\bar{q}
, or coincides with
\bar{q}
.
This implies that the orthogonal projection of the line segment
[p,q]
contains
[\bar{p},\bar{q}]
.
Whence
➊
follows.
∎

D
Shortest paths on convex surfaces

14.4. Theorem.

Assume a surface
\Sigma
bounds a closed convex region
R
, and
p,q\in\Sigma
.
Denote by
W
the outer closed region of
\Sigma
;
in other words,
W
is the union of
\Sigma
and the complement of
R
.
Then

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma\geqslant|{p}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}

for any path
\gamma
in
W
from
p
to
q
.
Moreover, if
\gamma
does not lie in
\Sigma
, then the inequality is strict.

Proof.

Let
\bar{\gamma}
be the nearest-point projection of
\gamma
to
R
.
The projection
\bar{\gamma}
lies in
\Sigma
and connects
p
to
q
; therefore,

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\bar{\gamma}\geqslant|{p}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}.

To prove the first statement, it remains to show that

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma\geqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\bar{\gamma}.

➋

Consider a polygonal line
\bar{p}_{0}\dots\bar{p}_{n}
inscribed in
\bar{\gamma}
.
Let
p_{0}\dots p_{n}
be the corresponding polygonal line inscribed in
\gamma
;
that is
p_{i}=\gamma(t_{i})
if
\bar{p}_{i}=\bar{\gamma}(t_{i})
.
By
14C
,
|p_{i}-p_{i-1}|\geqslant\penalty|\bar{p}_{i}-\bar{p}_{i-1}|
for any
i
.
Therefore,

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits p_{0}\dots p_{n}\geqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\bar{p}_{0}\dots\bar{p}_{n}.

Applying the definition of length, we get
➋
.

It remains to prove the second statement.
Suppose there is a point
w=\penalty\gamma(t_{1})\notin\Sigma
;
note that
w\notin R
.
Then there is a plane
\Pi
that cuts
w
from
\Sigma
; see
0D
).

The curve
\gamma
must intersect
\Pi
at two points: one point before
t_{1}
and one after.
Let
a=\penalty\gamma(t_{0})
and
b=\gamma(t_{2})
be these points.
The arc of
\gamma
from
a
to
b
is strictly longer that
|a-b|
;
indeed its length is at least
|a-w|+|w-b|
, and
|a-w|+|w-b|>|a-b|
since
w\notin[a,b]
.

Remove from
\gamma
the arc from
a
to
b
, and replace it with the line segment
[a,b]
.
Denote the obtained curve by
\gamma_{1}
, then

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma>\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{1}.

Since
\gamma_{1}
runs in
W
, we get

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{1}\geqslant|{p}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}.

Hence the second statement follows.
∎

14.5. Exercise.

Suppose
\Sigma
is an open or closed smooth surface with positive Gauss curvature, and
\nu
is the unit normal field on

.
Show that

|{p}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}\leqslant 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\frac{|p-q|}{|\nu(p)+\nu(q)|}

for any two points
p,q\in\Sigma
.

14.6. Exercise.

Suppose
\Sigma
is a closed smooth surface that bounds a convex region
R

in
\mathbb{R}^{3}

and
\Pi
is a plane that cuts a hat
\Delta
from
\Sigma
.
Assume the reflection of the interior of
\Delta
across
\Pi
lies in the interior of
R
.
Show that
\Delta
is
convex
with respect to the intrinsic metric of
\Sigma
;
that is,
if both endpoints of a shortest path in
\Sigma

lie in
\Delta
,
then the entire path lies in
\Delta
.

Let us define the
intrinsic diameter
of a closed surface
\Sigma
as the least upper bound of the lengths of shortest paths in the surface.

14.7. Exercise.

Assume a closed smooth surface

with positive Gauss curvature lies in a unit ball
B
.

Show that the intrinsic diameter of

cannot exceed
\pi
.

4{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
.

Chapter 15
Geodesics

A
Definition

A smooth curve
\gamma
in a smooth surface is called a
geodesic
if, for any
t
, the acceleration
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t)
is perpendicular to the tangent plane
\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t)}
.

15.1. Exercise.

Show that the helix
\gamma(t)\mathrel{:=}\penalty(\cos t,\sin t,t)
is a geodesic on the cylindrical surface
x^{2}+y^{2}=1
.

Geodesics can be understood as the trajectories of particles that slide on
\Sigma
without friction and external forces.
Indeed, since there is no friction, the force that keeps the particle on
\Sigma
must be perpendicular to
\Sigma
.
Therefore, by Newton’s second law of motion,
we get that the acceleration
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
is perpendicular to
\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t)}
.

From a physics point of view, the following lemma is a corollary of the law of conservation of energy.

15.2. Lemma.

Let
\gamma
be a geodesic in a smooth surface
\Sigma
.
Then
|\gamma^{\prime}|
is constant.
Moreover, for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, the curve

\gamma_{\lambda}(t)\mathrel{:=}\gamma(\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t)
is a geodesic as well.

In other words, any geodesic has a constant speed, and multiplying its parameter by a constant yields another geodesic.

Proof.

Since
\gamma^{\prime}(t)
is a tangent vector at
\gamma(t)
,
we have that
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t)\perp\penalty\gamma^{\prime}(t)
, or equivalently
\langle\gamma^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime}\rangle=0
for any
t
.
Hence

\langle\gamma^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}\rangle^{\prime}=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\gamma^{\prime\prime},\gamma^{\prime}\rangle=0
.
That is,
|\gamma^{\prime}|^{2}=\langle\gamma^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime}\rangle
is constant.

The second part follows since

\gamma_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}(t)=\lambda^{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\gamma^{\prime\prime}(\lambda t)
.
∎

The statement in the following exercise is called
Clairaut’s relation
;
it can be obtained from the conservation of angular momentum.

15.3. Exercise.

Let
\gamma
be a geodesic on a smooth surface of revolution.
Suppose
r(t)
denotes the distance from
\gamma(t)
to the axis of rotation
and
\theta(t)
— the angle between
\gamma^{\prime}(t)
and the latitudinal circle thru
\gamma(t)
.

Show that the value
r(t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\theta(t)
is constant.

Recall that an
asymptotic line
is a curve in the surface with vanishing normal curvature.

15.4. Exercise.

Assume a curve
\gamma
is a geodesic and, at the same time, an asymptotic line of a smooth surface.
Show that
\gamma
is a straight-line segment.

15.5. Exercise.

Assume a smooth surface
\Sigma
is mirror-symmetric with respect to a plane
\Pi
.
Suppose
\Sigma
and
\Pi
intersect along a smooth curve
\gamma
.
Show that
\gamma
, parametrized by arc-length, is a geodesic on
\Sigma
.

B
Existence and uniqueness

The following proposition says that the motion without friction and external forces depends smoothly on its initial data.
The formulation uses the smoothness of the map
w
, which spits a point in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
for a point
p
on the surface
\Sigma
, a tangent vector
v
at
p
, and a real parameter
t
.
In a chart
s
on
\Sigma
, the point
p
is described by a pair of coordinates
(u,v)
, and the vector
v
can be expressed as the sum
a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s_{u}+b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s_{v}
.
Therefore, in local coordinates,
w
is defined by the mapping
(u,v,a,b,t)\mapsto w(p,\text{\sc{v}},t)
from a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{5}
to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, to which the usual definition of smoothness applies.
Now, the map
(p,\text{\sc{v}},t)\mapsto\penalty w(p,\text{\sc{v}},t)
is considered to be
smooth
if it is described by a smooth mapping
(u,v,a,b,t)\mapsto w(p,\text{\sc{v}},t)
in any local coordinates on
\Sigma
.

15.6. Proposition.

Let
\Sigma
be a smooth surface without boundary.
Given a tangent vector
v
to
\Sigma
at a point
p
,
there is a unique geodesic
\gamma\colon\mathbb{I}\to\Sigma
defined on a maximal open interval
\mathbb{I}\ni 0
that starts at
p
with velocity vector
v
;
that is,
\gamma(0)=p
and
\gamma^{\prime}(0)={\text{\sc{v}}}
.
Moreover, we have the following.

(a)

The map
w\colon(p,{\text{\sc{v}}},t)\mapsto\gamma(t)
is smooth, and it has an open domain of definition
1
1

1

That is, if
w
is defined for a triple
p\in\Sigma
,
{\text{\sc{v}}}\in\mathrm{T}_{p}
, and
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
then it is also defined for any triple
q\in\Sigma
,
\text{\sc{u}}\in\mathrm{T}_{p}
, and
s\in\mathbb{R}
, where
q
,
u
, and
s
are sufficiently close to
p
,
v
, and
t
, respectively.
.

(b)

If
\Sigma
is proper, then
\mathbb{I}=\mathbb{R}
; that is,
\gamma
is defined on the entire real line.

A surface that satisfies the conclusion of
15B
for any tangent vector
v
is said to be
geodesically complete
.
So part
15B
says that
any proper surface without boundary is geodesically complete
.
This is a part of the
Hopf–Rinow theorem

[
42
]
.

In the proof, we will rewrite the definition of a geodesic using a differential equation and then apply Theorems
0H
and
0H
.

15.7. Lemma.

Let
f
be a smooth function defined on an open domain in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
A smooth curve
t\mapsto\gamma(t)=(x(t),y(t),z(t))
is a geodesic in the graph
z=f(x,y)
if and only if
z(t)=f(x(t),y(t))
for any
t
and the functions
t\mapsto x(t)
and
t\mapsto y(t)

satisfy a system of differential equations

\begin{cases}x^{\prime\prime}=g(x,y,x^{\prime},y^{\prime}),\\
y^{\prime\prime}=h(x,y,x^{\prime},y^{\prime}),\end{cases}

where
g
and
h
are smooth functions of four variables uniquely determined by
f
.

Proof.
The first condition
z(t)=f(x(t),y(t))
simply means that
\gamma(t)
lies on the graph
z=f(x,y)
.

In the following calculations, we often omit the arguments;
in other words, we may use shortcuts
x=x(t)
,
f=f(x,y)=f(x(t),y(t))
, and so on.

First, let us express
z^{\prime\prime}
in terms of
f
,
x
, and
y
.

\displaystyle z^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=f(x,y)^{\prime\prime}=

➊

\displaystyle=\left(f_{x}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x^{\prime}+f_{y}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y^{\prime}\right)^{\prime}=

\displaystyle=f_{xx}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(x^{\prime})^{2}+f_{x}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x^{\prime\prime}+2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f_{xy}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}x^{\prime}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y^{\prime}+f_{yy}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(y^{\prime})^{2}+f_{y}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}y^{\prime\prime}.

The condition

\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t)\perp\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t)}

means that

\gamma^{\prime\prime}
is perpendicular to the two basis vectors in
\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t)}
; that is,

\begin{cases}\langle\gamma^{\prime\prime},s_{x}\rangle=0,\\
\langle\gamma^{\prime\prime},s_{y}\rangle=0,\end{cases}

where
s(x,y)\mathrel{:=}(x,y,f(x,y))
,
x=x(t)
, and
y=y(t)
.
Observe that

s_{x}=(1,0,f_{x})

and

s_{y}=(0,1,f_{y})
.
Since
\gamma^{\prime\prime}=\penalty(x^{\prime\prime},y^{\prime\prime},z^{\prime\prime})
, we can rewrite our system as

\begin{cases}x^{\prime\prime}+f_{x}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}z^{\prime\prime}=0,\\
y^{\prime\prime}+f_{y}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}z^{\prime\prime}=0.\end{cases}

It remains to plug in
➊
for
z^{\prime\prime}
, combine the similar terms, and simplify.
∎

Proof of
15B
.

Let
z=f(x,y)
be a description of
\Sigma
in tangent-normal coordinates at
p
.
By Lemma
15B
, the condition
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t)\perp\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t)}
can be written as a system of second-order differential equations.
From
0H
and
0H
we get the existence and uniqueness of a geodesic
\gamma
in an interval
(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
for some
\varepsilon>0
.

Let us extend the geodesic
\gamma
to a maximal open interval
\mathbb{I}
.
Suppose there is another geodesic
\gamma_{1}
with the same initial data that is defined on a maximal open interval
\mathbb{I}_{1}
.
Suppose
\gamma_{1}
splits from
\gamma
at some
t_{0}>0
;
that is,
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma
coincides on the interval
[0,t_{0})
, but they are different on any interval
[0,t_{0}+\delta)
with
\delta>0
.
By continuity,
\gamma_{1}(t_{0})=\gamma(t_{0})
, and
\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(t_{0})=\gamma^{\prime}(t_{0})
.
Applying
0H
and
0H
again, we get that
\gamma_{1}
coincides with
\gamma
in a small neighborhood of
t_{0}
— a contradiction.

The same argument shows that
\gamma_{1}
cannot split from
\gamma
at
t_{0}<0
.
It follows that
\gamma_{1}=\gamma
;
in particular,
\mathbb{I}_{1}=\mathbb{I}
.

If
\Sigma
is the graph of a smooth function, then part
15B
follows from
0H
,
0H
, and the lemma.
In this case, the mapping

\text{\sc{w}}(p,\text{\sc{v}},t)\mathrel{:=}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}w(p,\text{\sc{v}},t)

is also smooth.
Note that
\text{\sc{w}}(p,\text{\sc{v}},t_{0})\in\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t_{0})}
is the velocity vector of the geodesic
\gamma\colon t\mapsto w(p,\text{\sc{v}},t)
at time
t_{0}
.

In the general case, suppose
w(p,{\text{\sc{v}}},b)
is defined for
b\geqslant 0
; that is, the geodesic
\gamma\colon t\mapsto w(p,{\text{\sc{v}}},t)
is defined on the interval
[0,b]
.
Then there exists a partition
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{n}=b
of the interval
[0,b]
such that each geodesic segment
\gamma|_{[t_{i-1},t_{i}]}
is covered by a chart defined by some tangent-normal coordinates.
Set
p_{i}=\gamma(t_{i})
and
\text{\sc{v}}_{i}=\gamma^{\prime}(t_{i})
, so
p_{0}=p
and
\text{\sc{v}}_{0}=\text{\sc{v}}
.
Since
\gamma|_{[t_{i-1},t_{i}]}
lies in a graph, by the previous reasoning, we conclude that for each
i
, the mappings

w
and
w
are defined and smooth in a neighborhood of the triples
(p_{i-1},\text{\sc{v}}_{i-1},t_{i}-t_{i-1})
.
Note that
p_{i}=w(p_{i-1},\text{\sc{v}}_{i-1},t_{i}-t_{i-1})
and
\text{\sc{v}}_{i}=\text{\sc{w}}(p_{i-1},\text{\sc{v}}_{i-1},t_{i}-t_{i-1})
for each
i
.
Since the composition of smooth mappings is smooth, the mapping
w
is smoothly defined in a neighborhood of the triple
(p,\text{\sc{v}},b)
.

The case
b\leqslant 0
is similar; so, we have obtained the general case in
15B
.

Assume
15B
does not hold;
that is, the maximal interval
\mathbb{I}
is a proper subset of
\mathbb{R}
.
Without loss of generality, we may assume that
b=\sup\mathbb{I}<\penalty\infty
.
(If not, switch the direction of
\gamma
.)

By
15A
,
|\gamma^{\prime}|
is constant; in particular,
t\mapsto\gamma(t)
is a uniformly continuous function.
Therefore, the limit point

q=\lim_{t\to b}\gamma(t)

is defined.
Since
\Sigma
is a proper surface,
q\in\Sigma
.

Applying the argument above in a tangent-normal coordinate chart at
q
, we conclude that
\gamma
can be extended as a geodesic beyond
q
.
Therefore,
\mathbb{I}
is not maximal — a contradiction.
∎

15.8. Exercise.

Let
\Sigma
be a smooth torus of revolution; that is,
a surface of revolution whose generatrix is a smooth closed curve.
Show that any closed geodesic on
\Sigma
is noncontractible.

(In other words, if
s\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\Sigma
is the natural bi-periodic parametrization of
\Sigma
, then
there is no closed curve
\gamma
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
s\circ\gamma
is a geodesic.)

C
Exponential map

Let
\Sigma
be a smooth surface and
p\in\Sigma
.
Given a tangent vector
{\text{\sc{v}}}\in\mathrm{T}_{p}
, consider a geodesic
\gamma_{\text{\sc{v}}}
in
\Sigma
that starts at
p
with initial velocity
v
;
that is,
\gamma(0)=p
and
\gamma^{\prime}(0)={\text{\sc{v}}}
.

Let us define the
exponential map
2
2

2

Explaining the reason for this term, would take us far away from the subject.

at
p
by

\exp_{p}\colon\text{\sc{v}}\mapsto\gamma_{\text{\sc{v}}}(1).

By
15B
, this map is smooth, and it is defined in a neighborhood of zero in the tangent plane
\mathrm{T}_{p}
;
moreover, if
\Sigma
is proper,
then
\exp_{p}
is defined on the entire plane
\mathrm{T}_{p}
.

The exponential map
is defined on the tangent plane (or an open subset of it), which is a smooth surface,
and its target is another smooth surface.
We can identify the plane
\mathrm{T}_{p}

with its tangent plane
\mathrm{T}_{0}\mathrm{T}_{p}
, so the differential
d_{0}(\exp_{p})\colon\text{\sc{v}}\mapsto D_{\text{\sc{v}}}\exp_{p}
maps
\mathrm{T}_{p}
to itself.
Furthermore, by Lemma
15A
, this differential is the identity map; that is,
d_{0}\exp_{p}(\text{\sc{v}})=\text{\sc{v}}
for any
\text{\sc{v}}\in\mathrm{T}_{p}
.

Summarizing, we get the following statement:

15.9. Observation.

Let
\Sigma
be a smooth surface and
p\in\Sigma
.
Then:

(a)

The exponential map
\exp_{p}
is smooth, and its domain
\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits(\exp_{p})
contains a neighborhood of the origin in
\mathrm{T}_{p}
.
Moreover, if
\Sigma
is proper, then
\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits(\exp_{p})=\mathrm{T}_{p}

(b)

The differential
d_{0}(\exp_{p})\colon\mathrm{T}_{p}\to\mathrm{T}_{p}
is the identity map.

It is easy to check that
\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits(\exp_{p})
is
star-shaped
in
\mathrm{T}_{p}
;
the latter means that if
\text{\sc{v}}\in\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits(\exp_{p})
, then
\lambda{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{v}}\in\mathop{\rm Dom}\nolimits(\exp_{p})
for any
0\leqslant\lambda\leqslant 1
.

D
Injectivity radius

The
injectivity radius
of
\Sigma
at
p
(briefly
\mathop{\rm inj}\nolimits(p)
) is defined as least upper bound on radii
r_{p}\geqslant 0
such that the exponential map
\exp_{p}
is defined on the open ball
B_{p}\mathrel{:=}B(0,r_{p})_{\mathrm{T}_{p}}
,
and the restriction
\exp_{p}|_{B_{p}}
is a smooth regular parametrization of a neighborhood of
p
in
\Sigma
.

15.10. Proposition.

Injectivity radius is positive at any point on a smooth surface
\Sigma
(without boundary).
Moreover, it is
locally bounded away from zero
;
that is, for any
p\in\Sigma
there is
\varepsilon>0
such that if
|{p}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}<\varepsilon
for some
q\in\Sigma
, then
\mathop{\rm inj}\nolimits(q)\geqslant\varepsilon
.

In fact, it is true that
the function
\mathop{\rm inj}\nolimits\colon\Sigma\to(0,\infty]
is continuous

[
37
, 5.4]
.
The proof of the proposition uses
15C
and the inverse function theorem (
0G
).

Proof.

Let
z=f(x,y)
be a local graph representation of
\Sigma
in tangent-normal coordinates at
p
.
In this case, the
(x,y)
-plane coincides with the tangent plane
\mathrm{T}_{p}
.

Denote by
h
the composition of
\exp_{p}
with the projection
(x,y,z)\mapsto\penalty(x,y)
.
By
15C
, the differential
d_{0}h
is the identity;
in other words, the Jacobian matrix of
h
at
0
is the identity.
Applying the inverse function theorem (
0G
) we get the first part of the proposition.

The proof of second part is similar, but more technical.

Denote by
h_{q}
the composition of
\exp_{q}
with the orthogonal projection
(x,y,z)\mapsto(x,y)
.
Consider the chart
s\colon(u,v)\mapsto\penalty(u,v,f(u,v))
.
Let

m\colon(u,v,a,b)\mapsto h_{q}(\text{\sc{v}}),

where
q=s(u,v)
and
\text{\sc{v}}=a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s_{u}+b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s_{v}
.
By
15B
,
m
is a smooth map defined in a neighborhood of
0
.
Passing to a smaller neighborhood of
0
, we can assume that first and second partial derivatives of
m
are bounded.
Form above, the Jacobian matrix of
(a,b)\mapsto\penalty m(0,0,a,b)
at
0
is the identity.
It follows that for small fixed
u
and
v

the Jacobian matrix of
(a,b)\mapsto\penalty m(u,v,a,b)
at
0
is close to identity.
In particular, we can apply the second part of the inverse function theorem (
0G
) to
the map
(a,b)\mapsto\penalty m(u,v,a,b)
for small fixed
u
and
v
, hence the result.
∎

The proof of the following statement will be indicated in
18C
.

15.11. Proposition.

Let
p
be a point on a smooth surface
\Sigma
(without boundary).
If
\exp_{p}
is injective in
B_{p}=B(0,r)_{\mathrm{T}_{p}}
, then the restriction
\exp_{p}|_{B_{p}}
is a diffeomorphism between
B_{p}
and its image in
\Sigma
.

In other words,
\mathop{\rm inj}\nolimits(p)
can be defined as the least upper bound on
r
such that
\exp_{p}
is injective in the ball
B(0,r)_{\mathrm{T}_{p}}
.

E
Shortest paths are geodesics

15.12. Proposition.

Let
\Sigma
be a smooth surface.
Then any shortest path
\gamma
in
\Sigma
parametrized proportional to its arc-length is a geodesic in
\Sigma
.
In particular,
\gamma
is a smooth curve.

A local converse also holds: any point
p
in
\Sigma
has a neighborhood
U
such that any geodesic that lies entirely in
U
is a shortest path.

In particular, a sufficiently short arc of any geodesic is a shortest path.
A geodesic that is also a shortest path is called
minimizing
.
As one can see from the following exercise, geodesics might fail to be minimizing.

15.13. Exercise.

Let
\Sigma
be the cylindrical surface described by the equation
x^{2}+\penalty y^{2}=1
.
Show that the helix
\gamma\colon[0,2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi]\to\Sigma
defined by
\gamma(t)\mathrel{:=}\penalty(\cos t,\sin t,t)

is a geodesic, but not a shortest path on
\Sigma
.

A formal proof of the proposition will be given in Section
18B
.
The following informal physical explanation might be sufficiently convincing.
In fact, if one assumes that
\gamma
is smooth, then it is easy to convert this explanation into a rigorous proof.

Informal explanation.

Let us think about a shortest path
\gamma
as a stable position of a stretched elastic thread that is forced to lie on a frictionless surface.
Since it is frictionless, the force density
\text{\sc{n}}=\text{\sc{n}}(t)
that keeps
\gamma
in the surface must be proportional to the normal vector to the surface at
\gamma(t)
.

Denote the tension by
\tau
the tension in the thread.
It has to be the same at all points;
otherwise, the thread would move back or forth, and it would not be stable.

We can assume that
\gamma
has unit speed;
in this case, the net force from tension along the arc
\gamma_{[t_{0},t_{1}]}
is
\tau{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(\gamma^{\prime}(t_{1})-\gamma^{\prime}(t_{0}))
.
Hence, the density of the net force from tension at
t_{0}
is

\displaystyle\text{\sc{f}}(t_{0})

\displaystyle=\lim_{t_{1}\to t_{0}}\tau{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\frac{\gamma^{\prime}(t_{1})-\gamma^{\prime}(t_{0})}{t_{1}-t_{0}}=

\displaystyle=\tau{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t_{0}).

By Newton’s second law of motion,

\text{\sc{f}}+\text{\sc{n}}=0
; hence
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t)\perp\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t)}\Sigma
.
∎

15.14. Corollary.

Let
p
be a point on a smooth surface
\Sigma
, and
r\leqslant\penalty\mathop{\rm inj}\nolimits(p)
.
Then the exponential map
\exp_{p}
defines a diffeomorphism from
B(0,r)_{\mathrm{T}_{p}}
to
B(p,r)_{\Sigma}
.

Proof.

By
15D
, the restriction of
\exp_{p}
to
B_{p}={B(0,r)_{\mathrm{T}_{p}}}
is a diffeomorphism to its image
\exp_{p}(B_{p})\subset\Sigma
.

Evidently,
B(p,r)_{\Sigma}\supset\exp_{p}(B_{p})
.
By
15E
,
B(p,r)_{\Sigma}\subset\exp_{p}(B_{p})
, hence the result.
∎

By the corollary, the restriction
\exp_{p}|_{B(0,r)_{\mathrm{T}_{p}}}
admits an inverse map called
logarithmic
;
it is denoted by

\log_{p}\colon B(p,r)_{\Sigma}\to B(0,r)_{\mathrm{T}_{p}}.

According to the proposition above, any shortest path parametrized by its arc-length is a smooth curve.
This observation helps to solve the following two exercises:

15.15. Exercise.

Show that if two shortest paths have two distinct common points
p
and
q
, then either
p
and
q
are the endpoints of both shortest paths, or both shortest paths share a common arc from
p
to
q
.

Show by an example that nonoverlapping geodesics can cross each other an arbitrary number of times.

15.16. Exercise.

Assume a smooth surface
\Sigma
is mirror-symmetric with respect to a plane
\Pi
.
Show that no shortest path in
\Sigma
can
cross

\Pi
more than once.

In other words, if you travel along а shortest path, then you change sides of
\Pi
at most once.

15.17. Advanced exercise.

Let
\Sigma
be a smooth closed strictly convex surface
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}

and
\gamma\colon[0,\ell]\to\penalty\Sigma
be a unit-speed minimizing geodesic.
Set
p=\penalty\gamma(0)
,
q=\gamma(\ell)
, and

p^{s}=\gamma(s)-s{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\gamma^{\prime}(s).

Show that for any
s\in(0,\ell)
,
one cannot see
q
from
p^{s}
;
that is, the line segment
[p^{s},q]
intersects
\Sigma
at a point distinct from
q
.

Show that the statement does not hold without assuming that
\gamma
is minimizing.

15.18. Exercise.

Let
\Sigma
be a smooth closed surface.
Suppose that for any
p,q\in\penalty\Sigma
the distance
|{p}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}
depends only on the distance

F
Liberman’s lemma

A version of the following lemma was used by Joseph Liberman
[
62
]
.

15.19. Lemma.

Let
f
be a smooth locally convex function defined on an open subset of the plane.
Suppose
t\mapsto\gamma(t)=\penalty(x(t),y(t),z(t))
is a unit-speed geodesic on the graph
z=f(x,y)
.
Then
t\mapsto z(t)
is a convex function; that is,
z^{\prime\prime}(t)\geqslant 0
for any
t
.

Proof.

Choose the orientation on the graph so that the unit normal vector
\nu
always points up;
that is,
\nu
has a positive
z
-coordinate at each point.
Let us use the shortcut
\nu(t)
for
\nu(\gamma(t))
.

Since
\gamma
is a geodesic, we have
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t)\perp\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t)}
,
or equivalently the acceleration
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t)
is proportional to
\nu(t)
for any
t
.
Furthermore,

\gamma^{\prime\prime}=k{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nu,

where
k=k(t)
is the normal curvature of the graph at
\gamma(t)
in the direction of
\gamma^{\prime}(t)
.

Therefore,

z^{\prime\prime}=k{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\theta,

➋

where
\theta=\theta(t)
denotes the angle between
\nu(t)
and the
z
-axis.

Since
\nu
points up, we have
\theta(t)<\tfrac{\pi}{2}
, or equivalently
\cos\theta>0
.

Since
f
is convex, the tangent plane supports the graph from below at any point;
in particular,
k(t)\geqslant 0
for any
t
.
It follows that the right-hand side in
➋
is nonnegative;
whence the statement follows.
∎

Let
\Sigma
be the graph of a locally convex function defined on an open subset of the plane.
Show that
\Sigma
has no closed geodesics.

.

for any

G
Total curvature of geodesics

Recall that
\Phi({\gamma})
denotes the total curvature of the curve
\gamma
;
see
3E
.

15.22. Exercise.

Let
\gamma

.

.

15.23. Theorem.

Assume
\Sigma
is the graph of a convex
\ell
-Lipschitz function
f
defined on an open set in the
(x,y)
-plane.
Then the total curvature of any geodesic in
\Sigma
is at most
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell
.

This theorem was first proved by Vladimir Usov
[
96
]
.

Proof.

Let
t\mapsto\gamma(t)=(x(t),y(t),z(t))
be a unit-speed geodesic on
\Sigma
.
According to Liberman’s lemma (
15F
), the function

t\mapsto z(t)
is convex.

Since the slope of
f
is at most
\ell
, we have

|z^{\prime}(t)|\leqslant\frac{\ell}{\sqrt{1+\ell^{2}}}

for any
t
.
We can assume that
\gamma
is defined on the interval
[a,b]
.
Then

\displaystyle\int_{a}^{b}z^{\prime\prime}(t)dt

\displaystyle=z^{\prime}(b)-z^{\prime}(a)\leqslant 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\frac{\ell}{\sqrt{1+\ell^{2}}}.

➌

Also, note that
z^{\prime\prime}
is the projection of
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
to the
z
-axis.
The slope of the tangent plane
\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t)}\Sigma
cannot be greater than
\ell
for any
t
.
Because
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
is perpendicular to that plane, we have that

|\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t)|\leqslant z^{\prime\prime}(t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sqrt{1+\ell^{2}}.

By
➌
, we get that

\displaystyle\Phi({\gamma})

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{b}|\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t)|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt\leqslant

\displaystyle\leqslant\sqrt{1+\ell^{2}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\int_{a}^{b}z^{\prime\prime}(t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt\leqslant

\displaystyle\leqslant 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell.

∎

By the following exercise, the estimate in the theorem is optimal.

15.24. Exercise.

Let
\Sigma
be the graph

with the origin removed.
Show that any both-side-infinite geodesic
\gamma
in
\Sigma
has total curvature exactly
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell
.

Assume
f

on the graph

-Lipschitz function defined on the plane

15.26. Theorem.

Suppose a smooth surface
\Sigma
bounds a convex set
K
.
Assume
B(0,\varepsilon)\subset K\subset B(0,1)
.
Then the total curvatures of any shortest path in
\Sigma
can be bounded in terms of
\varepsilon
.

15.27. Proof-guided exercise.

Let
\Sigma
be as in the theorem, and
\gamma
be a unit-speed shortest path in
\Sigma
.
Denote by
\nu(t)
the unit normal vector at
\gamma(t)
that points outside
\Sigma
;

the angle between
\nu(t)
and the direction from the origin to
\gamma(t)
.

\rho(t)=\penalty|\gamma(t)|^{2}
; denote by

the curvature of

at

.

(a)

\cos[\theta(t)]\geqslant\varepsilon
for any

.

(b)

for any

.

(c)

Show that

\rho^{\prime\prime}(t)=2-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}k(t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\theta(t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|\gamma(t)|

for any
t
.

(d)

Use the nearest-point projection from the unit sphere to
\Sigma
to show that

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma\leqslant\pi.

(e)

Conclude that
\Phi({\gamma})\leqslant 100/\varepsilon^{2}
.

Remark.

The obtained estimate tends to infinity as
\varepsilon\to 0
,
but there is also an estimate that does not depend on
\varepsilon
;
this is a result by Nina Lebedeva and the first author
[
59
]
.
Aleksei Pogorelov proposed the hypothesis that there exists a bound on the length of the spherical image of a shortest path
[
81
]
.
According to
15G
, this hypothesis is stronger,
but counterexamples have been found for all of its possible variations
[
98
,
67
,
96
,
72
]
.

Chapter 16
Parallel transport

A
Parallel tangent fields

Let
\Sigma
be a smooth surface and
\gamma\colon[a,b]\to\penalty\Sigma
be a smooth curve.
A smooth vector-valued function
t\mapsto{\text{\sc{v}}}(t)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
is called a
tangent field
along
\gamma
if
the vector
{\text{\sc{v}}}(t)
lies in the tangent plane
\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t)}\Sigma
for each
t
.

A tangent field
v
on
\gamma
is called
parallel
if
{\text{\sc{v}}}^{\prime}(t)\perp\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t)}
for any
t
.

In general, the family of tangent planes
\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t)}\Sigma
is not parallel.
Therefore, one cannot expect to have a
truly
parallel field with
{\text{\sc{v}}}^{\prime}(t)\equiv 0
.
The condition
{\text{\sc{v}}}^{\prime}(t)\perp\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t)}
means that the family is
as parallel as possible
— it rotates together with the tangent plane but does not rotate inside the plane.

By the definition of geodesic, the velocity field
{\text{\sc{v}}}(t)=\penalty\gamma^{\prime}(t)
of any geodesic
\gamma
is parallel on
\gamma
.

16.1. Exercise.

Let
\Sigma
be a smooth surface and let

\gamma\colon[a,b]\to\Sigma
be a smooth curve.
Suppose
{\text{\sc{v}}}(t)
,
\text{\sc{w}}(t)
are parallel vector fields along
\gamma
.

(a)

Show that
|{\text{\sc{v}}}(t)|
is constant.

(b)

Show that the angle
\theta(t)=\measuredangle({\text{\sc{v}}}(t),\text{\sc{w}}(t))
is constant.

B
Parallel transport

16.2. Proposition-Definition.

Let
\gamma\colon[a,b]\to\penalty\Sigma
be a piecewise smooth curve on a smooth surface
\Sigma
.
Assume
p=\gamma(a)
and
q=\gamma(b)
.

Given a tangent vector
{\text{\sc{w}}}\in\mathrm{T}_{p}
there is a unique parallel field
{\text{\sc{w}}}(t)
along
\gamma
such that
{\text{\sc{w}}}(a)={\text{\sc{w}}}
.

The vector
{\text{\sc{w}}}(b)\in\mathrm{T}_{q}
is called the
parallel transport
of
{\text{\sc{w}}}(a)
along
\gamma
in
\Sigma
.

The parallel transport along
\gamma
will be denoted by
\iota_{\gamma}
;
so we can write
\text{\sc{w}}(b)=\iota_{\gamma}({\text{\sc{w}}}(a))
or
\text{\sc{w}}(b)=\iota_{\gamma}({\text{\sc{w}}}(a))_{\Sigma}
if we need to emphasize that
\gamma
lies on the surface
\Sigma
.
From Exercise
16A
, it follows that parallel transport
\iota_{\gamma}\colon\mathrm{T}_{p}\to\penalty\mathrm{T}_{q}
is an isometry.
In general, the parallel transport
\iota_{\gamma}\colon\mathrm{T}_{p}\to\penalty\mathrm{T}_{q}
depends on the choice of
\gamma
; that is, for another curve
\gamma_{1}
connecting
p
to
q
in
\Sigma
, the parallel transports
\iota_{\gamma_{1}}
and
\iota_{\gamma}
might be different.

Sketch of proof.

Assume
\gamma
is smooth and lies in a local chart
(u,v)\mapsto s(u,v)
of
\Sigma
;
so
\gamma(t)=\penalty s(u(t),v(t))
for smooth functions
t\mapsto u(t)
and
t\mapsto v(t)
.
Set

\text{\sc{u}}(t)=s_{u}(u(t),v(t)),\quad\text{\sc{v}}(t)=s_{v}(u(t),v(t)),\quad\text{and}\quad\nu(t)=\nu(\gamma(t)).

The conditions
\text{\sc{w}}(t)\in\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t)}
and
\text{\sc{w}}^{\prime}(t)\perp\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t)}
can be written as a system of equations:

\begin{cases}\ \langle\text{\sc{w}}(t),\nu(t)\rangle=0,\\
\ \langle\text{\sc{w}}^{\prime}(t),\text{\sc{u}}(t)\rangle=0,\\
\ \langle\text{\sc{w}}^{\prime}(t),\text{\sc{v}}(t)\rangle=0.\end{cases}

Rewriting this system in components of
u
,
v
,
w
, and
\nu
produces a system of ordinary differential equations on the components of
w
.
Applying
0H
, we get a solution
\text{\sc{w}}(t)
.
By
16A
,
|\text{\sc{w}}|
is constant;
therefore
0H
implies that
w
is defined on the whole interval
[a,b]
.

If
\gamma
is only piecewise smooth or not covered by one chart we can subdivide it into smooth arcs
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}
such that each
\gamma_{i}
lies in one chart.
Applying the statement consecutively to each
\gamma_{i}
proves it for
\gamma
.
∎

Suppose
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are two smooth curves in two smooth surfaces
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
.
Denote by
\nu_{i}\colon\Sigma_{i}\to\mathbb{S}^{2}
the Gauss maps of
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
.
If
\nu_{1}\circ\gamma_{1}(t)=\nu_{2}\circ\gamma_{2}(t)
for any
t
, then we say that the curves
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
have
identical normals
in
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
, respectively.

In this case, the tangent plane
\mathrm{T}_{\gamma_{1}(t)}\Sigma_{1}
is parallel to
\mathrm{T}_{\gamma_{2}(t)}\Sigma_{2}
for any
t
.
Therefore we can identify
\mathrm{T}_{\gamma_{1}(t)}\Sigma_{1}
with
\mathrm{T}_{\gamma_{2}(t)}\Sigma_{2}
.
In particular, if
\text{\sc{v}}(t)
is a tangent vector field along
\gamma_{1}
,
then it is also a tangent vector field along
\gamma_{2}
.
Moreover,
\text{\sc{v}}^{\prime}(t)\perp\mathrm{T}_{\gamma_{1}(t)}\Sigma_{1}
is equivalent to
\text{\sc{v}}^{\prime}(t)\perp\mathrm{T}_{\gamma_{2}(t)}\Sigma_{2}
; that is, if
\text{\sc{v}}(t)
is parallel along
\gamma_{1}
,
then it is also parallel along
\gamma_{2}
.

The discussion above leads to the following observation that will play a key role in the sequel.

16.3. Observation.

Let
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
be two smooth curves in two smooth surfaces
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
.
Suppose
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
have identical normals (as the curves in
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
, respectively).
Then the parallel transports
\iota_{\gamma_{1}}
and
\iota_{\gamma_{2}}
are identical.

16.4. Exercise.

Let
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
be two surfaces with a common curve
\gamma
.
Suppose
\Sigma_{1}
bounds a region that contains
\Sigma_{2}
.
Show that the parallel translation along
\gamma
in
\Sigma_{1}

coincides with the parallel translation along
\gamma
in
\Sigma_{2}
.

C
Bike wheel and projections

Here we give two interpretations of parallel transport;
they might help to build the right intuition, but will not help in writing rigorous proofs.
The first is an experiment with a bike wheel suggested by Mark Levi
[
61
]
.
The second one uses orthogonal projections of tangent planes.

Suppose
\gamma\colon[a,b]\to\Sigma
is a smooth arc in a smooth surface
\Sigma
.
Think of walking along
\gamma
and carrying a perfectly balanced bike wheel so that you keep the wheel’s axis normal to
\Sigma
and touch only its axis.
If the wheel is not spinning at the starting point
p=\gamma(a)
, then it will not be spinning after stopping at
q=\gamma(b)
.
Indeed, by pushing the axis one cannot produce torque to spin the wheel.
Then the map that sends the initial position of the wheel to the final position is the parallel transport
\iota_{\gamma}
.

By the way, physical intuition should tell you that
moving the axis of the wheel without changing its direction does not change the direction of the wheel’s spokes
;
this is essentially the statement of Observation
16B
.

On a more formal level, one can choose a partition
a=t_{0}<\dots<\penalty t_{n}=b
of
[a,b]

and consider the sequence of orthogonal projections
\varphi_{i}\colon\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t_{i-1})}\to\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t_{i})}
.
For a fine partition, the composition

\varphi_{n}\circ\dots\circ\varphi_{1}\colon\mathrm{T}_{p}\to\penalty\mathrm{T}_{q}

gives an approximation of
\iota_{\gamma}
.

Each
\varphi_{i}
does not increase the magnitude of a vector and neither the composition.
It is straightforward to see that if the partition is sufficiently fine, then the above composition is almost an isometry.
Therefore, the limit
\iota_{\gamma}
is an isometry
\mathrm{T}_{p}\to\penalty\mathrm{T}_{q}
.

16.5. Exercise.

Construct a loop
\gamma
in
\mathbb{S}^{2}
such that the parallel transport
\iota_{\gamma}
is not the identity map.

D
Total geodesic curvature

Recall that geodesic curvature is defined in Section
11A
.
It is denoted by
k_{g}
, and it measures how much a curve
\gamma
on an oriented surface diverges from being a geodesic;
it is positive where
\gamma
turns left and negative where
\gamma
turns right.
In particular, geodesics have vanishing geodesic curvature.

Suppose
\Sigma
is a smooth oriented surface, and
\gamma\colon\mathbb{I}\to\Sigma
is a smooth unit-speed curve.
The total geodesic curvature of
\gamma
is defined by the integral

\Psi({\gamma})\mathrel{:=}\int_{\mathbb{I}}k_{g}(t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt.

If
\Sigma
is a plane, then the geodesic curvature of
\gamma
coincides
with its signed curvature (see Section
6C
).
In particular, its total geodesic curvature is equal to its total signed curvature.
For that reason, we use the same notation
\Psi({\gamma})
; if we need to emphasize that we consider
\gamma
as a curve in
\Sigma
, we write
\Psi({\gamma})_{\Sigma}
.

If
\gamma
is a piecewise smooth curve in
\Sigma
, then
its
total geodesic curvature
is defined as the sum of all total geodesic curvatures of its arcs plus the sum of the signed exterior angles of
\gamma
at the joints.
More precisely, if
\gamma
is a concatenation of smooth curves
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}
, then

\Psi({\gamma})\mathrel{:=}\Psi({\gamma_{1}})+\dots+\Psi({\gamma_{n}})+\theta_{1}+\dots+\theta_{n-1},

where
\theta_{i}
is the signed external angle at the joint between
\gamma_{i}
and
\gamma_{i+1}
;
it is positive if
\gamma
turns left and negative if
\gamma
turns right, it is undefined if
\gamma
turns to the opposite direction.
If
\gamma
is closed, then

\Psi({\gamma})\mathrel{:=}\Psi({\gamma_{1}})+\dots+\Psi({\gamma_{n}})+\theta_{1}+\dots+\theta_{n},

where
\theta_{n}
is the signed external angle at the joint
\gamma_{n}
and
\gamma_{1}
.

If each arc
\gamma_{i}
in the concatenation is a minimizing geodesic, then
\gamma
is called a
broken geodesic
.
In this case,
\Psi({\gamma_{i}})=0
for each
i
.
Therefore the total geodesic curvature of
\gamma
is the sum of its signed external angles.

16.6. Proposition.

Assume
\gamma
is a closed broken geodesic in a smooth oriented surface
\Sigma
that starts and ends at the point
p
.
Then the parallel transport
\iota_{\gamma}\colon\mathrm{T}_{p}\to\mathrm{T}_{p}
is a clockwise rotation of the plane
\mathrm{T}_{p}
by the angle
\Psi({\gamma})
.

Moreover, the same statement holds true for smooth closed curves and piecewise smooth curves.

Proof.

Assume
\gamma
is a cyclic concatenation of geodesics
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}
.
Fix a tangent vector
v
at
p
, and extend it to a parallel vector field along
\gamma
.
Since
\text{\sc{t}}_{i}(t)=\gamma_{i}^{\prime}(t)
is parallel along
\gamma_{i}
, the oriented angle
\varphi_{i}
from
\text{\sc{t}}_{i}
to
v
stays constant along each
\gamma_{i}
.

Suppose
\theta_{i}
denotes the external angle at the joint between
\gamma_{i}
and
\gamma_{i+1}
.
Then

\varphi_{i+1}=\varphi_{i}-\theta_{i}\pmod{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi}.

Therefore, after going over all vertices we get that

\varphi_{n+1}-\varphi_{1}=-\theta_{1}-\dots-\theta_{n}=-\Psi({\gamma})\pmod{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi}.

Hence, the first statement follows.

For a smooth unit-speed curve
\gamma\colon[a,b]\to\Sigma
, the proof is analogous.
Denote by
\varphi(t)
is the signed angle from
\text{\sc{t}}(t)
to
{\text{\sc{v}}}(t)
.
Let us show that

\varphi^{\prime}(t)+k_{g}(t)\equiv 0

➊

Recall that
\mu=\mu(t)
denotes the counterclockwise rotation of
\text{\sc{t}}=\penalty\text{\sc{t}}(t)
by angle
\tfrac{\pi}{2}
in
\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t)}
.
Denote by
\text{\sc{w}}=\text{\sc{w}}(t)
the counterclockwise rotation of
\text{\sc{v}}=\text{\sc{v}}(t)
by angle
\tfrac{\pi}{2}
in
\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t)}
.
Then

t

\displaystyle=\cos\varphi{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{v}}-\sin\varphi{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{w}},

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle=\sin\varphi{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{v}}+\cos\varphi{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{w}}.

The vector fields
v
and
w
are parallel along
\gamma
; that is,
\text{\sc{v}}^{\prime}(t),\text{\sc{w}}^{\prime}(t)\perp\penalty\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t)}
.
Therefore,
\langle\text{\sc{v}}^{\prime},\mu\rangle=\penalty\langle\text{\sc{w}}^{\prime},\mu\rangle=\penalty 0
.
It follows that

\displaystyle k_{g}

\displaystyle=\langle\text{\sc{t}}^{\prime},\mu\rangle=

\displaystyle=-(\sin^{2}\varphi+\cos^{2}\varphi){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varphi^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-\varphi^{\prime}.

It proves
➊
.

By
➊
we get that

\displaystyle\varphi(b)-\varphi(a)

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{b}\varphi^{\prime}(t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt=

\displaystyle=-\int_{a}^{b}k_{g}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt=

\displaystyle=-\Psi({\gamma})

In the case when
\gamma
is a piecewise smooth curve, the result follows from a straightforward combination of the above two cases.
∎

Chapter 17
Gauss–Bonnet formula

A
Formulation

The following theorem was proved by Carl Friedrich Gauss
[
35
]

for geodesic triangles;
Pierre Bonnet and Jacques Binet independently
generalized the statement for arbitrary curves.

17.1. Theorem.

Let
\Delta
be a topological disc in a smooth oriented surface
\Sigma
bounded by a piecewise smooth curve
\partial\Delta
.
Suppose
\partial\Delta
is oriented so that
\Delta
lies on its left.
Then

\Psi({\partial\Delta})+\iint_{\Delta}K=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi,

➊

where
K
denotes the Gauss curvature.
