In this chapter,
we give an informal proof of this formula in a leading special case.
A formal computational proof will be given in Section
18E
.
Before going into the proofs, we suggest solving the following exercises using the Gauss–Bonnet formula.

17.2. Exercise.

Assume
\gamma
is a simple closed curve with constant geodesic curvature
1
in a smooth closed surface
\Sigma
with positive Gauss curvature.
Show that
\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma<2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
;
that is,
\gamma
is shorter than the unit circle.

17.3. Exercise.

Suppose that a disc
\Delta
lies in a graph
z=f(x,y)
of a smooth function,
the boundary of
\Delta
lies in the
(x,y)
-plane
and
\Delta
meets this plane at fix angle
\alpha
.
Show that
\iint_{\Delta}K=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(1-\cos\alpha)
.

17.4. Exercise.

Let
\gamma
be a simple closed geodesic on a smooth closed surface
\Sigma
with positive Gauss curvature.
Denote by
\nu
the spherical map on
\Sigma
.
Show that the curve
\alpha=\nu\circ\gamma
divides the sphere into two regions of equal area.

Conclude that
\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\alpha\geqslant 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi.

17.5. Exercise.

Let
\gamma
be a closed geodesic possibly with self-intersections on a smooth closed surface
\Sigma
with positive Gauss curvature.
Suppose
R
is one of the regions that
\gamma
cuts from
\Sigma
.
Show that
\iint_{R}K\leqslant 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
.

Conclude that any two closed geodesics on
\Sigma
have a common point.

17.6. Exercise.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed smooth surface with positive Gauss curvature.
Show that in a single coordinate chart, a closed geodesic in
\Sigma
cannot look like one of the curves on the following pictures.

(easy)

(tricky)

(hopeless)

The following exercise optimizes
15G
.

17.7. Exercise.

Suppose
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is a
\sqrt{3}
-Lipschitz smooth convex function.
Show that any geodesic in the graph
z=f(x,y)
has no self-intersections.

A surface
\Sigma
is called
simply-connected
if any simple closed curve in
\Sigma
bounds a disc.
Equivalently, any closed curve in
\Sigma
can be continuously
deformed into a
trivial curve
; that is, a curve that stands at one point all the time.

Planes and spheres are examples of simply-connected surfaces;
tori and the cylinders are not simply-connected.

17.8. Exercise.

Suppose
\Sigma
is a simply-connected open surface with nonpositive Gauss curvature.

(a)

Show that any two points in
\Sigma
are connected by a unique geodesic.
In particular, geodesics in
\Sigma
do not have self-intersections.

(b)

Conclude that for any point
p\in\Sigma
,
the exponential map
\exp_{p}
is a diffeomorphism from the tangent plane
\mathrm{T}_{p}
to
\Sigma
.
In particular,
\Sigma
is diffeomorphic to the plane.

B
Additivity

Let
\Delta
be a topological disc in a smooth oriented surface
\Sigma
bounded by a simple piecewise smooth curve
\partial\Delta
.
As before we suppose
\partial\Delta
is oriented in such a way that
\Delta
lies on its left.
Set

\mathop{\rm GB}\nolimits(\Delta)\mathrel{:=}\Psi({\partial\Delta})+\iint_{\Delta}K-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi,

➋

where
K
denotes the Gauss curvature.
The Gauss–Bonnet formula can be written as

\mathop{\rm GB}\nolimits(\Delta)=0,

and
\mathop{\rm GB}\nolimits
stands for Gauss–Bonnet.

17.9. Lemma.

Suppose the disc
\Delta
is subdivided into two discs
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
by a curve
\delta
.
Then

\mathop{\rm GB}\nolimits(\Delta)=\mathop{\rm GB}\nolimits(\Delta_{1})+\mathop{\rm GB}\nolimits(\Delta_{2}).

Proof.

Let us subdivide
\partial\Delta
into two curves
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
that share endpoints with
\delta
, so that
\Delta_{i}
is bounded by the arc
\gamma_{i}
and
\delta
for
i=1,2
.

Denote by
\varphi_{1}
,
\varphi_{2}
,
\psi_{1}
, and
\psi_{2}
the angles between
\delta
and
\gamma_{i}
marked on the picture.
Suppose the arcs
\gamma_{1}
,
\gamma_{2}
, and
\delta
are oriented as on the picture.
Then

\displaystyle\Psi({\partial\Delta})

\displaystyle=\Psi({\gamma_{1}})-\Psi({\gamma_{2}})+(\pi-\varphi_{1}-\varphi_{2})+(\pi-\psi_{1}-\psi_{2}),

\displaystyle\Psi({\partial\Delta_{1}})

\displaystyle=\Psi({\gamma_{1}})-\Psi({\delta})+(\pi-\varphi_{1})+(\pi-\psi_{1}),

\displaystyle\Psi({\partial\Delta_{2}})

\displaystyle=\Psi({\delta})-\Psi({\gamma_{2}})+(\pi-\varphi_{2})+(\pi-\psi_{2}),

\displaystyle\iint_{\Delta}K

\displaystyle=\iint_{\Delta_{1}}K+\iint_{\Delta_{2}}K.

It remains to plug in the results in the formulas for
\mathop{\rm GB}\nolimits(\Delta)
,
\mathop{\rm GB}\nolimits(\Delta_{1})
, and
\mathop{\rm GB}\nolimits(\Delta_{2})
.
∎

C
Spherical case

If
\Sigma
is a plane, then its Gauss curvature vanishes;
therefore the Gauss–Bonnet formula
➊
can be written as

\Psi({\partial\Delta})=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi,

and it follows from
6C
.
In other words,
\mathop{\rm GB}\nolimits(\Delta)=0
for any plane disc
\Delta
with piecewise smooth boundary.

If
\Sigma
is the unit sphere, then
K\equiv 1
;
in this case, Theorem
17A
can be formulated in the following way:

17.10. Proposition.

Let
P
be a spherical polygon bounded by a simple closed broken geodesic
\partial P
.
Assume
\partial P
is oriented such that
P
lies on the left of
\partial P
.
Then

\mathop{\rm GB}\nolimits(P)=\Psi({\partial P})+\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits P-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi=0.

Moreover, the same formula holds for any spherical region bounded by a piecewise smooth simple closed curve.

This proposition will be used in the next section.

Sketch of proof.

Suppose a spherical triangle
\Delta
has angles

\alpha
,
\beta
, and
\gamma
.
According to
0C
,

\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits\Delta=\alpha+\beta+\gamma-\pi.

Recall that
\partial\Delta
is oriented so that
\Delta
lies on its left.
Then its oriented external angles are
\pi-\alpha
,
\pi-\beta
, and
\pi-\gamma
.
Therefore,

\Psi({\partial\Delta})=3{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi-\alpha-\beta-\gamma.

It follows that
\Psi({\partial\Delta})+\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits\Delta=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
or, equivalently,
\mathop{\rm GB}\nolimits(\Delta)=0
.

We can subdivide a given spherical polygon
P
into triangles by cutting a polygon in two along a broken geodesic on each step.
By the additivity lemma (
17B
),

\mathop{\rm GB}\nolimits(P)=0

for any spherical polygon
P
.

The second statement can be proved by approximation.
One has to show that the total geodesic curvature of
a piecewise smooth simple curve can be approximated by
the total geodesic curvature of inscribed broken geodesics.
We omit the detailed proof,
but it close to
4F
.
∎

17.11. Exercise.

Let
\gamma
be a simple piecewise smooth loop on on the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}
.
Assume
\gamma
divides
\mathbb{S}^{2}
in two regions of equal area.
Denote by
p
the base point of
\gamma
.
Show that the parallel transport
\iota_{\gamma}\colon\mathrm{T}_{p}\mathbb{S}^{2}\to\mathrm{T}_{p}\mathbb{S}^{2}
is the identity map.

D
Intuitive proof

In this section, we prove a special case of the Gauss–Bonnet formula.
This case is leading — the general case can be proved similarly, using the signed area counted with multiplicity, but will do something else
18E
.

Proof of
17A
for open and closed surfaces with positive Gauss curvature.

Let
\nu\colon\Sigma\to\mathbb{S}^{2}
be the spherical map.
By
10D
, we have

\mathop{\rm GB}\nolimits(\Delta)=\Psi({\partial\Delta})+\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits[\nu(\Delta)]-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi.

➌

Choose a loop
\alpha
that runs along
\partial\Delta
so that
\Delta
lies on the left from it; suppose that
p\in\partial\Delta
is its base.
Consider the parallel translation
\iota_{\alpha}\colon\mathrm{T}_{p}\to\mathrm{T}_{p}
along
\alpha
.
According to
16D
,
\iota_{\alpha}
is the clockwise rotation by angle
\Psi({\alpha})_{\Sigma}
.

Set
\beta=\nu\circ\alpha
.
By
16B
, we have
\iota_{\alpha}=\iota_{\beta}
, where
\beta
is considered as a curve in the unit sphere.

Further,
\iota_{\beta}
is a clockwise rotation by angle
\Psi({\beta})_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}
.
By
17C

\mathop{\rm GB}\nolimits(\nu(\Delta))=\Psi({\beta})_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}+\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits[\nu(\Delta)]-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi=0.

Therefore,

\iota_{\beta}
is a counterclockwise rotation by
\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits[\nu(\Delta)]

Summarizing, the clockwise rotation by
\Psi({\alpha})_{\Sigma}
is identical to a counterclockwise rotation by
\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits[\nu(\Delta)]
.
The rotations are identical if the angles are equal modulo
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm GB}\nolimits(\Delta)

\displaystyle=\Psi({\partial\Delta})_{\Sigma}+\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits[\nu(\Delta)]-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}n{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi

➍

for an integer
n
.

It remains to show that
n=0
.
By
6C
, this is so for a topological disc in a plane.
One can think of a general disc
\Delta
as the result of a continuous deformation of a plane disc.
The integer
n
cannot change during the process since the left hand side in
➍
is continuous along the deformation; whence
n=0
for the result of the deformation.
∎

E
Simple geodesic

The following theorem provides an interesting application of the Gauss–Bonnet formula; it is proved by Stephan Cohn-Vossen
[
23
, Satz 9]
.

17.12. Theorem.

Any open smooth surface with positive Gauss curvature has a simple two-sided infinite geodesic.

Proof.

Let
\Sigma
be an open surface with positive Gauss curvature and
\gamma
a two-sided infinite geodesic in
\Sigma
.

If
\gamma
has a self-intersection, then it contains a simple loop;
that is, for some closed interval
[a,b]
,
the restriction
\ell=\gamma|_{[a,b]}
is a simple loop.

By
12D
,
\Sigma
is parametrized by an open convex region
\Omega
in the plane.
By Jordan’s theorem (
0B
),
\ell
bounds a topological disc in
\Sigma
; denote it by
\Delta
.
If
\varphi
is the internal angle at the base of the loop, then by the Gauss–Bonnet formula,

\iint_{\Delta}K=\pi+\varphi.

Recall that

\iint_{\Sigma}K\leqslant 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi;

➎

see
12D
.
Therefore,
0<\varphi<\pi
; that is,
\gamma
has no concave simple loops.

Assume
\gamma
has two simple loops, say
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
that bound discs
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
, respectively.
Then the discs
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
have to overlap;
otherwise, the curvature of
\Sigma
would exceed
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
contradicting
➎
.

It follows that after leaving
\Delta_{1}
, the geodesic
\gamma
has to enter it again before creating another simple loop.

Consider the moment when
\gamma
enters
\Delta_{1}
again;
two possible scenarios are shown in the picture.
On the left picture, we get two nonoverlapping discs which, as we know, is impossible.
The right picture is impossible as well — in this case, we get a concave simple loop.

It follows that
\gamma
contains only one simple loop.
This loop cuts a disc from
\Sigma

and goes around it either clockwise or counterclockwise.
This way we divide all the self-intersecting geodesics on
\Sigma

into two types which we call
clockwise
and
counterclockwise
.

The geodesic
t\mapsto\gamma(t)
is clockwise
if and only if the same geodesic traveled backwards

t\mapsto\gamma(-t)

is counterclockwise.
Let us shoot a unit-speed geodesic in each direction from a given point
p=\gamma(0)
.
This gives a one-parameter family of geodesics
\gamma_{s}
for
s\in[0,\pi]
connecting the geodesic
t\mapsto\gamma(t)
with
t\mapsto\gamma(-t)
; that is,
\gamma_{0}(t)=\penalty\gamma(t)
, and
\gamma_{\pi}(t)=\gamma(-t)
.

The subset of values
s\in[0,\pi]
such that
\gamma_{s}
is right (or left) is open in
[0,\pi]
.
That is, if
\gamma_{s}
is right, then so is
\gamma_{t}
for all
t
sufficiently close to
s
.
1
1

1

Informally speaking, this means that self-intersection cannot suddenly disappear. Try to convince yourself of this.

Since
[0,\pi]
is connected, it cannot be subdivided into two open sets.
It follows that for some
s
, the geodesic
\gamma_{s}
is neither clockwise nor counterclockwise;
that is,
\gamma_{s}
has no self-intersections.
∎

17.13. Exercise.

Let
\Sigma
be an open smooth surface with positive Gauss curvature.
Suppose
\alpha\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\Sigma
is a smooth loop such that
\alpha^{\prime}(0)+\alpha^{\prime}(1)=0
.
Show that there is a simple two-sided infinite geodesic
\gamma
that is tangent to
\alpha
.

F
General domains

The following generalization of the Gauss–Bonnet formula is due to Walther von Dyck
[
27
]
.

17.14. Theorem.

Let
\Lambda
be a compact domain on a smooth surface.
Assume
\Lambda
is bounded by a finite (possibly empty) collection of closed piecewise smooth curves
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}
.
Suppose that each
\gamma_{i}

is oriented in such a way that
\Lambda
lies on its left.
Then

\iint_{\Lambda}K=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\chi-\Psi({\gamma_{1}})-\dots-\Psi({\gamma_{n}})

➏

for an integer
\chi=\chi(\Lambda)
.

Moreover, if a graph with
v
vertices and
e
edges embedded in
\Lambda
subdivides it into
f
discs and contains all
\gamma_{i}
, then
\chi=v-e+f
.

The number
\chi=\chi(\Lambda)
is called the
Euler characteristic
of
\Lambda
.
The value
\chi
does not depend on the choice of the subdivision since the remaining terms in
➏
do not.
Formula
➏
is deduced from the standard Gauss–Bonnet (
17A
).
The argument is similar to
17B
, but the combinatorics gets trickier.

Before reading the proof, try to modify the argument in
17B
to derive the formula for the subdivision of the annulus
A
on the picture;
its graph has 4 vertices and 6 edges (one of them is a loop) and it subdivides
A
into two discs
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
.
Therefore,
\chi(A)=4-6+2=0
and

\iint_{A}K=-\Psi({\gamma_{1}})-\Psi({\gamma_{2}}).

Proof.

Suppose that a graph with
v
vertices and
e
edges subdivides
\Lambda
into
f
discs
\Delta_{1},\dots,\Delta_{f}
.
Lets us apply the Gauss–Bonnet formula for each disc, and sum up the results:

\displaystyle\iint_{\Lambda}K

\displaystyle=\iint_{\Delta_{1}}K+\dots+\iint_{\Delta_{f}}K=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi-\Psi({\partial\Delta_{1}})-\dots-\Psi({\partial\Delta_{f}}).

It remains to show that

\Psi({\gamma_{1}})+\dots+\Psi({\gamma_{n}})-\Psi({\partial\Delta_{1}})-\dots-\Psi({\partial\Delta_{f}})=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(v-e).

➐

To prove this identity, we compute the left-hand side adding up the contributions of each edge and each vertex separately.

Choose an edge
\sigma
of the graph.
If
\sigma
is not part of some
\gamma_{i}
,
then it appears twice in the boundary of the discs, say in
\partial\Delta_{i}
and
\partial\Delta_{j}
.
In the latter case, we may assume that
\Delta_{i}
lies on the left from
\sigma
and
\Delta_{j}
is on its right, so

\sigma
contributes
\Psi({\sigma})
to
\Psi({\partial\Delta_{i}})
and
-\Psi({\sigma})
to
\Psi({\partial\Delta_{j}})
; hence
\sigma
contributes nothing to the left-hand side of
➐
.
It might happen that
i=j
(as for one of the edges on the picture above);
in this case, we have one disc on both sides of
\sigma
, but still, it contributes nothing to
➐
.
If
\sigma
is part of some
\gamma_{i}
, then it also appears once in the boundary of some disc, say in
\partial\Delta_{j}
.
We may assume that both
\Lambda
and
\Delta_{j}
lie on the left from
\sigma
,
so it contributes
\Psi({\sigma})
to both
\Psi({\gamma_{i}})
and
\Psi({\partial\Delta_{j}})
.
These contributions cancel each other on the left-hand side of
➐
.

Summarizing, the contributions to the left-hand side of
➐
that come from the total geodesic curvatures of edges cancel out.

Let us now handle the external angles at the vertices.
Choose a vertex
p
.
Denote by
d
the
degree
of
p
;
that is, the number of edges that end at
p
.

Suppose
p
lies in the interior of
\Lambda
.
Denote by
\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{d}
the internal angles at
p
of the discs containing
p
as a vertex.
Let
\varphi_{i}=\pi-\delta_{i}
be the corresponding external angles.
Then
p
contributes

-\varphi_{1}-\dots-\varphi_{d}
to the sum.
Note that
\delta_{1}+\dots+\delta_{d}=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
;
so
p
contributes

-\varphi_{1}-\dots-\varphi_{d}=(\delta_{1}+\dots+\delta_{d})-d{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi=(2-d){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi,

to the left-hand side of
➐
.

If
p
lies on the boundary of
\Lambda
, then it is a vertex of
d-1
internal angles

\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{d-1}
, and
\varphi_{i}=\penalty\pi-\delta_{i}
are the corresponding external angles.
Note that

\delta_{1}+\dots+\delta_{d-1}=\penalty\pi-\theta,

where
\theta\in(-\pi,\pi)
denotes the external angle of
\Lambda
at
p
.
And again,
p
contributes

\theta-\sum\varphi_{i}=(2-d){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi

to the left-hand side of
➐
.

Summarizing, suppose
p_{1},\dots,p_{v}
are the vertices of the graph, and
d_{1},\dots,d_{v}
are their corresponding degrees.
Then the total contribution from external angles to the left-hand side of
➐
is

2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}v{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi-(d_{1}+\dots+d_{v}){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi.

➑

Since the edges contributed nothing, the left-hand side of
➐
equals
➑
.

It remains to observe that
d_{1}+\dots+d_{v}=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}e
.
Indeed,
d_{1}+\dots+d_{v}
is the total number of ends of all edges in the graph, and each of
e
edges has exactly two ends.
This finishes the proof of
➐
and
➏
.
∎

17.15. Exercise.

Find the integral of the Gauss curvature on each of the following surfaces:

(a)

A torus.

(b)

A Möbius band with geodesic boundary.

(c)

A pair of pants with geodesic boundary components.

(d)

A sphere with two handles.

(e)

A cylider such that its boundary curves have flat neighborhoods.

Chapter 18
Semigeodesic charts

This chapter contains computational proofs of several statements discussed above.
They include
the alternative definition of injectivity radius (
15D
),
that shortest paths are geodesics (
15E
),
and the Gauss–Bonnet formula (
17A
).
In addition, we discuss intrinsic isometries between surfaces and prove Gauss’ remarkable theorem, stating that
Gauss curvature is an intrinsic invariant
.

A
Polar coordinates

The property of the exponential map in
15D
can be used to define
polar coordinates
in a smooth surface.

Namely, let
p
be a point on a smooth surface
\Sigma
and
let
(r,\theta)\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 0}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
be polar coordinates on the tangent plane
\mathrm{T}_{p}
.
If
\text{\sc{v}}\in\mathrm{T}_{p}
has coordinates
(r,\theta)
,
then we say that
s(r,\theta)=\penalty\exp_{p}\text{\sc{v}}
is the point in
\Sigma
with polar coordinates
(r,\theta)
.

There might be many or no geodesics from
p
to a given point
x
;
so, the point
x
might be expressed in polar coordinates in multiple ways or it might have no polar coordinates.
However, from Proposition
15D
, we get the following.

18.1. Observation.

Let
(r,\theta)\mapsto s(r,\theta)
describe polar coordinates with respect to a point
p
in a smooth surface
\Sigma
.
Then there is
r_{0}>0
such that
s
is regular at any pair
(r,\theta)
with
0<r<r_{0}
.

Moreover, if
0\leqslant r_{1},r_{2}<r_{0}
, then
s(r_{1},\theta_{1})=\penalty s(r_{2},\theta_{2})
if and only if

r_{1}=r_{2}=0
or
r_{1}=r_{2}
and
\theta_{1}=\penalty\theta_{2}+2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}n{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
for an integer
n
.

Further we will always assume that polar coordinates on a surface are defined for
r<r_{0}
;
therefore, they behave in the usual way.

The following statement will play an important role in the formal proof that shortest paths are geodesics; see Section
18B
.

18.2. Gauss lemma.

Let
(r,\theta)\mapsto s(r,\theta)
be polar coordinates with respect to a point
p
in a smooth surface.
Then

s_{\theta}\perp s_{r}

for any
r
and
\theta
.

Proof.

Fix
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
.
By the definition of the exponential map, the curve
\gamma(t)=\penalty s(t,\theta)
is a unit-speed geodesic that starts at
p
.

(i)

Since
\gamma
has unit speed, we have
|s_{r}|=|\gamma^{\prime}|=1
.
In particular,

\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\langle s_{r},s_{r}\rangle=0.

(ii)

Since
\gamma
is a geodesic, we have
s_{rr}(r,\theta)=\gamma^{\prime\prime}(r)\perp\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(r)}
.
Therefore

\langle s_{\theta},s_{rr}\rangle=0.

It follows that

\displaystyle\tfrac{\partial}{\partial r}\langle s_{\theta},s_{r}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle s_{\theta r},s_{r}\rangle+\cancel{\langle s_{\theta},s_{rr}\rangle}=

➊

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\langle s_{r},s_{r}\rangle=

\displaystyle=0.

Since
s(0,\theta)=p
for any
\theta
,
we have

s_{\theta}(0,\theta)=0
;
in particular,

\langle s_{\theta},s_{r}\rangle=0

if
r=0
.
By
➊
, the value

\langle s_{\theta},s_{r}\rangle
does not depend on
r
for fixed
\theta
.
Therefore

\langle s_{\theta},s_{r}\rangle=0

for any
r
and
\theta
.
∎

B
Shortest paths are geodesics:

a formal proof

In this section, we use the construction of polar coordinates and the Gauss lemma (
18A
) to prove Proposition
15E
.

Proof of
15E
.

Let
\gamma\colon[0,\ell]\to\Sigma
be a shortest path parametrized by arc-length, and
p=\gamma(0)
.
Suppose
\ell=\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma
is sufficiently small, so
\gamma
can be described in the polar coordinates at
p
;
say as
\gamma(t)=s(r(t),\theta(t))
for functions
t\mapsto\theta(t)
and
t\mapsto r(t)
with
r(0)=0
.

By the chain rule, we have

\gamma^{\prime}=s_{\theta}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\theta^{\prime}+s_{r}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}r^{\prime}

➋

whenever the left-hand side is defined and
t>0
.
By the Gauss lemma
18A
,
s_{\theta}\perp s_{r}
, and by definition of polar coordinates,
|s_{r}|=1
.
Therefore,
➋
implies

|\gamma^{\prime}(t)|\geqslant r^{\prime}(t).

➌

for any
t>0
where
\gamma^{\prime}(t)
is defined.

Since
\gamma
parametrized by arc-length, we have

|\gamma(t_{2})-\gamma(t_{1})|\leqslant|t_{2}-t_{1}|.

In particular,
\gamma
is Lipschitz.
Therefore, by Rademacher’s theorem (
0F
) the derivative
\gamma^{\prime}
is defined almost everywhere.
By
2A
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\ell}|\gamma^{\prime}(t)|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt\geqslant

\displaystyle\geqslant\int_{0}^{\ell}r^{\prime}(t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt=

\displaystyle=r(\ell).

By the construction of polar coordinates, there is a geodesic of length
r(\ell)
from
p=\gamma(0)
to
q=\gamma(\ell)
.
Since
\gamma
is a shortest path, we get that
r(\ell)=\ell
, and, moreover,
r(t)=t
for any
t
.
This equality holds if and only if we have equality in
➌
for almost all
t
.
The latter implies that
\gamma
is a geodesic.

It remains to prove the partial converse.

Fix a point
p\in\Sigma
.
Let
\varepsilon>0
be as in
15D
.
Assume a geodesic
\gamma
of length less than
\varepsilon
from
p
to
q
does not minimize the length between its endpoints.
Then there is a shortest path from
p
to
q
, which is distinct from
\gamma
,
and becomes a geodesic when it is parametrized by its arc-length.
That is, there are two geodesics from
p
to
q
of length smaller than
\varepsilon
.
In other words, there are two vectors
{\text{\sc{v}}},\text{\sc{w}}\in\mathrm{T}_{p}
such that
|{\text{\sc{v}}}|<\varepsilon
,
|\text{\sc{w}}|<\varepsilon
, and

q=\exp_{p}\text{\sc{v}}=\penalty\exp_{p}\text{\sc{w}}
.
But according to
15D
, the exponential map
\mathrm{T}_{p}\to\Sigma
is injective in the
\varepsilon
-neighborhood of zero — a contradiction.∎

C
Gauss curvature

Let
s
be a smooth map from a (possibly unbounded) coordinate rectangle in the
(u,v)
-plane to a smooth surface
\Sigma
.
The map
s
is called
semigeodesic
if, for any fixed
v
, the map
u\mapsto s(u,v)
is a unit-speed geodesic and
s_{u}\perp s_{v}
for any
(u,v)
.

According to the Gauss lemma (
18A
), the polar coordinates on
\Sigma
are described by a semigeodesic map.

Let
\nu=\nu(u,v)
be the unit vector normal to
\Sigma
at
s(u,v)
.
For each pair
(u,v)
, consider an orthonormal frame
\nu
,
\text{\sc{u}}=\penalty s_{u}
, and
\text{\sc{v}}=\penalty\nu\times\text{\sc{u}}
.
Recall that since the vector
s_{v}(u,v)
is tangent to
\Sigma
at
s(u,v)
, we get
s_{v}\perp\text{\sc{u}}
and
s_{v}\perp\nu
.
Therefore, we have that
s_{v}=b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{v}}
for a smooth function
(u,v)\mapsto\penalty b(u,v)
.
1
1

1

For a fixed value
v_{0}
, the vector field
s_{v}=b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{v}}
describes the difference between
\gamma_{0}
and an
infinitesimally close
geodesic
\gamma_{1}\colon u\mapsto s(u,v_{1})
.
The fields with this property are called
Jacobi fields
along
\gamma_{0}
.

18.3. Proposition.

Suppose
(u,v)\mapsto s(u,v)
is a semigeodesic map to a smooth surface
\Sigma
and
\nu
,
u
,
v
, and
b
are as above.
Then

b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}K+b_{uu}=0,

where
K=K(u,v)
is the Gauss curvature of
\Sigma
at
s(u,v)
.

Moreover,

\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{u},\text{\sc{u}}\rangle=\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{u},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle=\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{v},\text{\sc{u}}\rangle=0,\quad\text{and}\quad\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{v},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle=b_{u}.

The proof is lengthy but straightforward.

Proof.

Let
\ell=\ell(u,v)
,
m=m(u,v)
, and
n=n(u,v)
be the components of the matrix describing the shape operator in the frame
\text{\sc{u}},\text{\sc{v}}
;
that is,

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits\text{\sc{u}}

\displaystyle=\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{u}}+m{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{v}},

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits\text{\sc{v}}

\displaystyle=m{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{u}}+n{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{v}}.

➍

Recall that (see Section
10C
)

K=\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}n-m^{2}.

The proposition follows from the identities

\displaystyle\text{\sc{u}}_{u}

\displaystyle=\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nu,

\displaystyle\text{\sc{u}}_{v}

\displaystyle=\phantom{-}b_{u}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{v}}+b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}m{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nu,

➎

\displaystyle\text{\sc{v}}_{u}

\displaystyle=m{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nu,

\displaystyle\text{\sc{v}}_{v}

\displaystyle=-b_{u}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{u}}+b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}n{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nu.

Indeed

\displaystyle b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}K

\displaystyle=b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}n-m^{2})=

\displaystyle=\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{u},\text{\sc{v}}_{v}\rangle-\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{v},\text{\sc{v}}_{u}\rangle=

(by
➎
)

\displaystyle=\left(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial v}\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{u},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle-\cancel{\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{uv},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle}\right)-\left(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial u}\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{v},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle-\cancel{\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{uv},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle}\right)=

\displaystyle=0-b_{uu}.

(by
➎
)

It remains to prove the four identities in
➎
.

Derivation of
\text{\sc{u}}_{u}=\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nu
.

Since the frame
\nu
,
u
, and
v
is orthonormal, this vector identity can be rewritten as the following three scalar identities:

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{u},\text{\sc{u}}\rangle

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{u},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{u},\nu\rangle

\displaystyle=\ell.

Since
u\mapsto s(u,v)
is a geodesic we have that
\text{\sc{u}}_{u}=s_{uu}(u,v)\perp\mathrm{T}_{s(u,v)}
.
Hence, the first two identities follow.
Now,

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{u},\nu\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle s_{uu},\nu\rangle=

(by
10D
)

\displaystyle=\langle\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits s_{u},s_{u}\rangle=

(since
\text{\sc{u}}=s_{u}
)

\displaystyle=\langle\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits\text{\sc{u}},\text{\sc{u}}\rangle=

(by
➍
)

\displaystyle=\ell.

Derivation of
\text{\sc{u}}_{v}=b_{u}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{v}}+b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}m{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nu
.

This vector identity can be rewritten as the following three scalar identities:

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{v},\text{\sc{u}}\rangle

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{v},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle

\displaystyle=b_{u},

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{v},\nu\rangle

\displaystyle=b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}m.

➏

Since
\langle\text{\sc{u}},\text{\sc{u}}\rangle=1
, we get

0=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial v}\langle\text{\sc{u}},\text{\sc{u}}\rangle=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{v},\text{\sc{u}}\rangle
;
hence the first identity in
➏
follows.
Further,

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{v},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle s_{vu},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle=

(since
s_{v}=b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{v}}
)

\displaystyle=\langle\tfrac{\partial}{\partial u}(b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{v}}),\text{\sc{v}}\rangle=

\displaystyle=b_{u}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{v}},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle+b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{v}}_{u},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle=

(since
\langle\text{\sc{v}},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle=1
and

\displaystyle=b_{u};

0=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial u}\langle\text{\sc{v}},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{v}}_{u},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle
)

hence the second identity in
➏
follows.
Finally,

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{v},\nu\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle s_{uv},\nu\rangle=

(by
10D
)

\displaystyle=\langle\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits s_{u},s_{v}\rangle=

(since
\text{\sc{u}}=s_{u}
and
s_{v}=b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{v}}
)

\displaystyle=\langle\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits\text{\sc{u}},b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{v}}\rangle=

(by
➍
)

\displaystyle=b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}m.

Derivation of
\text{\sc{v}}_{u}=m{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nu
and
\text{\sc{v}}_{v}=-b_{u}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{u}}+b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}n{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nu
.

Recall that
\text{\sc{v}}=\nu\times\text{\sc{u}}
.
Therefore,

\text{\sc{v}}_{u}=\nu_{u}\times\text{\sc{u}}+\nu\times\text{\sc{u}}_{u},\qquad\qquad\text{\sc{v}}_{v}=\nu_{v}\times\text{\sc{u}}+\nu\times\text{\sc{u}}_{v}.

➐

The expressions for
\text{\sc{u}}_{u}
and
\text{\sc{u}}_{v}
in
➎
are proved already.
Further,

\displaystyle-\nu_{u}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits s_{u}=

\displaystyle-\nu_{v}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits s_{v}=

\displaystyle=\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits\text{\sc{u}}=

\displaystyle=b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathop{\rm Shape}\nolimits\text{\sc{v}}=

\displaystyle=\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{u}}+m{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{v}},

\displaystyle=b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(m{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{u}}+n{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{v}}),

It remains to plug into
➐
the expressions for
\text{\sc{u}}_{u}
,
\text{\sc{u}}_{v}
,
\nu_{u}
, and
\nu_{v}
.
∎

A chart
(u,v)\mapsto s(u,v)
, as well as the corrsponding local coordinates, will be called
semigeodesic
if the map
(u,v)\mapsto\penalty s(u,v)
is semigeodesic.
Note that the function
b=b(u,v)
for a semigeodesic chart
s
has a constant sign.
Therefore, by changing the sign of
\nu
, we can (and always will) assume that
b>0
;
in other words,
b=|s_{v}|
.

18.4. Exercise.

Show that any point
p
in a smooth surface
\Sigma
can be covered by a semigeodesic chart.

18.5. Exercise.

Let
p
be a point on a smooth surface
\Sigma
.
Assume
\exp_{p}
is injective in the ball
B=B(0,r_{0})_{\mathrm{T}_{p}}
.
Suppose the semigeodesic map
(r,\theta)\mapsto s(r,\theta)
describes polar coordinates with respect to
p
, and the function
(r,\theta)\mapsto b(r,\theta)
is as above.

Prove the following statements:

(a)

b(r,\theta)
does not change its sign for
0\leqslant\penalty r<\penalty r_{0}
.

(b)

b(r,\theta)\neq 0
if
0<r<r_{0}
.

(c)

Apply
18C
and
18C
to prove
15D
.

A chart
(u,v)\mapsto s(u,v)
is called
orthogonal
if
s_{u}\perp s_{v}
for any
(u,v)
.
For example, any semigeodesic chart is orthogonal.

A solution of the following exercise is similar to
18C
.

18.6. Exercise.

Let
(u,v)\mapsto s(u,v)
be an orthogonal chart of a smooth surface
\Sigma
.
Denote by
K=K(u,v)
the Gauss curvature of
\Sigma
at
s(u,v)
.
Set

\displaystyle a=a(u,v)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}|s_{u}|,

\displaystyle b=b(u,v)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}|s_{v}|,

\displaystyle\text{\sc{u}}=\text{\sc{u}}(u,v)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}\tfrac{s_{u}}{a},

\displaystyle\text{\sc{v}}=\text{\sc{v}}(u,v)

\displaystyle\mathrel{:=}\tfrac{s_{v}}{b}.

Let
\nu=\nu(u,v)
be the unit normal vector at
s(u,v)
.

(a)

Show that

\displaystyle\text{\sc{u}}_{u}

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{b}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}a_{v}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{v}}+a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nu,

\displaystyle\text{\sc{v}}_{u}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{b}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}a_{v}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{u}}+a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}m{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nu

\displaystyle\text{\sc{u}}_{v}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{a}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}b_{u}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{v}}+b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}m{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nu,

\displaystyle\text{\sc{v}}_{v}

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{a}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}b_{u}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{u}}+b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}n{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nu,

where
\ell=\ell(u,v)
,
m=m(u,v)
, and
n=n(u,v)
be the components of the matrix describing the shape operator in the frame
\text{\sc{u}},\text{\sc{v}}
.

(b)

Show that

K=-\frac{1}{a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}b}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\left(\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\left(\frac{b_{u}}{a}\right)+\frac{\partial}{\partial v}\left(\frac{a_{v}}{b}\right)\right).

18.7. Exercise.

Suppose
(u,v)\mapsto s(u,v)
is a
conformal chart
;
that is, there is a function
(u,v)\mapsto b(u,v)
such that
b=|s_{u}|=|s_{v}|
and
s_{u}\perp s_{v}
for any
(u,v)
.
(The function
b
is called a
conformal factor
of the chart.)

Use
18C
to show that

K=-\frac{\triangle(\ln b)}{b^{2}},

where
\triangle
denotes the
Laplacian
; that is,
\triangle=\tfrac{\partial^{2}}{\partial u^{2}}+\tfrac{\partial^{2}}{\partial v^{2}}
, and

K=K(u,v)
is the Gauss curvature of
\Sigma
at
s(u,v)
.

It is useful to know that
any point on a smooth surface can be covered by a conformal chart
; the corresponding coordinates are usually referred to as
isothermal
.

D
Rotation of a vector field

Let
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to\Sigma
be a simple loop on a smooth oriented surface
\Sigma
.
Suppose
u
is a field of unit tangent vectors on
\Sigma
defined in a neighborhood of
\gamma
.
Denote by
v
the field obtained from
u
by a counterclockwise rotation by
\tfrac{\pi}{2}
of the tangent plane at each point; it could also be defined by
\text{\sc{v}}\mathrel{:=}\nu\times\text{\sc{u}}
, where
\nu
is the normal field on
\Sigma
.
Then the
rotation
of
u
around
\gamma
is defined as the integral

\mathop{\rm rot}\nolimits_{\gamma}\text{\sc{u}}\mathrel{:=}\int_{0}^{1}\langle\text{\sc{u}}^{\prime}(t),\text{\sc{v}}(t)\rangle{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt.

18.8. Lemma.

Suppose
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to\Sigma
is a simple loop with the base at a point
p
in a smooth oriented surface
\Sigma
, and
u
is a field of tangent unit vectors to
\Sigma
defined in a neighborhood of
\gamma
.
Then the parallel transport
\iota_{\gamma}\colon\mathrm{T}_{p}\to\mathrm{T}_{p}
is a clockwise rotation by the angle
\mathop{\rm rot}\nolimits_{\gamma}\text{\sc{u}}
.

In particular, rotations of different vector fields around
\gamma
may only differ by a multiple of
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
.

Proof.

As above, set
\text{\sc{v}}=\nu\times\text{\sc{u}}
.
Denote by
\text{\sc{u}}(t)
and
\text{\sc{v}}(t)
the vectors at
\gamma(t)
of the fields
u
and
v
, respectively.

Let
t\mapsto\text{\sc{x}}(t)\in\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t)}
be the parallel vector field along
\gamma
with
\text{\sc{x}}(0)=\penalty\text{\sc{u}}(0)
, and
\text{\sc{y}}\mathrel{:=}\nu\times\text{\sc{x}}
.

Note that there is a continuous function
t\mapsto\varphi(t)
such that

\text{\sc{u}}(t)
is a counterclockwise rotation of
\text{\sc{x}}(0)
by angle
\varphi(t)
.
Since
\text{\sc{x}}(0)=\text{\sc{u}}(0)
, we can and will assume that
\varphi(0)=0
.
Then

u

\displaystyle=\cos\varphi{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{x}}+\sin\varphi{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{y}}

v

\displaystyle=-\sin\varphi{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{x}}+\cos\varphi{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{y}}

It follows that

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{u}}^{\prime},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle=\varphi^{\prime}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\biggl(

\displaystyle(\sin\varphi)^{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{x}},\text{\sc{x}}\rangle+(\cos\varphi)^{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{y}},\text{\sc{y}}\rangle\biggr)=\varphi^{\prime}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm rot}\nolimits_{\gamma}\text{\sc{u}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\langle\text{\sc{u}}^{\prime}(t),\text{\sc{v}}(t)\rangle{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt=

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\varphi^{\prime}(t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt=

\displaystyle=\varphi(1).

Observe that

\diamond

\iota_{\gamma}(\text{\sc{x}}(0))=\text{\sc{x}}(1)
,

\diamond

\text{\sc{x}}(0)=\text{\sc{u}}(0)=\text{\sc{u}}(1),

\diamond

\text{\sc{u}}(1)
is a counterclockwise rotation of
\text{\sc{x}}(1)
by the angle
\varphi(1)=\penalty\mathop{\rm rot}\nolimits_{\gamma}\text{\sc{u}}
,

It follows that
\text{\sc{x}}(1)
is a
clockwise
rotation of
\text{\sc{x}}(0)
by angle
\mathop{\rm rot}\nolimits_{\gamma}\text{\sc{u}}
, and the result follows.

The last statement follows from
16D
.
∎

The following lemma will play a key role in the proof of the Gauss–Bonnet formula.

18.9. Lemma.

Let
(u,v)\mapsto s(u,v)
be a semigeodesic chart on a smooth surface
\Sigma
.
Suppose a simple loop
\gamma
bounds a disc
\Delta
that is covered completely by
s
.
Then

\mathop{\rm rot}\nolimits_{\gamma}\text{\sc{u}}+\iint_{\Delta}K=0,

where
\text{\sc{u}}=s_{u}
, and
K
denotes the Gauss curvature of
\Sigma
.

The calculations below use the so-called
Green formula
, which can be formulated the following way.

Let
D
be a compact region in the
(u,v)
-coordinate plane that is bounded by a piecewise smooth simple closed path
\alpha\colon t\mapsto(u(t),v(t))
.
Suppose
\alpha
is oriented in such a way that
D
lies on its left.
Then

\iint_{D}(Q_{u}-P_{v}){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}du{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dv=\int_{\alpha}(P{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}du+Q{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dv)\mathrel{:=}\int_{0}^{1}(P{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}u^{\prime}+Q{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}v^{\prime}){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt

for any two smooth functions
P
and
Q
defined on
D
.

The Green and Gauss–Bonnet formulas are similar — they relate the integral along a disc and its boundary curve.
So it shouldn’t be surprising that one helps to prove another.

Proof.

Let
u
,
v
, and
b
be as in Section
18C
.
Let us write
\gamma
in the
(u,v)
-coordinates:
\gamma(t)=s(u(t),v(t))
.
Then

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm rot}\nolimits_{\gamma}\text{\sc{u}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\langle\text{\sc{u}}^{\prime},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt=

(by the chain rule)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}[\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{u},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}u^{\prime}+\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{v},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}v^{\prime}]{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt=

(by
18C
)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}b_{u}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}v^{\prime}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt=\int_{s^{-1}\circ\gamma}b_{u}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dv=

(by the Green formula)

\displaystyle=\iint_{s^{-1}(\Delta)}b_{uu}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}du{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dv=

(
s_{u}\perp s_{v}\Longrightarrow\mathop{\rm jac}\nolimits s=|s_{u}|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|s_{v}|=b
)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\Delta}\frac{b_{uu}}{b}=

(by
18C
)

\displaystyle=-\iint_{\Delta}K.

∎

E
Gauss–Bonnet formula: a formal proof

Recall that the Gauss–Bonnet formula can be written as
\mathop{\rm GB}\nolimits(\Delta)=0
,
where

\mathop{\rm GB}\nolimits(\Delta)\mathrel{:=}\Psi({\partial\Delta})+\iint_{\Delta}K-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi,

\Delta
is a topological disc in a smooth oriented surface,
bounded by a piecewise smooth curve
\partial\Delta
that is oriented in such a way that
\Delta
lies on its left.

Proof of the Gauss–Bonnet formula (
17A
).

Assume
\Delta
is covered by a semigeodesic chart.
From
16D
,

18D
,
and
18D
we get

\mathop{\rm GB}\nolimits(\Delta)=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}n{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi,

➑

where
n=n(\Delta)
is an integer.

By
18C
, any point can be covered by a semigeodesic chart.
Therefore, applying the additivity of
\mathop{\rm GB}\nolimits
(
17B
) finite number of times, we get

➑
for any disc
\Delta
in
\Sigma
.
More precisely, we can cut
\Delta
by a smooth curve that runs from boundary to boundary
and repeat such subdivision recursively for the obtained discs;
see the picture.

After several such steps, every small disc is covered by a semigeodesic chart.
In particular,
➑
holds for each small disc.
Then applying
17B
several times we get
➑
for the original disc.

It remains to show that
n=0
.
Assume
\Delta
lies in a local graph realization
z=f(x,y)
of
\Sigma
.
Consider the one-parameter family
\Sigma_{t}
of graphs
z=t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}f(x,y)
;
denote by
\Delta_{t}
the corresponding disc in
\Sigma_{t}
, so
\Delta_{1}=\Delta
and
\Delta_{0}
is its projection onto the
(x,y)
-plane.
The function
h\colon t\mapsto\mathop{\rm GB}\nolimits(\Delta_{t})
is continuous.
By
➑
,
h(t)
is an integer multiple of
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
for any
t
.
It follows that
h
is a constant function.
Therefore,

\mathop{\rm GB}\nolimits(\Delta)=\mathop{\rm GB}\nolimits(\Delta_{0})=0;

the last equality follows from
6C
.

We proved that

\mathop{\rm GB}\nolimits(\Delta)=0

➒

if
\Delta
lies in a graph
z=f(x,y)
for some
(x,y,z)
-coordinate system. Any point of
\Sigma
has a neighborhood that can be covered by such a graph. Therefore, applying Lemma
17B
the same way as above, we get that
➒
holds for any disc
\Delta
in
\Sigma
.
∎

F
Rauch comparison

The following proposition is a special case of the so-called
Rauch comparison theorem
.

18.10. Proposition.

Suppose
p
is a point on a smooth surface
\Sigma
and
r\leqslant\mathop{\rm inj}\nolimits(p)
.
Given a curve
\tilde{\gamma}
in the
r
-neighborhood of
0
in
\mathrm{T}_{p}
, set

\gamma=\exp_{p}\circ\tilde{\gamma}\quad\text{or, equivalently}\quad\log_{p}\circ\gamma=\tilde{\gamma};

note that
\gamma
is a curve in
\Sigma
.

(a)

If
\Sigma
has nonpositive Gauss curvature, then the logarithmic map
\log_{p}
is length nonexpanding in the
r
-neighborhood of
p
in
\Sigma
;
that is,

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma\geqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\tilde{\gamma}

for any curve
\gamma
in the open ball
B(p,r)_{\Sigma}
.

(b)

If
\Sigma
has nonnegative Gauss curvature, then the exponential map
\exp_{p}
is length nonexpanding in the
r
-neighborhood of
0
in
\mathrm{T}_{p}
;
that is,

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma\leqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\tilde{\gamma}

for any curve
\tilde{\gamma}
in the open ball
B(0,r)_{\mathrm{T}_{p}}
.

Proof.

Suppose
(r(t),\theta(t))
are the polar coordinates of
\tilde{\gamma}(t)
and
s
as in
18A
.
Note that
\gamma(t)=\penalty s(r(t),\theta(t))
; that is,
(r(t),\theta(t))
are the polar coordinates of
\gamma(t)
with respect to
p
on
\Sigma
.

Let us show that
b(0,\theta)=0
, and
b_{r}(0,\theta)=1
for any
\theta
.
We may assume that
\theta=0
.
Choose the standard basis
\text{\sc{v}},\text{\sc{w}}
in
\mathrm{T}_{p}
; so
v
points in the direction of curve
t\mapsto s(t,0)
.
Note that
2
2

2

Recall that
(D_{\text{\sc{w}}}\exp_{p})(r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{v}})\mathrel{:=}h^{\prime}(0)
, where
h(t)=\exp_{p}(r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{v}}+t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{w}})
; see Section
9B
.

b(r,0)=r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|(D_{\text{\sc{w}}}\exp_{p})(r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{v}})|.

In particular,
b(0,0)=0
.
By
15C
,
|(D_{\text{\sc{w}}}\exp_{p})(0)|=1
.
In particular,
|(D_{\text{\sc{w}}}\exp_{p})(r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{v}})|\neq\penalty 0
for any small
r
.
Therefore, the function
r\mapsto|(D_{\text{\sc{w}}}\exp_{p})(r{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{v}})|
is differentiable at
r=0
.
Taking the partial derivative of the expression for
b(r,0)
, we get
b_{r}(0,0)=1
.

Set
b(r,\theta)\mathrel{:=}|s_{\theta}|
.
By
18C

b_{rr}=-K{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}b.

If
K\geqslant 0
, then
r\mapsto b(r,\theta)
is concave
and
if
K\leqslant 0
, then
r\mapsto b(r,\theta)
is convex for any fixed
\theta
.
Since
b(0,\theta)=0
, and
b_{r}(0,\theta)=1
,

\displaystyle b(r,\theta)\geqslant r\quad\text{if}\quad K

\displaystyle\leqslant 0;

➓

\displaystyle b(r,\theta)\leqslant r\quad\text{if}\quad K

\displaystyle\geqslant 0.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\tilde{\gamma}\colon[a,b]\to\mathrm{T}_{p}
is parametrized by arc-length;
in particular, it is a Lipschitz curve.
Note that

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\tilde{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{b}\sqrt{r^{\prime}(t)^{2}+r(t)^{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\theta^{\prime}(t)^{2}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt.

Applying
18A
, we get

\displaystyle\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma

\displaystyle=\int_{a}^{b}\sqrt{r^{\prime}(t)^{2}+b(r(t),\theta(t))^{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\theta^{\prime}(t)^{2}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt.

Now, both statements
18F
and
18F
follow from
➓
.
∎

G
Intrinsic isometries

Let
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{*}
be two smooth surfaces.
A map
f\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma^{*}
is called
length-preserving
if, for any curve
\gamma
in
\Sigma
, the curve
\gamma^{*}=f\circ\gamma
in
\Sigma^{*}
has the same length.
If in addition
f
is smooth and bijective, then it is called an
intrinsic isometry
.

An example of a length-preserving map can be obtained by wrapping a plane into a cylinder with the map
s\colon\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
given by

s(x,y)=\penalty(\cos x,\sin x,y)
.

18.11. Exercise.

Show that an intrinsic isometry between smooth surfaces maps geodesics to geodesics.

18.12. Exercise.

Suppose the Gauss curvature of a smooth surface
\Sigma
vanishes.
Show that
\Sigma
is
locally flat
;
that is, a neighborhood of any point in
\Sigma
admits an intrinsic isometry to an open domain in the Euclidean plane.

18.13. Exercise.

Suppose a smooth surface
\Sigma
has Gauss curvature equal to 1 at every point.
Show that a neighborhood of any point in
\Sigma
admits an intrinsic isometry to an open domain in the unit sphere.

18.14. Exercise.

Given
a>0
, show that there is a smooth unit-speed curve

\gamma(t)=(x(t),y(t))
with
y(t)=a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos t
and
y>0
.
Describe its interval of definition.

Let
\Sigma_{a}
be the surface of revolution of
\gamma
around the
x
-axis.

Show that the surface
\Sigma_{a}
has a unit Gauss curvature at each point.

Use
18G
to conclude that any small round disc
\Delta
in
\mathbb{S}^{2}
admits a smooth length-preserving deformation;
that is, there is a one-parameter family
\Delta_{t}
of surfaces with boundary, such that
\Delta_{0}=\Delta
and
\Delta_{t}
is not congruent to
\Delta_{0}
for
t\neq 0
.
3
3

3

In fact, any disc in
\mathbb{S}^{2}
admits a smooth length preserving deformation.
However, if the disc is larger than half-sphere, then the proof requires more;
it can be obtained as a corollary of two deep results of Alexandr Alexandrov: the gluing theorem and the theorem on the existence of a convex surface with an abstractly given metric
[
81
, p. 44]
.

The following exercise illustrates the final step in the proof that
any open surface with vanishing Gauss curvature a cylindrical surface
.
See the discussion after
10D
.

18.15. Advanced exercise.

Suppose
(u,v)\mapsto f(u,v)
describes an intrinsic isometry from the plane with standard
(u,v)
-coordinates to a surface
\Sigma
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
Assume that
f
maps
v
-axis to the
z
-axis isometrically.
Show that
\Sigma
is a cylindrical surface;
more precisely, is a union of a family of lines parallel to the
z
-axis.

H
The remarkable theorem

18.16. Theorem.

Suppose
f\colon\Sigma\to\Sigma^{*}
is an intrinsic isometry between two smooth surfaces;
p\in\Sigma
and
p^{*}=\penalty f(p)\in\Sigma^{*}
.
Then

K(p)_{\Sigma}=K(p^{*})_{\Sigma^{*}};

that is, the Gauss curvature of
\Sigma
at
p
is the same as the Gauss curvature of
\Sigma^{*}
at
p^{*}
.

Recall that the Gauss curvature is defined as a product of principal curvatures which might be different at the points
p
and
p^{*}
; however, according to the theorem, their products are the same.
In other words, the Gaussian curvature is an
intrinsic invariant
.
This theorem was proved by Carl Friedrich Gauss
[
35
]
who justifiably called it
remarkable
(
Theorema Egregium
).

In fact, the Gauss curvature can be found
intrinsically
,
by measuring the lengths of curves in the surface.
For example, the Gauss curvature
K(p)
appears in the following formula for the circumference
c(r)
of a geodesic circle centered at
p
in a surface:

c(r)=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}r-\tfrac{\pi}{3}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}K(p){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}r^{3}+o(r^{3}).

The theorem implies, for example, that there is no smooth length-preserving map that sends an open region in the unit sphere to the plane.
4
4

4

Smoothness is essential — there are plenty of non-smooth length-preserving maps from the sphere to the plane; see
[
75
]
and the references therein.

This follows since the Gauss curvature of the plane is zero and the unit sphere has Gauss curvature 1.
In particular, any geographic map has to have distortions.

Proof.

Choose a chart
(u,v)\mapsto s(u,v)
on
\Sigma
, and set

s^{*}=f\circ s
.
Note that
s^{*}
is a chart of
\Sigma^{*}
, and

\displaystyle\langle s_{u},s_{u}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle s_{u}^{*},s_{u}^{*}\rangle,

\displaystyle\langle s_{u},s_{v}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle s_{u}^{*},s_{v}^{*}\rangle,

\displaystyle\langle s_{v},s_{v}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle s_{v}^{*},s_{v}^{*}\rangle

at any
(u,v)
.
Indeed, since
f
preserves the lengths of the coordinate lines
\gamma\colon t\mapsto s(t,v)
and
\gamma\colon t\mapsto\penalty s(u,t)
, we get the first and the third identities.
Now, since
f
preserves the lengths of the curves
\gamma\colon t\mapsto\penalty s(t,c-t)
for any constant
c
, the first and the third identities imply the second one.

By
15E
, if
s
is a semigeodesic chart, then so is
s^{*}
.
It remains to apply
18C
and
18C
.
∎

Chapter 19
Comparison theorems

The comparison theorems provide a powerful means of applying Euclidean intuition in differential geometry.

A
Triangles and hinges

Recall that a shortest path between points
x
and
y
in a surface
\Sigma
is denoted by
[x,y]
or
[x,y]_{\Sigma}
, and

|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\Sigma}
denotes the
intrinsic distance
from
x
to
y
in
\Sigma
.

A
geodesic triangle
in a surface
\Sigma
is defined as a triple of points
x,y,z\in\penalty\Sigma
with a choice of minimizing geodesics
[x,y]_{\Sigma}
,
[y,z]_{\Sigma}
, and
[z,x]_{\Sigma}
.
The points
x,y,z
are called the
vertices
of the triangle,
the minimizing geodesics
[x,y]
,
[y,z]
, and
[z,x]
are called its
sides
;
the triangle itself is denoted by
[xyz]
, or
[xyz]_{\Sigma}
;
the latter notation is used if we need to emphasize that the triangle lies on the surface
\Sigma
.

A triangle
[\tilde{x}\tilde{y}\tilde{z}]
in the plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is called a
model triangle
of the triangle
[xyz]

if its corresponding sides are equal;
that is,

|{\tilde{x}}-\penalty{\tilde{y}}|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}=|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\Sigma},\quad|{\tilde{y}}-\penalty{\tilde{z}}|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}=|{y}-\penalty{z}|_{\Sigma},\quad|{\tilde{z}}-\penalty{\tilde{x}}|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}=|{z}-\penalty{x}|_{\Sigma}.

In this case, we write
[\tilde{x}\tilde{y}\tilde{z}]=\tilde{\triangle}xyz
.

A pair of minimizing geodesics
[x,y]
and
[x,z]
starting from one point
x
is called a
hinge
and is denoted by
[x\,{}^{y}_{z}]
.
The angle between these geodesics at
x
is denoted by
\measuredangle[x\,{}^{y}_{z}]
.
The corresponding angle
\measuredangle[\tilde{x}\,{}^{\tilde{y}}_{\tilde{z}}]
in a model triangle
[\tilde{x}\tilde{y}\tilde{z}]=\tilde{\triangle}xyz
is denoted by
\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{y}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt)
;

it is called
model angle
of the triangle
[xyz]
at
x
.

By side-side-side congruence condition,
the model triangle
[\tilde{x}\tilde{y}\tilde{z}]
is uniquely defined up to congruence.
Therefore, the model angle
\tilde{\theta}=\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{y}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt)
is uniquely defined as well.
By the cosine rule, we have

\cos\tilde{\theta}=\frac{a^{2}+b^{2}-c^{2}}{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}b},

where
a=|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\Sigma}
,
b=|{x}-\penalty{z}|_{\Sigma}
, and
c=|{y}-\penalty{z}|_{\Sigma}
.

19.1. Exercise.

Let
[x_{n}y_{n}z_{n}]
be a sequence of triangles in a smooth surface
\Sigma
.
Set
a_{n}=|{x_{n}}-\penalty{y_{n}}|_{\Sigma}
,

b_{n}=|{x_{n}}-\penalty{z_{n}}|_{\Sigma}
,
and
c_{n}=|{y_{n}}-\penalty{z_{n}}|_{\Sigma}
, and
\tilde{\theta}_{n}=\tilde{\measuredangle}(x_{n}\,{}^{y_{n}}_{z_{n}}\hskip-0.86108pt)
.
Suppose the sequences
a_{n}
and
b_{n}
are bounded away from zero;
that is,
a_{n}>\varepsilon
and
b_{n}>\varepsilon
for a fixed
\varepsilon>0
and any
n
.
Show that

(a_{n}+b_{n}-c_{n})\to 0\qquad\iff\qquad\tilde{\theta}_{n}\to\pi

as
n\to\infty

B
Formulations

Part
19B
of the following theorem is called the
Cartan–Hadamard theorem
;
it was proved by
Hans von Mangoldt
[
63
]
and generalized by
Elie Cartan
[
16
]
and
Jacques Hadamard
[
39
]
.
Part
19B
is called the
Toponogov comparison theorem
and sometimes the
Alexandrov comparison theorem
;
it was proved by Paolo Pizzetti
[
80
]
, rediscovered by Alexandr Alexandrov
[
2
]
, and
generalized by Victor Toponogov
[
94
]
.

Recall that a surface
\Sigma
is called
simply-connected
if any simple closed curve in
\Sigma
bounds a disc.

19.2. Comparison theorems.

(a)

If
\Sigma
is an open simply-connected smooth surface with nonpositive Gauss curvature, then

\measuredangle[x\,{}^{y}_{z}]\leqslant\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{y}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt)

for any geodesic triangle
[xyz]
in
\Sigma
.

(b)

If
\Sigma
is a closed (or open) smooth surface with nonnegative Gauss curvature, then

\measuredangle[x\,{}^{y}_{z}]\geqslant\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{y}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt)

for any geodesic triangle
[xyz]
.

The proofs of parts
19B
and
19B
will be given in sections
19D
and
19E
, respectively.

19.3. Exercise.

Show that the conclusion in
19B
does not hold in the hyperboloid
\left\{\,{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\,:\,{x^{2}+y^{2}-z^{2}=1}\,\right\}
.
In particular,
19B
does not hold without assuming that
\Sigma
is simply-connected.

Let us compare the Gauss–Bonnet formula with the comparison theorems.
Suppose a disc
\Delta
is bounded by a geodesic triangle
[xyz]
with internal angles
\alpha
,
\beta
, and
\gamma
.
Then the Gauss–Bonnet implies that

\alpha+\beta+\gamma-\pi=\iint_{\Delta}K;

in particular, both sides of the equation have the same sign.
It follows that

\diamond

if
K_{\Sigma}\geqslant 0
, then
\alpha+\beta+\gamma\geqslant\pi
, and

\diamond

if
K_{\Sigma}\leqslant 0
, then
\alpha+\beta+\gamma\leqslant\pi
.

Now set

\hat{\alpha}=\penalty\measuredangle[x\,{}^{y}_{z}]
,

\hat{\beta}=\penalty\measuredangle[y\,{}^{z}_{x}]
,
and
\hat{\gamma}=\penalty\measuredangle[z\,{}^{x}_{y}]
.
Note that
\hat{\alpha},\hat{\beta},\hat{\gamma}\in[0,\pi]
.
Since the angles of any plane triangle add up
\pi
, from the comparison theorems, we get that

\diamond

if
K_{\Sigma}\geqslant 0
, then
\hat{\alpha}+\hat{\beta}+\hat{\gamma}\geqslant\pi
, and

\diamond

if
K_{\Sigma}\leqslant 0
, then
\hat{\alpha}+\hat{\beta}+\hat{\gamma}\leqslant\pi
.

Now we see that despite the Gauss–Bonnet formula and the comparison theorems are closely related,
this relationship is not straightforward.

For example, suppose
K\geqslant 0
.
Then the Gauss–Bonnet formula does not forbid the internal angles
\alpha
,
\beta
, and
\gamma
to be simultaneously close to
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
.
But
\hat{\alpha}=\alpha
if
\alpha\leqslant\pi
, and otherwise
\hat{\alpha}=\pi-\alpha
;
that is,

\displaystyle\hat{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\min\{\,\alpha,2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi-\alpha\,\},

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}

\displaystyle=\min\{\,\beta,2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi-\beta\,\},

\displaystyle\hat{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\min\{\,\gamma,2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi-\gamma\,\}.

Therefore,
if
\alpha
,
\beta
, and
\gamma
are each close to
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
, then
\hat{\alpha}
,
\hat{\beta}
, and
\hat{\gamma}
are close to
0
.
The latter is impossible by the comparison theorem.

19.4. Exercise.

Let
p
and
q
be points on a closed convex surface
\Sigma
that lie at a maximal intrinsic distance from each other;
that is, for any
x,y\in\Sigma
we have
|{p}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}\geqslant\penalty|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\Sigma}
.
Show that
\measuredangle[x\,{}^{p}_{q}]\geqslant\tfrac{\pi}{3}

for any point
x\in\Sigma\setminus\{p,q\}
.

19.5. Exercise.

Let
\Sigma
be a closed (or open) surface with nonnegative Gauss curvature.
Show that

\tilde{\measuredangle}(p\,{}^{x}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\tilde{\measuredangle}(p\,{}^{y}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\tilde{\measuredangle}(p\,{}^{z}_{x}\hskip-0.86108pt)\leqslant 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi.

for any four distinct points
p,x,y,z
in
\Sigma
.

C
Local comparisons

The following local theorem is the first step in the proof of the comparison theorems (
19B
);
it will be deduced follows from the Rauch comparison (
18F
).

19.6. Theorem.

The comparison theorem (
19B
) holds in a small neighborhood of any point.

Moreover, let
\Sigma
be a smooth surface without boundary.
Then for any
p\in\Sigma
there is
r>0
such that if
|{p}-\penalty{x}|_{\Sigma}<r
, then
\mathop{\rm inj}\nolimits(x)_{\Sigma}>r
, and the following statements hold:

(a)

If
\Sigma
has nonpositive Gauss curvature, then

\measuredangle[x\,{}^{y}_{z}]\leqslant\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{y}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt)

for any geodesic triangle
[xyz]
in
B(p,\tfrac{r}{4})_{\Sigma}
.

(b)

If
\Sigma
has nonnegative Gauss curvature, then

\measuredangle[x\,{}^{y}_{z}]\geqslant\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{y}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt)

for any geodesic triangle
[xyz]
in
B(p,\tfrac{r}{4})_{\Sigma}
.

Proof.

The existence of
r>0
follows from
15D
.
Let
[xyz]
be a geodesic triangle in
B(p,\tfrac{r}{4})
.

Since
r<\mathop{\rm inj}\nolimits(x)_{\Sigma}
, we can choose
\text{\sc{v}},\text{\sc{w}}\in\mathrm{T}_{x}
such that

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=\exp_{x}\text{\sc{v}},

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=\exp_{x}\text{\sc{w}},

\displaystyle|\text{\sc{v}}|_{\mathrm{T}_{x}}

\displaystyle=|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\Sigma},

\displaystyle|\text{\sc{w}}|_{\mathrm{T}_{x}}

\displaystyle=|{x}-\penalty{z}|_{\Sigma},

\displaystyle\measuredangle[0\,{}^{\text{\sc{v}}}_{\text{\sc{w}}}]_{\mathrm{T}_{x}}

\displaystyle=\measuredangle[x\,{}^{y}_{z}]_{\Sigma};

in particular,
|\text{\sc{v}}|,|\text{\sc{w}}|<\tfrac{r}{2}
.

19C
.

Consider a minimizing geodesic
\gamma
joining
y
to
z
.
Since
|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\Sigma},|{x}-\penalty{z}|_{\Sigma}<\penalty\tfrac{r}{2}
, the triangle inequality implies that
\gamma
lies in the
r
-neighborhood of
x
.
In particular,
\log_{x}\circ\gamma
is defined, and the curve

\tilde{\gamma}\mathrel{:=}\log_{x}\circ\gamma
lies in an
r
-neighborhood of zero in
\mathrm{T}_{x}
.
Note that
\tilde{\gamma}
connects
v
to
w
in
\mathrm{T}_{x}
.

By Rauch comparison (
18F
), we have

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\tilde{\gamma}\leqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma.

Since
|{\text{\sc{v}}}-\penalty{\text{\sc{w}}}|_{\mathrm{T}_{x}}\leqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\tilde{\gamma}
and
\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma=|{y}-\penalty{z}|_{\Sigma}
, we get

|{\text{\sc{v}}}-\penalty{\text{\sc{w}}}|_{\mathrm{T}_{x}}\leqslant|{y}-\penalty{z}|_{\Sigma}.

By angle monotonicity (
0C
), we get

\measuredangle[0\,{}^{\text{\sc{v}}}_{\text{\sc{w}}}]_{\mathrm{T}_{x}}\leqslant\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{z}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt),

hence the result.

19C
.

Consider the line segment
\tilde{\gamma}
joining
v
to
w
in the tangent plane
\mathrm{T}_{x}
, and set
\gamma\mathrel{:=}\exp_{x}\circ\tilde{\gamma}
.
By Rauch comparison (
18F
), we have

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\tilde{\gamma}\geqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma.

Since
|{\text{\sc{v}}}-\penalty{\text{\sc{w}}}|_{\mathrm{T}_{x}}=\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\tilde{\gamma}
and
\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma\geqslant|{y}-\penalty{z}|_{\Sigma}
, we get

|{\text{\sc{v}}}-\penalty{\text{\sc{w}}}|_{\mathrm{T}_{x}}\geqslant|{y}-\penalty{z}|_{\Sigma}.

By the angle monotonicity (
0C
), we obtain

\measuredangle[0\,{}^{\text{\sc{v}}}_{\text{\sc{w}}}]_{\mathrm{T}_{x}}\geqslant\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{z}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt),

hence the result.
∎

D
Nonpositive curvature

Proof of
19B
.

Sine
\Sigma
is simply-connected,
17A
implies that

\mathop{\rm inj}\nolimits(p)_{\Sigma}=\infty

for any
p\in\Sigma
.
Therefore,
19C
implies
19B
.
∎

E
Nonnegative curvature

We will prove
19B
, first assuming that
\Sigma
is compact.
The general case requires only minor modifications; they are indicated in Exercise
19E
at the end of the section.
The proof is taken from
[
5
]
, and it is close to the proof found independently by Urs Lang and Viktor Schroeder
[
58
]
.

Proof of
19B
in the compact case.

Assume
\Sigma
is compact.
From the local theorem (
19C
), we get that there is
\varepsilon>0
such that the inequality

\measuredangle[x\,{}^{p}_{q}]\geqslant\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{p}_{q}\hskip-0.86108pt).

holds for any hinge
[x\,{}^{p}_{q}]
with

|{x}-\penalty{p}|_{\Sigma}+|{x}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}<\varepsilon
.
The following lemma states that in this case, the same holds true for any hinge
[x\,{}^{p}_{q}]
such that
|{x}-\penalty{p}|_{\Sigma}+|{x}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}<\tfrac{3}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\varepsilon
.
Applying the key lemma (
19E
) a few times we get that the comparison holds for an arbitrary hinge, which proves
19B
.
∎

19.7. Key lemma.

Let
\Sigma
be an open or closed smooth surface.
Assume the comparison

\measuredangle[x\,{}^{y}_{z}]\geqslant\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{y}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt)

➊

holds for any hinge
[x\,{}^{y}_{z}]
with

|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\Sigma}+|{x}-\penalty{z}|_{\Sigma}<\frac{2}{3}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell
.
Then the comparison
➊

holds for any hinge
[x\,{}^{y}_{z}]
with
|{x}-\penalty{y}|_{\Sigma}+|{x}-\penalty{z}|_{\Sigma}<\ell
.

Given a hinge
[x\,{}^{p}_{q}]
consider a triangle in the plane
with angle
\measuredangle[x\,{}^{p}_{q}]
and two adjacent sides
|{x}-\penalty{p}|_{\Sigma}
and
|{x}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}
.
Let us denote by
\tilde{\curlyvee}[x\,{}^{p}_{q}]
the third side of this triangle;
it will be called the
model side
of the hinge.

The next computational exercise plays a role in the following proof.

19.8. Exercise.

Suppose hinges
[x\,{}^{p}_{q}]
and
[x\,{}^{p}_{y}]
have a common side
[x,p]
and
[x,y]\subset[x,q]
.
Show that

\frac{|{x}-\penalty{p}|+|{x}-\penalty{q}|-\tilde{\curlyvee}[x\,{}^{p}_{q}]}{|{x}-\penalty{q}|}\leqslant\frac{|{x}-\penalty{p}|+|{x}-\penalty{y}|-\tilde{\curlyvee}[x\,{}^{p}_{y}]}{|{x}-\penalty{y}|}.

Proof.

By the angle monotonicity (
0C
), we have

\measuredangle[x\,{}^{p}_{q}]\geqslant\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{p}_{q}\hskip-0.86108pt)\quad\iff\quad\tilde{\curlyvee}[x\,{}^{p}_{q}]\geqslant|{p}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}.

Therefore, it is sufficient to prove that

\tilde{\curlyvee}[x\,{}^{p}_{q}]\geqslant|{p}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma},

➋

assuming that
|{x}-\penalty{p}|_{\Sigma}+|{x}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}<\ell
.

Let us produce a new hinge
[x^{\prime}\,{}^{p}_{q}]
for a given hinge
[x\,{}^{p}_{q}]
such that

\tfrac{2}{3}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell\leqslant|{p}-\penalty{x}|_{\Sigma}+\penalty|{x}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}<\ell.

Assume
|{x}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}\geqslant|{x}-\penalty{p}|_{\Sigma}
; otherwise, switch the roles of
p
and
q
in the construction.
Take
x^{\prime}\in[x,q]
such that

|{p}-\penalty{x}|_{\Sigma}+3{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{x}-\penalty{x^{\prime}}|_{\Sigma}=\tfrac{2}{3}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell

➌

Choose a geodesic
[x^{\prime},p]
, and consider the hinge
[x^{\prime}\,{}^{p}_{q}]
formed by
[x^{\prime},p]
and
[x^{\prime},q]\subset[x,q]
.

By the triangle inequality, we have

|{p}-\penalty{x}|_{\Sigma}+|{x}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}\geqslant|{p}-\penalty{x^{\prime}}|_{\Sigma}+|{x^{\prime}}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}.

➍

Let us show that

\tilde{\curlyvee}[x\,{}^{p}_{q}]\geqslant\tilde{\curlyvee}[x^{\prime}\,{}^{p}_{q}]

➎

By
➌
, we have that

\displaystyle|{p}-\penalty{x}|_{\Sigma}+\penalty|{x}-\penalty{x^{\prime}}|_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle<\tfrac{2}{3}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell,

\displaystyle|{p}-\penalty{x^{\prime}}|_{\Sigma}+\penalty|{x^{\prime}}-\penalty{x}|_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle<\tfrac{2}{3}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell.

Therefore, the assumption imply that

\displaystyle\measuredangle[x\,{}^{p}_{x^{\prime}}]\geqslant\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{p}_{x^{\prime}}\hskip-0.86108pt)\quad\text{and}\quad\measuredangle[x^{\prime}\,{}^{p}_{x}]\geqslant\tilde{\measuredangle}(x^{\prime}\,{}^{p}_{x}\hskip-0.86108pt).

➏

Consider the model triangle

[\tilde{x}\tilde{x}^{\prime}\tilde{p}]=\penalty\tilde{\triangle}xx^{\prime}p
.
Take
\tilde{q}
on the extension of
[\tilde{x},\tilde{x}^{\prime}]
beyond
x^{\prime}
such that
|{\tilde{x}}-\penalty{\tilde{q}}|_{\Sigma}=|{x}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}
, and therefore
|{\tilde{x}^{\prime}}-\penalty{\tilde{q}}|_{\Sigma}=\penalty|{x^{\prime}}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}
.

From
➏
, we get

\measuredangle[x\,{}^{p}_{q}]=\measuredangle[x\,{}^{p}_{x^{\prime}}]\geqslant\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{p}_{x^{\prime}}\hskip-0.86108pt).

Therefore,

\tilde{\curlyvee}[x\,{}^{q}_{p}]\geqslant|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{q}}|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}.

Further, since
\measuredangle[x^{\prime}\,{}^{p}_{x}]+\measuredangle[x^{\prime}\,{}^{p}_{q}]=\pi
,
the inequalities in
➏
imply

\pi-\tilde{\measuredangle}(x^{\prime}\,{}^{p}_{x}\hskip-0.86108pt)\geqslant\pi-\measuredangle[x^{\prime}\,{}^{p}_{x}]\geqslant\measuredangle[x^{\prime}\,{}^{p}_{q}].

Therefore,

|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{q}}|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\geqslant\tilde{\curlyvee}[x^{\prime}\,{}^{q}_{p}]

and
➎
follows.

Set
x_{0}=x
; apply inductively the above construction to get a sequence of hinges
[x_{n}\,{}^{p}_{q}]
with
x_{n+1}=x_{n}^{\prime}
.
By
➎
and
➍
, both sequences

s_{n}=\tilde{\curlyvee}[x_{n}\,{}^{p}_{q}]\quad\text{and}\quad r_{n}=|{p}-\penalty{x_{n}}|_{\Sigma}+|{x_{n}}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}

are nonincreasing.

The sequence might terminate at
x_{n}
only if
r_{n}<\tfrac{2}{3}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell
.
In this case, by the assumptions of the lemma, we have

s_{n}=\tilde{\curlyvee}[x_{n}\,{}^{p}_{q}]\geqslant|{p}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}.

Since the sequence
s_{n}
is nonincreasing, we get

\tilde{\curlyvee}[x\,{}^{p}_{q}]=s_{0}\geqslant s_{n}\geqslant|{p}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma};

whence
➋
follows.

It remains to prove
➋
if the sequence
x_{n}
does not terminate.
By
➌
, we have

|{x_{n}}-\penalty{x_{n-1}}|_{\Sigma}\geqslant\tfrac{1}{100}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell.

➐

By
➍
,
|{x_{n}}-\penalty{p}|,|{x_{n}}-\penalty{q}|<\ell
for any
n
.
In case
x_{n+1}\in[x_{n},q]
, apply
19E
for the hinges
[x_{n}\,{}^{p}_{q}]
and
[x_{n}\,{}^{p}_{x_{n+1}}]
.
By
➏
,
|{p}-\penalty{x_{n+1}}|\leqslant\tilde{\curlyvee}[x_{n}\,{}^{x_{n+1}}_{p}]
.
Therefore,

r_{n}-s_{n}\leqslant 100{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(r_{n}-r_{n+1})

➑

In the case
x_{n+1}\in[x_{n},p]
, inequality
➑
follows if one applies
19E
for the hinges
[x_{n}\,{}^{p}_{q}]
and
[x_{n}\,{}^{x_{n+1}}_{q}]
.

The sequences
r_{n}
and
s_{n}
are nonincreasing and nonnegative;
so, they have to converge.
In particular,
(r_{n}-r_{n+1})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.
Therefore,
➑
implies that

\lim_{n\to\infty}s_{n}=\lim_{n\to\infty}r_{n}.

By the triangle inequality,
r_{n}\geqslant|{p}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}
for any
n
.
Since
s_{n}
is nonincreasing, we get

\tilde{\curlyvee}[x\,{}^{p}_{q}]=s_{0}\geqslant\lim_{n\to\infty}s_{n}=\lim_{n\to\infty}r_{n}\geqslant|{p}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}

which finishes the proof of
➋
.
∎

F
Alexandrov’s lemma

A reformulation of the following lemma (
19G
) will be used in the next section to produce a few equivalent reformulations of the comparison theorems.

19.10. Lemma.

Consider two quadrangles in the Euclidean plane
pxyz
and
p^{\prime}x^{\prime}y^{\prime}z^{\prime}
with equal corresponding sides.
Assume the sides
[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]
and
[y^{\prime},z^{\prime}]
extend each other; that is,
y^{\prime}
lies on the line segment
[x^{\prime},z^{\prime}]
.
Then the following expressions have the same signs:

(i)

|p-y|-|p^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|
;

(ii)

\measuredangle[x\,{}^{p}_{y}]-\measuredangle[x^{\prime}\,{}^{p^{\prime}}_{y^{\prime}}]
;

(iii)

\pi-\measuredangle[y\,{}^{p}_{x}]-\measuredangle[y\,{}^{p}_{z}]
.

Proof.

Take
a point
\bar{z}
on the extension of

[x,y]
beyond
y
so that
|{y}-\penalty{\bar{z}}|=|{y}-\penalty{z}|
(and therefore
|{x}-\penalty{\bar{z}}|=|{x^{\prime}}-\penalty{z^{\prime}}|
).

By the angle monotonicity (
0C
),
the following expressions have the same sign:

(i)

|p-y|-|p^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|
;

(ii)

\measuredangle[x\,{}^{y}_{p}]-\measuredangle[x^{\prime}\,{}^{y^{\prime}}_{p^{\prime}}]=\measuredangle[x\,{}^{\bar{z}}_{p}]-\measuredangle[x^{\prime}\,{}^{z^{\prime}}_{p^{\prime}}]
;

(iii)

|p-\bar{z}|-|p^{\prime}-z^{\prime}|=|p-\bar{z}|-|p-z|
;

(iv)

\measuredangle[y\,{}^{\bar{z}}_{p}]-\measuredangle[y\,{}^{z}_{p}]
.

The statement follows since

\measuredangle[y^{\prime}\,{}^{z^{\prime}}_{p^{\prime}}]+\measuredangle[y^{\prime}\,{}^{x^{\prime}}_{p^{\prime}}]=\pi\quad\text{and}\quad\measuredangle[y\,{}^{\bar{z}}_{p}]+\measuredangle[y\,{}^{x}_{p}]=\pi.

∎

G
Reformulations

For any triangle
[xyz]
in a surface
\Sigma
, and its model triangle
[\tilde{x}\tilde{y}\tilde{z}]
, there is a natural map
p\mapsto\tilde{p}
that isometrically sends the geodesics
[x,y]
,
[y,z]
,
[z,x]
to the line segments
[\tilde{x},\tilde{y}]
,
[\tilde{y},\tilde{z}]
,
[\tilde{z},\tilde{x}]
, respectively.
The triangle
[xyz]
is called
fat
(
thin
)
if the inequality

|{p}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}\geqslant|\tilde{p}-\tilde{q}|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\qquad\text{(or, respectively,}\quad|{p}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}\leqslant|\tilde{p}-\tilde{q}|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}})

holds for any two points
p
and
q
on the sides of
[xyz]
.

19.11. Proposition.

Let
\Sigma
be an open or closed smooth surface.
Then the following three conditions are equivalent:

(a)

For any geodesic triangle
[xyz]
in
\Sigma
we have

\measuredangle[x\,{}^{y}_{z}]\geqslant\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{y}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt).

(b)

For any geodesic triangle
[pxz]
in
\Sigma
and
y
on the side
[x,z]
we have

\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{p}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)\geqslant\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{p}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt).

(c)

Any geodesic triangle in
\Sigma
is fat.

Similarly, the following three conditions are equivalent:

(A)

For any geodesic triangle
[xyz]
in
\Sigma
we have

\measuredangle[x\,{}^{y}_{z}]\leqslant\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{y}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt).

(B)

For any geodesic triangle
[pxz]
in
\Sigma
and
y
on the side
[x,z]
we have

\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{p}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)\leqslant\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{p}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt).

(C)

Any geodesic triangle in
\Sigma
is thin.

Let us rewrite the Alexandrov lemma (
19F
) in the language of comparison triangles and angles.

19.12. Reformulation of Alexandrov lemma.

Let
[pxz]
be a triangle in a surface
\Sigma

and
y
a point on the side
[x,z]
.
Consider its model triangle
[\tilde{p}\tilde{x}\tilde{z}]=\penalty\tilde{\triangle}pxz
, and let
\tilde{y}
be the corresponding point on the side
[\tilde{x},\tilde{z}]
.

Then the following expressions have the same signs:

(i)

|{p}-\penalty{y}|_{\Sigma}-|{\tilde{p}}-\penalty{\tilde{y}}|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}
;

(ii)

\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{p}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)-\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{p}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt)
;

(iii)

\pi-\tilde{\measuredangle}(y\,{}^{p}_{x}\hskip-0.86108pt)-\tilde{\measuredangle}(y\,{}^{p}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt)
;

Proof of
19G
.

We will prove the implications
19G
\Rightarrow
19G
\Rightarrow
19G
\Rightarrow
19G
.
The implications
19G
\Rightarrow
19G
\Rightarrow
19G
\Rightarrow
19G
can be proved in the same way with all inequalities reversed.

19G
\Rightarrow
19G
.

Note that
\measuredangle[y\,{}^{p}_{x}]+\measuredangle[y\,{}^{p}_{z}]=\pi
.
By
19G
,

\tilde{\measuredangle}(y\,{}^{p}_{x}\hskip-0.86108pt)+\tilde{\measuredangle}(y\,{}^{p}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt)\leqslant\pi.

It remains to apply Alexandrov’s lemma (
19G
).

19G
\Rightarrow
19G
.

Applying
19G
twice, first for
y\in[x,z]
and then for
w\in[p,x]
, we get that

\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{w}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)\geqslant\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{p}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)\geqslant\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{p}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt),

and therefore

|{w}-\penalty{y}|_{\Sigma}\geqslant|{\tilde{w}}-\penalty{\tilde{y}}|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}},

where
\tilde{w}
and
\tilde{y}
are the points corresponding to
w
and
y
points on the sides of the model triangle.

19G
\Rightarrow
19G
.

Since the triangle is fat, we have

\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{w}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)\geqslant\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{p}_{z}\hskip-0.86108pt)

for any
w\in[x,p]\setminus\{x\}
and
y\in[x,z]\setminus\{x\}
.
Note that
\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{w}_{y}\hskip-0.86108pt)\to\measuredangle[x\,{}^{p}_{z}]
as
w,y\to x
.
Whence the implication follows.
∎

2A
.

Choose a partition

By construction

\displaystyle|\gamma(t_{0})

\displaystyle-\gamma(t_{1})|+|\gamma(t_{1})-\gamma(t_{2})|+\dots

\displaystyle\hskip 17.00024pt\dots+|\gamma(t_{n-1})-\gamma(t_{n})|=

(t_{i})
is arbitrary, we get

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\beta\geqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma.

Remarks.

It is instructive to compare this exercise with
2H
.

The inequality might be strict.
It happens if
\beta
runs back and forth along
\gamma
.
In this case the partition
(\tau_{i})
above is
not
arbitrary.

If one assumes only
\beta([0,1])\supset\gamma([0,1])
, then the problem becomes harder.
It follows from the following inequality
[
13
, 2.6.1+2.6.2]
:

h\leqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma.

where
h
denotes the 1-dimensional Hausdorff measure of the image
\gamma([0,1])
.
Moreover the equality holds if and only if
\gamma
is simple.

2B
.

Suppose

is a rectifiable curve and let

with factor

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{k}=k{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma.

Now suppose the arc

2D

Use

2D
.

We have to assume

or

;
in particular, the velocity is constant.

s=\penalty t/\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}

It remains to substitute

2E
.

Choose a closed polygonal line
p_{1}\dots p_{n}
inscribed in
\beta
.
By
2E
, we can assume that its length is arbitrarily close to the length of
\beta
;
that is, given
\varepsilon>0

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits(p_{1}\dots p_{n})>\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\beta-\varepsilon.

Show that we may assume in addition that each point
p_{i}
lies on
\alpha
.

Since
\alpha
is simple, the points
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}
appear on
\alpha
in the same cyclic order;
that is, the polygonal line
p_{1}\dots p_{n}
is also inscribed in
\alpha
.
In particular,

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\alpha\geqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits(p_{1}\dots p_{n}).

It follows that

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\alpha>\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\beta-\varepsilon.

for any
\varepsilon>0
.
Whence

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\alpha\geqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\beta.

If
\alpha
has self-intersections, then the points
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}
might appear on
\alpha
in a different cyclic order, say
p_{i_{1}},\dots,p_{i_{n}}
.
Apply the triangle inequality to show that

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits(p_{i_{1}}\dots p_{i_{n}})\geqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits(p_{1}\dots p_{n})

and use it to modify the given proof.

2F
.

Denote by
\ell_{\text{\sc{u}}}
the line segment
obtained by orthogonal projection of
\gamma
to the line in the direction
u
.
Since
\gamma_{\text{\sc{u}}}
runs along
\ell_{\text{\sc{u}}}
back and forth, we get

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma_{\text{\sc{u}}}\geqslant 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\ell_{\text{\sc{u}}}.

By the Crofton formula,

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma\geqslant\pi{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\overline{\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\ell_{\text{\sc{u}}}}.

In the case of equality, the curve
\gamma_{\text{\sc{u}}}
runs exactly back and forth along
\ell_{\text{\sc{u}}}
without additional zigzags for almost all (and therefore for all)
u
.

Let
K
be a closed set bounded by
\gamma
.
The last statement implies that every line may intersect
K
only along a closed segment or a single point.
It follows that
K
is convex.

2F
.

The proof is identical to the proof of the standard Crofton formula.
To find the coefficients it is sufficient to check it on a unit interval.
The latter can be done by integration:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits\mathbb{S}^{2}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\iint_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}|x|;

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathop{\rm area}\nolimits\mathbb{S}^{2}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\iint_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}\sqrt{1-x^{2}}.

The answers are
k_{1}=2
and
k_{2}=\tfrac{4}{\pi}
.

Remark.
The converse to the first statement does not hold in general.
Consider for example, the following metric on the upper half-plane

The only-if part is trivial.

The spherical curve shown on the picture does not have antipodal pairs of points.

2H
.

\gamma
