).
In particular, it cannot divide
\mathbb{S}^{2}
into two regions of equal area — a contradiction.

2H
.

The first sentence is wrong — it is
not
sufficient to show that the diameter is at most 2.
For example, if an equilateral triangle has circumradius slightly above
1
,
then its diameter (which is defined as the maximal distance between its points) is slightly bigger than
\sqrt{3}
, so it is smaller than
2
.

On the other hand, it is easy to modify the proof of the hemisphere lemma (
2H
) to get a correct solution.
That is, (1) choose two points
p
and
q
on
\gamma
that divide it into two arcs of the same length;
(2) set
z
to be a midpoint of
p
and
q
,
and (3) show that
\gamma
lies in the unit disc centered at
z
.

Remarks.

Lines crossing a curve as in the exercise are called

It is known that any

and the references therein.

5B
.

One should get

\displaystyle\text{\sc{t}}(t)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{(-a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sin t,a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos t,b)}{\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}},

5C
.
By the product rule, we get

\displaystyle\text{\sc{b}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(\text{\sc{t}}\times\text{\sc{n}})^{\prime}=\text{\sc{t}}^{\prime}\times\text{\sc{n}}+\text{\sc{t}}\times\text{\sc{n}}^{\prime}.

5C
.

This is a consequence of the equation
\text{\sc{b}}^{\prime}=-\tau{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{n}}

|\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(t)|\geqslant|\gamma_{0}^{\prime}(t)|\hskip 8.50012pt\text{and}\hskip 8.50012pt|\gamma_{1}^{\prime}(t)|\geqslant|\kappa(t)_{\gamma_{0}}|

6C
.

Observe that

\gamma^{\prime}_{a}(t)=(1+a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos t,-a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sin t);

that is,
\gamma^{\prime}_{a}
runs clockwise along a circle with center at
(1,0)
and radius
|a|
.

Case
|a|>1
.
Note that
\text{\sc{t}}_{a}(t)=\gamma^{\prime}_{a}/|\gamma^{\prime}_{a}|
runs clockwise and makes a full turn in time
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
.
Therefore,
\Psi({\gamma_{a}})=-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
and
\Phi({\gamma_{a}})=\penalty|\Psi({\gamma_{a}})|=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
.

Case
|a|<1
.

Set
\theta_{a}=\arcsin a
.
Show that
\text{\sc{t}}_{a}(t)=\gamma^{\prime}_{a}/|\gamma^{\prime}_{a}|
starts with the horizontal direction
\text{\sc{t}}_{a}(0)=(1,0)
, turns monotonically to angle
\theta_{a}
, then monotonically to
-\theta_{a}
and then monotonically back to
\text{\sc{t}}_{a}(2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi)=(1,0)
.
It follows that

\Psi({\gamma_{a}})=0
and
\Phi({\gamma_{a}})=4{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\theta_{a}
.

Case

.

The velocity
\gamma^{\prime}_{-1}(t)
vanishes at
t=0
and
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
.
Nevertheless, the curve admits a smooth regular parametrization — find it.
You should get
\Phi({\gamma_{-1}})=-\Psi({\gamma_{-1}})=\pi
.

Case
a=1
.

The velocity
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}(t)
vanishes at
t=\pi
.
At
t=\pi
the curve has a cusp;
that is,
\gamma_{1}
turns exactly back at the time
\pi
.
So
\gamma_{1}(t)
has undefined total signed curvature.
The curve is a joint of two smooth arcs with external angle
\pi
, and
the total curvature of each arc is
\tfrac{\pi}{2}
, so

\Phi({\gamma_{1}})=\tfrac{\pi}{2}+\pi+\tfrac{\pi}{2}=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
.

Computational solution.

We will assume that the curvature does not vanish at
p
, the remaining case is simpler.
We may assume that
\gamma
is unit-speed,
p=\gamma(0)
,
and the center of curvature of
\gamma
at
p
is the origin;
in other words,
\kappa(0){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\gamma(0)+\text{\sc{n}}(0)=0
.

Consider the function
f\colon t\mapsto\langle\gamma(t),\gamma(t)\rangle
.
Direct calculations show that

\displaystyle f^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\langle\gamma,\gamma\rangle^{\prime}=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{t}},\gamma\rangle,

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{t}},\gamma\rangle^{\prime}=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\kappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{n}},\gamma\rangle+2,

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\kappa^{\prime}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{n}},\gamma\rangle+2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\kappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{n}}^{\prime},\gamma\rangle+\cancel{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\kappa{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\text{\sc{n}},\text{\sc{t}}\rangle}.

Observe that
\text{\sc{n}}^{\prime}(0)\perp\gamma(0)
.
Therefore,
f^{\prime}(0)=0
,
f^{\prime\prime}(0)=0
, and
f^{\prime\prime\prime}(0)=\penalty-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\kappa^{\prime}/\kappa
.

If the osculating circle supports
\gamma
at
p
,
then the function
f
has a local maximum or minimum at
0
.
Therefore
f^{\prime\prime\prime}(0)=0
and
\kappa^{\prime}=0
.

8F
.

To prove the if part, use that the composition of smooth functions is smooth.

Now, choose a graph representation
z=\penalty f(x,y)
of a neighborhood of
p
in
\Sigma
.
Note that
g
is smooth in the neighborhood if and only if the function
\hat{g}\colon(x,y)\mapsto g(x,y,f(x,y))
is smooth in its domain of definition.
Define
f(x,y,z)\mathrel{:=}\penalty\hat{g}(x,y)
; it solves the only-if part.

For the last part, consider function
g\colon(x,y)\mapsto\tfrac{1}{x^{2}+y^{2}}
defined on the
(x,y)
-plane with removed origin.
Observe that

cannot be extended to a continuous function on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

Remark.
Suppose

Then

A proof uses the so-called

read about it and try to prove this statement.

8F
.

Check that the image of
s
lies in the unit sphere centered at
(0,0,1)
;
that is, show that

\left(\tfrac{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}u}{1+u^{2}+v^{2}}\right)^{2}+\penalty\left(\tfrac{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}v}{1+u^{2}+v^{2}}\right)^{2}+\penalty\left(\tfrac{2}{1+u^{2}+v^{2}}-1\right)^{2}=1.

for any

and

.

Show that the map

(x,y,z)\mapsto\penalty\tfrac{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(x,y)}{x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2}}

describes the inverse of
s
, which is continuous away from the origin.
In particular,
s
is an embedding that covers the entire sphere except for the origin.

Show that

is regular; that is,
s_{u}
and
s_{v}
are linearly independent at all points of the
(u,v)
-plane.

Remark.

The map
s
in the exercise can be visualized as

(u,v,1)\mapsto(\tfrac{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}u}{1+u^{2}+v^{2}},\tfrac{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}v}{1+u^{2}+v^{2}},\tfrac{2}{1+u^{2}+v^{2}})

which is called
stereographic projection
from the plane

to the unit sphere with center at
(0,0,1)
.
Note that the point
(u,v,1)
and its image lie on the same half-line emerging from the origin.

8G
.
Apply the inverse function theorem (
0G
) in charts for both surfaces.

8G
.

The solutions of these exercises are built on the following general construction known as the
Moser trick
.

Suppose
\text{\sc{u}}_{t}
is a smooth time-dependent vector field on a plane.
Consider the ordinary differential equation
x^{\prime}(t)=\text{\sc{u}}_{t}(x(t))
.
Consider the map
\iota\colon x(0)\mapsto x(1)
where
t\mapsto x(t)
is a solution of the equation.
The map
\iota
is called the
flow
of the vector field
\text{\sc{u}}_{t}
for the time interval
[0,1]
.
By
0H
, the map
\iota
is smooth in its domain of definition.
Moreover, the same holds for its inverse
\iota^{-1}
;
indeed,
\iota^{-1}
is the flow of the vector field
-\text{\sc{u}}_{1-t}
.
That is,
\iota
is a diffeomorphism from its domain of definition to its image.

Therefore, to construct a diffeomorphism from one open subset of the plane to another, it is sufficient to construct a smooth vector field such that its flow maps one set to the other;
such a map is automatically a diffeomorphism.

8G
.

Consider two sets
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\}
and
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{q_{1},\dots,q_{n}\}
.
Choose smooth paths
\gamma_{i}\colon[0,1]\to\penalty\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
\gamma_{i}(0)=p_{i}
,

\gamma_{i}(1)=q_{i}
, and
\gamma_{i}(t)\neq\gamma_{j}(t)
if
i\neq j
.

Choose a smooth vector field
\text{\sc{v}}_{t}
such that
\text{\sc{v}}_{t}(\gamma_{i}(t))=\gamma^{\prime}_{i}(t)
for any
i
and
t
.
We can assume in addition that
\text{\sc{v}}_{t}
vanishes outside a sufficiently large disc; this can be arranged by multiplying the vector field by an appropriate bell function;
for example,
\sigma_{1}(R-|x|)
, where
R
is large and

10D
.

We can assume that
\gamma
is parametrized by arc-length.
Denote by
\nu_{1}(s)
and
\nu_{2}(s)
the unit normal vectors to
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
at
\gamma(s)

By the assumption,
\langle\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\rangle=c
for some constant

We know that

\nu_{1}^{\prime}
is proportional to
\gamma^{\prime}
and need to show that
\nu_{2}^{\prime}
is proportional to
\gamma^{\prime}
.
If

, and the statement trivially holds.

Note that

;

to show that

.
Show that
\langle\nu_{2},\nu_{2}^{\prime}\rangle=0
.
Conclude that
\nu_{2}^{\prime}
is proportional to

.

12B

12C
.

We can assume that the Gauss curvature of the surface is positive; otherwise, the statement is evident.
By
12C
, the surface bounds a convex region that contains a line segment of length
\pi
.

cannot exceed

.

from the inside by a surface of revolution of the described type.
(Compare to

Remark.

The exercise can be deduced from the following deeper result:
if the Gauss curvature of
\Sigma
is at least
1
,
then
the intrinsic diameter of
\Sigma
cannot exceed
\pi
.
The latter means that any two points in
\Sigma
can be connected by a path in
\Sigma
that has length at most
\pi
.
This theorem was proved by Heinz Hopf and Willi Rinow
[

]
and
named after Sumner Myers who generalized it
[

]
.

13A
.

Denote by

\Sigma
at

.

Show that the derivative

and the derivative changes sign if

If

.
Therefore, the function

is monotone on

.

13A
.

This problem follows easily from the so-called
Morse lemma
.
The following sketch is a slightly stripped version of it.
A more conceptual proof
[
1
]

can be built on the Moser trick; see the solution of
8G
.

Choose tangent-normal coordinates at
p
so that the coordinate axes point in the principal directions;
let
z=f(x,y)
be the local graph representation of
\Sigma
.
We need to show that the solution of
f(x,y)=0
is a union of two smooth curves that intersect transversely at
p
.

Prove the following claim:

Suppose
x\mapsto\penalty h(x)
is a smooth function defined in an open interval
\mathbb{I}\ni 0
such that
h(0)=h^{\prime}(0)=0
and
h^{\prime\prime}(0)>0
.
Then, for a smaller interval
\mathbb{J}\ni 0
there is a unique smooth function
a\colon\mathbb{J}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
h=a^{2}
,
a(0)=0
and
a^{\prime}(0)>0
.

Note that passing to a small square domain
|x|,|y|<\varepsilon
, we can assume that
f_{xx}>\penalty\varepsilon
and
f_{yy}<-\varepsilon
.
Show that if
\varepsilon
is small, then for every
x
there is unique
y(x)
such that
f_{x}(x,y(x))=0
;
moreover, the function
x\mapsto\penalty y(x)
is smooth.

Set
h(x)=f(x,y(x))
.
Note that
h(0)=\penalty h^{\prime}(0)=0
and
h^{\prime\prime}>0
.
Applying the claim, we get a function
a
such that
h=a^{2}
, where
a(0)=0
and
a^{\prime}(0)>0
.

Observe that
g(x,y)=h(x)-f(x,y)\geqslant 0
,
g_{y}(x,y(x))=\penalty g(x,y(x))=\penalty 0
, and
g_{yy}>0
.
Applying the claim to each function
y\mapsto g(x,y)
with fixed
x
, we get that
g(x,y)=b(x,y)^{2}
for a smooth function
b
such that

b(x,y(x))=0
and
b_{y}(x,y(x))>0
.

It follows that

\displaystyle f(x,y)

\displaystyle=a(x)^{2}-b(x,y)^{2}=

\displaystyle=(a(x)-b(x,y)){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(a(x)+b(x,y)).

That is
f(x,y)=0
if
a(x)\pm b(x,y)=0
.

It remains to observe that the two functions
g_{\pm}(x,y)=a(x)\pm b(x,y)
have distinct non-zero gradients at
0
.
Therefore, each equation
a(x)\pm b(x,y)=0
defines a smooth curve in a neighborhood of
p
;
see Section
1E
.

.
Look at the pseudosphere described in
11B
.

.

Use

and the hemisphere lemma (

).

For the second part, consider a thin tube bounded by two closed spherical curves.

13B
.

Assume
\Sigma
is an open saddle surface that lies in a cone
K
.
Show that there is a plane
\Pi
that cuts
\Sigma
and cuts from
K
a compact region.
Conclude that
\Pi
cuts from
\Sigma
a compact region as well.

By
9G
one can move the plane
\Pi
slightly so that it cuts from
\Sigma
a compact surface with boundary.
Apply
13B
.

13B
.

Consider the radial projection of
F_{\varepsilon}
to the sphere
\Sigma
with center at
p=(0,0,\varepsilon)
;
that is, a point
q\in F_{\varepsilon}
is mapped to a point
s(q)
on the sphere that lies on the half-line

.

Show that

is a diffeomorphism from

to the southern hemisphere of
\Sigma
.
It remains to observe that the unit disc is diffeomorphic to the hemisphere.

13B
.

By the fundamental theorem of affine geometry, any affine transformation is smooth.
It remains to apply
13B
.

.

Find an example among the surfaces of revolution;
use

.

13C
.
Look at the sections of the graph by planes parallel to the
(x,y)
-plane and to the
(x,z)
-plane, then apply Meusnier’s theorem (
11B
).

13C
.

Suppose the orthogonal projection of
\Sigma
to the
(x,y)
-plane is not injective.
Show that there is a point
p\in\Sigma
with a vertical tangent plane;
that is,
\mathrm{T}_{p}\Sigma
is perpendicular to the
(x,y)
-plane.

Let
\Gamma_{p}
be the connected component of
p
in the intersection of
\Sigma
and the affine tangent plane
\mathrm{T}_{p}\Sigma
.
Use
13A
to show that
\Gamma_{p}
is a union of smooth curves that can cross each other transversely.
Moreover, two of these curves pass thru
p
, and
\Gamma_{p}
does not bound a compact region on
\Sigma
.

Show that
\Gamma_{p}
must have at least 4 ways to escape to infinity.
On the other hand, since
\Sigma
is a graph outside the compact set
K
, we have that
\Gamma_{p}\setminus K
is a graph of a real-to-real function that has only two ways to escape to infinity — a contradiction.

14A
.

Cut the lateral surface of the ice-mountain by a line from the cowboy to the tip.
Unfold it on the plane (see the picture) and try to figure out what is the image of the strained lasso.

We learned this problem from Joel Fine, who attributed it to Frederic Bourgeois

]
.

14D
.

Note that by
12C
,
\Sigma
bounds a strictly convex region.
Therefore, we can assume that
\nu(p)\neq\nu(q)
; otherwise,
p=q
, and the inequality is evident.

Further, we can assume that
\nu(p)+\nu(q)\neq\penalty 0
; otherwise, the right-hand side is undefined.

In the remaining case, the tangent planes
\mathrm{T}_{p}
and
\mathrm{T}_{q}
intersect along a line, say
\ell
.
Set
\alpha=\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\measuredangle(\nu(p),\nu(q))
.
Show that
2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\alpha=\penalty|\nu(p)+\nu(q)|
.
Let
x\in\ell
be the point that minimizes the sum
|p-x|+\penalty|x-q|
.
Show that
\measuredangle[x\,{}^{p}_{q}]\geqslant\pi-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\alpha
.
Conclude that

|p-x|+|x-q|\leqslant\tfrac{|p-q|}{\cos\alpha}.

Apply
14D
to show that

|{p}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}\leqslant|p-x|+\penalty|x-q|
.

14D
.

Suppose there is a minimizing curve
\gamma\not\subset\Delta
with endpoints
p
and
q
in
\Delta
.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that only one arc of
\gamma
lies outside
\Delta
.
Reflection of this arc with respect to
\Pi
together with the remaining part of
\gamma
forms another curve
\hat{\gamma}
from
p
to
q
;
it runs partly along
\Sigma

and partly outside
\Sigma
,
but does not get inside.
Note that

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\hat{\gamma}=\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma.

Denote by
\bar{\gamma}
the nearest-point projection of
\hat{\gamma}
on
\Sigma
.
The curve
\bar{\gamma}
lies in
\Sigma
,
it has the same endpoints as
\gamma
,
and by
14D

\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\bar{\gamma}<\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits\gamma.

This means that
\gamma
is not length minimizing —
a contradiction.

14D
.

Show that the nearest-point projection
\mathbb{S}^{2}\to\Sigma
is surjective.
Apply
14C
and
9E
.

15A
.
Show that
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t)
is proportional to
\nabla_{\gamma(t)}f
, where
f=x^{2}+y^{2}
.
Apply
9A
.

15A
.

We can assume that the origin lies on the axis of revolution, and
i
points in the direction of such axis.
Use
15A
to show that it is sufficient to prove that

\langle\gamma^{\prime}\times\gamma,\text{\sc{i}}\rangle

is constant.

Since
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t)\perp\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t)}
, the three vectors
i
,
\gamma
, and
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
lie in the same plane.
In particular,
\langle\gamma^{\prime\prime}\times\gamma,\text{\sc{i}}\rangle=0
.
Therefore,

\langle\gamma^{\prime}\times\gamma,\text{\sc{i}}\rangle^{\prime}=\langle\gamma^{\prime}\times\gamma^{\prime},\text{\sc{i}}\rangle+\langle\gamma^{\prime\prime}\times\gamma,\text{\sc{i}}\rangle=0.

15A
.
By Lemma
15A
,
we can assume that
\gamma
is parametrized by arc-length.
By the definition of geodesic, we have that
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(s)\perp\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(s)}
.
Therefore,

\gamma^{\prime\prime}(s)=k_{n}(s){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nu(\gamma(s)),

where
k_{n}(s)
is the normal curvature of
\gamma
at time
s
.
Since
\gamma
is asymptotic,
k_{n}(s)\equiv 0
;
that is,
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(s)\equiv 0
, therefore
\gamma^{\prime}
is constant and hence
\gamma
runs along a line segment; see
3B
.

15A
.

Denote by
\mu
a unit vector perpendicular to
\Pi
.
Since
\gamma
lies in
\Pi
, we have that
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
is parallel to
\Pi
, or equivalently
\gamma^{\prime\prime}\perp\mu
.
Since
\gamma
is ,
3A
implies that
\gamma^{\prime\prime}\perp\gamma^{\prime}
.

Since
\Sigma
is mirror-symmetric with respect to the plane
\Pi
,
the tangent plane
\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t)}\Sigma
is also mirror-symmetric with respect to
\Pi
.
It follows that
\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t)}\Sigma
is spanned by
\mu
and
\gamma^{\prime}(t)
.
Hence,
\gamma^{\prime\prime}\perp\mu
and
\gamma^{\prime\prime}\perp\gamma^{\prime}
imply
\gamma^{\prime\prime}\perp\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t)}\Sigma
;
that is,
\gamma
is a geodesic.

15B
.

By
15A
, any meridian of
\Sigma
is a closed geodesic.
Consider an arbitrarily closed geodesic
\gamma
.

If
\gamma
is tangent to a meridian at some point, then by the uniqueness part of Proposition
15B
,
\gamma
runs along that meridian;
in particular, it is non-contractible.

In the remaining case,
\gamma
can intersect meridians only transversely.
Therefore, the longitude of
\gamma
is monotone.
Whence again
\gamma
is non-contractible.

15E
.

Check that
\nu(\gamma(t))=(\cos t,\sin t,0)
.
Calculate
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t)
, and show that it is proportional to
\nu(\gamma(t))
.
The latter is equivalent to
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t)\perp\mathrm{T}_{\gamma(t)}
.

Note that the line segment from
\gamma(0)
to
\gamma(2{{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}}\pi)
is contained in
\Sigma
.

15E
.

Assume two shortest paths
\alpha
and
\beta
have two common points
p
and
q
.
Denote by
\alpha_{1}
and
\beta_{1}
the arcs of
\alpha
and
\beta
from
p
to
q
.
Suppose
\alpha_{1}
is distinct from
\beta_{1}
.

Note that
\alpha_{1}
and
\beta_{1}
are shortest paths with the same endpoints;
in particular, they have the same length.
Exchanging
\alpha_{1}
in
\alpha
to
\beta_{1}
produces a shortest path, say
\hat{\alpha}
, that is distinct from
\alpha
.
By
15E
,
\hat{\alpha}
is a geodesic.

Suppose
\alpha_{1}
is a proper subarc of
\alpha
;
that is,
\alpha_{1}\neq\alpha
, or equivalently,
p
or
q
is not an endpoint of
\alpha
.
Then
\alpha
and
\hat{\alpha}
share one point and velocity vector at this point.
By
15B
,
\alpha
coincides with
\hat{\alpha}
— a contradiction.

It follows that
p
and
q
are the endpoints of
\alpha
.
Analogously,
p
and
q
are the endpoints of
\beta
.

For the second part, one could consider two distinct geodesics of the form

\gamma_{b}(t)=(\cos t,\sin t,b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}t),t\in\mathbb{R}

in the cylinder
x^{2}+y^{2}=1
.

15E
.

Assume a shortest path
\alpha
crosses
\Pi
at least twice.
In this case, there is a subarc
\alpha_{1}
of
\alpha
that lies strictly on one side on
\Pi
;
only its endpoints are on
\Pi
, and these endpoints are distinct from the endpoints of
\alpha
.

Let us remove the arc
\alpha_{1}
from
\alpha
and replace it with the reflection of
\alpha_{1}
across
\Pi
.
Note that the obtained curve, say
\beta
, lies on the surface;
it has the same length as
\alpha
, and it connects the same pair of points, say
p
and
q
.
Therefore,
\beta
is a shortest path from
p
to
q
in
\Sigma
that is distinct from
\alpha
.

We may assume that both
\alpha
and
\beta
have arc-length parametrization.
By
15E
,
\alpha
and
\beta
are geodesics.
Since
\alpha
and
\beta
have a common subarc, they share one point and velocity vector at this point;
by
15B

\alpha
coincides with
\beta
— a contradiction.

15E
.

Let
W
be the closed region outside
\Sigma
.
Show that the distance
|{p^{s}}-\penalty{q}|_{W}
is constant with respect to
s
.
This implies that the concatenation of the line segment
[p^{s},\gamma(s)]
and the arc
\gamma|_{[s,\ell]}
is a shortest path from
p^{s}
to
q
in
W
.

Since
\Sigma
is strictly convex,

|{\gamma(s)}-\penalty{q}|_{W}>|{\gamma(s)}-\penalty{q}|_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}

for all
s<\ell
.
Hence,

\displaystyle|{p^{s}}-\penalty{q}|_{W}

\displaystyle=|{p^{s}}-\penalty{\gamma(s)}|_{W}+|{\gamma(s)}-\penalty{q}|_{W}>

\displaystyle>|{p^{s}}-\penalty{\gamma(s)}|_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}+|{\gamma(s)}-\penalty{q}|_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\geqslant

\displaystyle\geqslant|{p^{s}}-\penalty{q}|_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}.

If the segment
[p^{s},q]
was entirely contained in
W
for some
s<\ell
, then
|{p^{s}}-\penalty{q}|_{W}=|{p^{s}}-\penalty{q}|_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}
, which would be a contradiction.

15F
.
Assume

.

15F
.

Equip
\Sigma
with unit normal field
\nu
that points inside.
Denote by
k(t)
the normal curvature of
\Sigma
at
\gamma(t)
in the direction of
\gamma^{\prime}(t)
.
Since
\Sigma
is convex,
k(t)\geqslant 0
for any

.

Since
\gamma
is a unit-speed geodesic, we have
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t)=k(t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\nu(\gamma(t))
and
\langle\gamma^{\prime}(t),\gamma^{\prime}(t)\rangle=1
for any
t
.
Without loss of generality, we can assume that

is the origin of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
Since
p
is inside
\Sigma
, we have that
\langle\gamma(t),\nu(\gamma(t))\rangle\leqslant 0
for any

.
It follows that

\langle\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t),\gamma(t)\rangle=k(t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\gamma(t),\nu(\gamma(t))\rangle\leqslant 0

for any
t
.
Conclude that

\displaystyle\rho^{\prime\prime}(t)=\langle\gamma(t),\gamma(t)\rangle^{\prime\prime}=

\displaystyle=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t),\gamma(t)\rangle+2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle\gamma^{\prime}(t),\gamma^{\prime}(t)\rangle\leqslant 2.

15G
.

We can assume
\gamma
is unit-speed.
Set
\nu(t)=\nu(\gamma(t))
.
Then
\langle\gamma^{\prime}(t),\nu(t)\rangle\equiv 0
. Also, since
\gamma
is a geodesic, we have that
\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t)\parallel\nu(t)
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle|\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t)|

\displaystyle=|\langle\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t),\nu(t)\rangle|=

\displaystyle=|\langle\gamma^{\prime}(t),\nu(t)\rangle^{\prime}-\langle\gamma^{\prime}(t),\nu^{\prime}(t)\rangle|=

\displaystyle=|\langle\gamma^{\prime}(t),\nu^{\prime}(t)\rangle|\leqslant

\displaystyle\leqslant|\gamma^{\prime}(t)|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|\nu^{\prime}(t)|=|\nu^{\prime}(t)|.

Integrating with respect to
t
, we get

\Phi({\gamma})\leqslant\mathop{\rm length}\nolimits(\nu\circ\gamma).

15G
.

➊

➋

and

\displaystyle\Phi({\gamma})

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}|\gamma^{\prime\prime}(t)|{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt=

\displaystyle=\sqrt{1+\ell^{2}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}z^{\prime\prime}(t){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}dt=

\displaystyle=2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell.

17A

17A
;

17A
.

It is sufficient to show that the surface has no geodesic loops.
Assume that there is a loop, estimate the integrals of the Gauss curvature over the entire surface and a disc surrounded by a geodesic loop.

17A
;
17A
.
By
14B
and
15E
, any two points in
\Sigma
can be connected by a geodesic.
Suppose points

and

.

and make a conclusion.

18C
.

By the Gauss lemma (
18A
), polar coordinates with respect to
q
produce a semigeodesic chart at any nearby point.
Therefore, it is sufficient to find a point
q\neq p
such that polar coordinates on
\Sigma
with respect to
q
cover
p
.
By
15D
, any
q
sufficiently close to
p
does the job.

18C
;
18C
.
Show that we can choose an orientation on
\Sigma
so that
b_{r}(0,\theta)=\penalty 1
for any
\theta
.
Conclude that we can assume that
b(r,\theta)>0
for all small
r>0
.

Suppose
b(r_{1},\theta_{1})<0
at a pair
(r_{1},\theta_{1})
such that
0<r_{1}<r_{0}
.
Observe that if
\theta_{2}
is sufficiency close to
\theta_{1}
, then the radial curves
r\mapsto b(r,\theta_{1})
and
r\mapsto b(r,\theta_{2})
defined on the interval
(0,r_{1})
intersect.
Therefore,
\exp_{p}
is not injective in
B
— a contradiction.

18C
.

Suppose
b(r_{1},\theta_{1})=0
.
Apply
18C
to show that
b_{r}(r_{1},\theta_{1})=0
.

Apply
18C
to conclude
b(r,\theta_{1})=0
for any
r
.
The latter contradicts that
b_{r}(0,\theta_{1})=1
.

18C
.

We need to show that
\exp_{p}
is regular in
B
.
Suppose vector
\text{\sc{v}}\in B
has polar coordinates
(r,\theta)
for some
r>0
.
Show that
\exp_{p}
is regular at
v
if
b(r,\theta)\neq 0
.
Conclude that
\exp_{p}
is regular in
B\setminus\{0\}
.

By
15C
,
\exp_{p}
is regular at
0
.
Whence
\exp_{p}|_{B}
is a regular injective smooth map.
Use the inverse function theorem (
0G
) to show that the restriction
\exp_{p}|_{B}
is a diffeomorphism to its image.

18C
;
18C
.
Since the frame
\text{\sc{u}},\text{\sc{v}},\nu
is orthonormal,
the first two vector identities are equivalent to the following six real identities:

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{u},\text{\sc{u}}\rangle

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{v}}_{u},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle

\displaystyle=0,

➊

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{u},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{b}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}a_{v},

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{v}}_{u},\text{\sc{u}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{b}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}a_{v},

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{u},\nu\rangle

\displaystyle=a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell,

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{v}}_{u},\nu\rangle

\displaystyle=a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}m.

Taking the partial derivatives of the identities

\langle\text{\sc{u}},\text{\sc{u}}\rangle=1
and

\langle\text{\sc{v}},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle=1
with respect to
u
,
we get the first two identities in
➊
.

Further, observe that

\displaystyle\text{\sc{v}}_{u}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial v}(\tfrac{1}{b}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s_{v})=\tfrac{1}{b}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s_{uv}+\tfrac{\partial}{\partial u}(\tfrac{1}{b}){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}s_{v}.

➋

Since
s_{u}\perp s_{v}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{v}}_{u},\text{\sc{u}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}b}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle s_{vu},s_{u}\rangle=

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}b}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\tfrac{\partial}{\partial v}\langle s_{u},s_{u}\rangle=

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}b}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\tfrac{\partial a^{2}}{\partial v}=

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{b}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}a_{v}.

Taking the partial derivative of
\langle\text{\sc{u}},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle=0
with respect to
u

we get

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{v}}_{u},\text{\sc{u}}\rangle+\langle\text{\sc{v}},\text{\sc{u}}_{u}\rangle

\displaystyle=0.

Hence, we get two more identities in
➊
.

Since
u
and
v
are orthonormal,
10D
implies

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{a^{2}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle s_{uu},\nu\rangle

\displaystyle=\ell,

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}b}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle s_{uv},\nu\rangle

\displaystyle=m,

➌

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}b}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle s_{vu},\nu\rangle

\displaystyle=m,

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{b^{2}}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle s_{vv},\nu\rangle

\displaystyle=n.

Applying
➋
,
➌
, and
s_{v}\perp\nu
we get

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{u},\nu\rangle

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{a}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle s_{uu},\nu\rangle=a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\ell,

\displaystyle\langle\text{\sc{v}}_{u},\nu\rangle

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{a}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\langle s_{uv},\nu\rangle=a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}m.

It implies the last two equalities in
➊
.

Therefore, the first two identities in
18C
are proved;
the remaining two identities can be proved along the same lines.

18C
.

Recall that the Gauss curvature equals the determinant of the matrix
(\begin{smallmatrix}\ell&m\\
m&n\end{smallmatrix})
;
that is,
K=\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}n-m^{2}
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}K

\displaystyle=a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}b{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(\ell{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}n-m^{2})=

\displaystyle=\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{u},\text{\sc{v}}_{v}\rangle-\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{v},\text{\sc{v}}_{u}\rangle=

\displaystyle=\left(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial v}\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{u},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle-\cancel{\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{uv},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle}\right)-

\displaystyle-\left(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial u}\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{v},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle-\cancel{\langle\text{\sc{u}}_{uv},\text{\sc{v}}\rangle}\right)=

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial v}(-\tfrac{1}{b}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}a_{v})-\tfrac{\partial}{\partial u}(\tfrac{1}{a}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}b_{u}).

18C
.

Apply
18C
assuming that
a=b
, and simplify.

18G
.

Verify that an intrinsic isometry maps shortest paths to shortest paths and apply
15E
.

18G
.

Note and use that by
18F
,
\exp_{p}
is length-preserving.

18G
.
Modify the proof of
18F
to show that
K\equiv 1
implies that
b(\theta,r)=\penalty\sin r
for all small
r\geqslant 0
.

18G
.

To find
x(t)
, one may solve the differential equation

x^{\prime}(t)=\sqrt{1-|y^{\prime}(t)|^{2}}
.
Its solution is defined while
y(t)>0
and
|y^{\prime}(t)|=\penalty|a{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\sin t|<1
.

Apply
11B
to calculate Gauss curvature of
\Sigma_{a}
.

By
18G
, small discs
\Delta_{a}
in
\Sigma_{a}
admit intrinsic isometry to a disc in a unit sphere.
To show that
\Delta_{a}
is not congruent to a spherical disc
\Delta_{1}
for
a\neq 1
, show that its principal curvature is not 1 at some point.

18G
.

Observe that for any two points
p
and
q
in the
(u,v)
plane we have
|f(p)-f(q)|\leqslant|p-q|
.

Let
f(u,v)=(x(u,v),y(u,v),z(u,v))
;
we can assume that
z(0,v)=v
for any
v
.

Apply the observation and the triangle inequality to points
(0,-a)
,
(u,v_{1})
,
(u,v_{2})
, and
(0,a)
for
a\to\infty
to show that

\displaystyle z(u,v_{1})

\displaystyle=v_{1},

\displaystyle z(u,v_{2})

\displaystyle=v_{2},

\displaystyle x(u,v_{1})

\displaystyle=x(u,v_{2}),

\displaystyle y(u,v_{1})

\displaystyle=y(u,v_{2}).

Make a conclusion.

19A
.

Apply the triangle inequality
c_{n}\leqslant a_{n}+b_{n}
and the bound
a_{n},b_{n}>\varepsilon
to show that the sequence

s_{n}=\frac{a_{n}+b_{n}+c_{n}}{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}a_{n}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}b_{n}}

is bounded.
Conclude that

\frac{(a_{n}+b_{n})^{2}-c_{n}^{2}}{2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}a_{n}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}b_{n}}=s_{n}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}(a_{n}+b_{n}-c_{n})\to 0

as
n\to\infty
.
Use the last statement together with the cosine rule

a_{n}^{2}+b_{n}^{2}-2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}a_{n}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}b_{n}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\cos\tilde{\theta}_{n}-c_{n}^{2}=0

to show that
\cos\tilde{\theta}_{n}\to-1
as
n\to\infty
;
conclude that
\theta_{n}\to\pi
.

19B
.

Consider the triangle on the hyperboloid with vertices
(1,0,0)
,
(-\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2},0)
, and
(-\tfrac{1}{2},-\tfrac{\sqrt{3}}{2},0)
.
Note that all its angles are
\pi
, but all model angles are
\tfrac{\pi}{3}
.

19B
.

Show that
|{p}-\penalty{x}|_{\Sigma}\leqslant|{p}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}
and
|{q}-\penalty{x}|_{\Sigma}\leqslant|{p}-\penalty{q}|_{\Sigma}
.
Conclude that

\tilde{\measuredangle}(x\,{}^{p}_{q}\hskip-0.68887pt)\geqslant\tfrac{\pi}{3}.

Apply
19B
.

19B
.

Show that

\measuredangle[p\,{}^{x}_{y}]+\measuredangle[p\,{}^{y}_{z}]+\measuredangle[p\,{}^{z}_{x}]\leqslant 2{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\pi
.
Apply
19B
.

19E
.

Choose vectors
u
, and
w

such that
|\text{\sc{w}}|=\penalty|{p}-\penalty{x}|
,
|\text{\sc{u}}|=1
, and
\measuredangle(\text{\sc{u}},\text{\sc{w}})=\measuredangle[x\,{}^{p}_{q}]
.
Consider the function

f\colon t\mapsto t+|\text{\sc{w}}|-|\text{\sc{w}}-t{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}\text{\sc{u}}|
.
Observe that
f(0)=\penalty 0
,

\displaystyle f(|{x}-\penalty{y}|)

\displaystyle=|{x}-\penalty{p}|+|{x}-\penalty{y}|-\tilde{\curlyvee}[x\,{}^{p}_{y}],

\displaystyle f(|{x}-\penalty{q}|)

\displaystyle=|{x}-\penalty{p}|+|{x}-\penalty{q}|-\tilde{\curlyvee}[x\,{}^{p}_{q}].

Show and use that
f
is concave.

19E
;
19E
.
Apply the Rauch comparison (
18F
) and the properties of the exponential map in
15D
.

19E
.
Argue by contradiction.
If the statement does not hold, then for any
p\in\Sigma
there is a point
q=q(p)\in\Sigma
such that

|{q}-\penalty{p}|_{\Sigma}<100{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}R_{p}

and

R_{q}<(1-\tfrac{1}{100}){\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}R_{p}
.

Start with any point
p_{0}
, and consider a sequence
p_{n}
defined by
p_{n+1}\mathrel{:=}\penalty q(p_{n})
.
Show that
p_{n}
converges, and
R_{p_{n}}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.
Arrive at a contradiction using
19E
.

19E
.
Repeat the proof assuming that
p
is provided by
19E
.

19G
.

Apply
19G
and
19G
.

19G
.
Use
19G
or
19G
twice:
first — for the triangle
[pxy]
and
\bar{x}\in[p,x]
;
second — for the triangle
[p\bar{x}y]
and
\bar{y}\in[p,y]
.
Then apply the angle monotonicity (
0C
).

19G
.

It is sufficient to prove the Jensen inequality;
that is,

\displaystyle|{\gamma_{1}(\tfrac{1}{2})}-\penalty{\gamma_{2}(\tfrac{1}{2})}|\leqslant\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}

\displaystyle|{\gamma_{1}(0)}-\penalty{\gamma_{2}(0)}|+

\displaystyle+\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}

\displaystyle|{\gamma_{1}(1)}-\penalty{\gamma_{2}(1)}|.

Let
\delta
be the geodesic path from
\gamma_{2}(0)
to
\gamma_{1}(1)
.
From
19G
, we have

\displaystyle|{\gamma_{1}(\tfrac{1}{2})}-\penalty{\delta(\tfrac{1}{2})}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{\gamma_{1}(0)}-\penalty{\delta(0)}|,

\displaystyle|{\delta(\tfrac{1}{2})}-\penalty{\gamma_{2}(\tfrac{1}{2})}|

\displaystyle\leqslant\tfrac{1}{2}{\hskip 0.5pt\cdot\penalty\hskip 0.5pt}|{\delta(1)}-\penalty{\gamma_{2}(1)}|.

Sum it up, and apply the triangle inequality.

Remark.
Modulo the comparison theorem,
the case of the Euclidean plane is just as hard.

19G
.
Consider a ball
B=\bar{B}[p,R]_{\Sigma}
.
Given a point
x\in B
choose a geodesic
[p,x]
and denote by
\tilde{x}
the point in
\mathrm{T}_{p}
that lies on the distance
|p-x|_{\Sigma}
from
p
in the direction of
[p,x]
.
The map
x\mapsto\tilde{x}
send
B
into the
R
-ball in
\mathrm{T}_{p}
;
by
19G
, this map is distance-noncontracting.
Whence the statement follows.

19G
.
Let
p\in\Sigma
be the center of
\Delta
.

19G
.

Consider geodesic triangle
[pxy]_{\Sigma}
with
|p-x|_{\Sigma}=|p-y|_{\Sigma}=R
;
set
\alpha=\measuredangle[x\,{}^{p}_{y}]
,

\beta=\measuredangle[y\,{}^{p}_{x}]
and
\ell=|x-y|_{\Sigma}
.
Use
19G
to show that

\pi-\alpha-\beta>\tfrac{\ell}{R}+o(\ell).

Denote by
\sigma
the arc of
\partial\Delta
from
x
to
y
.
Note that
[x,y]_{\Sigma}
lies in the region bounded by
[p,x]
,
[p,y]
, and
\sigma
.
Apply the Gauss–Bonnet formula to show that
\Psi({\sigma})>\pi-\alpha-\beta
.
Pass to a limit as
\ell\to 0
, and make a conclusion.

19G
.

By
19G
, the map
\exp_{p}\colon\mathrm{T}_{p}\to\Sigma
is distance-noncontracting.
By
15E
, it maps the
R
-ball in
\mathrm{T}_{p}
centered at the origin onto
\Delta
.
Whence the statement follows.

19G
.

Mimic the proof of
7E
using
19G
.
For the last statement, apply
19G
.

Suggested by Dmitri Burago.

Chapter 39
Afterword

After our book, if you continue with Riemannian geometry,
then half of the material in this subject will look familiar.
But first, you need to read a text on tensor calculus;
the book by Richard Bishop and Samuel Goldberg
[
9
]
is one of our favorites.

Let us list a few introductory texts we know and love that range from very detailed to very condensed and challenging:

\diamond

‘‘Riemannian manifolds’’
[
60
]
by John Lee.

\diamond

‘‘Riemannian geometry’’
[
15
]
by Manfredo do Carmo.

\diamond

‘‘Riemannsche Geometrie im Großen’’
[
37
]
by
Detlef Gromoll,
Wilhelm Klingenberg,
and Werner Meyer;
it is in German, plus there is a Russian translation.
In Russian, there is a more elementary textbook by Yurii Burago and Viktor Zalgaller
[
14
]
.

\diamond

‘‘Comparison geometry’’
[
21
]
by Jeff Cheeger and David Ebin.

\diamond

‘‘Sign and geometric meaning of curvature’’
[
38
]
by Mikhael Gromov.

Good luck.

Anton Petrunin and Sergio Zamora Barrera,

State College, Pennsylvania, May 11, 2021.
